### <a id="issue-634"></a>[#634](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/634) May the compiler run a call with constant arguments at build time as an optimisation, where no `comptime` asks for it?

`decision` · opened 2026-10-17 · updated 2026-10-17 · 0 comments

#### Where this comes from

The owner split this off from #633 (October 17, 2026). #633 decides that a call with constant arguments is compiled and run at build time **where a claim needs its value** (ADR-359 D11-D13, the ADR-321 path). What is left open is the other use: running such a call at build time **only to save work at run time**, where neither a `comptime` nor a claim asks for it.

#### The question

`let table = build_table(256)` is a `sync` call that touches nothing and has constant arguments. The program does not write `comptime`. May the compiler, on its own, run it while building and put the value into the program, the way ADR-321 runs a `comptime`?

#### What is known

* **Who pays.** At run time, every user pays on every run. At build time, the developer pays once per build, and with ADR-321 D4's cache, only when something changed. For a program that runs often, the shift pays off; for code that is rarely or never reached at run time, it is pure cost.
* **The meaning does not change.** ADR-321 D1 runs the code compiled by the same implementation, so an overflow or an abort behaves the same. A call that stops at build time would have stopped the program wherever it is reached. Whether it is reached is exactly what an optimisation does not know, so a stop there may not refuse the build.
* **LLVM already folds** small cases in a release build (`example(2)`). The gain lies where LLVM gives up: large loops, allocation, tables.
* **Bounds:** ADR-321 D7 (step budget, counted), D10 (memory), D11 (raised per `comptime`). Without a `comptime` there is no place to raise them.
* **Determinism:** only a body whose touch set is empty qualifies. `args read` means the builder's arguments at build time and the user's at run time.

#### Still owed before a decision round

1. Options: never (the program writes `comptime` when it wants the shift); a hint (`comptime` would move this to the build); automatic within a budget; automatic only in a release build.
2. For each option: who pays, the effect on build time and on the cache, what happens when the budget runs out (silently left to run time?), and how the user sees it.
3. How others do it: C++ (`constexpr` is evaluated only where required; optimisers fold), Zig (`comptime` only where written), Rust (`const` only; LLVM folds), GraalVM/partial evaluation.
4. A count: how many calls in the corpus would qualify, and what they cost at run time (callgrind, counted).
5. A recommendation (CLAUDE.md, *Decision rounds with the owner*).

### <a id="issue-597"></a>[#597](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/597) Decision basis: the text-tier walk cannot see that a pattern binding is a view, so a mixed `match` asks for `.clone()` where `EitherText` would do

`compiler`, `decision` · opened 2026-10-12 · updated 2026-10-12 · 1 comments

#### What it is

Text has three representations below, chosen per position by what flows in (ADR-282 D12-D15):
- owned;
- a view (`ref String`);
- both (`EitherText`).

The walk that chooses them, `tools/text_tiers.nika` (`text_tiers::refine`, called from the parser), runs **before** the checker. It has no types: it reads them off the syntax (`tier_struct_of`, `source_of`, `place_of`).

```nika
fn f(v: ref Val) -> String {
    return match v {
        Val::Int(n) => int_text(n),   // owned
        Val::Text(t) => t,            // a view: v is lent, so t is a view of its part (Part I 3.4)
        else => "none".clone(),
    }
}
```

By ADR-282 the result is mixed, so it should be `EitherText`. The walk does not bind pattern names. An unknown name counts as owned, so it decides `String`.

#### Where it comes from, and what needs it now

#562's analysis, by the session that took #562 (12 Oct). Order decided by the owner the same day:
1. First #596 fixes the checker, so this case becomes `NK1104` at `t`, naming `t.clone()`, instead of a rustc error. ADR-282 D13 ("leaves the old refusal and never a wrong program").
2. Then #562 fixes the lowering of a mixed `match`.
3. Only then this issue: the refusal is an **over-refusal**. The program is correct by ADR-282 D15 and runs as `EitherText` without a copy.

Nothing is blocked. A program writes `t.clone()` and pays a copy it would not need.

#### Facts (checked on 0.0.854 by the #562 session and in the owner's round)

- The same mix through a `ref String` **parameter** is decided correctly (`EitherText`). With two `return`s it runs; as a `match` it hits #562's lowering defect.
- It fails for every scrutinee form that lends: `match self.val`, `match v` in a `for` over `self.all`, `match first(h)`. Not only a `ref` parameter.
- **Over a name the function owns, the arm takes the part** (Part I 3.4). There `t` is owned and today's answer is right. So the kind of `t` is the kind of the scrutinee's place.
- The compiler guesses types from syntax in three places: the checker, `views.nika` (`type_of`, `fields_reached`) and `text_tiers.nika`.

#### Options

1. **Pattern bindings in the walk.**
   - The kind of a binding to a text payload is the kind of the scrutinee's place (`place_of`).
   - The payload's type comes from the enum's declaration, which is syntax: no inference.
   - Each scrutinee form the walk does not cover stays owned. After #596 that is an over-refusal with a `.clone()` help, never a wrong program.
   - Cost: the third syntax approximation grows.
2. **Every binding of unknown origin counts as "may be a view".**
   - Always safe for the representation.
   - But it makes positions mixed that are owned today, and in a `pub` function refuses them (`NK1104`, ADR-282 D22), where they run today.
3. **The tier decision moves after the checker.**
   - One source of types: the checker's, per expression, through the expression ids of ADR-340 (#558, open).
   - `views.nika` could read the same source.
   - It changes ADR-282 D13 ("runs to a fixpoint … before anything else looks at the program"), the order of the phases, and the self-hosting plan for check and emit (`docs/selfhosting-plan.md`).

#### Owed before the owner is asked

- Today's phase order and the one option 3 needs. Does the checker need the tiers? Where: `NK1104` and the `pub` refusals of D22-D25.
- An inventory of the places that guess types, with what each guesses.
- How many `.clone()`s option 1 would remove from `std`'s Nikaia half and the examples. Count the `.clone()`s on pattern bindings of a lent scrutinee.
- Whether #558 is far enough along for option 3.
- A recommendation.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-12](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/597#issuecomment-6131792792):

#### State after #596 (0.0.860) and #562 (0.0.861)

- **Mixed positions now run**, wherever the walk sees both kinds: `return match`, a tail `match`, `let r = match … return r`, `if` chains, a mixed field, and an argument to a mixed parameter. The `ref String` and `String` arms of an unannotated `let` meet at `String` where the function's result is mixed or not text.
- **What is left of this issue** is only where the walk does not see the view: a pattern binding of a lent scrutinee, as in this issue's `f`. Checked on `54f34f2`: it is `NK1104` at the `return match`, with *"Write `.clone()` to copy it here"*. That is a refusal, not a rustc error.
- **The refusal's caret** stands at the whole `return`/`let`, not at the arm: expressions carry no span of their own yet (#558 / ADR-340).

### <a id="issue-593"></a>[#593](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/593) Decision: generic traits (`trait Map[K, V]`), and a standard `Map` trait the ecosystem can build on

`language`, `decision` · opened 2026-10-12 · updated 2026-10-12 · 1 comments

#### What this is, from zero

A **trait** says what a type must be able to do, so different types can be used the same way (Part I 4.7). Nikaia's traits cannot take type parameters. The owner would like a standard **`Map` trait** for the ecosystem: one lowest-common-denominator interface so libraries can accept "a map" and be written against a shared standard, as other languages offer (Rust has none for maps; Java `Map`, C# `IDictionary`, Go `map` built in, Swift `Dictionary`/protocols, Kotlin `Map`). This is **not needed by the repository's own code or self-hosting**; the point is to let libraries share one interface while staying fast.

```
trait Map[K, V] {
    fn len(ref self) -> i64
    fn contains_key(ref self, key: ref K) -> bool
    fn get(ref self, key: ref K) -> V?
    fn insert(ref mut self, key: K, value: V)
    fn remove(ref mut self, key: ref K) -> V?
    fn clear(ref mut self)
}
fn total[M: Map[String, i64]](m: ref M) -> i64 { return m.len() }
```

#### What the user sees today (tried on 0.0.850)

* `trait Map[K, V] { … }` is a parse error: *Expected `{` here, but found `[`*. Traits have no type parameters.
* Part I 4.7: a trait method has **no default body**, a trait is not a type (**no `dyn`**), and there are **no associated types**.
* An `impl Trait for collections::BTreeMap[String, i64]` is allowed (4.7: a trait is how a std type gets methods), but the probe for it was refused with `NK2302` on `self.contains_key(key)` ("keeps `key`"), which is #585 item 4, not this issue.
* Rust's `get_mut(&mut self) -> Option<&mut V>` has no equivalent: no `ref mut` result (`import-rust-design.md` §7).

#### Where it comes from

The owner, in the #589 session (October 12, 2026), after the question whether a `BTreeMap` can be passed where a `HashMap` is wanted (it cannot: `NK1102`; they are two types, and the hasher of a `HashMap` is chosen by the compiler per ADR-010 D5/D9). Nothing concrete needs it now: it is ecosystem design.

#### Facts that bound the answer

1. **Generic traits are the actual feature**, and they are not map-specific: any shared interface (`Iterator`-like, `Stream[T]`, `Repository[T]`) needs them. Parser, ledger (a trait entry with parameters), bound checking (`NK1164` with arguments), lowering (Rust traits are generic already) all change.
2. **`HashMap` is two Rust types** (`TrustedMap` / keyed) chosen by the compiler; after #581 a hidden type parameter at package boundaries. A `Map` trait implemented for `HashMap` must cover that without the user naming either. #581 should land first or define the interaction.
3. **The key bound**: a `Map[K, V]` for both families needs `K: Hash + Eq` for one and `K: Ord` for the other. Without named bounds the trait could only be implemented for a fixed key type. See the std-bounds issue (filed beside this one).
4. **Views and keeps**: `get(ref self, key: ref K) -> V?` returns something that may point into the map; the NK2302 rules (ADR-283 D25) make "a result into `self` only" the natural reading, but a trait method has no body, so D25 uses the receiver. `ref mut` results do not exist, so no `get_mut`; mutation goes through `insert`/`update` shapes.
5. **Performance**: a trait bound is monomorphised (no `dyn`), so a generic function over `M: Map[K, V]` costs nothing over the concrete map. A `dyn Map` would cost an indirection and is not on offer.

#### Options

* **(a)** Generic traits in general (the language feature), then `Map[K, V]` is an ordinary library trait in `std::collections` with `impl`s for `HashMap`/`BTreeMap`. Needs the std-bounds decision for the key.
* **(b)** A built-in `Map` trait only (the compiler special-cases it). Quick, but a second mechanism and not available to libraries for their own interfaces.
* **(c)** No trait: libraries take a concrete map, or declare their own non-generic trait per key/value type.

#### Risks

(a) is a large, irreversible language addition and the first step toward associated types/default bodies/`dyn`, each requested later ("the pressure of `import rust`", `import-rust-design.md` §7). The language's stance is fewer features on purpose; generics for traits are the one Rust feature most libraries cannot work around, which argues for (a) rather than (b). A trait shared by the ecosystem is also hard to change after libraries adopt it: a method added later breaks every `impl`.

#### Not prepared well enough to ask

Missing: how Go/Swift/Kotlin/Zig do shared collection interfaces and what they paid; the NK2302 rules for a generic trait method; the interaction with #581; a measured cost (none expected, monomorphised). Prepare that basis first; this issue is the place for it.

#### Recommendation

File and keep as `decision`; take the std-bounds issue first (smaller, needed by this), then prepare (a) with the interaction with #581. Do not build anything yet.

Related: #581, #585, #589 (built), #514, and the std-bounds and `HashMap`-key issues filed beside this one.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-12](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/593#issuecomment-6122667042):

The issues "filed beside this one" are: #591 (a `HashMap`/`HashSet` key that cannot be hashed passes the checker) and #592 (the std bounds `Ord`/`Eq`/`Hash` cannot be named). Take #592 first.

### <a id="issue-588"></a>[#588](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/588) Decision: `fn longest(a: String, b: String) -> ref String` is NK2302 since D25. Keep the refusal?

`language`, `decision` · opened 2026-10-12 · updated 2026-10-12 · 0 comments

#### What this is, from zero

A function can hand back a *view* of one of its arguments instead of a copy:

```
fn first_word(text: String) -> ref String { text.split(' ')[0] }
```

The result points into `text`'s buffer, so it is valid as long as `text` is. Nikaia writes no lifetimes; the compiler works out which argument the result points into. That is easy with one source. With two it is a question:

```
fn longest(a: String, b: String) -> ref String {
    if a.len() > b.len() { return a }
    return b
}
```

Which buffer does the result point into? It depends on a run-time comparison. The caller cannot know, so it must keep **both** `a` and `b` alive as long as it uses the result. That is what the language did until 0.0.836 (parameters lowered as views shared one lifetime: "union"), silently: every caller met the constraint at its own line.

#### Where the question comes from

#580 (ADR-283 D25, written by the Opus review of the owner's note on NK2302, 2026-10-11): a result views **exactly one source** over all ways out; two sources chosen at run time are refused with `NK2302`. Part I 6.5 says `ref` on a parameter is only an assertion, so `longest(a: String, b: String)` and `longest(a: ref String, ...)` follow the same rule. This **took back something that compiled before**. It is isolated in `crates/nikaia/tests/result_source.rs::plain_string_longest_is_refused_since_d25` (the doc comment says how to revert). One existing test program had the shape (`tests/language/src/views_handed_back.nika`, `views_handed_back_first`) and was rewritten to return `ref a` or a literal, same output.

#### Facts

* Today: refused, with a message at buffer level and a caret on each way out; the program compiles when rewritten to return `ref a` or a copy (`a.clone()`), or split in two functions.
* Before: accepted, silently tying the result to both arguments; the caller cannot use the result after either argument ends (probe: "Cannot borrow `y`" for a function that returns only literals).
* In the compiler's remaining Rust: 4 of 41 functions that return views choose at run time (`payloads`, `options_of` and two more); 0 of the 17 in `check/`+`emit/` need the union.
* Rust itself writes this with an explicit lifetime on both arguments and the result.

#### Options

* **Keep the refusal** (D25 as written): honest per call, one rule for `ref` and plain parameters; costs a rewrite where a run-time choice is real.
* **Accept a union form by an explicit marker** (new syntax, e.g. `-> ref(a | b) String`, the spelling the ledger already uses for `ref(self | other)`): runs against ADR-283 "nothing is written"; every caller must keep both buffers.
* **Accept it implicitly again** (union): what compiled before; restores the silent constraint on every caller.

#### Risk if wrong

Keeping the refusal: a user with a real run-time choice must clone or split. Reverting: callers fail far from the definition.

#### Recommendation

Keep the refusal (Part I 6.5 and 6.6 are then consistent, and the owner's note chose a hard stop for run-time ambiguity). Revisit only with an explicit marker if a measured cost of the clone shows.

Related: #580, #579 (the importer meets this shape often), `docs/mitigation-hurdles.md` section 1.

### <a id="issue-540"></a>[#540](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/540) A benchmark table in the README, regenerated by a script, with build time beside run time

`performance`, `decision` · opened 2026-10-07 · updated 2026-10-09 · 0 comments

**Where it stands.** The README and the manifesto argue the speed claim in prose. The measurements exist and are thorough (`benches/brc` naive against tuned Rust, `benches/overlap`, `benches/lockfloor`, `benches/fallible`, the callgrind harness in `crates/nikaia/tests/measure.rs`, the CLBG programs `examples/n-body` and `examples/k-nucleotide`, `examples/1brc.nika`). They are written for the compiler's own decisions, though, and a reader deciding whether to try the language never sees a number.

**Proposal.** One small table near the top of the README, in the roadmap's style (*counted, not believed*):

| program | Python | Go | Nikaia | Rust (hand-written) | Nikaia build, cold / warm |
| :--- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| n-body | … | … | … | … | … |
| k-nucleotide | … | … | … | … | … |
| 1BRC (fixed row count) | … | … | … | … | … |
| one HTTP handler, requests/s | … | … | … | … | … |

* **Every number is re-derivable.** One script (`scripts/bench_table.py` or a `benches/table/` directory) builds and runs everything and rewrites the table, as `scripts/self_hosting.py` rewrites the self-hosting badge. It records the machine, the versions and the date beside the table.
* **The reference programs are the established ones** (the CLBG entries for n-body and k-nucleotide, the 1BRC baseline), not written for the occasion, so nobody can say the other side was handicapped.
* **The build-time column is there on purpose.** Lowering through `rustc` is where Nikaia is slow, and the cold/warm split (the per-machine `std` cache of Part III 13.2b, the content-addressed store of ADR-021) shows both the cost and what the cache buys. Leaving it out would be the kind of omission the roadmap refuses elsewhere.

**To decide.** Which comparison languages (Python and Go are the README's own "easy languages" and the stated target audience; Rust is the floor), whether the HTTP row waits for #121 (many connections at once), and whether CI checks the table against the script's output or only the script runs.

### <a id="issue-434"></a>[#434](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/434) The entry chain: measure TrustedMap on hashbrown directly

`libraries`, `performance`, `decision` · opened 2026-10-04 · updated 2026-10-09 · 1 comments

Part of #420. `entry(k).and_modify(f).or_insert_with(g)` on `TrustedMap` goes through std's `HashMap::entry`. That calls hashbrown's `rustc_entry` and converts its result into std's own `Entry` on every call. Measured on 1BRC's generated Rust, 1 M rows, callgrind:

| | instructions a row |
|---|---:|
| `entry` chain (as emitted) | 319.1 |
| `match get_mut(k) { Some(v) => f(v), None => insert(k, g()) }` | 313.1 |
| `get_mut`/`insert` with `record` inlined (#433) | 286.2 |

`get_mut`/`insert` is **not** a general lowering. It wins where keys are mostly present already, as in 1BRC (413 names, a million rows). Where keys are mostly new, it hashes and probes twice per insert. The compiler cannot know the ratio.

**To measure first:** `TrustedMap` as `hashbrown::HashMap<K, V, BuildHasherDefault<FxHasher>>`, whose `entry` has the same behaviour without std's conversion and without a second probe on insert. Does that take the 6 instructions, and does it cost anything on an insert-heavy program?

**What it would change:** trusted and untrusted maps become two Rust types instead of one std `HashMap` with two hashers. The type check that accepts `TrustedMap` as `HashMap`, the message translation in `tools/rustc_words.nika`, and every place that converts between the two would have to follow.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-04](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/434#issuecomment-5981791110):

**Measured.** `TrustedMap` swapped for `hashbrown::HashMap<K, V, BuildHasherDefault<nikaia_std::hash::FxHasher>>` (hashbrown 0.15) in the generated Rust, nothing else changed. Profile as `nikaia build` writes it: `opt-level = 3`, 256 codegen units, overflow checks in the program's crate only. Callgrind with `--branch-sim=yes`, 1 M rows / keys, same output byte for byte.

| | instructions | mispredictions |
|---|---:|---:|
| 1BRC, std `HashMap` (0.0.440, `record` already inlined by #433) | 300.2 a row | 1.58 a row |
| 1BRC, hashbrown directly | **297.2** a row | 1.58 a row |
| insert-heavy (`entry(k).and_modify(…).or_insert_with(…)`, every key new, `String` keys), std | 985.3 a key | 1.65 a key |
| insert-heavy, hashbrown directly | **953.9** a key | 1.65 a key |

Answers to the two questions:
- **It takes 3 of the 6 instructions** on 1BRC, not all of them: 300.2 → 297.2. The `get_mut`/`insert` lowering's remaining edge (286.2 in the table above) comes from skipping the entry machinery altogether, which hashbrown's `entry` does not do.
- **It costs nothing on an insert-heavy program.** It saves 3.2 %, because std's conversion of `rustc_entry` is paid on every call, new key or not.

So the direct map is a small, uniform gain with no case found where it loses. Against it is what the issue already lists: two Rust types instead of one, so the type check that accepts `TrustedMap` as `HashMap`, the message translation in `tools/rustc_words.nika` and every conversion have to follow. On 1BRC that is about 1 %, so whether to build it is a cost/benefit call, not one the measurement settles.

### <a id="issue-432"></a>[#432](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/432) The prover: a counter per consumed item cannot overflow, bounded by the input's size

`compiler`, `performance`, `decision` · opened 2026-10-04 · updated 2026-10-09 · 0 comments

#420 assumes the overflow checks in 1BRC's `Stats::add` cannot be proved, because "a sum over a file is unbounded". With four facts the compiler could have, they can:

1. **The input is bounded.** `fs::map` maps the file into the address space, which on x86-64 is at most 2^47 bytes for a user program. That is a platform fact, like a pointer's width.
2. **Each item consumes bytes.** The grammar says how few bytes a `MEASUREMENT` can have: `NAME` may be empty, then `;`, at least `0.0`, and `\n`, so 5. A `par_fold` therefore has at most 2^47 / 5 items.
3. **Each item adds a bounded amount.** `count += 1`. `sum += temp`, where |temp| ≤ 999 follows from `dec[i32](digit{1,2})` and the action under ADR-314 D1.
4. **An invariant over the fold and its merge.** `count` and |`sum`| grow by at most 1 and 999 per item, and `merge` only adds two partial results that each satisfy that bound.

From these: `count` ≤ 2.8·10^13 and |`sum`| ≤ 2.8·10^16, both far below `i64`'s 9.2·10^18. Both checks are provably unnecessary.

**What it is worth:** on 1BRC about 2 instructions a row, the two overflow branches, which are always predicted (callgrind, 0.0.433). It is more interesting in general: counters and sums in an aggregation are among the most common integer code, and today every one of them keeps its check.

**What it needs, beyond ADR-314:**
- a bound on input size per source;
- the minimum width of a grammar rule's match;
- a relational invariant across a `par_fold`'s step and merge, of the form "this field grows by at most k per item".

That is a decision for an ADR of its own. Until then the checks stay, as the language says (ADR-285 D1).

[Back to the list](#decision)

### <a id="issue-422"></a>[#422](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/422) A grammar over bytes, with only what becomes text checked as UTF-8

`libraries`, `compiler`, `performance`, `decision` · opened 2026-10-04 · updated 2026-10-09 · 0 comments

ADR-016 §4 defers it ("fuse the check into the piece - right, and deferred"); this issue keeps it in view, to be done last of the 1BRC work.

Today `fs::map` checks the whole mapping (ADR-016 D1, now with SIMD, D4) because every view a grammar cuts out of it is a `&str`. On 1BRC that is about 10 instructions a row after 0.0.424. `tuned` pays nothing for it: it works on bytes and turns only the 413 distinct names into text, once each, at the end - and, unlike Nikaia, it reads a stray byte in a temperature as a wrong number.

The shape that keeps D1's guarantee and costs what `tuned` pays:
- the parser backend (`winnow-grammar`) reads bytes;
- what the grammar matches as ASCII - digits, literals, separators - needs no check, since it was compared byte by byte;
- what becomes text - here `NAME`'s `until(…)` - is checked where it becomes a `ref String`, and a value a map already holds needs no second check.

A language-level decision about what a view is, so it needs its own ADR before code: the error a program sees for a bad byte, and where (today: before the parse, at its offset), must stay as good.

### <a id="issue-391"></a>[#391](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/391) A grammar rule's result as a bounded number, and a bound written in a type

`language`, `compiler`, `grammar`, `performance`, `decision` · opened 2026-10-03 · updated 2026-10-09 · 0 comments

Two points that [ADR-276](../blob/main/docs/specification/adr/adr-276.md) §7 and [ADR-272](../blob/main/docs/specification/adr/adr-272.md) §6 deliberately leave open.

#### 1. A rule's result as a bounded number (ADR-276 D1, last bullet)
- **The case:** `temp:TENTHS` is an `i32`. If every alternative of `TENTHS` is proved within `-999..=999`, its callers could know that too.
- **The mechanism:** this is ADR-276 D3 (`ensures`) applied to grammar rules. Each rule would get a postcondition derived from its actions.

#### 2. A bound written in a type (ADR-272 Option C, and FunArray segments)
- **The idea:** `Vec[u32 in 0..<32000]`, Wuffs/Ada style. It reaches what inference cannot: a list behind a parameter, or across a call.
- **Status:** ADR-272 defers it until the evidence shows what inference misses. `nikaia --bounds` and the measurements in `solver-workload.md` §8.4 would supply that evidence.

### <a id="issue-381"></a>[#381](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/381) Smaller inconsistencies found while consolidating the ADRs

`decision` · opened 2026-10-03 · updated 2026-10-09 · 0 comments

Found while every ADR was checked against the specification and the code (PR #371). Each item is small. None blocked the consolidation, and each needs either a one-line decision or a check against the code.

#### Records against each other or the code

- [x] **`RESERVED_BELOW` lists `ref` and `trait`** (`crates/nikaia/src/emit/mod.rs`). Its comment says it holds "the words the language below reserves and this one does not", but Nikaia reserves both (ADR-298). The entries are probably needed anyway: after `.` and `::` a reserved word is a name (ADR-298 D3), so `x.ref` has to be escaped. Either the comment or the list is wrong; decide which, and add a test for `x.ref` / `T::trait`. — *0.0.439: the comment was wrong; the list stays, and `reserved_below.rs` holds the four words both languages reserve to the escape.*
- [x] **ADR-251 D4 vs `std.contracts`:** the record says the ledger writes a receiver as `ref mut self`. `std.contracts` and ADR-288 D17 write `mut self: ref collections::HashMap[K, V]`. One spelling should win. — *0.0.441: neither has to; the ledger writes both. ADR-251 D4 now says `ref self` / `ref mut self` are short for `self: ref T` / `mut self: ref T`, the long form being what ADR-288 D17 needs to name type arguments.*
- [ ] **ADR-310 vs ADR-311:** ADR-310 says bytes that are not text have no crossed form and that `asset` refuses them. ADR-311 D1 says `ref Array[u8]` is the byte buffer that crosses, and ADR-179 mentions binary assets. Is a binary `asset("…")` allowed?
- [x] **ADR-283 D6:** says "the compiler warns" when a small view keeps a large buffer alive. The behaviour is a line in `--tethers`, not a warning. Warning or report? — *0.0.441: report, as built; D6 says so and why (a warning on every view of a mapping would fire on the programs the mapping is for).*
- [x] **ADR-280:** §3 gives 88 bytes for the three-field error envelope and D24 gives 96. Probably 88 beyond the 8-byte word, but check and make them agree. — *0.0.441: both were right, about different things (ADR-211's measurement): a `Result` 96 bytes wide where the bare error is 16, the three fields alone 88. The table row now says both.*
- [x] **ADR-282 D25:** its second case is described as unreachable because of D17. Drop it, or say when it is reachable. — *0.0.441: kept as a backstop, and D25 says why; probes with literals kept in fields and lists all go through D17.*
- [x] **ADR-282 §2:** the row for Nikaia's `Bytes` cites ADR-289 D22, which is about HTTP response bodies. Probably the wrong citation (ADR-283 D19-D21 decide `Bytes`). — *0.0.439: cites ADR-283 D19–D21.*
- [x] **ADR-123 D2:** listed `Cell` as not sendable. `Cell` is `!Sync`, not `!Send`. The text was changed to the built rule during the cleanup; confirm. — *Confirmed: ADR-123 now says `Cell`/`RefCell` are `!Sync` but may be moved to another thread, which is Rust's rule.*
- [x] **ADR-140 D4:** assumed a function type in a result was refused (`NK1142`, retired by ADR-217). A function returning a function type now has no place for its own promise word, and its `sync` is inferred. Confirm that is intended. — *Confirmed: D4's last paragraph says exactly this, and `fn adder(n: i64) -> fn(i64) -> i64` lowers to a plain `fn` and runs.*
- [x] **ADR-013 D3:** still lowers `throws` to `Box<dyn Error>`, which ADR-280 replaced with error types and envelopes. Update or mark D3 as changed by ADR-280. — *0.0.439: marked changed by ADR-280, and listed in the header.*
- [ ] **ADR-021 §3:** how the `target/nikaia/cache/` store is collected is undecided. D12.5 only covers the compiled-`std` trees.
- [ ] **ADR-005 D8:** requires the comparison on two operating systems, but only one is wired up in CI.
- [x] **ADR-037 §3 / D6:** after the cleanup, check that no other sentence still says `NK2501`/`NK2502` never fire. — *Checked: no sentence in `docs/specification` says either never fires; the only "never" is ADR-290 D7's "never on its absence".*
- [x] **ADR-080 D1:** says "three parts" and lists two. — *0.0.439: the two bullets are three checks (`NK1130` both ways, `NK1129`); the heading says "three checks under two codes".*
- [x] **ADR-286 §6:** "a module of this package" was also used by `crates/nikaia/tests/qualified_names.rs`'s doc comments and possibly elsewhere. Check that no help text or doc still offers a new file as a way out (ADR-286 D33 now says `use`). — *Checked: the phrase is nowhere in `docs/` or `crates/`, and no help text offers a new or separate file.*

#### Code comments

- [x] **`crates/nikaia/src/contracts/keeps.rs`:** says "nothing reads this column yet", which is no longer true. — *0.0.439: names its three readers.*

#### Left

Three need the owner: whether a binary `asset("…")` is allowed (ADR-310 vs ADR-311), how `target/nikaia/cache/` is collected (ADR-021), and whether CI gets a second operating system (ADR-005 D8).

### <a id="issue-646"></a>[#646](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/646) Lower the toolchain's package at `user_parallelism = yes` so the compiler may use `par_iter` (ADR-363 D1)

`ready` · opened 2026-10-17 · updated 2026-10-17 · 1 comments

#### How it came up

The owner asked whether `overlap` is the right way to parallelism, since the compiler itself does not use it (decision basis of 2026-10-17). Findings: the compiler's work (checking and lowering 210 tools files against one ledger, prover queries) is CPU-bound over a varying count, the job of `par_iter`/`task::scope`, not `overlap`. But `tests/sysroot.rs::stds_nikaia_half_lowers_the_same_at_both_switches` requires the tools package to lower byte-identically at `no` and `yes` (ADR-002 D4), and `par_iter`/`task::scope` lower differently per setting: one use in `tools/*.nika` turns develop red.

The rule exists for `std`: it is lowered once and linked into programs at either setting, and code lowered at `no` (a `Shared` with the cheap count, ADR-312 D10) is unsound at `yes`. The tools only share the crate (ADR-294 D18); no program runs them. Lowering at `yes` is the safe direction.

#### Decision (owner, 2026-10-17)

Option a: the tools package is always lowered at `yes`; `std`'s own modules keep the byte comparison. Option b (a crate of its own) later: #647.
* Why: [ADR-363](../blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-363.md); ADR-002 D4 notes the change. What: Part III 13.2b.

#### To build

1. `nikaia lower-std` lowers `src/tools` with a fixed build at `user_parallelism = yes` (`sysroot::lower_tools_files` / `lower_tools_at`), whatever the binary's default. Re-run `lower-std`; `package.rs` should not change today (nothing in it is switch-sensitive yet).
2. `tests/sysroot.rs`: the byte comparison keeps `std`'s modules and drops the tools package; a new assertion checks that the committed `package.rs` equals the tools lowered at `yes`. Update the test's comment (ADR-363).
3. The `std` crate links rayon already (`par::iter`); check that `task::scope_on_pool` and `par::iter` build inside `nikaia-std` without a feature, and that a program at `no` that never calls `tools::` is unaffected (`cargo test -p nikaia --test sysroot --test runtime`).
4. Pilot, its own commit: one loop in the tools written with `par_iter` where each element yields its own result (no outer name changed, `NK2107`), e.g. a per-file pure step; counted with callgrind instructions before/after (never seconds), output byte-identical. If no such loop is pure today, report which state blocks it (#558) instead of forcing one.

Related: the `overlap` decision basis (questions still open), #645 (concurrency defects, including `par_iter().map` over `Vec[i64]`, which step 4 may hit).

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-17](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/646#issuecomment-6240248545):

Steps 1-3 built in 6755f82 (0.0.1008): `lower-std` lowers `src/tools` at `yes` (`sysroot::tools_build`); the sysroot test compares `std`'s own modules at both settings and checks that `package.rs` is the package lowered at `yes`. A probe showed `par::iter` and `task::scope_on_pool` build inside `nikaia-std` without a feature.

For step 4 (the pilot):
* a function holding `task::scope` lowers as `pub async fn`, so its callers must pause or poll it — prefer `par_iter` for the pilot;
* `catch { 0 }` lowers to a `match` that trips `clippy::manual_unwrap_or_default` in the tools module: fix the emitter (or allow the lint on `tools` in `crates/nikaia-std/src/lib.rs`).

### <a id="issue-645"></a>[#645](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/645) Concurrency defects: two overlap branches changing one `mut` reach rustc; `par_iter().map` over `Vec[i64]` lowers `&i64`; NK2202 typo

`bug`, `ready` · opened 2026-10-17 · updated 2026-10-17 · 0 comments

Found while preparing the owner's question on `overlap` and parallelism (2026-10-17), each tried with `target/release/nikaia` on develop.

1. **Two `overlap` branches that change the same `mut` binding** (`overlap { a.push(1) a.push(2) }`) pass `nikaia check` (rc 0); `nikaia run` fails in rustc: *cannot borrow `a` as mutable more than once*. Part III C.1: what the checker accepts must build. The touch-set check (ADR-292 D3, `NK2104`) does not count a binding both branches change as a meeting. Fix: the checker refuses it with `NK2104`, naming the binding. Test: the program above refused with `NK2104`; two branches reading `a` still build.
2. **`ns.par_iter().map fn(n) { spin(n) }` over a `Vec[i64]`** lowers to `.map(|n| spin(n))` with `n: &i64`; rustc refuses it, at `user_parallelism` `no` and `yes`. Fix in the lowering (the element by value where it copies, as `iter()` does). Test: the program runs at both settings with the same output.
3. **`NK2202`'s message** reads "An. `overlap` block waits…": a stray period.
4. **`benches/overlap/README.md`** still describes `ordering = "effects"`, which no longer exists (no version raise).

Not here (the owner's decision first): whether `overlap` runs computations on several cores at `yes` (Part I 8.1.2's last paragraph promises it; `overlap2` polls on one thread), and the control questions.

### <a id="issue-643"></a>[#643](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/643) `[v; n]`: a list or an array of n copies of a value that copies (ADR-362 D1-D4)

`ready` · opened 2026-10-17 · updated 2026-10-17 · 0 comments

#### How it came up

The owner wrote `let safety_buffer = [0..181]` for 181 numbers (the loop program of #640). That is a list of one range; printing an element is `NK1201` (#638), indexing it with 110 aborts. Asking how to write 181 equal elements without a loop showed a gap, checked with the compiler on 2026-10-17:

| Written | Today |
| :--- | :--- |
| `(0..<181).map(fn(_i) { 0 }).collect()` | `Vec` of 181 zeros |
| `let mut xs = []` then `xs.resize(181, 0)` | `Vec` of 181 zeros (lowered to `vec[0; 181]`, solver-workload §8.1) |
| `let xs: Array[i64, 181] = (0..<181).collect()` | `NK1103` |
| `[0; 181]` | syntax error *Expected `]` here, but found `;`* |

An `Array[T, N]` of more than a handful of elements cannot be written at all, though ADR-152 made it the container without allocation.

#### Decision (owner, 2026-10-17)

* Spelling `[v; n]` (Rust's); rejected: a function `repeat(v, n)`, `[v] * n`, `[v ** n]`, nothing.
* A value that does not copy is **refused** (`NK1280`), not cloned.
* Why: [ADR-362](../blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-362.md). What: Part I 4.5 (lists, `Array[T, N]`), Part III's table (`NK1157`, `NK1280`).

#### To build

1. Parser: `[expr; expr]` as a list literal of its own kind (no other `;` inside brackets). Test: `[0; 3]`, `[x + 1; n]`, nested `[[0; 2]; 3]`; `[1, 2; 3]` is a syntax error.
2. Checker: the type from the use as every list literal (ADR-152 D4) — no use: `Vec[T]`; `Array[T, N]` wanted: `n` must be a `comptime` integer equal to `N`, else `NK1157` naming both (also for an `n` the build does not know). For a `Vec`, `n` is any whole number type. Tests: `let b: Array[u8, 4096] = [0; 4096]` builds; `[0; 4095]` there is `NK1157`; `let v = [0; n]` with a parameter `n` is a `Vec[i64]`.
3. `NK1280`: `v` not a value that copies (`String`, `Vec`, a struct owning one); the help writes `(0..<n).map(fn(_i) { v.clone() }).collect()`. Test: `[name; 3]` with `name: String` refused with the help; `[(1, 2); 3]` and `[Point { x: 0, y: 0 }; 3]` (a value struct of numbers) build.
4. Lowering: `vec[v; n as usize]` for a `Vec` (a negative `n` aborts as an index out of range does, ADR-293: check before the cast), `[v; N]` for an array. Test: the output of a program printing `len()` and elements; a negative length aborts with the index message.
5. Prover: `len()` of `[v; n]` is `n` (and `N` for an array). Test: `let xs = [0; 181]; assert(xs.len() == 181)` proved; an index `b` with `0 <= b && b < 181` known needs no check.
6. `tools/*.nika`: replace the `resize` workarounds where one builds a list of equal values, if any (`grep -n 'resize('`); not required.

The owner's program then writes `let safety_buffer = [0; 181]`.

Related: #640, #638; the warning for `[0..181]` (ADR-362 D5) is its own issue.

### <a id="issue-642"></a>[#642](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/642) The prover follows a `let mut` through each assignment (ADR-361 D9)

`ready` · opened 2026-10-17 · updated 2026-10-17 · 0 comments

#### How it came up

For the owner's loop program (#640), the prover had to read `let mut i = 0` and `let mut total = 0`, or it would not know the loop's start. Commit 8bfd1a0 made a `let mut` a name of the prover **until its first assignment** (where no call is handed it and no lambda changes it); after an assignment outside a loop, it forgets it. Part III 14.2 and ADR-269 D9 said only "bindings that do not change", so the build went past the text.

```nika
let mut x = 0
assert(x == 0)   // proved today: x has not changed yet
x = x + 1
assert(x == 1)   // checked at run time today; proved after this issue
```

#### Decision (owner, 2026-10-17)

Option B: follow every assignment, the static single assignment form Dafny, SPARK, CBMC and Kani use. Before the first assignment no SSA is needed: the binding has not changed (as built). Rejected: knowing a `let mut` only until its first assignment.

* Why: [ADR-361 D9](../blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-361.md); ADR-269 D9 notes the change.
* What: Part III 14.2, *What the prover reads*.

#### To build

1. An assignment `x = e` to a tracked `let mut` (no call handed it, no lambda or deferred body changes it) gives `x` a new name with `e`'s value over the old one; compound assignments (`+=`, `-=`, `*=` by a constant) likewise. Test: `x = x + 1; assert(x == 1)` proved; `x = x * 3; assert(x == 3)` after `x = 1` proved.
2. After an `if`/`else` that changes `x` on one side or both, `x` is either side's value with that side's condition (a disjunction over the branch condition), and an `if` without `else` keeps the old value on the other side. Test: `if c { x = 1 } else { x = 2 }; assert(x >= 1 && x <= 2)` proved; `assert(x == 1)` not proved.
3. A `match` arm that changes `x` forgets it after the `match` (as today), unless the arms are read; not required here. A loop keeps reading through its invariant (#640).
4. A binding handed to a call (`f(ref mut x)` or any `mut` parameter) or changed from a lambda stays out, from its `let` on. Test: such an `x` after `x = 1` gives no fact; never a false proof.
5. A claim decided without the new names never pays: second query only for a claim left open (ADR-361 D2). Counters: `NIKAIA_PROVER_COUNTS` before/after on the standalone files and `lower-std`; callgrind ≤ +5 %.
6. Refutations: a claim the assignments make false is `NK1207` only under ADR-269's rule for what reaches; check every new `NK1207` in the corpus by hand.

Related: #640 (loops), ADR-269 D9, D16.

### <a id="issue-641"></a>[#641](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/641) Result summaries reach fewer bodies than ADR-359 D1 says: any loop, assignment, match or lambda stops them

`ready` · opened 2026-10-17 · updated 2026-10-17 · 0 comments

#### How it came up

The agent that built ADR-359 D1-D3 (767cfc1, 0.0.997, #632) gave a result summary only to free functions whose body consists solely of `let`, `return` and `if`. A body with any of the following gets none, even where it does not touch the result:
- a loop;
- an assignment;
- a `match`;
- a block or lambda inside an expression.

D1 says more. A function gets a summary when every exit returns a whole number the prover reads, there is no **loop that changes a name it reads**, there is no `match`, and no call on the way can come back to it.

The build is therefore stricter than the text. It proves less than the specification promises, and never anything false. The owner decided (round on #632) to keep D1's text as it is and record the gap here rather than narrow the specification.

#### To build

1. **Loops and assignments that the result does not read.** A loop or an assignment that changes no name an exit's value or path reads does not stop the summary. Tests:
   - a function with a logging loop over an unrelated counter still answers `example(2) - 1 == 11`;
   - a function where the loop changes a name the result reads still has no summary.
2. **Lambdas and blocks inside an expression** that the result does not read do not stop it.
3. **Methods**, not only free functions, where D1's conditions hold. A method that reads `self`'s fields reads them as parameters.
4. **Loops that the result does read.** Once #640 (ADR-361) carries a claim through a loop, a loop whose invariant gives the exit's value may enter the summary. This is a decision for later; ADR-359 says "loops and recursion in a summary" is not decided. Do not build it here.
5. Re-run the `NIKAIA_PROVER_COUNTS=1` counters and callgrind over `lower-std` (limit: ADR-359 D10's +5 %). Check every new refutation by hand (D9).

### <a id="issue-640"></a>[#640](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/640) The prover reads through a loop: Horn clauses in the logic layer, a portfolio of strategies, every invariant checked, proposers (an LLM among them) only checked (ADR-361)

`ready` · opened 2026-10-17 · updated 2026-10-17 · 2 comments

#### How it came up

The owner's second example program:

```nika
fn main(a: int) {
   assert(a > 0)
   assert(a <= 10)
   let mut i = 0
   let mut total = 0
   while i < a {
      i = i + 1
      total = total + 10
   }
   let b = 4 * example(a) + total
   assert(b > 40)
   assert(b < 181)
   ...
}
```

Today both claims about `b` are reported `checked at run time: b is not a whole number the prover reads`. The cause:
- `total` is changed by the loop;
- the prover reads only names that do not change (ADR-269 D9);
- ADR-269 D16 stops at a loop, and its §6 left loop invariants to a later record.

The same program also showed a checker hole, `[0..181]` printed as a range. That is #638.

**Checked before deciding:**
- **ADR-269:** loop invariants are left open there.
- **ADR-270:**
  - three layers, and the logic layer knows no Nikaia syntax;
  - queries are self-contained, and every proof has a certificate;
  - the search is free, and a build uses the committed `nikaia.proofs`;
  - a strategy layer;
  - the logic is a public package.
- **ADR-306 D7:** an inductive bound, kept only where it carries itself.
- **ADR-314 §2:** SPARK unrolls short loops; Dafny and Wuffs need written invariants.
- **#599 point 4:** "state or infer loop invariants and prove initialization, preservation, and the loop-exit consequence", plus the balance between user invariants, inference and search.

#### The decision in short

The owner decided this in four rounds. [ADR-361](../blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-361.md) gives the reasons; Part III 13.2, 13.3 and 14.2 give the rule.

- **D1, the form:** a loop is a problem in Horn clauses, written in the logic layer without Nikaia syntax. It is public, so #599 and outside tools can use it too. SMT-LIB `HORN` is used at the edge. `break`, `continue` and `return` are each a way out of their own.
- **D2, when it is asked:** a claim that reads a name a loop changes, and is left open without the loop, is asked once more through the loop. Only the claim that needs it pays.
- **D3, writing invariants:** nobody has to write one, because the compiler finds it. An `assert` in a loop is a claim **by the flow and by the names it reads, not by its place in the body**. Once proved, it is a fact the search may use.
- **D4, checking:** every invariant used is checked with three queries and their certificates: it holds at entry, a turn keeps it, and with each way out it gives the claim. It is kept in `nikaia.proofs` together with its origin. The origin never decides whether it is believed.
- **D5, the portfolio:** strategies are added one by one:
  1. closed forms;
  2. intervals and octagons;
  3. k-induction;
  4. guess and check;
  5. **PDR/IC3, when the others are exhausted and we are ready.**
- **D6, proposers:** programs named in `[prove] proposers`, for example an LLM, a learner from test runs, or another solver.
  - They run only under `nikaia prove --propose`, never in a build.
  - They receive the open loop problems and answer with candidate invariants, and each candidate goes through D4.
  - Code leaves the machine only through that command.
  - **The exchange (round 4, 0.0.998):** each proposer runs as a child process, with one JSON line per problem in each direction, the way `protoc` plugins and language servers work. HTTP and MCP were rejected; ADR-361 §3 gives the reasons.
- **D7, the report:** `--asserts` names the invariant and its origin, or the loop and the names it changes.
- Unrolling for special cases is #639.

#### To build

1. **The loop's form in the logic layer (D1).** Add a language-neutral type to the logic `.nika` files for a loop problem:
   - state names;
   - entry facts;
   - condition;
   - the step relation over primed names;
   - the exits;
   - goals at points.

   Add SMT-LIB `HORN` export next to `logic_smtlib.nika`. Tests: the problem of §1 written out, and its SMT-LIB text.
2. **Checking an invariant (D4).** One operation turns a loop problem plus a candidate into the three queries, and answers proved only when all three come back proved with certificates. Store the invariant and its origin in the `nikaia.proofs` entry. Tests:
   - a true invariant passes;
   - an invariant that holds at entry but is not kept by a turn fails;
   - an invariant that is kept but too weak for the goal fails.
3. **Translating loops in the frontend (D1, D2).** Turn a `while` or a `for` whose body changes names that an open claim reads into the loop problem. `break`, `continue` and `return` become exits. Ask only for a claim left open, as ADR-359 D2 does.
4. **Closed forms (D5.1).** A name changed by the same constant on every path of a turn becomes `start + k·n`, with a fresh `n >= 0`. A counter with condition `i < a` and step +1 ends at `i == a` when `a >= start`, and otherwise stays at its start. Tests:
   - the owner's program with `b > 40` and `b < 181` proved;
   - a loop with `break` where the end value must not be claimed;
   - a countdown;
   - two counters with different steps.
5. **Intervals (D5.2).** Intervals with widening, and each bound confirmed through step 2. Tests: `while x < 100 { x = x + 3 }` gives `x <= 102`.
6. **The report (D7).** `--asserts` names the invariant and its origin for a claim proved through a loop, and the loop and the names it changes for one left open. Keep the red/light-blue/green groups from 0.0.990.
7. **Measure.** Count instructions with callgrind over `lower-std` and the standalone files; the limit is at most +5 %. Check every new refutation by hand.
8. **Proposers (D6, Part III 13.2 and 13.3).** Build `[prove] proposers = ["cmd arg", …]` in the manifest, `nikaia prove --propose`, and `--propose=CMD` for a single run. The exchange:
   - Start each proposer once as a child process.
   - Write one JSON line per open loop problem to its stdin: `{"version": 1, "id": …, "problem": …, "source": …, "names": […]}`. `problem` is SMT-LIB `HORN`, `source` is the loop's source text, `names` are the names it reads. Close stdin after the last line.
   - Read one JSON line per answer from its stdout: `{"id": …, "candidates": […]}`. Each candidate is an SMT-LIB term over the names.
   - Show the proposer's stderr to the user.
   - Skip a proposer that crashes, ends early, or writes a line in another shape; the other proposers and the built-in strategies continue.
   - D4 checks each candidate. Write the ones that pass to `nikaia.proofs`, with the proposer as their origin.

   Tests, with a stub proposer written in Nikaia and run with `nikaia run`:
   - one true and one false candidate: only the true one is written;
   - a proposer that crashes: the run continues;
   - a line in the wrong shape is skipped;
   - an unknown `version` is refused with a message;
   - `nikaia build` never starts a proposer;
   - `--locked` reads the written entry.
9. Later, each as its own step: octagons, k-induction, guess and check, and PDR when D5 says.

Steps 1-7 close the owner's program. Step 8 is the LLM interface. Step 9 stays open after that.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-17](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/640#issuecomment-6236757444):

Status after d3d27e2 (steps 1-2) and 8bfd1a0 (steps 3-6, `while` only). The owner's program has `assert(b > 40)` and `assert(b < 181)` proved through the loop, with the invariant `i >= 0 && total >= 0 && 10 * i == total && i < a + 1` found by closed forms.

Still missing for steps 1-7:
1. **Step 3, `for` loops.** Only `while` is translated today (`prover_loops.nika`, `loop_record`). `prover_for` only forgets what its body changes.
2. **Step 2, the invariant's own line in `nikaia.proofs`.** The three queries are recorded as ordinary entries. A line `KEY invariant origins;smt-term` (an `Entry::Invariant` in `proofs.rs`, the `invariant` kind accepted by `pb_read`, with `ProverState.invariants_noted` filled in `through_loops` and handed over in `prove.rs`) was written and then taken out unbuilt. Rebuild it from that description and add a test with a project build.
3. **Step 4 test, two counters:** the test uses steps +1/-3. With +2/-5, `i == 10` is not proved: it needs parity, and Fourier-Motzkin tightening does not find it.
4. **Step 7, the counts.**
   - 26 standalone files (`benches/`, `examples/`) with the new binary: 3 844.1 M instructions. No baseline was counted.
   - `lower-std` was not counted.
   - No new refutation can come from a loop, because loop facts are never used to refute. A `let mut` now gives facts until its next assignment, so new NK1207s are possible. `lower-std` raised none, but the corpus has not been checked yet.
5. **The counters:** `NIKAIA_PROVER_COUNTS` prints loop claims and loop queries. For the owner's program it printed 64 / 192: the invariant is found again on every fresh `ProverState`, that is each `prove()` round and each check. It could be cached across rounds.
6. **Lowering defect:** D45 is filed in `docs/lowering-defects.md`.

### <a id="issue-638"></a>[#638](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/638) A range, a tuple, a struct or an enum in an f-string hole passes the checker and fails in rustc

`bug`, `ready` · opened 2026-10-17 · updated 2026-10-17 · 0 comments

#### How it came up

The owner wrote `let safety_buffer = [0..181]; let d = safety_buffer[b]; println(f"... {d}")`. `[0..181]` is a list holding **one range**, so `d` is a range. `nikaia check` accepted the program, and the build then failed in rustc:

```
error: `Through<{integer}>` doesn't implement `std::fmt::Display`.
```

Checked on today's compiler, a hole of each of these types reaches rustc and fails there:
- a range,
- a tuple,
- a struct of the program,
- an `enum` of the program.

Only a list, a map, a set, a `T?` and a `Path` in a text `f"…"` are refused today, with `NK1201` or `NK1102`.

#### Decision

A checker that passes what rustc refuses is a defect. The rule is the one `NK1201` already states, "a hole whose value has no text form" (ADR-319 D4). Part III C's code table now lists the types that have a text form and those that have none (0.0.992, c8a0a7b):
- **Text form:** text, a number, a `bool`, a `char`, and an error, whose text is its message. The lowering writes a `Display` for errors and error sums.
- **No text form:** a collection, a range, a tuple, and any other struct or `enum`.

Whether a data-less `enum` should print its variant's name is **not decided here**.

#### To build

1. In the checker's `f"…"` hole check (`check_exprs.nika`, `check_name_in_text.nika`), refuse a hole of type range, tuple, or a struct or `enum` the lowering writes no `Display` for, with `NK1201`.
   - Use the same sentence shape as for a list.
   - The help names the fields as holes for a struct (`{p.x}`).
   - Before refusing, find every type the lowering gives a `Display`, and keep each of them allowed.
   - A hole whose `:` asks for a form with `?` stays allowed, as today.
2. Tests: one refusal each for a range, `[0..5][0]`, a tuple, a struct and an enum. Positive cases: an error type in a hole runs, and a number, `bool`, `char` and text run.
3. `lower-std` if a tools file changes. Commit with `Fixes #N`.

### <a id="issue-637"></a>[#637](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/637) A `comptime` whose run stopped is reported as NK1152; ADR-321 D16 says NK1244

`bug`, `ready` · opened 2026-10-17 · updated 2026-10-17 · 0 comments

Found while building #633 (October 17, 2026): a `comptime` whose compiled run stops (an overflow, an index past the end, a `panic()`), e.g. `comptime C = sq(100000)`, is reported on the command line as **NK1152** ("`C` stopped while the program was built").

[ADR-321](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-321.md) D16: a build-time run that stopped is **NK1244**, in the words the program would print, at the line where it stopped, with the `comptime` it was computing. `NK1152` keeps what the rule refuses before anything runs and D7/D10's bounds; `NK1127` what this compiler cannot compute.

#### To build

1. Where a compiled `comptime` run stops, report `NK1244` with the run time's message, the line it stopped at and the `comptime`; keep `NK1152` only for the rule's refusals and the step/memory bounds.
2. Check Part III's code table for `NK1244` and `NK1152` against D16; fix the table if it disagrees.
3. Tests: `comptime C = sq(100000)` is `NK1244` with "attempt to multiply with overflow"; a body exceeding the step budget stays `NK1152`; a refused touch stays `NK1152`.

### <a id="issue-636"></a>[#636](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/636) The interpreter fallback for build-time values goes: a `comptime` or an option default that calls a function is compiled and run, or refused with NK1127

`ready` · opened 2026-10-17 · updated 2026-10-17 · 2 comments

#### How it came up

While building #633 (October 17, 2026) the agent checked where `tools/build_eval.nika`, the tree interpreter for build-time code, is still used. It is still the fallback:

* `check_host.nika` `build_time_value`: for a `comptime` the compiled path does not answer, i.e. a check with no workshop (a plain library `check`, as in most tests), the `NotHere` cases, and an initialiser that runs a grammar.
* `crates/nikaia/src/contracts/mod.rs` `computed_literal`: an option's default (ADR-318) when no workshop is present.

So a plain `check` today computes `comptime B = 4 / example(2)` with the interpreter instead of refusing it. That is a second implementation of the language's meaning beside the one that runs the program; where the two disagree (an overflow, a narrowing, a `std` function), a build-time value would differ from the run-time one.

#### Decision (already taken)

[ADR-321](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-321.md) D1: build-time code that calls a function is compiled to Rust and run, **never interpreted**. D19: a check with no place to compile build-time code refuses with `NK1127` and a note; it does not compute or leave the value open. §5: the interpreter's call-evaluating part goes. ADR-359 D11 (#633) already runs claims' calls on the compiled path only. The owner's direction (October 17, 2026): the fallback is a risk and an inconsistency; remove it. No new decision is needed.

#### To build

1. `build_time_value` (`check_host.nika`): no interpreter fallback. Without a workshop a `comptime` that calls a function is `NK1127` with D19's note; with one, every case goes through `compute_comptime`. A literal-only initialiser stays folded by the checker (D1's last paragraph).
2. The `NotHere` cases and an initialiser that runs a grammar: each either goes the compiled path or is refused with the code ADR-321 gives it (`NK1127` for what this compiler cannot compute); name each case in the commit.
3. Option defaults (`contracts/mod.rs` `computed_literal`, ADR-318): the same rule, compiled with a workshop, `NK1127` without.
4. Remove the interpreter's call-evaluating part (`build_eval.nika` and whatever only it uses); keep only what folding literals needs, if anything. `lower-std`.
5. Tests that relied on the fallback: give them a workshop (as the command line has) or expect `NK1127`; never re-add a fallback. New tests: a library check without a workshop refuses `comptime B = 4 / example(2)` with `NK1127` and the note; the command line computes it; an option default that calls a function, both ways.
6. Specification: Part II 10.2 / Part III must say nothing that implies interpretation; fix any sentence that does (raises the version with the build).

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-17](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/636#issuecomment-6234495740):

State after one session (October 17, 2026). Nothing of this is on `develop` yet. It is on branch `wip/636-no-interpreter` (commit 7bb68d6, on top of 9918a8c).

**Built on the branch**
1. `build_eval.nika` only folds literals now: it handles literals, tuples, lists, structs, variants, `f"…"`, operators, index, field, an `if` whose branches are single values, `asset("…")` and a grammar run on a text. It runs no call, method, body, loop or statement. A call that reaches it gets `NK1127` with the note "build-time code that runs is compiled and run with the program, never interpreted". `BtCallee`, `contract`/`callee`, `TooDeep`/`NotAllowed`, the call depth and the evaluation thread are gone. `a_build_time_call_went_too_deep` is removed.
2. `check_host.nika` `compiled_build_time_value`: an initialiser that is not literals alone (`host_folds`) goes the compiled path. Without a workshop it is `NK1127` with D19's note ("This check has no place to compile build-time code in, …"). Each `NotHere` case of `comptime_run::compute` now carries its reason and is reported as `NK1127`. The cases are: the type has no written form; an earlier constant has no literal; the program could not be built (lowering, driver, `DidNotBuild`); the answer could not be read; a left-out default; a run nested inside another run; `NoCrossedForm`; nowhere to build. A grammar run is still compiled by the grammar's workshop, and only its input is folded.
3. A constant read before its turn (a called function reading another file's `comptime`, or one declared below) is worked out first (`worked_out_ahead`). It is folded if it is literals, and compiled if it runs code and declares a type. Before this change the interpreter covered these reads (`modules::a_constant_a_foreign_body_reads_comes_from_its_own_file`).
4. `contracts::computed_literal` folds literals first, otherwise compiles. It never interprets.
5. New tests in `comptime_compiled.rs`:
   - a library check without a workshop refuses `comptime B = 4 / example(2)` with `NK1127` and the note;
   - with a workshop and on the command line (`nikaia lower`) the value is `1`;
   - an option default that calls a function, checked both ways.

   `blocks_in_expressions.rs` gets a workshop so that it still tests the rule (`NK1152`). `build_time::a_table_is_computed…` now builds the table with `push`, because `xs[i] = v` on an `Array[T, N]` does not build at run time either (lowering defect D44, filed). The interpreter used to compute such a table.

**Still missing**
- A ring through a default (`comptime X = f()`, `fn f(t: i64 = X)`) is now reported as `NK1127` twice instead of `NK1168`. The compiled path needs ring detection over called bodies and option defaults, as the interpreter's `resolving` stack had (`option_defaults::a_default_that_needs_itself_is_a_ring`).
- `build_time::a_constant_can_read_the_one_above_it` and `comptime::a_comptime_says_which_wall_it_met` failed in one parallel run and passed when run alone. Check whether concurrent workshop compiles now come out as `NK1127`, since nothing hides them any more.
- The `tests/build-time` package fails on `OPTION_DEFAULTS_HI`: "has no value computed at build time". A binary built before this work fails the same way, so this is not caused by this change.
- The `tests/language` package (`field_defaults.nika`, `step = 3 * 4`) should be fixed by the fold-first change in `computed_literal`. Re-run it.
- Lowering defect D43: `throw made()`, where `made()` returns an error type, is boxed. `build_eval.nika` works around it by throwing the constructor.
- The specification text needs no change: Part II 10.2 and Part III say nothing that implies interpretation.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-17](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/636#issuecomment-6236396652):

Built on `develop` in 9a09fdc (`Fixes #636`). It carries the branch `wip/636-no-interpreter` (7bb68d6) ported onto develop, plus what was still missing:

- **The ring.** `comptime X = f()` with `fn f(t: i64 = X)` is `NK1168` again, once. `check_host.nika` (`host_ring`) walks the value statically before anything is folded or compiled. It follows each item `comptime` read, each free function named (its body), and the default of each option a call leaves out. A cycle through a `comptime` or a default is refused. A function that only calls itself is not a ring. `said_rings` keeps it to one finding per ring.
- **The flaky tests.** The workshop had races between two builds compiling the same program at once. `rustc` names its object files after the crate, so two runs in one directory broke each other's link. The binary and the `answer` were also written non-atomically. Now each run compiles in a directory of its own, and files are written whole (temp file, then rename). `option_defaults::a_struct_default_is_recorded` reproduced this on a cold cache. The two tests named in the last comment pass in parallel runs now.
- **`a_comptime_says_which_wall_it_met`.** This failed every time once the branch was on develop. An undeclared callee now gets its own `NK1127` note naming it.
- **`tests/build-time` (`OPTION_DEFAULTS_HI`).** On the branch, the "worked out ahead" re-run went round without end. A constant carried into the run as a `let` was taken for one that was left out. Every compile that failed was then compiled again, so the package took over 50 minutes and failed. Now a run goes again only when it worked out a constant anew, and a program that did not compile is not compiled again in the same process. The package passes (41 tests). I did not check whether develop's own binary failed this package before this change.
- `tests/language` passes (395).
- Lowering defects filed as D45 (`throw made()`) and D46 (`xs[i] = v` on an `Array`), because develop already had a D43 and a D44.

Tested: comptime, build_time, option_defaults, comptime_compiled, comptime_item, comptime_order, modules, grammar_at_build_time, blocks_in_expressions, sysroot, and clippy with `-D warnings`.

Still open: D45 and D46 (workarounds in `build_eval.nika` and `build_time::a_table_is_computed…`).

### <a id="issue-635"></a>[#635](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/635) `main`'s parameters are the command line; its preconditions are checked where the words arrive, a breach exits with status 2

`ready` · opened 2026-10-17 · updated 2026-10-17 · 1 comments

#### How the question came up

The owner wrote, as the next target after #632/#633:

```nika
fn main(a: int) {
   assert(a>0)
   let b = 4*example(a)
   let c = (0*4+1)
   assert(c==1)
   assert(b>40)
   println(f"Hello, Nikaia! {b} {c}")
}
fn positive(whole: int) -> int { assert(whole > 0); return whole }
fn example(a: int) -> int { let whole = 10 + a; return positive(whole) }
```

Checked with the compiler on October 17, 2026 (with `i64` for `int`):

* `nikaia check` accepts `fn main(a: i64)`; `nikaia run` fails in rustc: *`main` function has wrong type, expected `fn()`*. No ADR and no part of the specification says whether `main` takes parameters; `emit_entry.nika` treats only a `main` without arguments, options and return type as the entry.
* `--asserts`: `assert(a > 0)` *checked at run time: `main` has no callers*; `assert(c == 1)` proved (#632 step 1); `positive`'s claim is carried back to `example` as `10 + a > 0` and proved at the call; `assert(b > 40)` checked at run time. With `4 * (10 + a)` written out, `b > 40` is proved: the fact `a > 0` already flows. What remains for `b > 40` is ADR-359 D1-D3 (#632); `int` is #630.

The owner's direction: an `assert` about `main`'s parameter is documentation, the contract with the caller (checked at run time, where the arguments arrive), and a fact for every later proof; the contract is derived by the ordinary rules of carrying back, as for any function or method. A decision basis compared clap (derive), Swift ArgumentParser, Python typer and Go `flag`; the owner chose: parameters of `main` are the command line (option C), exit status 2 for a wrong word, a small first scope.

#### Decision

[ADR-360](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-360.md), specification Part I 5.5, Part III 14.2, 17.1, A.2, `NK1273`, `NK1274`:

* **D1** subject parameters are positional and required; options are `--name value` / `--name=value` with their defaults, `_` written `-`, a `bool` option a bare `--name`; `--` ends named words; `-` followed by a digit is a value.
* **D2** types: `int`, fixed widths, `f64`, `bool`, `String`, these behind `?`, a last subject `Vec[T]`; numbers in `std`'s text shape; anything else refused (`NK1273`), as are an option `help` and two options with one name (`NK1274`), and parameters on `wasm32-unknown`.
* **D3** `main`'s preconditions are derived as every function's (ADR-269 D5, D14-D21): carried back to the entry, reading only parameters.
* **D4** checked after the words are read, before the body; this is ADR-269 D6's check where outside data arrives, so no `NK1202`; a breach or wrong word: one line on stderr (parameter, claim, value, the `assert` it came from), short usage, **exit status 2**, no body ran.
* **D5** `--help`/`-h` from `///` above `main`, types, defaults, preconditions; status 0.
* **D6** parameters, defaults, preconditions in the ledger. **D7** a call to `main` in the program is ordinary; `cli::args()` unchanged.

**Not decided:** short flags, subcommands, enums as choices, environment defaults, a version flag.

#### To build

1. **Checker**: accept parameters on `main` per D2; `NK1273`, `NK1274` (codes reserved, text in Part III's table). Tests: each refused type; `help`; `max_depth` + `max-depth`; `wasm32-unknown`.
2. **Prover/report**: `main`'s carried-back preconditions are reported by `--asserts` as *precondition of `main`, checked where the words arrive* (not *checked at run time: `main` has no callers*), and are facts in the body. Test: the owner's program above (once #630 and ADR-359 D1-D3 are in) reports `a > 0` as `main`'s precondition, `b > 40` proved; a variant without `assert(a > 0)` reports `10 + a > 0` as `main`'s precondition.
3. **Lowering + `std::cli`**: Rust `fn main()` reads `std::env::args`, fills the parameters per D1/D2, checks the preconditions, on failure prints the line and usage to stderr and exits 2; `--help`/`-h` print the help and exit 0; then calls the program's `main` body. Tests (run the binary): `prog 5` runs; `prog` / `prog x` / `prog 5 6` / `prog -3` exit 2 with the message; `prog --times=3 5`, `prog --verbose 5`, `prog -- 5`; `prog --help` output and status 0; a precondition carried back from a callee names the `assert` it came from.
4. **Ledger**: `main`'s parameters, defaults and preconditions in `nikaia.contracts`.
5. `lower-std`, commit `package.rs`.
6. `examples/` (no version raise): move the `cli::args().nth(1) ?? "…"` programs to a `main` option where it reads better.

Order: after #630 (`int`); step 2's full test after #632's D1-D3.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-17](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/635#issuecomment-6233053619):

Built in 42aa0fa (step 2, prover) and 0f78405 (steps 1, 3, 4, 5), with `i64` and the other types:

* **Step 1**: `check_main.nika`. `NK1273` covers a struct, `HashMap`, tuple, `ref`, a `Vec` that is not the last subject, and `Vec[Vec[..]]`. `NK1274` covers an option named `help`. Tests are in `crates/nikaia/tests/main_parameters.rs`.
* **Step 2**: `prover_walk.nika` no longer sets "`main` has no callers", so `main`'s preconditions are carried back by the ordinary machinery. `--asserts` says *precondition of `main`, checked where the words arrive*, and `assert(b > 40)` after `assert(a > 0)` with `4 * (10 + a)` is proved.
* **Step 3**: `emit_entry.nika` and `std::cli` (`Interface`, `Param`, `Words`). Run-the-binary tests cover `prog 5`, missing, `x`, `5 6`, `-3`, `--times=3`, `--be-loud`, `--`, `--help`/`-h`, an optional subject, a `Vec` rest, `f64`, `String?`, and a carried-back precondition naming `assert(whole > 0)` in `positive`.
* **Step 4**: the ledger already writes `main`'s signature, defaults, `requires` and `from`.
* **Step 5**: `package.rs` is committed.

**Still missing:**
1. **`int`** (#630): `int` is already on the checker's list of word types (`main_word_type`), but there is no test with `int`. If `int` lowers to something other than `i64`, `std::cli::Word` needs an impl for it. The owner's program as written (step 2's full test) waits on #630 and ADR-359 D1-D3.
2. **The variant without `assert(a > 0)`**: `10 + a > 0` is `main`'s precondition in the ledger, in `--help`, and in the exit-2 message. But `--asserts` lists asserts by line and has no row for a carried-back precondition. `positive`'s assert still reads *precondition of `positive`: proved at every call*. A row for a computed precondition of `main` would be new prover-report work.
3. **`NK1273` on `wasm32-unknown`**: no check exists. Every build for that target is refused before the source is read (`Target::unbuildable`).
4. **`NK1274` for "two options one name once `_` is `-`"**: a name cannot contain `-`, so `max_depth` + `max-depth` does not parse. The check is in place but cannot be reached.
5. **Step 6** (examples): not done.
