### <a id="issue-633"></a>[#633](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/633) Prover: evaluate a call with constant arguments at compile time, where a claim needs it?

`ready` · opened 2026-10-17 · updated 2026-10-17 · 2 comments

#### Where this comes from

This was split off from #632 by the owner (October 17, 2026). ADR-359 lists it under *Not decided here*. It needs its own decision round.

The owner's program refuses `assert(4 / example(2) > 10)` once ADR-359 is built, because `example` is straight-line code whose result summary the prover can derive. A summary cannot follow a loop or recursion. So a claim over `fib(10)` or `sum_to(100)` stays a run-time check, although every argument is a constant and the compiler could compute the value.

#### The question

Where a claim is still left open after ADR-359, and it reads a call `f(k1, ...)` whose arguments are all known constants, should the compiler **evaluate that call while compiling** and use `result == value` as a fact?

#### What the owner set as constraints

* **Such a call can be expensive.** Evaluation at compile time must have a bounded cost, and only the program whose claim needs it may pay for it. That is the same principle as ADR-359 D2: never all calls, never every build.
* **Only `sync` functions qualify.** A function that suspends, touches the outside world or runs at once is not evaluated.

#### Facts already found (decision basis for #632, option ii)

* There are two evaluation paths at build time today:
  * `crates/nikaia-std/src/tools/build_eval.nika` is a tree evaluator for program functions. It has a step budget, runs only `sync` code with `touches = []`, and refuses `std` and other packages.
  * A `comptime` with a call is compiled with rustc and run. That is far too expensive to use for a claim.
* **Correctness risk:** a second implementation of the language's meaning sits in the path that refuses a program. If the evaluator differs from the compiled program, a correct program is refused wrongly. Places it could differ: overflow, division of negative numbers, an `assert` inside the body (`build_eval` has no explicit `assert`). The `comptime` path avoided this risk on purpose by compiling and running.
* **Coverage:** it handles loops and recursion, which ADR-359's summaries cannot, but only for constant arguments. That is rare in real programs and common in tests and examples. `example(2)` would be refuted, while `example(n)` with `n == 2` known from a guard would not (ADR-258 D4, weaker).
* **Nothing new would be stored:** the value goes into the query as a fact, and the answer into `nikaia.proofs`.

#### Still owed before a decision round

1. **The cost model.** What counts as the budget (steps of the evaluator, counted in work, the same on every machine like the prover's budget)? What is the limit? What happens when it is exceeded (the claim stays a run-time check, with a note)?
2. **Whether `build_eval` has the compiled program's meaning.** Run a differential test over the corpus's `sync` functions that use constant arguments: evaluated against compiled-and-run output, byte for byte, covering overflow aborts, negative division and `assert`s in the body.
3. **How `sync` is decided here.** Is the existing `sync` marker and colour enough, or does it also need `touches = []` (no I/O, no shared state)?
4. **How often it would fire.** Count, in the corpus and the tests, the claims left open after ADR-359 that read a call with constant arguments.
5. **How other languages handle it:** Zig `comptime` (eval branch quota), C++ `constexpr` (implementation limits on steps and depth), Rust `const fn` (Miri-based, with a step limit), and Dafny's and SPARK's handling of ground calls.
6. **Options with who pays**, and a recommendation (CLAUDE.md, *Decision rounds with the owner*).

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-17](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/633#issuecomment-6231687973):

#### Correction, and decided (October 17, 2026)

**Correction to the issue text above.** The issue proposed `build_eval.nika`, a tree evaluator, and called the rustc path "far too expensive". That contradicts [ADR-321](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-321.md) D1: build-time code is compiled and run, never interpreted, so that one implementation computes both the build-time and the run-time answer. The owner pointed this out.

With the ADR-321 path, the "second implementation" risk described above does not exist: the call computes exactly what the program would. No such divergence had ever been observed anyway; it was a risk class, not a found bug. The cost bounds are already decided:
- one build-time crate per package, cached (D4);
- a counted step budget (D7);
- a memory bound (D10).

**Decision** ([ADR-359](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-359.md) D11-D13, Part III 14.2, 0.0.980):
- **D11.** Where a claim is still open after ADR-359 D1-D7, and it reads a call whose arguments are all known to the build (literals, folded constants, `comptime` bindings), the call is compiled and run as a `comptime` is.
  - Its body must be `sync`, with an empty touch set: no `args read`, because at build time those are the builder's arguments.
  - It runs under ADR-321's budget, and the result is cached.
  - Its value is a fact.
- **D12.**
  - A call that cannot run leaves the claim a run-time check, and `--asserts` says why: not `sync`, touches something, outside the build, or over budget. This is not an error.
  - A call whose run stops (overflow, an `assert` in the callee) is `NK1207`, in the program's words.
- **D13.** Only the claim that needs it pays.

Running such a call as an **optimisation** where no claim needs it is split off as #634 (`decision`).

#### To build (ADR-359 D10 puts this after #632 step 1, folding `/` of literals and the `comptime` fact)

1. **Prerequisite, #632 step 1.** `/` and `%` of two literals fold in `prove_terms.nika`; an integer `comptime` binding is a fact. Tests are as listed in #632.
2. **Find the candidates.** In `prover_walk.nika`, for a claim whose answer is *unknown*, collect the calls it reads (also through `let`s) whose arguments fold to constants. Qualifying callees are `sync`, have an empty touch set, and are in this build (ADR-321 D8).
3. **Run them on ADR-321's path.** Use the compiler's existing build-time machinery that runs `comptime` (the same lowering, crate, budget and cache; never `build_eval`). Feed `call == value` back as a fact and ask the claim once more.
4. **D12 reporting.**
   - A callee that cannot run: the `--asserts` line names the reason.
   - A run that stops: `NK1207` with the program's message.
5. **Tests in `prove.rs` / `assert.rs`:**
   - `assert(fib(10) == 54)` with a recursive `fib` is proved, and `assert(fib(10) == 55)` is refused (`NK1207`).
   - `example(2) == 12` over the owner's `example` is proved.
   - With step 1: **the owner's program** `let b = 4 / example(2); assert(b > 10)` is refused with `NK1207` (a `tests/` program with `NAME.stderr`).
   - A callee that reads `cli::args` or does I/O is not run, and the claim stays a check.
   - A callee that overflows for its constant argument gives `NK1207` with the overflow message.
   - A program whose claims are all decided runs nothing at build time (count the build-time runs: 0).
6. **Measure** with callgrind (counted): `lower` over the standalone files and `lower-std`, at most +5 %. Check every new refutation in the corpus by hand and fix it in code (ADR-359 D9).

Version raises and NK codes are the supervisor's (CLAUDE.md).

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-17](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/633#issuecomment-6233477498):

#### Built

Commits:
- **8246f86**: step 1 (#632 step 1).
- **ef12f50**: steps 2-5.
- **7d481e6**: step 6, the measurement. This commit carries `Fixes #633`. Its push was rejected because `origin/develop` had moved, and it waits in the shared checkout until someone rebases it.

**Owner's program.** `assert(b > 10)` is refused with NK1207:

> `b > 10` is false every time it is reached. note: `b` is 0. note: `example(2)` is 12, run while the program was built.

`assert(1 == 1)` is proved, and `assert(whole > 0)` remains a precondition of `positive`, proved at every call.

**How it works**
- **Finding the calls** (`prover_walk.nika`). For a claim that is still checked at run time, the walk collects the calls it reads. That includes calls read through the `let`s it reads (`ProverScope.calls_of`).
  - A call qualifies when its callee hands back a whole number and every argument folds (`folded_whole`): literals, `comptime`s, and calls already run.
  - Qualifying calls are added to `ProverState.wanted`.
- **Running them** (`prove.rs`). This only happens when something is wanted and the build has a workshop.
  - Each callee-and-arguments pair runs once. The walk is then repeated with the values in `pinned`, which `lin_term` reads as constants.
  - At most 4 rounds, so `f(g(2))` works.
  - `prove::claim_runs()` counts the runs.
- **The run itself** (`check/host.rs` `run_for_a_claim`). It goes through `compute_comptime`, which is ADR-321's path: the program's lowering, the build-time crate, the budget and the cache. `build_eval` is never used.
  - Only a `sync` function of this package that cannot throw and has an empty touch set is run.
  - `args read` is refused with the reason D11 gives.
- **D12 reporting.**
  - A call that is not run adds its reason to the `--asserts` line, e.g. *`noisy(2)` was not run while the program was built: it touches the world outside the program*.
  - A run that stops is NK1207 in the program's words, e.g. *`sq(100000)` stops the program every time it is reached ... attempt to multiply with overflow*.
- **`--asserts` now checks the way the build does**, with the program's files, reads and workshop. Before, it ran a second check with no workshop and disagreed with the build.

**Tests**, all passing:
- `prove.rs` (with a workshop):
  - `fib(10) == 55` is proved, and `== 54` is NK1207 with a note naming 55.
  - `example(2) == 12` and `example(2) - 1 == 11` are proved.
  - A callee doing I/O or reading `cli::args` is not run, and the line says why.
  - `sq(100000)` gives NK1207 with the overflow message.
  - A program whose claims are all decided does 0 runs; one claim that needs a run does exactly 1.
- `assert.rs`: the owner's program through the command line, compared with `tests/fixtures/four_by_example.stderr`.
- Plus #632 step 1's tests in `prove.rs` and `comptime_compiled.rs`.

**Step 6, measured** (callgrind instruction counts, 8506ecd against ef12f50, same inputs, recorded in `docs/solver-workload.md` §8.6):

| | before | after |
| :--- | ---: | ---: |
| 26 files that lower alone | 3 696.0 M | 3 710.5 M (+0.39 %) |
| `lower-std` | 679 084.7 M | 681 571.7 M (+0.37 %) |

These are instructions only, without `--branch-sim`.

**New refutations:** none in `examples/`, `tests/samples`, `tests/language`, `guide/`, `benches/` (each lowered with a workshop) or `tools/*.nika`.

**Found on the way:**
- Lowering defect D43, filed in `docs/lowering-defects.md`: `f(m[k] ?? continue)` over a map of a struct hands `&&T` to a `ref` parameter. It is worked around with a `let` in `prover_claim_ran`.
- An `assert` condition cannot call a function that touches anything (NK1194). So D12's "touches anything" case is only reached through a `let`, as in the tests.

### <a id="issue-632"></a>[#632](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/632) The prover: a quotient, and a function's computed result without a written postcondition (decision basis)

`ready` · opened 2026-10-17 · updated 2026-10-17 · 3 comments

#### What the feature is

An `assert` is proved while the program is built where the prover can read it (ADR-269 D4, D9), and shown false - refused with NK1207 - where what is known rules it out (D8). What the prover reads: comparisons of whole numbers built from names, literals, `+`, `-`, unary `-` and a `*` by a constant; names are immutable whole-number bindings; a call's result is known only by the callee's postconditions (D17), and a `sync` function whose body is one `return e` ensures `result == e` (ADR-314 D3).

#### Where it comes from

The owner's program (reported in #631):

```nika
fn main() {
   let b = 4/example(2)
   assert(b>10)          // checked at run time; the owner expects a refusal
}
fn positive(whole: i64) -> i64 {
    assert(whole > 0)
    return whole
}
fn example(a:i64) -> i64 {
    let whole = 10 + a
    return positive(whole)
}
```

`example(2)` is 12 and `4 / 12` is 0, so `b > 10` is false every time it is reached. A refusal needs two things nothing decides today:

1. **`example(2) == 12`.** `example`'s body is not one `return e` (ADR-314 D3), and no `assert` states its result (D17). `positive`'s `assert(whole > 0)` before `return whole` gives `positive` the postcondition `result > 0`, nothing more. ADR-269 §6 leaves *interprocedural facts beyond contracts* undecided; ADR-314 rejected Option D (reading each callee's body at each call).
2. **`4 / r`.** A quotient is outside D9; ADR-314 §7 leaves *products and quotients of two unknowns* open. The solver has no division term (ADR-270's certificate format has the extension step for `div` by a constant, `0 <= L - m*q <= m - 1`, but the terms do not use it).

#631 built what is already decided (a call inside a `let`'s value is read with its callee's `ensures`, and the report names the unread binding). With it, `let b = 2 * example(2)` over an expression-function `example` is refused today.

#### Options

**Computed results**
* **A - as today.** The author writes the fact: `fn example(a: i64) -> i64 sync { return 10 + a }` is read; any other body needs an `assert` on its result.
* **B - widen ADR-314 D3** to a straight-line body (lets, asserts, guards; no loop, no `match`, no recursion) whose every exit returns a value the prover reads, computed forward: it ensures `result == e` per exit, under the path. `positive` would ensure `result == whole`, `example` `result == 10 + a`. Within a package as an observation (ADR-269 D14), or published like D3's. Cost: more bodies become contracts; D19 warns on more edits; a change in a body changes a caller's proof (the reason Option D lost, softened because the summary is computed once and kept in the ledger).
* **C - read the callee's body at each call** (ADR-314 Option D). Already rejected.

**Quotients**
* **D - a quotient by a constant**: `e / k` with `k > 0` a literal is a fresh name `q` with `k*q <= e <= k*q + k - 1` where `e >= 0`, and the mirrored bounds where `e < 0` (truncation toward zero, Part I 2.2), as ADR-270's extension step; `%` alike. Sound and linear; proves `n / 2 < n` for `n > 0`.
* **E - D, and a divisor the facts pin to one value** is that constant (the solver shows `r == 12`, then `4 / r` is `4 / 12`). Needed for the owner's program.
* **F - interval arithmetic** for `*`, `/`, `%` beside Fourier-Motzkin (ADR-314 §7).

#### Risks

A wrong summary or quotient rule refuses a correct program (an error with a bypass, `refuted-claims = "warn"`) or proves a false claim (a check removed only under `remove-*-checks:on`). Truncation toward zero for negative dividends is the place a rule would be wrong.

#### Recommendation

D now (small, linear, decided by ADR-270's step already), B within a package as an observation, E after D. Together they refuse the owner's `assert(b > 10)`. Not decided here: whether B's summaries are published across packages.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-17](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/632#issuecomment-6231374573):

#### Decided (October 17, 2026)

**How it came up.** The owner's program: `let b = 4 / example(2)` with `fn example(a: i64) -> i64 { let whole = 10 + a; return positive(whole) }`, then `assert(b > 10)`, is false every time it is reached (`example(2)` is 12, `4 / 12` is 0), and it was only checked at run time. #631 fixed one defect (a call inside a `let`'s value); this issue held the rest. A decision basis checked the code:
- even `let b = 4 / 12; assert(b > 10)` is not refused, because the prover has no `/` term;
- a `comptime B = 4 / example(2)` is lowered to `const B = 0` but the prover doesn't read it;
- the prover already walks every body on every build, so a result summary costs almost nothing to compute; the cost is in using it in solver queries.

It also found that ADR-314's Option D was rejected for two reasons: every call paid for it, and the body crossed the package boundary. Neither reason holds for on-demand use within a package.

**Decision** ([ADR-359](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-359.md), specification Part III 13.3, 14.2 and C.3, 0.0.978): the guiding principle is that only who needs it pays.
- **D1-D3 — result summaries within a package.** A summary is used only where a claim is left open, and is written as `inferred` in the ledger: an observation, not a promise, and invisible to other packages.
- **D4 — a hint across a package boundary.** Where the package is read from a path (the user's own code), a hint, `NK1263`, names the `assert` on the result that would make it a contract. A package fetched from elsewhere gets no hint.
- **D5/D6 — division.** `/` and `%` by a positive constant, or by a divisor that what is known pins to one value.
- **D7** — a `comptime` binding is its value.
- **D8** — `NK1207`'s note names each call value it read.
- **D9** — a refutation found in our code is fixed right away; `refuted-claims = "warn"` is only for emergencies.

**Not decided here:** evaluating a call with constant arguments while building, quotients by a divisor that is not pinned (intervals), and loops or recursion in a summary.

#### To build (in this order, ADR-359 D10)

1. **Folding and `comptime` (D5, D7).** `prove_terms.nika`: `/` and `%` of two literals fold, with the same semantics as Part I 2.2's folder. `prover_walk.nika`: an integer `comptime` binding is the fact `B == v`. Tests (`prove.rs`):
   - `let b = 4 / 12; assert(b > 10)` gives `NK1207` with `b` = 0;
   - `comptime B: i64 = 4 / example(2); assert(B > 10)` gives `NK1207`.
2. **Quotient by a positive constant (D5).** `e / k` becomes a fresh name with ADR-270 D5's bounds (truncation toward zero), and `e % k` is `e - k·q`. Tests:
   - `n / 2 < n` is proved for `n > 0`;
   - `(-7) / 2 == -3` and `(-7) % 2 == -1` are proved;
   - `n / 2 > n` is refused for `n == 4`;
   - `for n in -10..<10 { assert(n / 3 * 3 + n % 3 == n) }` is proved (no wrong refusal for a negative dividend).
3. **Result summaries (D1).** `prover_walk.nika`/`prover_passes.nika` record `path → result == e` at every exit and compose them through calls, leaves first. A loop that changes a read name, a `match`, recursion or the term bound means no summary. Unit tests:
   - `positive` gives `result == whole`;
   - `example` gives `result == 10 + a`;
   - `clamp` gives `(n < 0 → result == 0) ∧ (n >= 0 → result == n)`;
   - a body with a `while` gives none.
4. **On demand (D2).** A second query only for a claim the first one left open and that reads a call result. Tests:
   - the number of second queries is 0 for a program whose claims are all decided without summaries;
   - `example(2) - 1 == 11` is proved.
5. **Message (D8).** `NK1207`'s note lists the call values it read and where they came from.
6. **Ledger (D3).** `ledger.nika` and `ledger_text.nika` read and write `inferred = [...]`, and only the package itself reads it. Tests (`contracts_across.rs`):
   - in an app, `assert(lib::example(2) > 30)` stays a run-time check;
   - `NK1208` is not given when an `inferred` line changes.
7. **Pinned divisor (D6).** Substitute linear equalities; otherwise a model plus a proof query. Test: **the owner's program** (a `tests/` program with `NAME.stderr`) is refused with `NK1207`, and the note names `b` = 0 and `example(2)` = 12.
8. **Hint across a package (D4), `NK1263`.** A path dependency gets the hint, naming the fact and the `assert` to write; a fetched package gets none. Tests in `contracts_across.rs`.
9. **Measure and check.** Count instructions with callgrind (at most +5 % for `lower` over the standalone files and `lower-std`) and record them in `docs/solver-workload.md`. Fix every new refutation in the corpus and in `tools/*.nika` in the code (D9), not with `warn`. Then `Fixes #632`.

Version raises and new NK codes are the supervisor's (CLAUDE.md); `NK1263` is already assigned.

### <a id="issue-631"></a>[#631](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/631) The prover: a call inside a let's value is read with what its callee ensures, and an unread claim names the binding

`ready` · opened 2026-10-17 · updated 2026-10-17 · 0 comments

#### How it came up

The owner's program:

```nika
fn main() {
   let b = 4/example(2)
   assert(1==1)
   assert(b>10)
   println(f"Hello, Nikaia! {b}")
}

fn positive(whole: i64) -> i64 {
    assert(whole > 0)
    return whole
}

fn example(a:i64) -> i64 {
    let whole = 10 + a
    return positive(whole)
}
```

`nikaia run --asserts` (or `lower --asserts`) reports:

```
assert(1 == 1)     proved
assert(b > 10)     checked at run time: it is not a comparison of whole numbers the prover reads
assert(whole > 0)  precondition of `positive`: proved at every call the compiler sees; ...
```

#### What was checked

* `assert(whole > 0)` is a precondition (ADR-269 D5); `example` carries `10 + a > 0` back to its entry (D15) and `main`'s `example(2)` proves it. That is the decided outcome, not a defect.
* `assert(b > 10)`: `b`'s value `4 / example(2)` is outside what the prover reads (ADR-269 D9): a quotient, and a call inside an arithmetic expression. Two parts of that are defects against what is already decided:
  1. **ADR-314 D3** says *at a call* the callee's `ensures` is a fact about the result. The assert prover applied it only to `let r = f(x)`; `let e = 10 + f(2)` read nothing (`e` was not a name of the proof).
  2. **ADR-269 D7** asks the report to name the part the prover could not read. It said "not a comparison of whole numbers" for `b > 10`, which is one; the part not read is `b`.
* What stays undecided (a quotient, a function's computed return value without a written postcondition) is #632.

#### To build

1. `prover_walk.nika`: each call in a `let`'s value whose callee returns a whole number becomes a loose name of the proof (`result@<id>`, ADR-340 id), with its callee's postconditions about it; `prove_terms.nika`'s `lin_term` reads such a call as that name. Test: `prove.rs` `a_call_inside_a_lets_value_is_read_with_what_its_callee_ensures` (`10 + example(2) == 22` proved, `example(example(1)) - 1 == 20` proved, `2 * example(2) > 30` refused with NK1207).
2. `prover_state.nika`: a claim the prover cannot read names the first binding it reads that is not a whole number of the proof. Test: `a_claim_over_a_name_the_prover_does_not_read_names_it`.
3. Part III 13.x (the `assert` paragraph of `30-nikaia-tooling.md`): say that a call inside a `let`'s value is known by its callee's postconditions, as `let c = clamp(n)` is.

### <a id="issue-630"></a>[#630](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/630) `int`: the values of an `i64`, its representation the compiler's choice; a fixed width is exact and is written where the representation shows

`ready` · opened 2026-10-17 · updated 2026-10-17 · 2 comments

#### How the question came up

The owner noticed confusion about when an integer type must or should be written. A read-only analysis (October 17, 2026) checked today's rules (ADR-285), counted the annotations and compared other languages:

* `tools/*.nika` holds 2,175 integer type tokens, almost all `i64`; 72% are in signatures and fields, where the grammar requires a type, 17% are casts, 10% are on `let`s. `examples/` and `guide/` already write none on a `let`.
* About 160 widths other than `i64` stand outside any `extern`, nearly all deliberate: `u32` spans, FNV/CHD hashes, the SAT solver's layout (ADR-270 D10/D16), bytes, row ids (ADR-299), 1BRC's 24-byte `Stats`.
* Reproduced traps: `let mut total = 0` summed over `text.bytes()` is inferred `u8` and aborts on "hello world"; a counter nothing types stays `i32` and aborts past 2³¹; `NK1200` suggests `as` where `i32` → `i64` needs none.
* Three checker defects, filed as #629.

The first proposal on the table, *widths only at the C/Rust interfaces, a warning elsewhere*, was dropped: it would fire on the deliberate widths, and no surveyed language warns about a written width.

The owner's direction then: `i32` was preferred as a fallback to keep room for optimisation (32-bit targets, SIMD lanes, smaller lists). But because an overflow aborts, a narrower **type** changes what a program computes. The room belongs in the **representation**: as with `String`, where the compiler chooses between borrowing and owning without changing the result, the compiler may choose an integer's width where nothing observable changes. And a programmer who writes `i64` expects exactly that; so the soft promise needs a name of its own: `int`.

#### Decision

[ADR-285](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-285.md) D36-D39:

* **D36** — `int` holds the values of an `i64` and aborts where an `i64` would; `int` and `i64` take each other without `as`.
* **D37** — its representation is the compiler's choice, narrower only where no value, result or abort changes; a written `i64`, `i32`, `u8`, `u32` or `u64` is exactly that width in memory and in arithmetic.
* **D38** — where the representation shows (`extern "C"`, a described Rust contract), a fixed width is written; `int` there is refused.
* **D39** — `int` is the integer ordinary code writes; a fixed width says the width is the point.

**Not decided here** (ADR-285 §6): whether a number no use asks about falls back to `int` instead of `i32` (D21); whether an arithmetic operand may narrow an open number (the `u8` byte sum); an integer bound for generics (`fn f[T: Integer]`); whether `len()` and an index become `int`; a hint for an `i64` where `int` would do; which narrower representations are built, and when.

#### To build

Prerequisite: #629 (D-1 to D-3), so that no annotation change can turn an abort into a wrong value.

1. **Specification** (raises the version): Part I 2.2 gains `int` (D36, D37) and the rule of thumb (D39: `int` for most numbers, a fixed width where the width is the point); Part I 2.4 says `int` and `i64` take each other; Part III's table gains a code for `int` in an `extern "C"` signature or a described contract (D38).
2. **Checker**: `int` in `tools/types.nika` as a built-in type with every rule of `i64`; `int` ↔ `i64` without `as` in both directions; the D38 refusal. Tests: `fn f(a: int) -> int { return a + 5 }` checks and runs; `let x: i64 = f(1)` checks without `as`; `extern "C" fn g(n: int)` is refused with the new code; `int` overflow aborts at the `i64` bound (`9223372036854775807 + 1` read at run time).
3. **Lowering**: `int` is emitted as `i64` (D37 allows no narrowing yet). Test: the generated Rust of the program in 2 equals that of the same program written with `i64`.
4. `lower-std`, commit `package.rs`.
5. `guide/` and `examples/`: write `int` where the width is not the point (no version raise).
6. `tools/*.nika`: move to `int` when a file is next reworked; not in bulk.
7. Before any narrowing (D37) is built, measure with callgrind: an unannotated literal list as `Vec[i32]` against `Vec[i64]`, and one SIMD kernel; then a decision round on which narrowings to build first.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-17](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/630#issuecomment-6230641897):

Step 1 (specification) is done in d0231d7 (0.0.976): Part I 2.2 gains `int` beside the five fixed widths, and Part III C.3 gains `NK1262` for an `int` in an `extern "C"` signature or a described Rust contract. Steps 2 to 7 are still open, with #629 as the prerequisite.

### <a id="issue-629"></a>[#629](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/629) Open numbers: an `as` truncates silently, a method call makes the function `async`, and NK1135 lets `usize`/`i16`/`u128` through

`ready` · opened 2026-10-17 · updated 2026-10-17 · 0 comments

#### How this came up

A read-only analysis of integer annotations (October 17, 2026) removed 110 bare integer `let` annotations from `tools/*.nika` one at a time and compared the generated Rust. 91% gave the same Rust; the rest exposed three checker defects. All three were reproduced with `target/release/nikaia run` on probe files. None is in `docs/lowering-defects.md`.

They block the integer decision filed beside this issue: until D-1 is fixed, removing or changing an annotation can turn an abort into a wrong value.

#### D-1 — an `as` on an open number is Rust's truncating cast

```nika
let mut v = -1                      // open number (ADR-285 D23)
v = text::parse_i64(s) ?? 0
return v as u8                      // s = "300"
```

prints **44**. With `let mut v: i64 = -1` the same function stops with *"the value does not fit in an `u8`"*: the annotated version lowers to `u8::try_from(v)`, the unannotated one to `v as u8`. Neither is refused with `NK1241`, which Part I 2.2 promises for an `as` not shown to fit (ADR-285 D12). Seen in `check_texts.nika:33` (`u8::try_from(byte)` became `byte as u8`) and `check_takes.nika:327` (`u32::try_from(used)` became `used as u32`) when their annotations were stripped.

#### D-2 — a method call on an open number makes the function `async`

```nika
fn digits() -> i64 { let n = 5; return n.abs() }
```

lowers to `async fn digits()` and every caller to `.await`; with `let n: i64 = 5` it is a plain `fn`. Seen in `parse_numbers.nika::number_at` and `benches/solver-kernels.nika` (`multiply`, `bignum`). It changes a function's colour silently and costs a state machine per call (ADR-258 D3).

#### D-3 — `NK1135` does not refuse the integer types the language does not offer

Part I 2.2: *a written `usize`, `isize`, `i8`, `i16`, `u16`, `i128` or `u128` is refused with `NK1135`*. Today `fn g(n: i16) -> usize`, `struct S { a: i8 }`, `let x: usize = 3` and `fn g(n: u128)` all check clean and lower to Rust as written; only `as usize` is refused (`NK1122`). Cause: the `BUILT_IN` list in `crates/nikaia-std/src/tools/types.nika` holds all twelve Rust integer names.

#### To build

Each one its own commit, with a test in `crates/nikaia/tests/` (grep the NK code):

1. **D-1:** an `as` on an open number gets the same treatment as on a typed one: the checked conversion where the value is not shown to fit, or `NK1241` where Part I 2.2 says so. Test: the program above aborts with the *does not fit* message, never prints 44.
2. **D-2:** a method call on an open number does not make the enclosing function `async`. Test: the lowered `digits` is a plain `fn`.
3. **D-3:** `NK1135` for the seven names outside `extern "C"` and described contracts, in parameters, results, fields and `let`s. Test: each of the four declarations above is refused; an `extern "C"` signature with `usize` still checks. Note: `tools/types.nika` is being edited for #628 right now; build this after that work has landed.
4. Re-run `lower-std` and commit the `package.rs` diff; raise the version (a checker change).

### <a id="issue-628"></a>[#628](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/628) `ref String` largely disappears: `String` is a view unless a copy is cheaper; the ledger states the public interface; `ref` stays only at the C boundary

`ready` · opened 2026-10-16 · updated 2026-10-17 · 2 comments

#### How it came up

While fixing the guide's examples (`fn greet(name: ref String; ...)` → `String`, 0.0.974), the owner asked what `ref String` is still needed for.

**Today** (Part I, Text): wherever a program declares `String`, the compiler decides per value whether it is a view or text of its own (ADR-282). There is never a hidden copy: where a view does not suffice, the compiler refuses and names `.clone()`. `ref String` is "the same text with a promise attached: a borrowed view, no copy". In private code that promise changes nothing in the code emitted. Only one rule of the specification still needs the word: *"a published field, parameter or result is never the third"* (that is, never "either, per value"). That makes `ref String` the way to say "view" at `pub`, and it is required for views in struct fields (`struct Reading { name: ref String }`, NK2302).

#### Considerations (the owner's direction)

1. **`String` is always a view, and text of its own only where that is cheaper.** The language promises no hidden copy, so `ref` as a promise is redundant.
2. **At `pub` too, no `ref`: the ledger states what the public interface can do.** Whether a result is a view and which parameter it points into, and whether a parameter is lent or kept, are read off the body and recorded in `nikaia.contracts`. A change shows up in review as a ledger diff. ADR-094 and ADR-353 already work this way for parameters and results.
3. **Exception: the C boundary.** C reads no ledger. A signature that changed silently would leave C callers compiling while the program has undefined behaviour. At `extern` and `pub extern fn` the signature is therefore written (`ref String`, `ref Array[u8]`) and is required. The compiler checks the body against what is written, rather than deriving what is written from the body.
4. **Transition: warn first.** `ref String` inside Nikaia is accepted for now and gets a warning (new NK code: "`ref` is redundant here, the ledger records it"). It becomes an error later. `tools/*.nika`, the examples and the specification lose it step by step.

#### ADRs to check

| ADR | Why |
| :--- | :--- |
| [ADR-282](../blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-282.md) | Text is one type, decided per position. Holds the "published is never the third" rule that falls here |
| [ADR-283](../blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-283.md) | Tethers and NK2302: a view that escapes. What a struct field carries without `ref` |
| [ADR-353](../blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-353.md) | A result's views point where the body says: already ledger-based. Check that it covers fields |
| [ADR-094](../blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-094.md) | Lent or kept is the callee's, inferred into the ledger: the pattern this extends |
| [ADR-202](../blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-202.md) | A view of the subject handed back without `ref`: the precedent |
| [ADR-186](../blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-186.md) | A parse keeps the text its record views: records with view fields |
| [ADR-302](../blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-302.md), [ADR-284](../blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-284.md) | C boundary: what crosses is a view for the call. The exception in point 3 has to agree with them |
| [ADR-311](../blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-311.md) | Build-time values cross as views (`ref Array[T]`): does the same apply to `ref Array`? |

Open: does the same direction apply to `ref Array[T]` and other `ref T` outside the C boundary, or only to text?

#### Next step

A deciding session writes the ADR (a new D in ADR-282, or a new record) and the specification text. That includes the warning's NK code in Part III, and the C-boundary rule checked against ADR-302 and ADR-284. It then adds a numbered *To build* list here, with tests.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-16](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/628#issuecomment-6224264373):

#### Addition: `ref` on scalars (owner's direction: for all scalar types)

**Today.** For a value the size of a machine word (`i8`…`i128`, `u8`…`u128`, `isize`/`usize`, `f32`/`f64`, `bool`, `scalar`), `ref` is handled inconsistently:
- `let n: ref i64 = counts[0]` is refused with `NK1257` (Part I, just before "Nothing here inserts a copy"). The reason given: such a value is copied at a read, so there is no view of it to declare.
- As a parameter it is **accepted**. `fn f(x: ref i32) -> i32 { return x + 1 }` compiles, and `f(2)` prints `3` (checked on 0.0.974).
- `tools/*.nika` and `examples/` contain no `ref i32` or `ref i64`.

**Direction.** `ref` on a scalar type says nothing: the copy is always cheaper than a pointer. So it is refused outside `extern "C"` everywhere (parameter, field, result, `let`) with `NK1257`, not just at `let`. Nothing uses it, so no warning phase is needed first. A parameter the function is meant to change stays `mut name: T` (ADR-094 D3).

**Exception: the C boundary, as for `ref String`.** In an `extern "C"` declaration, `ref T` and `ref mut T` are the pointer C expects (ADR-302 D5): `ref i32` is `const int32_t *`, and `ref mut i32` is `int32_t *`, for example an out-parameter such as `int *len`. Each lives for the call. It is not a raw pointer (D9). This stays, written out and checked.

**ADRs to check, in addition:**
- ADR-302 D5, D9 and the open point "an out-parameter of anything but a handle"
- ADR-094 D3 (`mut` instead of `ref mut`)
- the record behind `NK1257`: its rule widens from `let` to every position

**To build (for the deciding session to refine):**
1. Make `NK1257` cover a `ref` on a scalar type at a parameter, field and result, with a test for each position.
2. Test that `extern "C"` with `ref i32` and `ref mut i32` stays accepted.
3. Part I: write the rule once, for all scalar types.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-17](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/628#issuecomment-6231645599):

#### Decided, and what is built

**Decision** (3484280, owner's direction above):
- [ADR-282](../blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-282.md) D2: inside Nikaia `ref String` adds nothing. The ledger states the public interface. `ref String` outside the C boundary is the **warning** `NK1261`, and it becomes a refusal once the specification, `examples/` and `tools/*.nika` no longer use it.
- ADR-282 D28: at the C boundary (an `extern "C"` block, a `pub extern fn`) a view is written, required, and the body is held to it.
- [ADR-354](../blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-354.md) D2: `ref` on a word by its kind (a number, `bool`, `scalar`, or an enum whose variants hold nothing) is `NK1257` at every position outside `extern "C"`. In an extern declaration, `ref i32` / `ref mut i32` stay.
- Part I 2.2 / 6.5 and Part III C.1 say this. [ADR-302](../blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-302.md) D5 now points to ADR-354 D2.
- d2b3b07: corrected the text from `pub extern "C" fn` to `pub extern fn`, the spelling of ADR-324 D6.
- Still open, as before: whether D2 also covers non-text `ref T` (`ref Array[T]`, `ref Vec[T]`, structs). It is listed in ADR-282's open points. Nothing needs the answer yet.

**Built** (2e20ee1): `NK1257` at a parameter, a field and a result, including a trait method's parameter and result, and on bare enums. It was built in `tools/types.nika` and tested in `tests/element_views.rs`:
- `a_word_declared_ref_is_refused_at_every_position`
- `a_word_declared_ref_at_the_c_boundary_stays`

The enum parameters in `tools/*.nika` and `tests/language` lost their `ref`. Lowering defect D42 is filed in `docs/lowering-defects.md`: an untyped `let x = m[k] ?? return` over a map of words keeps a `&T`.

#### What is missing: `NK1261`

I implemented the warning: parameter, field, result and `let`, outside `extern "C"` and `pub extern fn`, with tests. Then I backed it out, for two reasons:
1. **About 70 tests fail because the warning is added.** Their fixtures write `ref String` and assert that no warning is produced.
2. **Removing `ref` from those fixtures changes what the program does today.** Dropping `ref String` → `String` broke about 40 of them in other ways, in `build_time`, `grammar_at_build_time`, `bytes`, `comptime_compiled`, `dsl_parameters`, `jump_expressions`, `nullable`, `project` and `typecheck`. The affected cases include:
   - a const string sent as a view;
   - a grammar rule's run crossing as a view;
   - a view of a local tethered;
   - a view out of a temporary held for the block.

So the compiler does not yet meet D2's premise that `String` does whatever `ref String` does. A warning that tells people to drop `ref` would point them at programs that then behave differently, or are refused.

##### To build
1. For each position where today `String` without `ref` is not what `ref String` is, make `String` behave the same, with a test per position. The failing cases above are the inventory: run the suite with `ref String` → `String` in the fixtures and collect what changes.
2. Then turn on `NK1261`. A ready patch with its tests exists and can be re-done from this description:
   - `types_text_by_ref` in `types.nika`, next to `types_word_by_ref`, with `warning: true`;
   - tests `a_ref_string_inside_nikaia_is_a_warning_at_every_position` and `a_ref_string_at_the_c_boundary_is_no_warning`.
3. Migrate the fixtures, `examples/`, the specification's examples (then regenerate `tests/specification/EXPECTED.txt`) and `tools/*.nika`.
4. Make `NK1261` a refusal (ADR-282 D2).

The label changes from `decision` to `ready`: the decision is taken, and step 1 is building work.

### <a id="issue-626"></a>[#626](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/626) The form NK1101's help recommends, `fn(pair) { let (name, v) = pair … }`, is refused by rustc: the parts are references (C.1)

`ready` · opened 2026-10-15 · updated 2026-10-15 · 0 comments

#### What happens

On develop 0.0.966, `NK1101` refuses `filter(fn(name, v) { v > 1 })` over pairs, and its help says: *Take the tuple and name its parts in the body: `fn(pair) { let (name, v) = pair … }`.* That recommended program passes Nikaia's checker, and the Rust it lowers to is refused by rustc (Part III C.1):

```nika
fn main() {
    let pairs: Vec[(String, i64)] = [("a".clone(), 1), ("b".clone(), 2)]
    let n = pairs.iter().filter(fn(pair) {
        let (name, v) = pair
        v > 1
    }).count()
    println(f"{n}")
}
```

```
error: Mismatched types.
 --> t.nika:5:9
  |
5 |         v > 1
  |         ^^^^^
  = note: Consider dereferencing the borrow.
```

`filter` hands its lambda a reference to the item (`&&(String, i64)` in Rust). The `let` destructures it into references, so `v` is `&i64`, and the comparison with a plain `1` is lowered without the dereference.

#### To build

1. In the lowering, a part bound by a tuple `let` out of a referenced tuple is read as a value where it is a copy type (`*v` or a `let (name, &v)`-style pattern), and as a view otherwise, as a `for (name, v) in pairs` head already does.
2. Tests: the program above prints `1`; the same with `map` and with a map's `iter()`; `name` used as text in the body.

Related: #623 (whether the lambda parameter itself may be a tuple pattern).

### <a id="issue-625"></a>[#625](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/625) A catch over two or more error types needs `else` (Part I 7.1), but not through a function value: the checker answers one question two ways

`ready` · opened 2026-10-14 · updated 2026-10-15 · 1 comments

#### What happens

Part I 7.1: *"A `match` over `error` that more than one error type reaches needs `else`, because the set of error **types** is open (`NK1151`)."* When the set grows, every `catch` over that callee is named once in the build output (`NK2402`), and its `else` takes the new error.

Checked on develop 0.0.964:

| Call | Arms `Bad::Oops`, `Worse::Boom`, no `else` |
| :--- | :--- |
| a direct call `step(2)` whose body throws both | `NK1151`, as Part I says |
| a method that calls stored code, `r.fire()` | `NK1151`, as Part I says |
| a call through an element of a list of code, `for run in all { run() }` (`function_value_errors::a_list_of_functions_fails_with_the_union`) | **accepted**, against Part I |

#### Why this needs a decision

Built under #620 (ADR-351), the checker treats the set at a call through a function value as closed and exhaustive. For every other call, Part I keeps it open. One of the two has to give.

- **A. Part I stands.** A `catch` over two or more types needs `else` everywhere, including through function values. The tests from #620 that rely on exhaustiveness get an `else`. A precise set is still worth something: one type needs no `else`, an empty set is `NK1134`, fatal errors are seen, and `NK2402` notices growth.
- **B. Exhaustive everywhere.** The inferred set is whole-program and known, so a `catch` that names every type needs no `else`. A callee gaining a failure then breaks the build at every exhaustive `catch` instead of being reported by `NK2402`. Part I 7.1 and the `NK2402` text change.

Found while preparing #620's register/fire question.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-15](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/625#issuecomment-6181293594):

#### Decision (owner, 2026-10-15): B, a `catch` that names every type is complete

Recorded in [ADR-357](../blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-357.md) (D1-D5), Part I 7.1 and Part III C.3 (`NK1151`, `NK2402`, new `NK2404`). It changes ADR-023 D4, ADR-280 D18 and ADR-308 D11 (*Changed by* notes, *What supersedes what*). Version 0.0.966, commit af76c02.

1. The set of error types arriving at a `catch` is closed. A `match` over `error` is complete when every **non-fatal** type in it is covered: each variant named, or the type taken by a bare name. A fatal type passes on unless named (ADR-345 D2). `else` stays allowed (D1).
2. `"?"` or a `from(p)` not yet replaced in the set needs `else` (`NK1151`) (D2).
3. When a callee's set grows, a `match` that named every type without `else` is `NK1151`. The message names the new type, the callee and the call that brings it in, from the ledger diff. A handler with `else`, or one that does not match, keeps the `NK2402` warning (D3).
4. `NK2404` (warning): the author of a `pub` function whose set grew since the committed ledger is told, at the statement that brings the new type in, that callers naming every type will stop building. Committing the ledger acknowledges it, as ADR-288 D32 does (D4).
5. A complete `match` lowers with no catch-all arm (D5).

What it costs, as weighed with the owner: a new error type in a `pub` function stops dependents' complete handlers. That is the same cost a new variant already had. A caller who wants robustness writes `else`. There is no run-time cost.

#### To build

1. **Checker.** Use one completeness rule for every `catch`, with direct calls, methods and calls through code alike. Exclude fatal types. `"?"` or an open `from(p)` needs `else`. Tests:
   - a direct call throwing `Bad` and `Worse`, with both named and no `else`, compiles and runs;
   - the same with `Worse` missing is `NK1151`;
   - a set with `process::Exit` is complete without naming it, and `exit(3)` still ends the program with 3;
   - a `catch` over an undescribed call needs `else`;
   - `function_value_errors::a_list_of_functions_fails_with_the_union` keeps passing.
2. **Lowering.** A complete `match` writes no `_` arm. Test: the Rust of the direct-call example has no `_ =>`.
3. **Growth.** `NK1151` with the ledger narration where a committed set grew under a complete `match`, and `NK2402` only for handlers with `else` or without a `match`. Tests: grow `step`'s set against a committed ledger, once with a complete `match` (refused, message names the new type and the call) and once with `else` (warning).
4. **`NK2404`.** Warn when a `pub` function's set grows against the committed ledger, at the statement that brings the type in. Test: a library function gains a `throw`, and the build warns once, naming it.

The commit that builds the last step says `Fixes #625`.

### <a id="issue-624"></a>[#624](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/624) A catch over a method that calls stored code matches the boxed error by its types without opening the box: rustc refuses (C.1)

`ready` · opened 2026-10-14 · updated 2026-10-14 · 0 comments

#### What happens

Nikaia accepts this program, and the Rust it lowers to is refused by rustc (Part III C.1). Checked on develop 0.0.964.

```nika
enum Bad { Oops }
enum Worse { Boom }
impl Error for Bad { fn message(ref self) -> String { return "oops".clone() } }
impl Error for Worse { fn message(ref self) -> String { return "boom".clone() } }

struct Task { run: fn() throws }
struct Registry { tasks: Vec[Task] }

impl Registry {
    fn register(ref mut self, t: Task) { self.tasks.push(t) }
    fn fire(ref self) throws { for t in self.tasks { t.run() } }
}

fn fail() throws { throw Bad::Oops }
fn worse() throws { throw Worse::Boom }

fn main() {
    let mut r = Registry { tasks: [] }
    r.register(Task { run: fail })
    r.register(Task { run: worse })
    r.fire() catch {
        match error {
            Bad::Oops => println("oops"),
            Worse::Boom => println("boom"),
            else => println("other"),
        }
    }
}
```

rustc reports `Mismatched types` at `match error` twice: expected `Box<dyn std::error::Error>`, found `Bad`, and the same for `Worse`.

#### Why

`fire` lowers to `async fn fire(&self) -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>>`, because the stored code's channel is the box. The `catch` at the caller is lowered as if the error were the sum: `match error { Bad::Oops => …, Worse::Boom => …, _ => … }`. It does not open the box by the type each arm names. The opening that exists for a call through a field or a name of code (0.0.956, `check_boxes.nika`) does not reach a call to a **method** whose body calls stored code.

#### To build

1. In the emitter, a `catch` over a call whose callee's channel is the box opens it by the type each arm names (`downcast`), whatever path made the callee boxed. A method, a free function and a call through a value should all go through the same path.
2. Test: the program above runs and prints `oops`.

Related: #620 (ADR-351; this is the registry case discussed there, and #620 may lower it without the box). Independent of how #620's open question is decided, Nikaia must not accept a program that rustc refuses.

### <a id="issue-623"></a>[#623](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/623) A parameter may take a tuple apart, in a function and a lambda (ADR-358)

`ready` · opened 2026-10-14 · updated 2026-10-15 · 1 comments

#### What this is about, from zero

A **lambda** is a function written in place: `xs.iter().filter(fn(x) { x > 1 })`. A walk (`filter`, `map`, `any`, ...) hands its lambda **one item** at a time. When the items are tuples - a `Vec[(String, i64)]`, a map's pairs - that one item is a pair:

```nika
let pairs: Vec[(String, i64)] = [("a".clone(), 1), ("b".clone(), 2)]
let n = pairs.iter().filter(fn(pair) { let (name, v) = pair
    v > 1 }).count()
```

Authors (and rs2nika's drafts) write the shorter form, naming the parts as parameters:

```nika
let n = pairs.iter().filter(fn(name, v) { v > 1 }).count()
```

**Today** (0.0.952) the checker refuses that form with `NK1101`: *This lambda takes 2 arguments, but it is handed one tuple*, and the help writes the one-argument form with `let (name, v) = pair`. Before 0.0.952 it reached `rustc`, which refused `|name, v|` with E0593 (lowering-defects D18). So nothing is broken now; the question is whether the short form should **mean** the destructuring.

#### Where the question comes from

`docs/lowering-defects.md` D18, met when rs2nika drafts of `check/mod.rs` were run through `lower-std` (2026-10-11). It is a language question, not a defect: no ADR, spec section or issue decides it (searched: ADR-231 decides how `filter`'s lambda takes its item, not tuples; Part I's lambda section names no pattern parameters). Nothing needs the answer now: the refusal names the spelling that works. It is a convenience question.

#### How other languages do it

| Language | Two names for one pair | Spelling |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Rust | refused (E0593) | `\|(name, v)\|` - a **pattern** in the parameter |
| Swift | accepted until Swift 4 (SE-0110 removed the implicit splat, then a special case brought it back for closures) | `{ (name, v) in }` |
| Kotlin | refused for `Pair` | `{ (name, v) -> }` - parentheses mark destructuring, distinct from `{ name, v -> }` |
| JavaScript | refused in effect (second parameter is the index) | `([name, v]) => ...` |
| Python | refused since Python 3 (PEP 3113 removed tuple parameters) | `lambda pair: ...` |

Every one of them that allows it marks the destructuring with **brackets**; none lets the count of parameters decide silently, because `fn(a, b)` also means "two arguments" for `fold` and for function values.

#### Options

1. **Keep the refusal** (today). One spelling, `let (a, b) = pair` in the body. Cost: one line per lambda.
2. **A pattern in the parameter, with brackets**: `fn((name, v)) { .. }`, lowering to Rust's `|(name, v)|`. Explicit, matches Rust and Kotlin; needs a grammar change (a parameter becomes a pattern) and the checker must type it.
3. **Splat by count**: `fn(name, v)` takes a pair apart when the place hands one tuple of that size. Shortest; but `fn(a, b)` then means two things depending on the item type, and a change of the item type silently changes the meaning (Swift's SE-0110 story).

#### Risk if decided wrong

Option 3 makes a program's meaning depend on an inferred type; a refactor of the item type turns a destructuring into an arity error or the reverse. Option 2 adds grammar for a convenience. Option 1 costs nothing but a line.

#### Recommendation

**Option 1 now, option 2 if a later round wants it**: the refusal with its help is already built (0.0.952) and teaches the working form; bracketed pattern parameters are the only form other languages converged on and can be added later without breaking anything. Not option 3.

#### To build (only if option 2 is chosen)

1. Grammar: a lambda parameter may be a tuple pattern `(a, b)`.
2. Checker: the pattern's names are typed from the tuple the place hands; a size mismatch is `NK1101`.
3. Lowering: `|(a, b)|`.
4. Tests: `filter`, `map` over pairs, a map's `iter()`; the refusal of `fn(a, b)` stays.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-15](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/623#issuecomment-6181642478):

#### Decision (owner, 2026-10-15): option 2 for functions and lambdas alike

**How it went.** The issue recommended option 1 now and option 2 for lambdas later. Two points came up with the owner.
- *Python 3 removed tuple parameters (PEP 3113). Is that a problem for us?* Python's reasons were these: a signature is an object asked at run time, the pattern parameter had no name (`.0`), annotations and keyword calls could not reach it, and it was seldom used. None of that holds here. The signature is the ledger's and is read by the compiler, the parts have names, and options (the only parameters a call names) stay names. As for use: about 171 of roughly 1070 closures in the compiler's own Rust take a tuple apart (counted by regex), and those are exactly what rs2nika ports.
- *Allowing it in a lambda and not in a function would be an inconsistency.* Opening both means one rule, and the ledger gets one rule as well. A lambda that one day needs a ledger entry of its own is named the same way.

**Checked on 0.0.966:**

| Form | Result |
| :--- | :--- |
| `fn(name, v)` | `NK1101` |
| `fn((name, v))` | parse error |
| `fn(pair) { let (name, v) = pair … }`, the form `NK1101`'s help recommends | **refused by rustc** (`v` is a reference). Filed as **#626** |

**Decision.** Recorded in [ADR-358](../blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-358.md) (D1-D5), Part I 2.1 (*Taking a tuple apart*) and 5.3, and Part III 13.5 and C.3. Version 0.0.967, commit 4069587.
1. A positional parameter of a function or a lambda may be one irrefutable tuple pattern, ADR-356's: `fn dist((x, y): (f64, f64))`, `fn((name, v))`. A function writes the whole type after it. An option is never a pattern, and a variant is `NK1259` (D1).
2. It stays **one parameter** for callers, the function's type and arity. `fn(a, b)` stays two arguments. `NK1101`'s help now writes `fn((name, ty))` (D2).
3. `ref` and `mut` belong to the whole parameter: the parts become views or changeable (D3).
4. **Ledger:** `signature` writes the pattern as written, and every other key names the parts by name. A caller maps a part to its argument's component (D4).
5. **Lowering:** a hidden parameter name plus a `let` at the top of the body (D5).
6. Rejected:
   - refusing it;
   - lambdas only;
   - splitting by the number of names.

#### To build

1. **Parser.** A parameter of `fn` (with `: Type`) and of a lambda may be a tuple pattern. `(p)` is `p`. A variant there is `NK1259`, and an option written as a pattern is refused.
2. **Checker.** Bind the parts with ADR-356's walk, from the declared type (function) or from the item the place hands (lambda). Keep `NK1101` for `fn(a, b)` over one tuple, with the new help.
3. **Lowering.** A hidden parameter name and a `let` taking it apart at the top of the body. Parts of a borrowed tuple are read as the `for` head reads them (this is the same fix as #626).
4. **Ledger.** Write the pattern in `signature`. Every key that names a parameter (`keeps`, `returns`, `from(p)`, `code`, `maps`, `views`, `touches`, `sharing`) names parts. Readers map a part to the argument's component.
5. **Tests:**
   - `filter(fn((name, v)) { v > 1 })` over `Vec[(String, i64)]` prints `1`;
   - `map(fn((k, v)) { … })` over a map's `iter()`;
   - a nested pattern `fn((a, (b, c)))`;
   - `fn dist((x, y): (f64, f64))` called with a tuple;
   - `fn pick(ref (a, b): (String, String)) -> ref String` returning `a` or `b`, with the ledger writing `ref(a | b)` and a caller in another package reading it;
   - a `mut (a, b)` parameter whose change reaches the caller;
   - `fn(Some(x))` is `NK1259`;
   - `fn(a, b)` over pairs is still `NK1101`, with the new help.

The commit that builds the last step says `Fixes #623` (and `#626` if it lands with step 3).

### <a id="issue-620"></a>[#620](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/620) A function value keeps the error set the compiler inferred for it, whether it is called at once or kept

`ready` · opened 2026-10-14 · updated 2026-10-15 · 8 comments

#### What this is about, from zero

In Nikaia a function says only `throws`. *What* it can fail with is inferred by the compiler from its body, across the whole program, and written to the ledger (Part I 7.1: *"`throws` names no types. What a function can fail with follows from its body."*). In a `catch`, `error` is *"the sum of the errors that can arrive at this point, as the compiler inferred them"*. Since ADR-345, `process::exit` is one of them: the fatal error `process::Exit`.

A function can also be a **value**: handed to a parameter, stored in a field, a list, a `supervisor::Child`, or a server's handler.

```nika
enum Bad { Oops }
impl Error for Bad { fn message(ref self) -> String { return "oops" } }

struct Job { run: fn() throws }

fn fail() throws { throw Bad::Oops }
fn now(f: fn() throws) throws { f() }

fn main() {
    now(fail) catch { match error { Bad::Oops => println("immediate") } }   // compiles: the set arrives

    let job = Job { run: fail }
    job.run() catch { match error { Bad::Oops => println("kept") } }        // refused
}
```

Part I 5.4 C draws the line between the two: *"For an immediate parameter the lambda's `touches` and `throws` count for the callee […]. For a kept parameter the callee's promises follow the type."* A kept function value is just `fn() throws`, "can fail with something". **The inferred error set is dropped where a function value is kept.**

#### Where this comes from

The owner, 2026-10-14, while reading #605's item 5 (a `catch` written directly on `supervisor::run`). The decision basis there recommends wrapping a child's fatal error in `supervisor::Handed` (Java's `ExecutionException` model), *because* a child's error set is not known: ADR-346 §5, D7: *"The hand-over is a type of its own because `run`'s channel is typed and a child's is not."* The owner's view: a language meant for formal proofs must not throw away on the way what it inferred, all the more since the ledger keeps so much at every function boundary. This issue is the question behind #605's item 5, and **#605 is blocked by it**.

Something concrete needs the answer: #605's last open item, the defect below, and every `std` entry that keeps a function.

#### What is already decided

- **Part I 7.1**: error sets are inferred whole-program; `error` in a catch is their sum. Not touched here.
- **Part I 5.4 C**: kept function values follow their type, so the set is dropped. **This is what the question is about.**
- **ADR-346 D5** already went the other way for one kept function: a `TaskHandle` carries its body's error set and `join` throws it. D7 did not do the same for `supervisor::Child`.
- **ADR-288**: for `sync`, the ledger already carries a property through function values: 27 entries in `std.contracts` say `sync = "from(f)"` or `sync = "sync(f)"`. For `throws` there is no such form; not one entry has it.

#### What is true today (checked on develop, 2794745)

**1. The checker puts a kept function's errors at the wrong place.** The program above:

```text
error[NK2605]: This function can fail, because `fail` can fail.
  --> a.nika:16:5
 16 |     let job = Job { run: fail }
error[NK1134]: Nothing here can fail, so the `catch` has nothing to handle.
  --> a.nika:17:5
 17 |     job.run() catch {
```

Storing `fail` is counted as a failing call. The actual call, `job.run()`, is counted as unable to fail, so its error **cannot be caught at all**. With `main() throws` and no `catch`, the program runs and ends with `Error: oops`: the runtime hands the error on in the box; only the checker's view is wrong. This is a defect under every option below.

**2. `std` entries that keep a function**, and where their errors go:

| Entry | Contract | The kept function's errors |
| :-- | :-- | :-- |
| `supervisor::child(body: fn() throws)` | `keeps = ["body"]` | restart policy; a fatal one becomes `supervisor::Handed` (ADR-346 D7) |
| `net::serve(address, handler: fn(net::Connection) throws)` | `keeps = ["handler"]`, `throws = ["io::IoError"]` | `net.rs` `said()`: `eprintln!` and dropped. **A fatal `Exit` from a handler sets no exit status**, against ADR-346 D1/D6 |
| `task::Scope::spawn(body: fn() throws)` | `sync = "sync(body)"` | handed on by the scope (ADR-346 D7, 0.0.915) |
| `task::scope(body: fn(ref task::Scope) -> T throws)` | `throws = ["task::Crashed"]` | the tasks' errors travel in a box |
| `SharedMut::supervised(build: fn() -> T sync)` | no `throws` | cannot fail |

**3. Two runtime registries exist because the types were dropped:**
- `process.rs` `FATAL` (0.0.906): `main` registers which types are fatal, so a box can tell at run time.
- `task.rs` `CROSSING` (0.0.909): `main` registers which error types may cross threads as themselves.

Both answer at run time a question the compiler had answered and then forgot.

#### How others do it

From memory (m); to be checked before an ADR quotes it.

| Language | A function value's error type | Kept (stored) functions |
| :-- | :-- | :-- |
| Java (m) | checked exceptions are written by hand; `java.util.function` interfaces declare none | lambdas that throw are wrapped in unchecked exceptions; a generic `throws E` carries one type only. The typical failure of hand-written effects |
| Swift (m) | `rethrows` for a parameter called at once; typed throws `throws(E)` (Swift 6) is generic | `throws(E)` is part of the closure's type, so a stored closure keeps it |
| Rust (m) | the error is in the result type, `impl Fn() -> Result<T, E>` / `Box<dyn Fn() -> Result<T, E>>` | the type keeps `E`; a list of mixed closures needs one `E` (an enum or a box) |
| Koka (m) | effect rows, inferred and polymorphic | a stored function keeps its row; a list of them has their union |
| Kotlin, C# (m) | unchecked; nothing in the type | nothing to keep |

Java failed because the sets had to be **written**. Nikaia infers them, so its cost is the one Koka pays: sets that travel through types.

#### Options

**A — As today: a kept function is "can fail with anything".** Fix defect 1 so `job.run()` counts as failing with `?` and a `catch` with `else` is allowed. *Pro:* small. *Con:* a catch over a kept call is never exhaustive; `Exit` cannot be seen statically; `Handed` and both registries stay; `net::serve`'s lost fatal error stays a run-time special case. *Worst case:* the compiler cannot prove what it could have proved: a missing proof, not a wrong value.

**B — A function value's type carries its inferred error set, kept or not.** `fn() throws` in source still names no types; the compiler infers the set at each value (a named function, a lambda) and carries it through `let`, fields, lists, maps, returns and parameters. A container of functions with different sets carries their union, the same sum 7.1 already builds for a `catch`. A `pub` entry that keeps a function says so in the ledger as it does for `sync`: its calls and the types that hold it throw what `f` throws (`throws = "from(f)"`-style). *Pro:* 7.1 holds everywhere; `job.run() catch` is exhaustive; #605 item 5 becomes option 1 for any list; `Handed`, `FATAL` and probably `CROSSING` can go; `net::serve` knows statically whether a handler can `exit`. *Con:* error sets become part of type identity. Two functions with different sets are different types, unified to the union where they meet. That is new checker work through fields, generics and `with`. A library that keeps a caller's function needs the `from(f)` form across package boundaries, and a change to what a kept function throws changes a type its users see (ADR-288's weakened-contract warning would apply). *Worst case:* a refusal where unification is not yet implemented: visible, not a wrong value.

**C — Only `std` types carry it, case by case.** `TaskHandle` (done), then `Child`, `serve`'s handler; user fields stay as A. *Pro:* covers today's concrete cases. *Con:* two rules for the same thing; every new `std` type that keeps a function repeats the work; a user's `struct Job { run: fn() throws }` stays unprovable.

#### Recommendation

**B.** It is 7.1 taken at its word, and it is what ADR-346 D5 already did for one type. The ledger has the mechanism for `sync`; this extends it to `throws`. The cost is real but of the refusal kind, and it removes two run-time registries and a wrapper type that exist only because the information was lost.

**Risk of the recommendation:** the unification of error sets through generic containers (`Vec[fn() throws]`, `HashMap[K, fn() throws]`) is the part nobody has built yet; it may surface cases where the union grows larger than a reader expects. That is a message to design, not a wrong program.

#### Specification text proposed (Part I 5.4 C), if B is taken

Replace *"For an immediate parameter the lambda's `touches` and `throws` count for the callee, while … For a kept parameter the callee's promises follow the type"* with:

> A function value keeps the error set the compiler inferred for it (7.1), wherever it goes: a `let`, a field, an element, a result, a parameter. Calling it can fail with that set. Where function values with different sets meet, as two elements of one list or two arms of one `if`, the value carries their union. For an immediate parameter the lambda's `touches` count for the callee; whether the call may pause, and the shape it is lowered to, follow the parameter's type.

#### To build (if B is taken)

1. **Defect 1 first, under any option:** a function value stored is not a call; a call through a field, element or variable is. Tests: the program above compiles under B with the `kept` arm exhaustive, and under A with an `else` arm; `NK2605` is not reported on the `let`.
2. Checker: a function value's type carries its error set; union where values meet (`if`, `match`, list and map literals, `push`, `insert`). Tests: a `Vec[fn() throws]` of two functions with different errors, a `catch` over a call of an element takes both by name; a list of non-failing functions is `fn()` and a `catch` over it is `NK1134`.
3. Ledger: a `throws` form for entries that keep a function (`from(f)`), and for a `pub` function of a user package that keeps one. Tests across a package boundary.
4. `std`: `supervisor::child` and `supervisor::run`: `run` throws `Escalated` plus the fatal members of its children's sets; remove `Handed` (ADR-346 D7 amended). `net::serve`: a fatal error from a handler goes to the exit status as a detached task's does (ADR-346 D6). Tests for both.
5. Remove `process.rs` `FATAL` where the static set answers; check whether `task.rs` `CROSSING` can go too (the `Send` question remains).
6. ADR (new record, amending ADR-346 D7 and Part I 5.4 C), Part I 5.4 C text above, Part II 12.8.

Then #605's item 5 is option 1, and #605 can close.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-14](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/620#issuecomment-6166217137):

#### Decision (owner, 2026-10-14): B

**A function value keeps the error set the compiler inferred for it, called at once or kept.** Recorded in [ADR-351](../blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-351.md) (D1-D7). The rule is in Part I 7.1 (*"A function value keeps its set"*) and 5.4 C. The supervisor part is in Part II 12.8, the ledger's `from(p)` in Part III 13.5, and the handler rule beside `net::serve` in Part III. ADR-346 D7 is changed (*Changed by* note, index *What supersedes what*). Version 0.0.929, commit 7d40c23.

In short:
1. A named function or a lambda used as a value carries its body's inferred set through `let`, fields, list and map elements, results and parameters (D1).
2. A call through it fails with that set. Writing, storing, handing on or returning it fails with nothing (D2).
3. Where values meet (arms of an `if`/`match`, elements of one list or map, values assigned to one variable or field, the arguments every caller hands one parameter), the place carries the union. A place no failing value reaches cannot fail (D3).
4. The ledger writes a call through a given function as `throws` member `"from(p)"`, with `p` a parameter or a field path of one. The caller replaces it with what it passed. A type holding a function value carries the value's set, as `TaskHandle[T]` does (D4).
5. `supervisor::run` throws `Escalated` and its children's fatal errors themselves. `supervisor::Handed` is withdrawn (D5).
6. A `net::serve` handler's error is a detached task's error: long form on stderr, and a fatal one sets the exit status at `main`'s end (D6).
7. `"?"` stays for code no ledger describes, never for a function value (D7).

Not decided: an explicit set in source (`fn() throws(E)`), and how a large union is shown in a message.

#### To build

1. **The misattribution, first.** Storing a function value is not a call; a call through a field, an element or a variable is. Tests: the program at the top of this issue compiles, the `kept` arm is exhaustive with no `else`, and `NK2605` is not reported on the `let`. `NK1134` over a call through a value that carries the empty set.
2. **Checker: sets on function values and their union.** Cover `if`/`match` arms, list and map literals, `push`/`insert`, reassignment, fields, `with`, and parameters across all callers. Tests:
   - a `Vec[fn() throws]` of two functions with different errors: a `catch` over a call of an element takes both by name;
   - a list of functions that cannot fail: a `catch` over a call of an element is `NK1134`.
3. **Ledger: `"from(p)"`.** Write it for a function that calls a function value it was given (parameter or field path), read it at callers, and do the same across a package boundary (`nikaia describe`-free, a `path` dependency). Also apply ADR-288's warning when what such a function throws widens. Tests across two packages.
4. **`std` contracts.** `supervisor::child`/`Child` carry the body's set. `supervisor::run` throws `Escalated` plus the fatal members. `net::serve`'s handler and `task::Scope::spawn`/`task::scope` carry their bodies' sets.
5. **Runtime.** Remove `supervisor::Handed` and make `run` throw the child's error. In `net::serve`, `said()` hands a handler's error to the detached-task path (`task.rs` `DETACHED`), so a fatal one sets the status. Remove `process.rs` `FATAL` where the static set answers. Check whether `task.rs` `CROSSING` can go (the `Send` question remains). Tests:
   - a child calling `exit(4)`: `run` throws `process::Exit`, a `catch` arm `process::Exit` takes it, and without a catch the status is 4;
   - a handler calling `exit(3)`: the status is 3 at `main`'s end.
6. **#605's item 5** becomes ADR-351 D5. Close #605 with the commit that builds step 5.

The last commit of this list says `Fixes #620, #605`.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-14](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/620#issuecomment-6174036139):

**Course correction: no `?` for function values (ADR-351 D7).**

The owner asked whether treating a function value's errors as open (`?`) is sound architecture. Checked against ADR-351, it is not what was decided:
- D7 says "A function value is never a reason for `?`".
- D3 asks for the union over every value that reaches a place.
- D4 asks for `from(p)` in the ledger, substituted at the call site, and a function value's set carried as part of the type of whatever holds it.

What is built today falls back to `?` in several places:
- 0.0.940/0.0.941: `mut` names, push, assignment to a field, `with`.
- 0.0.947: a function named as a value, a `pub` field of a `pub` type.

That fallback is sound: a `catch` over it needs `else` (`NK1151`), so nothing reaches `unreachable!`. It is still not specific, so it is a temporary safety net and debt, not the design.

**To build, in place of the open steps:**
1. Replace each `?` source above with the specific set: D3 for mutation, push, assignment and `with`; D4 `from(p)` for parameters and for functions used as values; the set carried on the holding type for fields, `pub` ones included. Each with a test: a `catch` without `else` over that set compiles, and a missing member is refused.
2. `from(p)` is read back as itself, not as `?` with a side map.
3. Then std contracts, then the runtime (remove `Handed` and `FATAL`, `net::serve`).
4. The commit that says `Fixes #620, #605` only lands when no function value produces `?`.

If a step needs a language decision that ADR-351 does not cover, it is asked here, not settled with `?`.
