**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-14](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/620#issuecomment-6175094338):

#### Question: where the code a library keeps reaches a call with no parameter to name (ADR-351 D4)

##### From zero
A function value carries the error set the compiler inferred for it (ADR-351 D1). When a function calls code it was **given**, the ledger says so with `from(p)`, and the caller replaces `p` with what it passed (D4, Part III 13.5):

```nika
pub fn now(f: fn() throws) throws { f() }        // ledger: throws = ["from(f)"]
// caller in another package:
lib::now(fail) catch { match error { Bad::Oops => … } }   // exhaustive: from(f) = what `fail` throws
```

That case is buildable and was built (branch `wip/fnsets-from-p`: the ledger writes `from(f)`, the call site substitutes). The owner's correction (no `?` for any function value, D7) also asks to replace the remaining safety nets: a function named as a value, a `pub` field of a `pub` type, and `mut` names / `push` / field assignment / `with`. Most of that is D3 (a union over everything that reaches the place) and needs no decision. One case does.

##### The case D4 does not cover
A library **keeps** code in one call and **calls it in another**, so the calling function has no parameter that `from(p)` could name:

```nika
pub struct Task { pub run: fn() throws }
var tasks: Vec[Task] = []                         // library state
pub fn register(t: Task) { tasks.push(t) }        // keeps
pub fn fire() throws { for t in tasks { t.run() } }   // calls: from(?)
```

`fire`'s set is the union of what every caller of `register`, in every package, hands it (D3). The library cannot know that set when it is built, and the caller of `fire` cannot know it either. The same happens with a `pub fn make() -> Task` whose result's field set has to be published: Part III has no spelling for "the set of field `run` of the result".

##### Where it comes from
The owner's course correction of 2026-10-14 to this issue's *To build* step 3 (ledger `from(p)`). Something concrete needs the answer: a server or registry-style library (`net::serve` keeps its handler, but calls it through its own parameter, so `serve` is covered; a user library with `register`/`fire` is not). The step can't finish without an answer, and `Fixes #620` waits for it.

##### What is already decided
- Part I 7.1: sets are inferred **over the whole program**.
- ADR-351 D3: a place carries the union over everything that reaches it, "the arguments handed to one parameter by every caller" included.
- ADR-351 D4: `from(p)` names a parameter or a field path of one. Nothing names a place inside the library's state or a result.
- ADR-351 D7: a function value is never a reason for `?` (the owner confirmed this today).

##### What the compiler does today (develop, 0.0.950)
`fire`'s call through `tasks` is `?` (the 0.0.947 safety net: a `pub` field of a `pub` type is stored into by other packages). A `catch` over `fire()` needs `else` (NK1151). Nothing panics at run time, but nothing is proved either.

##### Options
**A. Places in the ledger, resolved by the program package.** The library publishes the place by name: `register` says `keeps = ["t → place tasks.run"]`, and `fire` says `throws = ["from(place tasks.run)"]`. The library's own set for `fire` stays open. The package that builds `main` sees every package, unions what each one hands `register` (D3), and closes the set there, as 7.1's whole-program inference already does for named functions. *Pro:* D3 and D7 hold everywhere, with no `?`. *Con:* a new ledger spelling (places, and `result.field` for `make`). A library's `catch` over `fire()` **inside the library** can never be exhaustive, because its set is only known at the program; such a catch needs `else`, and that `else` is something the library cannot name, not a `?`. *Worst case:* a refusal in the library, never a wrong value.

**B. A type that holds code is generic over its set.** `Task` is `Task[E]` invisibly; `tasks: Vec[Task]` in a library has one fixed `E` per program, inferred at the program, which is A seen through types. *Pro:* this is D4's second half taken literally ("the set is part of the value's type"). *Con:* the same ledger spelling question, plus generic sets through `Vec`, maps and globals in the checker. *Worst case:* as A.

**C. Refuse it.** A `pub` function may not call code that another `pub` function kept, except through `from(p)`. *Con:* rules out registries, event buses and plugin lists, which every language counts as basic.

##### Recommendation
**A** (B is the same rule stated through types; the ledger needs A's spelling either way). It is 7.1's whole-program inference extended by one kind of place. The library's own `catch` stays open, and that is honest: the library really does not know.

##### Owed before a build
- the Part III spelling of a place (`from(place tasks.run)`, `keeps` naming the place) and of a result's field (`from(result.run)`);
- **not a language question, just build work:** the Rust channel of a function whose set has a `from(…)` member varies with the caller. The plan is to keep the opaque box and lower a call site whose set is closed to a downcast into the caller's sum.

Label set to `decision`. Built so far for this step: the ledger's `from(p)` for parameters, and call-site substitution, on branch `wip/fnsets-from-p` (df61a87). It is **not** on develop, because it reads `from(p)` back as `?` plus a side map, which the correction rules out. Step 1's remaining D3 work (`mut`, `push`, assignment, `with`, named-as-value) does not depend on this question.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-14](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/620#issuecomment-6175121507):

**Addendum to the question above: how other languages handle code a library keeps in its own state.** These rows are from memory and have not been checked against current manuals.

| Language | `register(t)` / `fire()` over a library-held list |
| :--- | :--- |
| Rust | `static` list of `Box<dyn Fn() -> Result<(), E>>`. Either the library fixes `E`, or it uses `Box<dyn Error>`, which is open. A `static` cannot be generic. |
| Swift (typed throws) | A `Registry<E>` type can be generic. A global or shared registry needs a fixed `E` or `any Error`, which is open. |
| Koka | A mutable reference holding functions has one fixed effect row in its type. Effect polymorphism does not reach into global state. |
| Java, Kotlin, C# | Unchecked: always open. |
| Whole-program compilers (MLton) | They resolve every function value at link time, but for code, not for error types. No language from this list closes an error set there. |

So in every typed language, either **the library names the set**, or **it is open**. Option A, where the program package closes the set, would be new. It follows from Part I 7.1 (whole-program inference) and has no precedent elsewhere.

**One option is missing above:**

**D. An explicit set on the stored type** (`pub run: fn() throws(IoError, Timeout)`). ADR-351 §7 left this undecided. The library names what its stored code may throw. `register` refuses a `t` whose inferred set is larger, and `fire()` is exact inside the library and for every caller. No new ledger spelling is needed beyond the type.
- *Pro:* precise everywhere, including inside the library, which is how Rust and Swift do it.
- *Con:* a library author has to write the set, and a caller's own error types cannot pass through unless the library names them. Today `throws` names no types (ADR-023), and D would be the first place where they are written.
- D and A can be combined: written where the author wants precision, closed at the program where nothing is written.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-15](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/620#issuecomment-6181297020):

#### Decision on register/fire (owner, 2026-10-15): ADR-351 D8, the set belongs to the value that holds the code

**A correction to the question above.** Its example keeps code in library state, `var tasks: Vec[Task] = []`. That cannot be written in Nikaia: `var` is no keyword (`Didn't expect var here`), and ADR-281 D32 says *"This language has no global mutable data."* There are no trait objects either (Part I: *"a trait is not a type: there is no `dyn`"*); traits are bounds, resolved per type. So code that is kept always lives in **a value someone holds**, reached from a parameter or a result. The "place only the program can close" that option A needed does not exist.

**Checked on develop 0.0.964**, with a registry written in Nikaia (`struct Registry { tasks: Vec[Task] }`, `register(ref mut self, t)`, `fire(ref self) throws`):
- a `catch` over `r.fire()` naming `Bad` and `Worse` needed `else`. That followed Part I 7.1, which disagreed with this issue's function-value tests; that became #625 and is decided as ADR-357.
- with `else`, rustc refused the lowering: `fire` returns `Box<dyn Error>` and the `catch` matched the box as if it were the sum. This is filed as **#624**, and D8's lowering removes the box.
- a direct call with two error types lowers to the generated sum with **no box**. `now(fail)` (a `from(p)` callee) lowers to the box and is refused by rustc (the known defect, test ignored).

**Decision.** Recorded as [ADR-351 D8](../blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-351.md), Part I 7.1 and Part III 13.5 (new ledger key `code`). Version 0.0.966, commit af76c02.
1. Kept code lives in a value something holds, reached from a parameter or a result.
2. **The set belongs to the storage.** Every handle to the same storage (a `Shared`, both ends of a channel, positions `sharing` makes one allocation) shares it. It is the union of everything put there over the value's whole life, flow-insensitive.
3. **Ledger `code`**, in the shape of `maps`: `code = ["self.tasks.run: from(t)", "<result>.run: Bad"]`. A path is one field long below the parameter, through list or map elements. A caller adds the set, with `from(p)` replaced, to the value it holds.
4. **No box.** A type holding code whose set its holder decides, and a function whose set has a `from(…)` member, take a Rust type parameter for the error, filled with the place's generated sum. Code is converted where it enters the place, and a `catch` matches the sum. The box stays only for `"?"`.
5. Rejected:
   - places closed by the program package (no such place exists);
   - explicit sets `fn() throws(E)` (§7, still not needed);
   - box plus downcast (an allocation per error, a test per arm, and rustc refusals where an opening is missed).

With ADR-357 (#625), a `catch` over such a call that names every type is complete.

#### To build (replaces the open steps above)

1. **Ledger `code`.** Write it for a function that puts code into a place reached from a parameter or its result, read it at callers, and add the set to the caller's value at that path, with handles of one storage sharing it. Remove the `pub`-field `?` safety net. Tests:
   - the registry above, in one package: `r.fire()` with arms `Bad` and `Worse` and no `else` compiles and runs;
   - the same across two packages (`Registry` in a library, `register`/`fire` called from the app);
   - a `Shared` registry registered through one handle and fired through another;
   - code sent on a channel and called after `recv`;
   - `lib::setup(mut r)` adding a task, which then reaches `r.fire()`'s set.
2. **Lowering without a box.**
   - A function with a `from(p)` member becomes generic over the error: `fn now<E>(f: &impl Fn() -> Result<(), E>) -> Result<(), E>`.
   - A type holding holder-decided code takes a type parameter: `Task<E>`, `Registry<E>`.
   - Code is converted into the place's sum where it enters.
   - Tests: `now(fail)` runs (un-ignore `a_catch_over_a_library_call_takes_what_was_passed`), the registry runs, and the lowered Rust has no `Box<dyn std::error::Error>` in either.
   - This also fixes #624.
3. **The remaining `?` sources.** A parameter of code forwarded to another function (`g(f)`) gives `from(f)`. A `let mut` name handed to a method is covered by `code`.
4. Then the unchanged steps: std contracts (`supervisor`, `task::scope`/`spawn`, `net::serve`), and the runtime (remove `Handed` and `FATAL`, the `net::serve` detached path).

The last commit says `Fixes #620, #605, #624`.

State after 0.0.971 (37df596). Part of #620; nothing closed.

**Built**
- **A parameter of code called in a `throws` function hands its failure on.** Before this, `now`'s body `f()` lowered to `f().await;` with no `?`, so the error was dropped silently. This was a lowering defect. The checker now records the call (fact `run_code_fails`), and the emitter writes the `?`.
- **NK2402 through `from(p)`.** A `catch` over `now(fail)` is told what `fail` gained since the committed ledger, even though `now` still says `from(f)`. Test: `new_error_reaches_a_catch::a_passed_function_that_gained_an_error_is_named`.
- **New list step 3: a forwarded parameter.** A parameter handed on (`twice(g) { now(g) }`) is the place `@twice::g`, not `?`.
  - `twice` publishes `["Bad", "from(g)"]`, and `now` publishes `["Bad", "from(f)"]`.
  - A `from(G::q)` that reaches F through F's own parameter place is published as F's `from(p)`.
  - A `catch` over `twice(fail)` with only a `Bad` arm compiles and runs. Test: `a_parameter_handed_on_chains_from`.
- **A `let mut` name handed to a `mut` parameter of an own function** holds what that parameter's place holds. Test: `a_mut_list_handed_to_a_mut_parameter`, which now accepts both arms with no `else` and refuses one arm alone.

**Open (new list)**
- Step 1: the ledger key `code`, and removing the `pub`-field net.
- Step 2: the box-free lowering (also #624).
- Step 3, remaining:
  - a `let mut` name handed to a **method**, or to a function in **another package**, is still `?`. Per the new list, ledger `code` covers it.
- Step 4: std contracts and the runtime.

Tested after the final rebase (release):

### <a id="issue-609"></a>[#609](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/609) Single-variant enum shorthand for enums from other modules and packages (ADR-347)

`ready` · opened 2026-10-13 · updated 2026-10-13 · 0 comments

ADR-347 (#608, fa6bd8a) lets `Typ(x)` stand for `Typ::Variant(x)` when `Typ` is an enum with exactly one variant. The build rewrites the short form right after parsing (`crates/nikaia/src/one_variant.rs`), and it only knows the enums of the same file and of `std`. An enum declared in another module of the program or in a package is not recognised yet, so the short form is refused there although the rule (Part I 3.4) applies to every enum.

#### To build

1. Recognise single-variant enums from other modules of the program (resolved through `use`), e.g. by moving the rewrite to where module types are known, or by giving the rewrite the module's enum table.
2. The same for enums from packages (`packages/<name>/`, dependencies).
3. Tests: a two-module program constructing and matching `m::Wrap(x)`; a package enum used with the short form; `NK1252` still fires for a two-variant enum from another module.

### <a id="issue-604"></a>[#604](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/604) `json` as an official package: `read[T]`/`write[T]` by ADR-299's rules, a build-time schema, `json::Value` (ADR-344)

`libraries`, `ready` · opened 2026-10-13 · updated 2026-10-13 · 3 comments

#### History

ADR-338 (#539) made JSON an official package that reads and writes, but left open where it lives, what a read returns and what is checked when. The first concrete need: the facts tool (`crates/rs2nika/facts`) reads LSIF with a hand-written reader (#579) - JSON of a shape no struct declares up front.

Checked on 2026-10-13 with `target/release/nikaia run`: a loop over `T::fields` unrolls per type and reads `.name`/`.of(value)`, so `write[T]` can be written in Nikaia now. Building a value from fields does not exist (`field.set(…)` is `NK1180`), and a type passed as a parameter (ADR-330, `schema(CreateUser)`) is not parsed. A `String?` field in an unrolled `println` reaches rustc as a `Display` error instead of a Nikaia diagnostic.

Owner's answers of 2026-10-13: packages in `packages/<name>/`, own version only; first a plain package by path dependency, no `dsl` export, no ADR-338 D3; `json::read[T](text)` as a call with a build-time decoder and a thrown error with position; mapping by ADR-299 D16-D18, unknown members refused unless the type allows it; a JSON Schema as a build-time input, run-time constraints not decided; `enum json::Value` with `Int(i64)`/`Float(f64)`; `json::write[T]`.

#### Decision

[ADR-344](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-344.md), Part III 17.3 (`json`), codes `NK1250` (field type JSON cannot be) and `NK1251` (`T` does not match the schema) in C.3.

Proposals the owner has not yet confirmed (ADR-344 D4): the opt-in for unknown members as the attribute `@json::Open` (and `@json::Rest` on a `Map[String, json::Value]` field to keep them); whether a missing member goes into a `T?` as `null` by default.

#### To build

1. `packages/json/` (`nikaia.toml`, own version 0.1.0): `enum Value`, `parse(text) -> Value throws Error`, `Error` with line, column, path. Tests: every JSON value kind, escapes and surrogate pairs, `1` is `Int`, `1.0`/`1e3`/`2^63` are `Float`, member order kept, an error position on a truncated text.
2. `json::write[T]` and `write` of a `Value` over `T::fields` with `@json::Name`. Tests: round trip `parse(write(v)) == v`; field order; `null` for an empty `T?`; escaping.
3. Building a value from reflected fields and ADR-330's type parameter in the compiler (spec text for the construction first, Part II 10.3), then `json::read[T]`. Tests: by name in any order; a duplicate member, a missing field, an unknown member, `null` into a non-`T?` each throw with path; `@json::Open`; nested structs, `Vec`, `Map`; `NK1250` on a field of a function type.
4. `schema:` read through `asset`, `T` checked at build time. Tests: one `.nika` per `NK1251` case (missing required, extra field, type mismatch, nullable into plain), and a passing case with `additionalProperties`.
5. Move `crates/rs2nika/facts` from its hand-written LSIF reader (#579) to `json::parse` / `read[T]`, by path dependency. Test: the facts output byte for byte the same on the existing fixtures.
6. ADR-338 D3 (a `use json` takes the package in) and the grammar export (#520, #485), see #539.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-13](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/604#issuecomment-6151121407):

#### Owner's answers of 2026-10-13 (second round), in ccd3993 (0.0.882)

1. **Missing members:** a field with no member takes its default if it has one (Part I field defaults), is `null` in a `T?`, and is otherwise an error where the text is read. Replaces the strict "missing is an error as in ADR-299 D17" for `T?`. This is a run-time throw, so no NK code; the compile-time case (a schema requires a property `T` lacks) stays `NK1251`.
2. **Unknown members:** not decided. The specification keeps ADR-299 D17's strict rule (error). `@json::Open` / `@json::Rest` are removed from Part III 17.3 and stand in ADR-344 D4 as *proposal, not decided*.
3. **Errors:** `json::Error` has the form of a grammar's `ParseError` (ADR-001 D2, Part III Appendix C): line, column and the stack of what was being read; the path `$.a[3].b` is that stack as JSON names it, not a second report.
4. **`json::parse`** does not exist; removed.
5. **`json::Value`** put back as a whole (out of Part III, ADR-344 "Not decided here"), with the owner's direction: numbers kept as text/a view and converted on access (writers put `12.0` for integers); member order put back with it. Old D8 is now D7.

`NK1250` (field type JSON cannot be; `json::Value` no longer in the list) and `NK1251` still hold as written in C.3.

#### To build (replaces the list above)

1. `packages/json/` (`nikaia.toml`, own version 0.1.0): `Error` in `ParseError`'s form (line, column, rule stack with path). Tests: an error position and path on a truncated text and on a bad value.
2. `json::write[T]` over `T::fields` with `@json::Name`. Tests: field order; `null` for an empty `T?`; escaping; nested struct, `Vec`, `Map`.
3. Building a value from reflected fields and ADR-330's type parameter in the compiler (spec text for the construction first, Part II 10.3), then `json::read[T]`. Tests: by name in any order; round trip `read[T](write(v)) == v`; a missing `T?` member is `null`; a missing member with a default takes the default; a missing required member, a duplicate member, an unknown member, `null` into a non-`T?` each throw with path; escapes and surrogate pairs; `NK1250` on a field of a function type.
4. `schema:` read through `asset`, `T` checked at build time. Tests: one `.nika` per `NK1251` case (missing required, extra field, type mismatch, nullable into plain), and a passing case with `additionalProperties`.
5. ADR-338 D3 (a `use json` takes the package in) and the grammar export (#520, #485), see #539.
6. **Waits for the dynamic form** (not decided, ADR-344): move `crates/rs2nika/facts` from its hand-written LSIF reader (#579) to `json`. Test: the facts output byte for byte the same on the existing fixtures.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-13](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/604#issuecomment-6151302453):

**Owner decision, 2026-10-13: unknown members are skipped.** (commit 7d37ef5, version 0.0.884)

**How it came up:** ADR-344 D4 had left open whether `json::read[T]` may accept a member `T` has no field for. Until then ADR-299 D17's rule applied: such a member was an error. There was also a proposal, `@json::Open`, to skip unknown members on a single type.

**Decision:** a member the target type has no field for is ignored. It is not an error. The owner's reason: in practice these members are protocol extensions, and those must not cause failures. This departs from ADR-299 D17 on purpose. A SQL result has exactly the columns the query names. JSON from outside grows without the program doing anything. With this rule, `@json::Open` is no longer needed.
- Why, with the comparison to serde, Go, Swift, Jackson, kotlinx and Pydantic and to the robustness principle (RFC 761/1122) and its critique (RFC 9413): [ADR-344 D8](../blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-344.md); D4 and the rejected alternatives are updated to match.
- The rule: Part III 17.3, the `json` paragraph.
- Not decided here: `@json::Rest`, because it needs the dynamic form, which is itself deferred. An opt-in strict mode, because no program needs one yet.

**To build (addition):**
- Test: `{"name":"a","email":null,"extra":{"x":[1,2]}}` read with `json::read[Contact]` into `struct Contact { name: String, email: String? }` gives `Contact { name: "a", email: null }` and no error. A malformed value inside the extra member is still a `json::Error`.

### <a id="issue-602"></a>[#602](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/602) A number literal has no span: a refusal at it stands at the whole statement (after #107)

`compiler`, `ready` · opened 2026-10-13 · updated 2026-10-13 · 0 comments

Since #107 (0.0.869), a refusal about an expression stands at that expression, using `Parsed::expr_spans`, keyed by the `NodeId`. **Number literals are left out.** The parser builds them through `int_literal` with `NodeId::fresh()` instead of a parse-order id, so they have no span entry. A refusal at one falls back to the enclosing statement.

```nika
fn f(c: bool) -> i64 {
    let y: String = if c { "a".clone() } else { 1 }
    return y.len()
}
```

This is `NK1103` (#600), with the caret under the whole `let`, not under `1`.

#### To build

1. Number literals the parser reads take a parse-order id and a span entry, like every other parsed expression. Find the `NodeId::fresh()` in the parser's `int_literal` path; check the float literal path too. Literals the compiler builds stay `fresh()` (ADR-340 D2).
2. **Tests:**
   - `expr_ids.rs`: two parses give the same ids and spans for a file with number literals, and a literal's span is its own text;
   - `typecheck.rs`: the program above puts the caret on `1` (line 2).
3. Check that nothing keyed on a literal's id changes meaning (grep `int_literal` and the tables keyed by `NodeId`). Run the `nikaia check` corpus comparison: positions may move, codes must not.

### <a id="issue-601"></a>[#601](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/601) A named function handed to a parameter the callee keeps passes the checker and fails in rustc (`Kept<dyn Fn…>` against a fn item)

`bug`, `compiler`, `ready` · opened 2026-10-13 · updated 2026-10-17 · 1 comments

Found by the session that wrote #558's per-table tests (13 Oct), and reproduced on `b7ca8849`.

```nika
struct One {
    f: fn(i64) -> i64,
}

fn keep(f: fn(i64) -> i64) -> One {
    return One { f: f }
}

fn double(x: i64) -> i64 {
    return x * 2
}

fn main() {
    let o = keep(double)
    let g = o.f
    println(f"{g(4)}")
}
```

The checker passes this. Then rustc refuses it: "Mismatched types … Expected struct `nikaia_std::func::Kept<(dyn Fn(i64) -> Pin<Box<dyn Future<Output = i64>>>)>`", found a fn item, at `keep(double)`.

A parameter the callee keeps is lowered as `Kept<dyn Fn…>`, and a lambda handed to it is wrapped into one. A **named** function handed there is passed bare. The same function goes in correctly through a struct literal (`One { f: double }`) or an annotated `let`. This breaks Part III C.1.

#### To build

1. A named function, or a method used as a value, handed to a kept parameter is wrapped as a lambda handed there is. Find the lambda path: `kept_lambdas`, `kept_functions`, `emit_core_args.nika`.
2. **Tests** (`function_values.rs`):
   - the program above prints `8`;
   - the same with a function that may pause, where the kept type is the boxed future;
   - with two arguments, one named function and one lambda, in one call.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-17](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/601#issuecomment-6232301627):

Steps 1 (named function) and 2 (tests) are built in 3d278b6: `check_core_args_other` now hands a named function of this program that lands in a kept parameter to `check_core_a_kept_lambda`, the same path a struct literal's field takes. `a_named_function_handed_to_a_kept_parameter_runs` in `function_values.rs` covers the program above, a function that pauses (`slow`), a `sync` kept type, and a named function beside a lambda in one call.

**Still missing: a method used as a value.** `keep(P::add)`, with `fn add(ref self, n: i64) -> i64` and `keep(f: fn(ref P, i64) -> i64)`, still fails in rustc with the same `Kept` against fn item error. So does `One { f: P::add }`, so this is not only the argument path. `check_core_a_kept_lambda` handles only `Expr::Variable`, and the host's `kept_call` (`Parsed::kept_function_call`) builds the call from a plain name. A `Path` arm needs to go through the checker, `CheckHost::kept_call` in `check/host.rs`, and the `Expr::Variable` arm in `emit/mod.rs` that writes `kept_functions`. I left it open because `check/host.rs` was being changed by a parallel agent at the time.

### <a id="issue-595"></a>[#595](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/595) Build ADR-342: a chain over a list runs element by element; an overflow in it may stop it at the end; a filter without a branch

`performance`, `ready` · opened 2026-10-12 · updated 2026-10-12 · 0 comments

#### How it came up

An outside proposal (generated by Gemini) suggested four kinds of "pipeline fusion" so that Nikaia reaches Mojo's speed:
- fuse `map`/`filter` chains into one loop;
- merge filter conditions;
- vectorize the fused loop;
- fuse producer and consumer tasks.

Its premises were partly wrong. It assumed an arena that is built (ADR-327 decides one, but it is not built, see #490), and it claimed that `&&` is "one decision point". The owner asked what can be taken from it. The goal: Nikaia should compete with Mojo where the plain chain a model writes is as fast as the loop an expert tunes, with every check kept. Mojo gets its speed from written `SIMD` and `vectorize`, and does not check overflow.

#### What was checked (0.0.854)

- **Chains over a sequence are one loop already.** `xs.iter().map(…).filter(…)` lowers to Rust's iterator adapters (ADR-293 D20). LLVM fuses those, so a separate fusion pass would add nothing there.
- **A list's `map` is eager.** `xs.map fn(x) { x * 2 }.map fn(y) { y + 1 }` lowers to `xs.map(|x| …).map(|y| …)` on `nikaia_std::list::ListExt`, which is `into_iter().map(f).collect()`. That is two passes. Rust reuses the buffer where the element sizes match; elsewhere a new list is built. Under ADR-327 the list between the steps takes room in the task's block.
- **A list has no `filter`.** It is `xs.iter().filter`. Part III's paragraph on `Seq` said `xs.map fn …` hands back a sequence, which contradicts Part I 5.3 and #517. It now says `xs.iter().map fn …`.
- **`a && b` stays two branches** after inlining, because of the short circuit. One branch needs `&`, which is only allowed where the second condition cannot panic.
- **The overflow checks block vectorization:** each one is a branch per element. They are the reason a checked chain is not vectorized, more than where the data lies.
- **Fusing tasks would remove concurrency the program wrote.** Not done. What a value costs when it moves between tasks is ADR-327 D9 (#490).
- **Found on the way:** #594. A `filter` after `map` over a sequence fails in rustc.

#### Decision

[ADR-342](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-342.md), with the rule in Part I 4.5 and Part III A.2:

- **D1:** a list's `map` whose result goes straight into the next step runs element by element with it, where every lambda touches nothing and changes no name outside it. A chain with I/O or a lock runs as written.
- **D2:** in such a chain, the order of the lambdas is not fixed, and neither is which panic stops it.
- **D3:** an overflow may stop the chain after its last element, and still names its own line.
- **D4:** a filter's conditions may be joined with `&`, or the filter may run without a branch.
- **D5:** what can only be faster is built without a measurement. What can be slower (D3, D4, and D1 in one shape) is measured first, and emitted only for the chain shapes where it is not slower than today.

#### To build

1. **D1, the fusion.** In the emitter, a list's `map` whose result is not named and goes into another `map`, `iter()`, a `for` or `len()` is lowered as `into_iter().map(f).map(g)…`, with one `collect` where a list is wanted. The condition is the one `par_iter` lambdas meet (`touches` empty, no `NK2107`), without the demand not to pause.
   - **Test:** `xs.map(f).map(g)` gives the same output as today.
   - **Test:** a chain whose first lambda `println`s keeps today's order: all of `f`, then all of `g`.
2. **D1, the one measurement it owes.** A fused loop where one step is vectorized on its own and the other is not, against two passes. Count it with callgrind (instructions and mispredicted branches), output byte for byte the same. Where fusion is slower for that shape, that shape is not fused.
3. **D3, measure first.** A chain `map`/`sum` over `i64`, three ways:
   - today's code;
   - `overflowing_*` with the flags OR-ed and one check after the loop;
   - the same, per run of elements.

   Then the same chain where LLVM does not vectorize. Emit D3 only where it is not slower. A value that overflowed must reach no index, division or condition before the check.
   - **Test:** an overflow in such a chain stops the task with `src/main.nika:N: … attempt to add with overflow`, naming the line of the `+`.
4. **D4, measure first.** A filter with two cheap conditions, on random data and on sorted data:
   - `&&` (today);
   - `&`;
   - without a branch (write every element, move the end by the condition).

   Emit each form only where it is not slower.
   - **Test:** a filter whose second condition can panic keeps `&&`.
5. **Specification:** nothing left. Part I 4.5 and Part III A.2 already say it.

#### Not decided here (ADR-342 §6)

- `filter` as a method of a list.
- D3 per piece in `par_iter`.
- Vector types for code that is not a chain.

### <a id="issue-594"></a>[#594](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/594) A list of untyped number literals passes the checker and fails in rustc when a lambda reads its items (`filter fn(v) { v > 4 }`)

`bug`, `ready` · opened 2026-10-12 · updated 2026-10-17 · 1 comments

Found while checking how chains are lowered for ADR-342.

```nika
fn main() {
    let xs = [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]
    let n = xs.iter().map fn(x) { x * 3 }.filter fn(v) { v > 4 }.count()
    println(f"{n}")
}
```

`nikaia run` (0.0.854) passes the checker, then rustc refuses it (`expected &_, found integer`). Rust's `filter` hands its lambda a reference; ADR-231 D2 says that where the item copies, the parameter takes it by pattern (`|&v|`), recorded in `filter_patterns`.

**The cause (comment of October 17, 2026): not the `map`.** `xs.iter().filter fn(v) { v > 4 }` alone fails the same way; with `let xs: Vec[i64]` (now `Vec[int]`) both forms work. The checker types `[1, 2, 3, 4, 5]` as `Vec[?]`: Part I says a list of number literals is one number without a type, decided as a `let`'s is (2.4), and that happens in the pass after the walk (`check_numbers3.nika`, `number_lets`). While `filter` is checked the item is `Unknown`, and the `filter` arm (`check_core_method.nika`) rightly refuses to guess (`!item.is_unknown()`): `|&v|` on an item that does not copy would break other programs.

No decision is needed: the language says the program is valid (Part III C.1: a program that passes the checker and fails in rustc is a defect). The owner asked for it to be built (October 17, 2026).

#### To build

1. **The walk knows an item is a number without a type yet.** Every integer and float type copies, so that alone settles the pattern. Either carry such a marker through the walk (`iter`, `into_iter`, `map` with arithmetic, `filter`, `take`, `skip`, `rev`, … - wherever an item type flows), or settle the pattern in the number pass from the binding at the root of the receiver chain; choose the one that fits the checker's existing handling of open numbers (see #629 D-2, ca79333: a method on an open number already resolves as `i64`'s), and say why in the commit. Not a local special case in the `filter` arm only.
2. The same for the other adapters that hand a reference to their lambda where ADR-231 D2 applies (`filter`, `position`, `any`/`all` if they borrow, `skip_while`, `take_while`, …): one rule, not one arm each.
3. **Tests:** the program above prints `4`; `xs.iter().filter fn(v) { v > 4 }.count()` over untyped literals prints `1`; the same over a list of `String`s with `fn(s) { s.len() > 1 }` still compiles; a float literal list; a literal list whose type a later use decides as `u8` still gets that type and the pattern.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-17](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/594#issuecomment-6232303322):

Checked on 8506ecd. This still fails, but the `map` is not the cause, so the guess in the issue is wrong:

| program | result |
| :--- | :--- |
| `let xs = [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]` + `xs.iter().filter fn(v) { v > 4 }.count()` | rustc: expected `&&_`, found integer |
| same with `let xs: Vec[i64] = …` | prints `1` |
| `let xs = [1, 2, 3, 4, 5]` + the chain above | rustc: expected `&_` |
| same with `let xs: Vec[i64] = …` | prints `4` |

The checker types `[1, 2, 3, 4, 5]` as `Vec[?]`. Part I says a list of number literals is "one number without a type, decided as a `let`'s is (2.4)", and that is decided only in the pass after the walk (`check_numbers3.nika`, `number_lets`). So while `filter` is checked, the `Seq`'s item is `Unknown`, and the `filter` arm in `check_core_method.nika` correctly refuses to guess (`!item.is_unknown()`). A `|&v|` on an item that does not copy would break other programs.

**So what has to be built is bigger than step 1.** During the walk, the checker has to know that an item is a number without a type yet. Every integer type copies, so that alone settles the pattern. One way is a marker the walk carries for such an item, through `iter`, `map` with arithmetic, `filter` and the other steps. Another is to settle the filter pattern in the number pass, from the binding at the root of the receiver chain. Either one is a change to how the checker types number literals, not a local fix in the `filter` arm. Until then, `let xs: Vec[i64] = …` avoids the problem.

### <a id="issue-591"></a>[#591](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/591) A `HashMap`/`HashSet` key that cannot be hashed passes the checker and fails in rustc (the sibling of D15)

`compiler`, `ready` · opened 2026-10-12 · updated 2026-10-14 · 3 comments

#### What this is, from zero

A `HashMap` finds a value by hashing its key, so the key type must be hashable and have an equality that is an equivalence (Rust: `Hash + Eq`). A float is neither (`NaN != NaN`), and a `struct` the program declares derives `PartialEq`, `Eq` and `Copy` where its parts allow, but not `Hash`.

```
struct P { x: i64 }
let mut a: collections::HashMap[P, i64] = collections::HashMap()
a.insert(P { x: 1 }, 1)        // the checker accepts; rustc refuses
```

#### Where it comes from

Found while building `NK1247` for #589 (ordered maps: ADR-010 D10), which refuses a `BTreeMap`/`BTreeSet` key with no order. The same question for the hash maps was not asked then. Nobody's program needs it yet; it is a Part III C.1 hole: a rustc error about generated code reaches the user.

#### Facts (tried on 0.0.850, `nikaia run`)

`HashMap[P, i64]` (declared struct), `HashMap[f64, i64]` and `HashMap[Q, i64]` (a struct holding an `f64`) all pass the checker; each fails in rustc with *the method `insert` exists for struct `HashMap<..>`, but its trait bounds were not satisfied* (three errors, one per map). `Comparable::asking` already has `Strength::Totally` (`Eq`), used only to decide derives (`compares_totally`); nothing asks it for a map key.

#### Options

* **(a)** Refuse a hash-map key that is not `Eq`-comparable and whose type cannot be hashed, with a Nikaia message, as `NK1247` does for order. Declared types stay unhashable. Cheapest; a user keys by text or number.
* **(b)** Refuse, and **derive `Hash`** for a declared type whose parts hash (the same walk as `compares_totally`), so `HashMap[P, V]` works. More useful; field order then matters to nothing (a hash is not an order), so the risk is small. Adds a ledger column `hashes` for library types (as `orders` was added).
* **(c)** Leave it to rustc (rejected: C.1).

#### Risk

(a): none beyond refusing programs that never compiled. (b): a derived `Hash` for a type with a custom `==` (there is none: equality is always derived) is the only way the two could disagree; and with #581 (hasher per class) the derive must work with both `Fast` and `Keyed` hashers (it does: `Hash` is hasher-independent).

#### Recommendation

(b), by the `NK1247` pattern: `Strength::Hashes` in `check/mod.rs`, ledger column `hashes`, `describe` writes it where the crate derives `Hash`, the emitter derives `Hash` where `compares_totally` holds. Code `NK1248` (check that it is free). If the owner wants the smaller step first: (a).

#### To build (after the decision)

1. Tests first (`crates/nikaia/tests/hashed_keys.rs`): keys that hash pass (numbers, `String`, `bool`, `char`, tuples/lists/options of those, declared structs and enums of those under (b)); `f64`, a struct holding one, a library type without `hashes` are refused in Nikaia's words; `BTreeMap` is not asked.
2. Checker + ledger column + `describe` + `described.contracts` (`Symbol` hashes).
3. Emitter derive under (b).
4. Part III: column row and the code; ADR-010 D11. Version raise.

Related: #589 (ordered keys, built), #586 (checker-accepts-rustc-refuses family), #581, #497 (an unknown type is not refused: this check inherits that).

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-12](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/591#issuecomment-6122983579):

Note from #592 (ADR-341): `NK1248` and `NK1249` are taken by that decision, so this issue's check takes the next free code, not `NK1248`. `Hash` as a bound is the follow-up to #592 and shares the ledger-column pattern (`orders`, `equates`, and here `hashes`).

---
_Generated by [Claude Code](https://claude.ai/code)_

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-14](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/591#issuecomment-6172210350):

#### Decision (owner, 2026-10-14): (b), derive

The owner took it as settled that a declared type used as a hash-map key gets a hash derived from its values. No record said so, so it is recorded now.

**Where the question stood before.** No ADR decided it. Two records left it to this issue:
- [ADR-204](../blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-204.md) §4: *"A hash, and with it a declared type as a map's key … a thing to want before it is a thing to build"*;
- [ADR-341](../blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-341.md) §3/§6: *"`Hash` follows with the hash-map key check (#591)"*.

The closest decided rules are ADR-204 and ADR-341 D4 (equality derived from the parts) and [ADR-010](../blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-010.md) D10 (ordered keys, `NK1247`, #589). D10 refused a derived **order** because field order would become a promise. A hash makes no such promise. No other issue covers this.

**One fact changed since this issue was written.** ADR-341 D5 now lets a program write `impl Eq` for its own type. *"Equality is always derived"* (Risk above) no longer holds. A hash derived from the fields would disagree with a hand-written equality, so such a type does not hash.

**Recorded** in [ADR-010 D11](../blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-010.md). Part I (map keys), Part III 13.5 (ledger column `hashes`) and C.3 (`NK1260`; `NK1248` went to ADR-341 and `NK1257` to ADR-354). ADR-204 §4 and ADR-341 §6 now point to D11. Version 0.0.942, commit 5488b2d.

In short:
1. A `HashMap`/`HashSet` key is checked by the structural walk `==` uses (`NK1188`).
2. A declared `struct` or `enum` hashes where every field hashes. The hash is **derived from its fields in the order written**, as equality is, so equal values hash alike. No one writes `hasher.add(...)`.
3. A float, a map, a set, and a declared type with a hand-written `impl Eq` do not hash. A library type hashes only where its ledger says `hashes = true`.
4. A key that does not hash is `NK1260` at every position a type is written. The message names the part that does not hash, or the hand-written equality.
5. A text view hashes its text. The hash function stays ADR-010 D1-D4's (fast or keyed by provenance, #581); the derived hash works with both.

Not decided: `Hash` as a bound (`[T: Hash]`), ADR-341 §6.

#### To build

1. **Tests first** (`crates/nikaia/tests/hashed_keys.rs`).
   - Keys that hash pass: numbers, `String`, `bool`, `char`, `Bytes`, tuples/lists/options of those, and declared structs and enums of those, used in `insert`, `get`, `contains_key` and a `HashSet`.
   - `NK1260` in Nikaia's words for: `f64`; a struct holding one; a declared type with `impl Eq`; a library type without `hashes`.
   - `BTreeMap` is not asked.
2. **Checker:** `Strength::Hashes` beside `compares_totally` in the walk, with `impl Eq` read as *does not hash*. Report `NK1260` at every type position.
3. **Ledger:** the `hashes` column, read for library types. `nikaia describe` writes it where the crate derives `Hash`. Add `hashes = true` in `std.contracts` / `described.contracts` where it holds (`Symbol` among them).
4. **Emitter:** `#[derive(Hash)]` on a declared type where the walk says it hashes.
5. **Run-time test:** a `HashMap[P, i64]` keyed by a two-field struct, two equal keys find one entry, at both hash classes (a `Trusted` and an `Untrusted` map).

The commit that builds the last step says `Fixes #591`.

---
_Generated by [Claude Code](https://claude.ai/code)_

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-14](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/591#issuecomment-6172634031):

**Addendum (owner's decision): a type with its own `impl Eq` hashes through an `impl Hash`.**

The decision above refused every declared type with a hand-written `impl Eq` as a hash key. The owner narrowed that: such a type is refused **unless it also writes `impl Hash`**. Recorded as [ADR-010 D12](../blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-010.md), Part I (map keys, the traits paragraph) and Part III (`NK1249`, `NK1260`), 0.0.944 (58e366d).

```nika
impl Hash for Name {
    fn hash(ref self, mut into: Hasher) sync {
        into.add(self.text.to_lowercase())
    }
}
```

* `Hash` is a trait of the language, in scope like `Eq` and `Ord`; `Hasher`'s one method `add(x)` takes a value that hashes. It lowers to Rust's `Hash` (`into.add(x)` is `x.hash(state)`). The hash function stays the map's (D5): the body feeds values and never returns a number, so it cannot weaken a hardened map.
* `impl Hash` is allowed for any declared type that is `Eq`, by derivation or by `impl Eq`; it replaces the derived hash. Writing it for a foreign type or for a type that is not `Eq` is `NK1249`. A body that can pause is `NK1129`. A value handed to `add` that does not hash is `NK1260`.
* The law (values that `eq` calls equal feed `add` the same values) is the program's and is not checked, as for `Eq` (ADR-341 D5).
* `[T: Hash]` as a bound stays open (ADR-341 §6).

**To build, in addition to steps 1-5 above:**

6. Checker: register `Hash` and `Hasher` as language names. Answer the key check with "hashes" for a type with an `impl Hash`. Apply `NK1249` (foreign type, or not `Eq`), `NK1129` (pausing body) and `NK1260` (argument of `add`).
7. Emitter: write `impl std::hash::Hash for P { fn hash<H: std::hash::Hasher>(&self, into: &mut H) { … } }` with `into.add(x)` as `(x).hash(into)`, and omit the `Hash` derive for `P`.
8. Tests:
   * `Name` with case-insensitive `impl Eq` + `impl Hash` as a `HashMap` key: `"A"` and `"a"` find the same entry;
   * the same type without `impl Hash` is `NK1260`, and the message names the `impl Hash`;
   * `impl Hash` on a type that is not `Eq` is `NK1249`;
   * `into.add(1.5)` is `NK1260`.

`ready` · opened 2026-10-12 · updated 2026-10-12 · 0 comments

#### Found

By the `nikaia import rust` design review (2026-10-11), checked on origin/develop 72363084 with its binary.

#### Today

1. `CLAUDE.md` (the self-hosting section) says `lower-std` reports every refusal "as `nikaia check` does", and builders look for a `nikaia check` command. **There is no `check` subcommand**; `nikaia lower` is the way to see the checker's findings.
2. Part I 2.6 says there is no `s.len()` on text, and the checker accepts `s.len()` (a byte length; `"äb".len()` is 3 in both Nikaia and Rust).

#### To build

1. Decide per item whether the document or the compiler moves: for 1, add the `check` subcommand (the checker without lowering; it is a thin driver and every builder wants it) or correct `CLAUDE.md`; for 2, state the rule (is it a byte length? what about non-boundary positions?) in Part I 2.6, or refuse `s.len()`. If it changes what a program means or what the toolchain does, the version rises (`python3 scripts/bump.py new`); if it only fixes the text of `CLAUDE.md`, no.
2. A test for each: `nikaia check f.nika` prints the checker's findings and exits non-zero on a refusal; `s.len()` is accepted or refused as the text now says.

### <a id="issue-587"></a>[#587](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/587) The parser takes exponential time on nested parentheses, ifs and blocks, and overflows the stack at about 900 nested calls

`compiler`, `ready` · opened 2026-10-12 · updated 2026-10-12 · 0 comments

#### Found

By the self-hosting sessions of 2026-10-11 (emit U1a, and the check-core planning). Not measured with callgrind yet (counts, never time, per CLAUDE.md); the figures below are observations.

#### Today

* Nested parentheses, `if`s and blocks are exponential: 16 levels took 0.6-1.7 s, 60 nested parentheses did not finish in 60 s, and 16 blocks with a missing `}` ran past 20 s.
* Deep nesting of calls overflows the stack in the parser at about 900 levels; a `.abs()` chain overflows after parsing between 1,600 and 1,800 (default 8 MB stack).
* The tests `emit_deep_nesting.rs` and `check_deep_nesting.rs` therefore chain (calls 500, lists 500, `??` 500, a chain of 1,000) and never nest parentheses, ifs or blocks.

#### To build

1. A test that parses nested parentheses, `if` expressions and blocks 64 deep and finishes (today it does not); a missing-`}` case that reports the error without backtracking blow-up. Measure with callgrind (instructions) at 8, 12 and 16 levels to show the growth.
2. Find the cause (a backtracking alternative that re-parses a sub-expression: `callgrind_annotate`) and remove the re-parse (parse once and decide, or memoise by position).
3. A stack guard in the parser (a depth limit with a Nikaia message, Part III C.2) so nested calls fail with a diagnostic instead of a stack overflow, at a depth above the deep-nesting tests.
4. Extend the deep-nesting tests to parentheses, ifs and blocks. `Fixes #<this issue>` on the last commit.

Related: #125, the checker core plan (`docs/selfhosting-plan.md`, "C-6 in units": a 2,953-line `match` is one large frame).

### <a id="issue-586"></a>[#586](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/586) Lowering defects found while moving the compiler: D1-D3, D8, D14-D19 and four new ones

`selfhosting`, `compiler`, `ready` · opened 2026-10-12 · updated 2026-10-12 · 1 comments

#### How it came up

The self-hosting runs (#125) found lowering defects: the checker accepts a program and rustc refuses the lowered Rust (or the lowering writes something clippy refuses). That is a compiler defect (Part III C.1; ADR-294 D3: fixed in the compiler, not bent around in a `.nika`). `docs/lowering-defects.md` holds 19 entries, each with a probe, the expected and the actual result, and the workaround; 13 were reproduced on 2026-10-11.

#### Today

From `docs/lowering-defects.md`: D1 a by-value read-only parameter lowers as `&T` and `p with {..}` writes `..p`; D2 `m.get(k)?.clone() ?? d` on a map clones a reference; D3 `set.insert(..)` as the last call of a match arm / `if` is `bool` against `()`; D4 a `for` over `Vec[Symbol]` binds `ref Symbol` (checker-side); D7 `x.len() as i64` writes a doubled cast (clippy); D8 a cast inside a map read lowers as `&x as i64`; D10 `x ?? 0` over an `Integer?` lowers to a plain integer; D11/D11b a typed `throw` in a trait impl is not boxed / a thrown struct without `impl Error` reaches rustc; D14 a `Copy` value bound in a `match` inside a guard arrives as `&T` (same family as #578); D15 `BTreeMap[Symbol, V]` passes the checker and fails in rustc; D16 a `match` as a guard needs brackets; D17-D19 three more accepted by the checker and refused by rustc.

New since, not yet in that file (add them): `println!(..)` passes the checker and fails in rustc; `xs.iter().filter fn(x) { x % 2 == 0 }` fails rustc (`&&i64`); a Nikaia method named `expect` resolves to `Option::expect`, so a callback passed through it lowers as `Kept<dyn Fn>` instead of `impl Fn`; `fn(ref String, ref String) -> ref String` passes the checker and fails rustc (E0106; may be closed by #580, re-check).

#### To build

1. For each defect a minimal test (`.nika` that the checker accepts, lowered and compiled with rustc, run) that fails today; collect them in `crates/nikaia/tests/` (one file per family).
2. Fix each in the lowering or make the checker refuse it with a Nikaia message (Part III C.2: no Rust words); small each (one commit per defect, a version raise per compiler change via `python3 scripts/bump.py new`).
3. Remove the workarounds from `tools/*.nika` and the entries from `docs/lowering-defects.md` as they land; `scripts/rs2nika.py --lint` flags D1-D3, D7, D8 in hand-written Nikaia: keep it in step.
4. D5, D12, D13 are by design (message wording only). The last commit says `Fixes #<this issue>`.

Related: #578, #573.

Its sibling, a `HashMap`/`HashSet` key that cannot be hashed (float, declared struct), is the same kind of defect and is filed as #591.

### <a id="issue-585"></a>[#585](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/585) NK2302 follow-ups: over-refusals, the coarse binding of a local, one caret at a declaration

`language`, `compiler`, `ready` · opened 2026-10-12 · updated 2026-10-13 · 2 comments

#### How it came up

#580 (ADR-283 D25/D26) is built (46a0ba06 ... 61e21b9d). The builders measured over-refusals (sound programs refused) and left three imprecisions. Nothing here makes an unsound program compile; they refuse more than needed. A rustc error must never reach a user (Part III C.1), so each fix keeps that.

#### Today

1. **A local without a `let` annotation that a method call or a `mut` parameter touches always binds the other arguments** (B1/B2). Over-refused: a few valid programs. Measured: 0 over-refusals in `tools/*.nika`, `tests/**`, `examples/**`, `benches/**`.
2. **The result walk does not know the fields of a type declared in another file** (`world.words.resolve(..)` beside a second view parameter is refused; the previous builder worked around it by passing the interner as a parameter). `declared.nika` / the ledger would have to give the fields. Noted in `docs/mitigation-hurdles.md` section 1.
3. **A declaration-site refusal (D26) has one caret**; a way out in a body has one label per way out.
4. Std entries with no `ref(..)` map to every argument including the key (`self.cache.get(world.key)` is refused); a result typed as the receiver's type parameter should map to the receiver only.

#### To build

1. Tests first: the over-refused programs of 1, 2 and 4 (accepted and compiled in rustc after the fix) and the dangle-attempt set that must stay `NK2302` (`tests/result_source.rs`).
2. Tighten B1/B2 by the type of the local (unannotated: infer it from the checker's type) and the callee's `mut` parameter type.
3. Read the fields of a type from another file from the ledger.
4. D26: carets on every parameter named.
5. A std entry whose result is typed as the receiver's type parameter maps to the receiver only.
6. Callgrind (Ir + mispredictions, `--branch-sim=yes`) on a large `nikaia lower` before and after; `scripts/c6/oracle.py` 0 differences; `Fixes #<this issue>`.

Related: #580, #579.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-12](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/585#issuecomment-6131791877):

#### A fifth over-refusal: a view stored in a local struct that never leaves the function (found 12 Oct, 0.0.861)

```nika
struct Holder {
    name: String,
}

fn two(s: ref String, c: bool) -> i64 {
    if c {
        let h = Holder { name: s }
        return h.name.len()
    }
    let g = Holder { name: f"{c}" }
    return g.name.len()
}
```

This is refused with `NK2302`: *"`two` keeps `s` after this call … It's stored in `Holder.name`. `Holder.name` is a `ref String`"*. Two things are wrong:
- Nothing keeps `s` past the call: `h` is a local, and only a length leaves.
- `Holder.name` gets a view and text of its own. By ADR-282 D21 that is a mixed field, not a `ref String`.

The same holds when the field is fed by `if c { s } else { f"{c}" }` directly. There is no `if`/`match` in `two`, so this is not #596's or #562's. It is sound, and nothing reaches rustc. A program must write `s.clone()` it does not need.

**For the To build list:**
- a test that `two` runs and prints `2` and `4`;
- the stored-in-a-field way out of the result walk asks whether the struct value itself leaves the function.

- A `let x = Struct { … }` whose name the rest of the block reads only through its fields no longer counts its fields as stored (`views_a_local_struct` in `views.nika`). Where those field reads go is still asked.
- A struct that is returned, pushed into a kept list, or stored in another struct is still `NK2302` (`stored_views.rs::a_local_struct_keeps_a_view_only_where_it_leaves`).

### <a id="issue-583"></a>[#583](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/583) Self-hosting emit: the rest of the core (E9 U9-U10, E10, E11)

`selfhosting`, `compiler`, `ready` · opened 2026-10-12 · updated 2026-10-12 · 0 comments

#### How it came up

Part of #125 / #558. `emit/mod.rs` is moved to Nikaia in sequential units through a host trait (`EmitHost`), decided by a read-only architecture review. The plan, the method form, the oracle and the risks are in `docs/selfhosting-plan.md`, section "E9 in units". Cost and speed of the runs so far: `docs/tokenspeed.md`.

#### Today (develop, 0.0.848)

Done: E1-E8 and E9 U0a-U8 (`Out`, `EmitState`/`EmitWalk`/`EmitWorld`, `EmitFlow`/`EmitScope`, the layer below the core, leaves, reads, match, args, calls, places, flow): commits 0bb1721 ... f46ac49c. `scripts/self_hosting.py` shows the share.

#### To build (strictly one unit at a time; `package.rs` and the version files are shared)

1. **U9** `tools/emit_core_stmt.nika`: all 15 arms of `a_stmt` (Let, Comptime, Assign (5 guarded arms together, original order), While, For, Break, Return, Expr ...), one Nikaia function per variant; the Rust `a_stmt` stays the dispatcher until U10.
2. **U10** `tools/emit_core_expr.nika`: the remaining arms of `expr_as_written` (literals, Variable x6, Dsl, Path, Block, Unsafe, Overlap, Select, If), the methods `expr`, `expr_after_the_copy`, `expr_as_written`, `block_opening_with`, `block_plain`, `stmt`, `a_stmt`. Measure with callgrind (Ir and mispredictions, `--branch-sim=yes`) before U0 and after U10, as the plan says; no callgrind before U10 (each crossing allocates an `EmitScope`).
3. **E10** the layer above (`function`, `item`, `function_body`, `program`, `grammar`, `fold`) as `tools/emit_items_core.nika` with `Declared`/`MethodOf` as owned structs.
4. **E11** `emit/mod.rs` shrinks to the adapter (`with_contracts`, entry points, `SourceMap`, `Build`, `EmitRefused` to anyhow); update `scripts/self_hosting.py`.

Per unit (see the plan's task template): `lower-std` with the previous binary and the new one, `package.rs` identical except the additions; `scripts/c6/oracle.py OLD NEW .` 0 differences; clippy, fmt, the tests the moved code touches + `examples`, `sysroot`, `self_hosting`, `refusal_lines`, `emit_deep_nesting` after the final rebase; `python3 scripts/bump.py new`/`rebase-fix`/`check`; no wip commits; at most ~120 turns per agent.

Known traps: a host method must not touch `self.walk(..)` (pass the walk in); `take_lend`/`insert_after_first_brace` stay Rust (no `String::pop`/`insert_str` in `std`); `reached_copy` is not restored after a thrown error (no try/finally); prover strings use `rust_string` where Rust used `{:?}` (differs only for exotic control characters).

### <a id="issue-582"></a>[#582](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/582) A function called through a variable with a map argument fails in rustc (E0308)

`compiler`, `ready` · opened 2026-10-11 · updated 2026-10-11 · 0 comments

#### Found

By the #581 step 4 session (2026-10-11, fb4e044a). Not touched there. A rustc error reaching the user is a compiler defect (Part III C.1).

#### Today

A function that takes a map and is called through a variable gives rustc `E0308` (mismatched types) for any map argument:

```
fn count(m: HashMap[String, i64]) -> i64 { .. }
fn main() {
    let f = count
    let a = HashMap()
    f(a)
}
```

The class computation of #581 (ADR-010 D9) treats a function used as a value as keyed ("a function used as a value ... touches it"), so the map at the call site and the parameter of `count` are not given the same lowered type. The direct call `count(a)` is fine.

#### To build

1. A test that lowers the program above, compiles the lowered Rust with rustc, and runs it (it fails today with E0308); one more with the function passed as an argument (`apply(count, a)`) and one stored in a struct field.
2. The class of a function used as a value includes the call sites through the variable: the parameters of `count`, the argument of `f(a)` and the map `a` are one class, keyed if any member is untrusted (the same join as for a direct call). If the call sites are not all known (the value escapes the package), the parameter is a boundary position and follows the boundary rule of ADR-010 D9.
3. Commit with `Fixes #N`.

Related: #581, #580.

### <a id="issue-581"></a>[#581](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/581) One lowered type per map: the hasher is decided per package, and at a package's boundary it is a type parameter (ADR-010 D9)

`language`, `compiler`, `ready` · opened 2026-10-11 · updated 2026-10-15 · 3 comments

#### How it came up

In the self-hosting runs (#125, #558) the builders write `BTreeMap`/`BTreeSet` in every `tools/*.nika` file, because a `collections::HashMap` field did not keep its Rust type: `docs/lowering-defects.md` D6, `docs/mitigation-hurdles.md` §3, `docs/import-rust-design.md` decision D (#579). The owner's direction (October 11, 2026): *the compiler should be clever enough to fix the type only once all the information is there*, and, for the package boundary, *as with generics, both variants must be possible*.

#### What was checked (on develop a392be71, binary of 46a0ba06)

What decides a map's Rust type today (`emit_exprs.nika` `map_name`, `emit/mod.rs` `trusted_input`/`trusted_lets`, `contracts/trust.rs`): **the provenance of the unit being lowered**. A trusted unit writes `TrustedMap` (Fx, fixed seed) for **every** `HashMap` it mentions, types and constructors alike; an untrusted unit writes std's keyed `HashMap`, except a `let` map that never leaves its function and is filled only from trusted values (#431). Which unit's provenance is used:

* **A project** (`modules.rs` `emit_reading`): the **entry file's** analysis, for every file of the package. Probe: `main.nika` reads nothing, `serve.nika` reads `net::Connection::read` and fills a map from it. The map lowers to `TrustedMap` (fixed seed, attacker keys), while `--trust` prints *untrusted, keyed* for `serve.nika`. **A hash-flooding hole, and the report and the code disagree** (D7).
* **The tools package** (`sysroot.rs` `lower_tools_at` → `emit_std_items_against_at`): **each file's own** analysis, against that file alone. A method call on a value whose type another file declares cannot be resolved there, and an unresolved call counts as untrusted (D1). Probe: `hm_a.nika` declares `pub struct HmNames { seen: HashSet[String], ids: HashMap[String, i64] }` → `TrustedSet`/`TrustedMap`; `hm_b.nika` builds it and calls `n.seen.insert("a")` → `HashSet::new()`: rustc E0308. Without the `insert`, both are `TrustedMap`. **This is D6**: not *whether the file builds one*, but whether that file reads anything it cannot resolve.
* **Across packages**: each package by its own analysis; the ledger line `fields = ["pub counts: collections::HashMap[String, i64]"]` says nothing about the hasher. Probe 1: a trusted `lib` with `pub struct Tally`, an app that reads a socket and writes `lib::Tally { counts: collections::HashMap() }` → **a rustc error reaches the user** (*Expected struct `HashMap<String, i64>` found `HashMap<_, _, RandomState>`*; Part III C.1). Probe 2: the same app passes socket bytes to `lib::count_in(t, key)` → compiles, **attacker keys in a fixed-seed map**.
* The compiler's own code: 0 `HashMap`/`HashSet` type positions in `tools/*.nika` (BTreeMap/BTreeSet everywhere), 32 mentions left in the Rust of `check/` and `emit/`, 9 `TrustedMap`/`TrustedSet` in `package.rs`.
* Precedent: ADR-037 D7-D8 decides `Rc`/`Arc` per class of values by union-find and puts the atomic floor at every public signature, because *a published artifact has already chosen*. The owner's boundary answer differs on purpose: a Nikaia package is lowered and compiled with its consumer, so Rust monomorphises a hidden parameter there. Whether `Shared` should follow is **not decided here**.

#### The decision

[ADR-010 D9](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-010.md), Part III 17.1 *`std::collections` - and where keys come from*, the `maps` row of Part III 13.5, `NK1246` in C.3, one sentence in Part I 3.x (maps).

1. **Classes, per package.** Every place a map type is written is a position. Positions a map value passes between (initialised, assigned, passed, returned, stored, compared, the arms of an `if`/`match`) are one class and get one Rust type. A class is keyed if any key that reaches any value of it is untrusted, or it touches a barrier; otherwise fast. Decided after **every file of the package** is read; the order of files and which file builds or fills a map change nothing.
2. **At the boundary, a type parameter.** A map position in a `pub` function's signature or in a `pub` type is a hidden type parameter of that item, one per position in declaration order. Each use fills it, inside the package and outside, as a generic's use does.
3. **The ledger `maps` key** per `pub` function: which positions are one class, and what the body puts in: `either`, `from(p, ...)`, `keyed`. The caller joins it. Absent = keyed. It is a permission key (a wrong `either` lets a caller pick the fast hash for attacker keys), written by the compiler from the body only.
4. **Fixed keyed positions (barriers):** a trait method's signature, a described Rust crate's signature, a binding for another language.
5. **Rust names:** `nikaia_std::hash::Fast`, `nikaia_std::hash::Keyed`; a `pub` type's parameters default to `Keyed`, so Rust code that names none gets the safe map.
6. **Conflicts:** the join never conflicts (keyed wins, which is safe). Only `hasher:` (ADR-316 D4, not built) can fix a class to something else; two different named hashers in one class are refused, `NK1246`.

#### To build

Tests first, each in `crates/nikaia/tests/map_trust.rs` unless named:

1. **Package-wide trust (the security fix, first, alone):** the probe above (`main.nika` reads nothing, `serve.nika` fills a map from `net::Connection::read`) lowers that map keyed, and `--trust` and the code agree. Replace the entry-only analysis in `modules.rs` `emit_reading` and the per-file one in `sysroot.rs` `lower_tools_at` with one analysis over all units; method calls resolve against the whole package.
2. **Test: one type per class across files.** A struct declared in one file, built in a second, filled in a third (with and without a method call on it): every mention lowers to the same Rust type; rustc compiles it. The same with the files in the other order. Then the tools probe (`hm_a`/`hm_b`) through `nikaia lower-std` on a scratch sysroot.
3. **Test: a conflict by join.** A private `fn count(m: ref HashMap[String, i64])` called with a map from a file and one from a socket: both keyed; without the socket call: both fast.
4. **Classes in the compiler** (union-find over positions, seeded by D1's sources and barriers), replacing `trusted_input` and `trusted_lets` in `EmitState` with a per-position answer; `--trust` lists the classes.
5. **Test: the boundary as a type parameter.** A two-package test as `contracts_across.rs` does: `lib` with `pub struct Tally { counts: HashMap[String, i64] }`, `pub fn tally_new() -> Tally`, `pub fn count_in(mut t: Tally, word: ref String)`; an app that fills it from a file gets `Fast`, one that fills it from a socket gets `Keyed`; an app that writes `lib::Tally { counts: collections::HashMap() }` compiles in both. Then build it: hidden parameters `S: nikaia_std::hash::Seed` (trait over `BuildHasher + Default + Clone`), default `Keyed`, maps made at such a position with `S::default()`.
6. **Test: the ledger line.** `nikaia.contracts` of `lib` carries `maps = ["t.counts: from(word)"]` for `count_in` and `maps = ["<result>.counts: either"]` for `tally_new`; a `lib` function that inserts socket bytes says `keyed`, and the app's map becomes keyed through it. Then write the key (Part III 13.5), sorted (ADR-005 D8).
7. **Test: barriers.** A map handed to a trait method lowers keyed; a described Rust function taking `&HashMap<K, V>` makes its class keyed.
8. **Test: a Rust adapter names it.** A Rust file using `HmNames` (default) and `HmNames<nikaia_std::hash::Fast, nikaia_std::hash::Fast>` compiles against the lowered tools package, whatever the other files of the package do.
9. **Test: determinism.** The tools package lowers to the same `package.rs` with its files read in reverse order.
10. **Measure (callgrind, counts only, CLAUDE.md):** 1BRC (`benches/brc/brc.sh`) must stay at its table's count; `nikaia lower crates/nikaia-std/src/tools/ty.nika` before and after one moved module switches a `BTreeMap` to `HashMap`. Record both in the benches README and here.
11. **Tools may use `HashMap`/`HashSet` again** where step 10 shows it counts fewer instructions and nothing iterates the map into output (ADR-005 D8). Update `docs/lowering-defects.md` D6, `docs/mitigation-hurdles.md` §3 and `scripts/rs2nika.py`'s `HashMap` rule.
12. `NK1246` is specified ahead of `hasher:` (ADR-316 D4); its reproduction test belongs to the commit that builds `hasher:`. The last commit of steps 1-11 says `Fixes #N` for this issue.

Not decided here: whether `Shared`'s count (ADR-037 D8) should become a type parameter at the boundary the same way.

Related: #125, #558, #579, #431.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-11](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/581#issuecomment-6112407336):

The decision is written in 943e6286 (0.0.838): [ADR-010 D9](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-010.md), Part III 17.1 (the two paragraphs after the hasher paragraph), the `maps` row of Part III 13.5, `NK1246` in C.3, and one sentence in **Part I 4.5** (not 3.x as the body says). Notes updated: `docs/mitigation-hurdles.md` §3 and its ranking row, `docs/import-rust-design.md` decision D and its table row, `docs/lowering-defects.md` D6.

Corrections to the body: the last commit of steps 1-11 says **`Fixes #581`**. Step 1 is a security fix of its own (a socket read outside the entry file gets the fixed seed today) and can ship before the rest.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-12](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/581#issuecomment-6118277690):

Status at 8505a0fc (0.0.848): steps 1-7 are built (package-wide trust, classes, hidden parameters, the `maps` ledger key, barriers). Measured (callgrind `--branch-sim=yes`, 200,000 rows, `benches/brc/brc.sh`): 1BRC 310.9 instructions and 1.70 mispredictions per row; a `pub struct` with a map costs the same as a private one (310.8 / 1.70); a keyed map costs 480.4 / 2.83 (+54 % instructions, +1.1 mispredictions); a pub function used with both `Fast` and `Keyed` adds 4.8 KB of text (+0.65 %). A technical review found no cheaper mechanism at run time (a keyed floor at every pub position, a run-time hasher branch, a random-seed fast hash for all maps, one answer per package each break a goal).

**Still to build:**

* Steps 8-11 as listed above (Rust adapter names `HmNames`, determinism with the files read in reverse order, callgrind on `lower` before/after one `BTreeMap` to `HashMap` switch in the tools, then the tools use `HashMap` again where it counts fewer instructions and nothing iterates it into output).
* **The fill per class at use sites.** Trust at a use site is still one flag for the whole package. `handle(state, request)` has separate ledger lines per position (`state.config: from()`, `state.sessions: from(request)`), but an app that itself reads a socket keys `state.config` too. Keeping `state.config` Fast inside such an app needs the class-wise fill at use sites (steps 4/5 do not do it). A non-pub mention of a pub struct is filled from the package-wide answer, not its class (an untrusted package keys every pub struct's maps; not measured how much slower).
* A body the analyser cannot read to its end falls back to sharing by written type (rustc-safe for the shapes tested; exotic ones untested).
* `fs::map(...; trusted: false)` is not implemented (NK1109), so a keyed program is made untrusted by a socket read in a test.
* `NK1246` has no test until `hasher:` (ADR-316 D4) is built.
* Calling a function through a variable with a map argument fails in rustc: #582.
* Whether `Shared` (ADR-037 D8) should also be a boundary type parameter is not decided.
* `bfad5449` is not green on its own (the test `a_map_the_library_hands_on_keys_the_callers_map` needs 8505a0fc); the tip is.
