### <a id="issue-579"></a>[#579](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/579) A Rust-to-Nikaia converter for the rest of #125: what exists to reuse, and what to build

`selfhosting`, `compiler`, `ready` · opened 2026-10-11 · updated 2026-10-13 · 5 comments

#### How it came up

The self-hosting runs of 2026-10-10/11 (#125, #558) moved about 8,000 lines of `check/` and `emit/` into `tools/*.nika` with LLM builder agents, 26 commits (0.0.787-0.0.830). About 23,000 Rust code lines are left. The owner asked: do we need a Rust-to-Nikaia transpiler, and what can be reused? A read-only evaluation (Opus, high effort) and the builders' own reports answered it: **not a full transpiler, a converter that writes a first draft** the builder finishes against `nikaia check` / `lower-std`. This issue collects what exists and what to build. Nothing here changes the language.

#### Measured (nothing below is a guess)

* Five slices (8060d63, 5928082, 1d9a53f, 190d784, b3bb1c9): 2,027 Rust lines removed, 2,573 Nikaia lines added, 31 % of those only braces. Hand audit of 100 sampled content lines: **53 % mechanical (one rule), 46 % adaptation to Nikaia's limits, 1 % new design.** The automatic split counts too many lines as new; do not quote it.
* `scripts/rs2nika.py` (52 rules) reproduces 10 % of the content lines exactly (6-17 % per slice). On 15 unmoved plain functions of `check/mod.rs`: 60 % of the draft lines (65 of 109, 9 of 15 functions) pass `nikaia check` unchanged, 38 % also compile in rustc. Raw drafts of whole slices pass 0 %: one slice pattern or one missing callee drops everything (hence "leaves first").
* Real use (C-5b, the box/pattern group, 8 functions): about half of the draft text survived, about 60 % of its lines were rewritten by hand, it saved roughly one turn; copying the twin function in the other module (`emit_types.nika` for `check_boxes.nika`) saved more.
* Cost per moved Rust line, in context tokens read: two long agents 143k; eleven fresh one-slice agents 71k (`docs/tokenspeed.md`). Target of the ledger: below 70k.

#### What exists and can be reused

| What | Where | Use for the converter |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| The draft script, 52 rules R1-R52, TODO markers, name table, profiles `check`/`emit`, `--file`, `--prefix`, `--stats`, `--lint` | `scripts/rs2nika.py`, `scripts/rs2nika.names` | The base. `--rules` prints the rules; `--lint F.nika` flags known defects D1-D3, D7, D8 in hand-written Nikaia. |
| Pass-rate harness: puts a draft through `lower-std`, drops every function an error points into, repeats | `scripts/rs2nika_passrate.py` | The metric (share of draft lines that survive). |
| Corpus extractor and classifier: for a commit, the Rust functions it emptied and the Nikaia lines it added; the line classes | `scripts/rs2nika_extract.py`, `scripts/rs2nika_classify.py` | Builds the gold pairs (Rust in, accepted Nikaia out) from `git log`. |
| A gold corpus: every self-hosting commit is a Rust-before / Nikaia-after pair that passed review and CI | `git log --grep 'E[0-9]\|C-[0-9]\|#558'` | Regression set and few-shot examples; twin pairs between `check` and `emit` (`emit_types.nika` / `check_boxes.nika`, `emit_below.nika` / `check_notes.nika`). |
| A Rust reader written in Nikaia (the reading half of `nikaia describe`, ADR-290 D13) | `crates/nikaia-std/src/tools/rust.nika`, `describe.rs`, `docs/specification/adr/adr-290.md` | A grammar over Rust source. Today it reads a file's public surface; whether it can be extended to bodies is for step 3 to find out. |
| The inverse mapping: Nikaia lowered to Rust (`ref`, `T?`, `Vec[T]`, `??`, `throws`) | `emit/` and the `emit_*.nika` files, `docs/lowering-defects.md` | What each Nikaia form becomes tells what each Rust form should become. |
| The known lowering defects with probes and workarounds (19 entries) | `docs/lowering-defects.md` | What the converter must avoid or flag. |
| The language options against the usual hurdles (NK2302, `Symbol` not `Ord`, `HashMap`, lifetimes) | `docs/mitigation-hurdles.md` | Which hurdles tooling can remove and which need the owner. |
| Call-graph tools: SCC, leaves first, missing callees, unit order | `scripts/e9/`, `scripts/c6/` (`cg.py`, `units_c.py`, `arms2.py`, `below2.py`), `/tmp/c5/an.py` style scans | Order of conversion; the script already lists callees that are not in Nikaia ("move them first"). |
| Two-binary oracle: lowers 142 files of `tests/**` and `examples/**` with an old and a new binary | `scripts/c6/oracle.py` | Checks a converted unit changes nothing. |
| The process around a move: version bump, collision repair, plan, ledger, task templates | `scripts/bump.py`, `docs/selfhosting-plan.md`, `docs/tokensaverstrategy.md`, `docs/tokenspeed.md` | Where the converter plugs in. |

Rules the builders found (the part a converter must know, from their reports):
`&x` at a use is dropped (Nikaia lends implicitly); `x: &mut T` becomes `mut x: T`; `Option<T>` becomes `T?`, `None`/`Some(x)` become `null`/`x`; `HashMap`/`HashSet` become `BTreeMap`/`BTreeSet`; `usize`/`u32` keys become `i64`; lifetimes are dropped; `matches!`, `if let`, `let ... else` become `match` / `?? return`; `.to_owned()` becomes `.clone()`; `format!` becomes an f-string; `self.parsed.text(x)` becomes `world.words.resolve(x)`; helper names get a module prefix (one namespace across `tools/*.nika`); every moved method gets `world: ref <World>` after `self`.

What the draft cannot do today (named by the builders): `for i in 0..<n` loops, `.enumerate()` loops, `?.clone()` on options, `is_some_and`, `let ... else` with slice patterns, `.iter().any(..)` chains, `Finding { .. }` literals, `Ty::named`, `out.extend`, `matches!` against `Option<&str>`, `Vec[ref Expr]` returns (NK2302), tuple keys in `[]` (needs a `let`), `BTreeSet` iteration (a host method), `span.start as i64` as an argument (needs a `let`).

#### To build

1. **Tests first.** A directory of gold pairs cut from five or more self-hosting commits (`scripts/rs2nika_extract.py`), each with the Rust input and the accepted Nikaia; a test (or a script run in CI) that prints the pass rate of `scripts/rs2nika.py` on them with `scripts/rs2nika_passrate.py`, so a rule change shows its effect in numbers. Record the starting values (10 % exact, 60 % / 38 % on the 15 unmoved functions).
2. **The missing rules** from the list above, one at a time, each raising the pass rate on the corpus; keep `--lint` in step (add D14-D19 of `docs/lowering-defects.md` where a rule can flag them).
3. **Decide the front end from a measurement, not a taste.** The script is line-based with a little brace tracking. Try, on the same corpus, a syntax-tree front end (a Rust parser, or `rust.nika` if it can be extended to function bodies) for the rules that need structure (`if let` chains, closures, nested matches). Report the pass rate of both; keep the simpler if the gain is under 10 points.
4. **Callee-aware drafts.** The script lists callees that are not in Nikaia yet. Make it refuse (or mark the whole function `TODO`) rather than emit a draft that cannot pass, and print the order to move them, using `scripts/c6/units_c.py` / `scripts/e9/units.py`.
5. **Put it into the builder task template** (`docs/selfhosting-plan.md`): run the script on the unit's functions first, finish by hand, and report in the commit message how much of the draft survived. Add the figure to `docs/tokenspeed.md` (`scripts/tokenspeed.py`) per unit; compare units with and without the draft before spending more here.
6. **Stop rule.** The converter is worth continuing while the pass rate on unmoved functions rises and the tokens per moved line fall; if after steps 2-5 the saving stays under one turn per unit, stop and keep it as it is. Say so in this issue.

#### Not decided here

Whether a converter becomes a user-facing tool (`nikaia import rust`) for programs outside this repository: nothing in the repository needs it, and Nikaia's types differ enough (no lifetimes, one ownership model) that an honest answer needs its own record. The language options in `docs/mitigation-hurdles.md` (NK2302 precision, `Ord` on `Symbol`, one lowered `HashMap` type, `-> ref(param)`) are owner decisions and are not part of this issue.

Related: #125, #558, #578, #573.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-11](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/579#issuecomment-6111556996):

NK2302 precision (mitigation-hurdles §1a, import design B) is decided in 0.0.836 (ADR-283 D25-D26, Part I 6.6) and the build order is #580. For the converter: once it is built, a function whose every way out hands back a view of one parameter (`fn m(ref self, world: ref CheckWorld) -> ref String` returning a view of `world`) needs no `.clone()`. `Vec[ref Expr]` returns from one source work too. Two sources stay `NK2302`; the explicit `-> ref(param)` syntax (E) is rejected.

The `HashMap` question of `docs/mitigation-hurdles.md` §3 (one lowered map type) is decided: #581, [ADR-010 D9](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-010.md). **Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-12](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/579#issuecomment-6118278534):

The design of `nikaia import rust` (the owner's direction of 2026-10-11) is written up in `docs/import-rust-design.md` (commit ef114139): claim levels L0-L4 per function ("no function reaches L2 and fails L3"), the table of Rust constructs (exact / proof obligation / refused with a named reason / needs a language decision), the architecture (front end, typed or untyped, the ownership question), the built-in verification, five phases with exit criteria, and three To build steps. Read it before building step 2 or 3 of this issue.

**Finding that shapes it:** `scripts/rs2nika.py` turns `for i in 0..n` into a Nikaia `0..n` (which includes `n`; the exclusive range is `0..<n`): the draft passes the checker and prints 55 where the Rust prints 45; rule R15 drops `format!` arguments (`{:.1}`) without a word. A builder catches both; a product would hand them to the user as silent wrong values. To build 2 of the document (no silent rule) comes first.

**Owner decisions the document lists (each needs its own record; only I is needed now):** A what reads Rust (a grammar extending `rust.nika`, ADR-290 D11/D13, against `syn`; this conflicts with step 3 of this issue "decide from a measurement"); F messages that must name Rust constructs (Part III C.2 forbids Rust words today); G a new `NK28xx` code range; H the baseline (Rust built with overflow checks on); I decouple the product from the self-hosting move (recommended). B (NK2302) is decided as ADR-283 D25/D26 (#580); C (an order on std traits as bounds) and E (explicit `ref(param)`) are not decided; D (maps) is decided as ADR-010 D9 (#581).

Steps 1 and 2 of this issue (the corpus with a pass-rate measure, the missing rules) can be built now; the document's To build 1-3 refine them.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-13](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/579#issuecomment-6144080947):

#### Decided (2026-10-13): the converter is a standalone tool, written in Nikaia, with a nightly rustc front end

**How it came up.** A session tried to run the self-hosting move through `scripts/rs2nika.py` with parallel agents. Two owner decisions followed:
1. Helper tools are written in Nikaia, not Python. Writing them in the language shows where it has to be sharpened (`CLAUDE.md`, ea472f4). `rs2nika.py` is only the specification of its rules and the oracle to compare against, and it is deleted once the Nikaia tool matches it.
2. The converter is **taken out of the compiler** and becomes a standalone tool, and that tool **may pin a nightly**. That nightly gives it what rustc alone knows exactly: the receiver type that picks the `std` call, and integer widths. `docs/import-rust-design.md` §2 names these as the two places that need Rust's types. The nightly withdrawal of 0.0.9 was about the compiler (a binary that does not relocate, a second CI leg, users installing it). It does not reach a tool no installation ships. The reasons are in [ADR-001 D5](../blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-001.md) (f18fd31). The specification is unchanged: Chapter 13's "stable and nothing else" is about an installation.

This supersedes the `nikaia rs2nika` subcommand inside `nikaia-std` that a session had begun on its branch, not yet on `develop`.

#### To build
1. **Layout:** `crates/rs2nika/` gets its own workspace and is excluded from the root `Cargo.toml` (like `crates/unsafe`). It has two parts:
   - `rustc-facts/` (Rust): its own `rust-toolchain.toml` with a nightly pinned to one date, plus `rustc-dev` and `llvm-tools-preview`.
   - `convert/` (a Nikaia project).
2. **`rustc-facts`:** a `rustc_driver` callback after type checking. For every expression it writes one line per fact to a text file: span, type, and the resolved method path (`Vec::get`, `HashMap::get`, …). Test it against 3 functions from `crates/nikaia/src/check` with the expected fact lines.
3. **`convert`:** the converter written in Nikaia, run with `nikaia run` or built as a project. It reads the `.rs` file and, optionally, the facts file, and writes the draft. It starts from the rules R1-R52 of `rs2nika.py` and reaches parity with it on 4 reference inputs, checked by diff. Then the rules that need facts follow: `get` by receiver type, integer widths. No silent change: `0..n` becomes `0..<n`, `format!` arguments are kept, and anything not mapped gets a marker.
4. **CI:** a job of its own, like `unsafe_crates`, that runs only when `crates/rs2nika/**` changes.
5. **Measurement:** on the functions already moved (their Rust from git history against today's `.nika`), count how many draft lines survive with and without facts. Record the result here.
6. **Cleanup:** delete `scripts/rs2nika.py` and `scripts/rs2nika_extract.py`, and point `CLAUDE.md`, `docs/selfhosting-plan.md` and `docs/import-rust-design.md` at the new tool.

Anything that blocks writing the converter in Nikaia (a missing `std` entry, a syntax gap, a lowering defect) is filed in #586 or its own issue and fixed in the compiler. It is not worked around in another language.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-13](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/579#issuecomment-6144530686):

#### Changed (2026-10-13): Rust's types come from rust-analyzer on stable, not from a nightly

**Why it changed.** With a pinned nightly, every agent that runs the converter would have had to install the nightly and `rustc-dev` (about 1.5 GiB) and build the front end. A probe showed that the stable toolchain already provides what we need. `rust-analyzer` 1.97 is a stable rustup component, a single binary of 45 MiB. I ran `rust-analyzer lsif .` on

```rust
pub fn f(v: Vec<u32>, m: HashMap<String,u16>, k: &str) -> usize {
    let a = v.get(0);  let b = m.get(k);
    let n = v.len() + a.map(|x| *x as usize).unwrap_or(0);
    n + b.copied().unwrap_or(0) as usize
}
```

It gives, for each source range:
- **Which method is called:** `v.get` resolves to `core::slice::get` and `m.get` to `HashMap::get`.
- **The type of each local:** `let a: Option<&u32>`, `let b: Option<&u16>`, `let n: usize`.
- **The integer width at an operator:** for the `+` it gives `fn add(self, other: usize) -> usize`.

A parser of our own, macros and constants included, would see only the spelling, not the types. ADR-001 D5 is rewritten accordingly (01d644f) and replaces f18fd31.

**Replaces steps 1, 2 and 4 of the build list above:**
1. **Layout:** `crates/rs2nika/` (its own workspace, excluded from the root) holds `convert/` and `facts/`, both Nikaia projects. No nightly, no `rust-toolchain.toml` of their own.
2. **`facts/`:** runs `rust-analyzer lsif <crate>` and reduces the dump to one line per fact: file, range, kind (`method`, `local`, `op`) and type or path. Test: the probe above, checked against the expected lines.
3. **`convert --facts F`:** uses the facts for the `std` call by receiver type and for integer widths. Where there is no fact, it writes a marker and does not guess.
4. **CI:** `rustup component add rust-analyzer` in a job of its own, run only when `crates/rs2nika/**` changes.
5. **Still to measure:**
   - how long the dump takes for `crates/nikaia` and how large it is;
   - how often rust-analyzer cannot tell a type;
   - how many draft lines survive with facts and without them.

### <a id="issue-578"></a>[#578](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/578) A `bool` bound inside a nested `match` lowers as `&bool`

`selfhosting`, `compiler`, `ready` · opened 2026-10-10 · updated 2026-10-10 · 0 comments

#### Found

By the `check/mod.rs` self-hosting session (2026-10-10, #125, #558). Worked around with a helper function that takes a `ref` parameter.

#### Today

A `bool` payload bound by a `match` that sits inside another `match` on a reference is bound as `&bool` in the emitted Rust, so `if flag { .. }` or `!flag` does not compile. The same program with one `match` works. This is the `bool` case of the family fixed for numbers in 0.0.771 (#567, a `Copy` payload read through a reference).

#### To build

1. A test: an enum with a `bool` payload, matched inside a second `match` on a `ref` parameter; the bound `bool` used in a condition, negated, and returned.
2. The lowering reads a `Copy` payload (`bool`, `char`, numbers) bound under a reference as the value, at any depth of nesting, as #567 does for numbers.
3. Remove the helper function in `crates/nikaia-std/src/tools/check_*.nika` that exists only for this.

### <a id="issue-557"></a>[#557](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/557) Build ADR-339: the grammar language gets `span(p)`, `here`, `same_line`; then the compiler's own grammar (`parser/mod.rs`) moves to Nikaia

`language`, `selfhosting`, `grammar`, `ready` · opened 2026-10-10 · updated 2026-10-10 · 0 comments

#### How it came up

The parser self-hosting session (2026-10-10, #125) moved everything around the compiler's `grammar!` block (numbers, parse errors, tree building, the interpreter) and stopped at the block itself: 3 185 lines, 318 rules, written in Rust. It uses three things Part II 10.8 does not offer: the span a rule matched (`@=`, 52 rules, 78 uses), a declared parser state (`state Trivia`: newline before a token, `///` docs) and hand-written rules (`extern rule`).

#### Decided (2026-10-10, 0.0.756)

[ADR-339](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-339.md) and Part II 10.8, on the owner's acceptance of the proposal:

* `span(p)` yields the byte range `p` matched (a `Span`, two `u32`, offered by `std::text`); `here` yields a position and consumes nothing.
* `same_line` matches nothing and fails where the whitespace skipped just before it held a line break. The generated parser records it by position, so it survives backtracking.
* `///` is an ordinary rule; a `///` where no item, field or variant follows is a parse error, as in `rustc`.
* No grammar declares a state or an `extern rule`: a lexical rule and an action that may fail (`fail("…")`) do what they did.

Not built yet; the specification text is ahead of the compiler.

#### To build

1. **Check first:** an action that fails inside an alternative behaves as 10.8's cut says, and its message survives in `ParseError`. Test: `rule n -> i64 = t:text(digit+) { parse(t) }` where `parse` fails, inside `a | b`.
2. **`span(p)` and `here`:** grammar front end, checker, lowering. Tests: `span(p)` equals the range the Rust `_span` gives for the same rule on `examples/json` input; `here` consumes nothing. `Span` is declared in `std::text` and `tools/ast.nika` uses that one.
3. **`same_line`:** the generated parser records by position what its last `WS` skip held. Tests: `a\n[0]` and `f\n(x)` are not an index and not a call (ADR-307 D4); `a [0]` is.
4. **Port in chunks of about 40 rules** into a `.nika` grammar, running `tests/` and `tests/errors/` after each; `///` as a rule (ADR-135 D3's cases and a stray `///` as a parse error); the number rules become lexical rules calling `parse_numbers`.
5. **Remove the Rust `grammar!` block** and the `Trivia` state. The commit that does so says `Fixes #557`.

### <a id="issue-556"></a>[#556](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/556) Spike: a workshop held in memory, so a check without one can still compute build-time code

`compiler`, `performance`, `ready` · opened 2026-10-09 · updated 2026-10-09 · 0 comments

#### Where this comes from

#525, question 4. A `comptime` that calls a function is compiled and run (ADR-321 D1). The compiler does that in a **workshop**: a directory where the bundle crate and the comptime crate are built (`grammar_run::Workshop`). Every command-line build has one. A check made through the library (`check::check`) may have none: `bounds_report.rs` and 52 test files call it that way, and a language server would too.

The owner decided (ADR-321 D19, 0.0.720) that such a check refuses with `NK1127` and a note, rather than leaving the value open. At the same time the owner asked for this spike: **can the compiler, when it is given no workshop, make one for itself, held in memory if need be, so the check computes the value instead of refusing?** If it can, D19's refusal remains only for when even that fails.

#### What to find out

1. **Where a workshop can live without a directory the caller gave.** Candidates: a temporary directory (`std::env::temp_dir`), a tmpfs such as `/dev/shm` on Linux, the user's cache directory (`~/.cache/nikaia`, where `nikaia run` already builds). What `rustc` and the linker need on disk: they write files, so "in memory" most likely means a RAM-backed filesystem, not a buffer. Check what macOS and Windows offer.
2. **What it costs, counted, not timed** (CLAUDE.md): instructions for the first check with a fresh workshop (the dependencies and the bundle dylib built once) and for a second check that finds it (cache hit, ADR-321 D4). Compare with a check that refuses (D19).
3. **How much memory a tmpfs workshop holds**: the bundle and `std` built once. The 29 MB library from #468 is a lower bound.
4. **Who cleans up**, and whether two checks in parallel (tests) can share one workshop safely.

#### Done when

The issue holds the counts and a recommendation: either an implicit workshop (where it lives, which callers get it) for a decision round, or "not worth it", in which case D19 stays as it is. Nothing is built into the compiler from this issue alone.

### <a id="issue-553"></a>[#553](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/553) A narrowing `as` is written only where it is shown to fit: `NK1241`, with `checked_<T>()` / `truncating_<T>()` (ADR-285 D12)

`language`, `compiler`, `ready` · opened 2026-10-08 · updated 2026-10-10 · 0 comments

#### How it came up

While deciding `f32` (#552), the owner set the rule for `f64 as f32`:

* the prover fails it early where it can;
* where nothing proves it safe, the program must handle it;
* there are named methods for Rust's behaviour.

Asked whether integers keep their run-time abort, the owner answered that *the same principle naturally holds for integers*.

#### Decided (2026-10-08, 0.0.703)

[ADR-285 D12, D13](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-285.md), Part I 2.2, Part III A.2 and C.3. A conversion that can lose a value is written `as` only where it is shown to fit. That covers a wider integer to a narrower one, signed to unsigned or back, and an `f64` to an integer or to an `f32`.

* A constant that does not fit is `NK1116` (as today, D29).
* A value the prover shows within the destination's range converts, and the emitted conversion carries no check.
* Any other narrowing `as` is `NK1241`. The program writes one of two things:
  * `checked_<T>()`, a `T?` that is `null` where the value does not fit;
  * `truncating_<T>()`, or `rounding_f32()`, which is the backend's conversion.
* `checked_*` now also converts out of an `f64`; NaN fits no integer.
* A conversion is no longer in A.2's list of run-time stops. **Arithmetic overflow (D5) is unchanged.**

#### Today (develop, 0.0.702)

A narrowing `as` is emitted as `try_from` (or a `std` helper out of an `f64`) and aborts at run time where it does not fit. The corpus has 83 `as` to an integer type: 60 `as i64`, 12 `as u64`, 8 `as u32` and 3 `as i32`. How many of them narrow, and how many the prover shows to fit, is what step 1 finds out.

#### To build (the order the owner set, 2026-10-08)

1. **Build the check as a warning.** A narrowing `as` that the prover does not show to fit is `NK1241` at warning severity. Its help names `checked_<T>()` and `truncating_<T>()`, and `rounding_f32()` for `f32`. A proved one emits a plain conversion. Unproved ones keep today's run-time check while the warning stands.

   Tests:
   * `let small = big as i32` with `big: i64` a parameter warns `NK1241`;
   * after `assert(big >= 0 && big < 1000)` the same line does not warn;
   * `5000000000 as i32` is `NK1116`;
   * `k as u64` after `if k >= 0` does not warn;
   * `f as i32` with `f: f64` warns.
2. **Go through every warning** the corpus and `nikaia lower-std` give. Each site is one of three cases:
   * **The prover is too weak, and the site is safe.** Rewrite the line with the name it means (`checked_i32() ?? …`, or an `assert` that proves it). Open an issue to strengthen the prover where that is possible, naming the site and the fact it missed.
   * **The code had a defect.** The value can really be out of range. Fix it: handle the `null` from `checked_<T>()`, or say `truncating_<T>()` where the low digits are meant. The output may change here, because that is the point of the fix. Say which outputs changed, and why, in the commit.
   * **The low digits were meant.** Write `truncating_<T>()`.
3. **`checked_*` out of an `f64`.** Add `std.contracts` entries (by hand, D36) and their lowering. Tests: `1e20.checked_i32()` is `null`; `(0.0 / 0.0).checked_i64()` is `null`; `3.7.checked_i32()` is `3`.
4. **Turn the warning into the refusal** once step 2 has no site left, and remove the run-time conversion check of D12's old form. Test: the first test of step 1 is now an error.

`f32`'s half (`checked_f32`, `rounding_f32`, `as f32`) is #552. Build its `as f32` step on this check.

### <a id="issue-552"></a>[#552](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/552) `f32` is a type of the language: literals typed by use, no mixing with `f64`, `f64 as f32` aborts beyond range (ADR-285 D33)

`language`, `compiler`, `ready` · opened 2026-10-08 · updated 2026-10-10 · 1 comments

#### How it came up

#470 asked whether a written `usize`, `i16` or `f32` is refused, since Part I 2.2 offers only `i64`, `i32`, `u8`, `u64`, `u32` and `f64`. In the decision round (2026-10-08) the owner answered (a) for the others: `usize`, `isize`, `i8`, `i16`, `u16`, `i128`, `u128` and `str` are refused with `NK1135`. For `f32` the owner's call was that it is clearly part of the language.

The reasons are in [ADR-285 D33](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-285.md):

* half the size per value is what pictures, model weights, sensor samples and wire formats are made of;
* C, Rust, Go, Swift, Java and C# all offer it.

#### What the specification now says (Part I 2.2, 0.0.701)

* `f32` is a float type beside `f64`.
* A float literal is an `f64` unless its use asks for an `f32`: `let w: f32 = 0.5`.
* An `f32` and an `f64` do not mix without `as`.
* `f32 as f64` always fits.
* `f64 as f32` rounds to the nearest `f32`. A finite value beyond the `f32` range aborts, as an integer that does not fit does; an infinity and NaN carry over.

The rules are taken from the ones already set for numbers (ADR-285), so `f32` adds no rule of its own. The abort on `f64 as f32` is the one derived choice: it follows D1 (*a value that does not fit stops the program*), where Rust saturates to infinity.

#### Today (develop, 0.0.700)

`fn take(x: f32) -> f32 { return x }` passes the checker, because `types.nika`'s `BUILT_IN` lists every Rust primitive. Arithmetic with a literal, or with an `f64`, then fails in rustc.

#### To build

1. **Checker:** `f32` is a number type everywhere `f64` is (`integer_named`'s float counterpart, `NK1122`'s list and its note *"The number types are …"*). A float literal takes `f32` from its use, as an open integer does (ADR-285 D23). Mixing `f32` and `f64` without `as` is refused like two integer types.
   Tests: `let w: f32 = 0.5`; `fn half(x: f32) -> f32 { return x * 0.5 }`; `x + y` with `x: f32`, `y: f64` refused, naming `as`.
2. **Lowering:** `f64 as f32` emits a checked narrowing that aborts on a finite value beyond `f32::MAX`. Tests: `1e39 as f32` aborts at run time; `(1.0 / 0.0) as f32` is infinity; `0.1 as f32` rounds.
3. **`std`:** the float methods `f64` has in `std.contracts` (`sqrt`, `abs`, `floor`, the `truncating_*` conversions, formatting in `f"…"`) get `f32` entries, written by hand as D36 says. Test: each called on an `f32`.
4. **Same output first:** a program that writes `f32` values prints them as Rust's `f32` `Display` does.

`f32` goes in with step 1, and `BUILT_IN` loses the types #470 refuses in the same change.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-08](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/552#issuecomment-6066242654):

#### Changed (owner, 2026-10-08): `f64 as f32` does not abort; it is shown to fit, or the program says what it wants

0.0.701 had `f64 as f32` abort on a finite value beyond the `f32` range. The owner's rule (0.0.702, [ADR-285 D33](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-285.md), Part I 2.2, Part III C.3):

* **The prover fails it early where it can.** A constant beyond the range is `NK1116`, as for integers (D29).
* **Where nothing proves it safe, the program has to handle it.** Any `as f32` the compiler does not show to fit is `NK1241`. The help names the two ways to write it:
  * `x.checked_f32()` gives an `f32?`, which is `null` beyond the range;
  * `x.rounding_f32()` is Rust's behaviour: the nearest `f32`, and infinity beyond the range.
* Losing digits is not a failure; only leaving the range is. Infinity and NaN carry over in all three forms.

**What that means today.** The prover reasons about integers and not about floats (ADR-270 D11). Until it does, every non-constant `f64 as f32` is `NK1241`. D1's integer `as` (an abort) is not changed by this.

#### To build (replaces steps 1 and 2 above)

1. **Checker:** `f32` is a number type everywhere `f64` is (`NK1122`'s list and its note). A float literal takes `f32` from its use (ADR-285 D23). Mixing `f32` and `f64` without `as` is refused like two integer types.
   Tests: `let w: f32 = 0.5`; `fn half(x: f32) -> f32 { return x * 0.5 }`; `x + y` with `x: f32`, `y: f64` refused, naming `as`.
2. **`f64 as f32`:**
   * a constant beyond `f32::MAX` is `NK1116`;
   * a constant within the range, or `(a as f64) as f32` with `a: f32`, converts;
   * any other `as f32` is `NK1241`, with both names in the help.

   Tests: `1e39 as f32` is `NK1116`; `0.5 as f32` converts; `x as f32` with `x: f64` a parameter is `NK1241`.
3. **`checked_f32` and `rounding_f32`** on `f64`, as `std.contracts` entries (written by hand, D36) with their lowering.
   Tests: `1e39.checked_f32()` is `null`; `1e39.rounding_f32()` is infinity; `0.1.checked_f32()` is the nearest `f32`; NaN carries over in all three forms.
4. **`std`:** the float methods `f64` has (`sqrt`, `abs`, `floor`, the `truncating_*` conversions, formatting in `f"…"`) get `f32` entries. Test: each called on an `f32`.
5. **Same output first:** `f32` values print as Rust's `f32` `Display` does.

### <a id="issue-551"></a>[#551](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/551) Releases from 0.1: crates.io under the nikaia_ prefix, then GitHub releases with the binary and one container image (ADR-336 D2, D3)

`tools`, `ready` · opened 2026-10-08 · updated 2026-10-08 · 0 comments

[ADR-336](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-336.md) decides which channels exist and when:

* `cargo install --git` before 0.1 (#541);
* crates.io from 0.1 (D2);
* a GitHub release from 0.2 with the release-built binary, its sysroot and the SHA-256, plus **one** container image for compiling or running a `.nika` file at once, published on GitHub's registry and on Docker Hub (D3);
* the VS Code extension before the system packages (D6);
* a `.deb` from 1.0 (D4).

This issue carries D2 and D3.

#### Crate names: the `nikaia_` prefix, with no exception (decided)

[ADR-286](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-286.md) D23 already makes a published Nikaia package `x` the crate `nikaia_x`. The tools add the prefix and remove it again (D24's rename), so it is never written in a `.nika` file. The owner's decision (2026-10-08) is that this holds for everything the project publishes, the same as for a user's package:

* `nikaia-std` is published as `nikaia_nikaia_std`;
* `nikaia-logic` is published as `nikaia_nikaia_logic`.

crates.io does not tell `-` from `_` (checked: `serde-json` resolves to `serde_json`). The compiler crate `nikaia` is not under the prefix. Two consequences:

* The project's crates take no name a user's package could want.
* `orchestrator` and `tether` being taken as bare names stops mattering.

#### Where the sysroot is when it is not a checkout (to be tried, not asked)

Today the compiler finds `std` in `NIKAIA_SYSROOT`, or else in the source tree it was built from (Part III 13.2b; `crates/nikaia/src/sysroot.rs`). The path is compiled into the binary. With `cargo install --git` (#541) that tree is Cargo's checkout. Checked on 2026-10-08: install, then a hello world, both work.

Neither D2 nor D3 has such a tree:

* **crates.io**: Cargo unpacks each crate into its own `~/.cargo/registry/src/…/name-version/` directory.
* **an archive or the image**: the binary is wherever it was unpacked.

The candidates are:

* relative to the binary (`../lib/nikaia`);
* `NIKAIA_SYSROOT`;
* the build tree;
* the `nikaia_nikaia_std` crate that Cargo unpacked beside the compiler's.

Each is tried against what `cargo publish --dry-run` packages and an unpacked archive actually leave on disk, and Part III 13.2b's rule is written from what works.

#### To build

1. **Names:** the publish step renames every crate of the workspace to `nikaia_<name>` (except the compiler crate `nikaia`), and the path dependencies between them follow. Test: `cargo publish --dry-run` for each crate passes and shows the prefixed name.
2. **`winnow-grammar` on crates.io**, under the same rule: it is a `git` dependency today, which crates.io refuses. The workspace depends on the published version.
3. **The sysroot, tried:** each candidate above against a dry-run package installed with `cargo install --path` from the packaged sources, and against an unpacked archive. Write 13.2b's rule from the one that works in both (a specification change, so the version moves). Test: a hello world builds from each layout with `NIKAIA_SYSROOT` unset.
4. **From 0.1:** publish, and *Getting started* gets `cargo install nikaia` as its first line.
5. **From 0.2:** a workflow on the version tag builds the release binary per target (`x86_64-linux`, `aarch64-linux`), the archive with `bin/nikaia` and the sysroot, and its SHA-256, and attaches them to a GitHub release.
6. **From 0.2:** one container image with `nikaia`, the Rust toolchain from `rust-toolchain.toml` and `std` compiled into the cache, pushed to GitHub's container registry and then to Docker Hub. Test: `docker run --rm -v $PWD:/work -w /work <image> nikaia run hello.nika` prints the greeting and compiles only the program.

### <a id="issue-550"></a>[#550](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/550) `build_time_dependencies` can hang: two grammar-action tests wait on each other's cargo

`ready` · opened 2026-10-08 · updated 2026-10-08 · 4 comments

#### What happened

CI run 692 (`develop` at 0.0.673, `80d359d`), job `rust_floor`: `scripts/check-floor.sh` ran the release suite on the floor toolchain. `tests/build_time_dependencies.rs` hung until the job's 90-minute timeout.

```text
13:11:50 test the_build_time_program_links_no_c_library ... ok
13:12:43 test a_grammar_action_calls_a_dependency_at_build_time has been running for over 60 seconds
13:12:43 test a_grammar_action_calls_another_file_of_its_package has been running for over 60 seconds
14:35:32 ##[error]The operation was canceled.
```

The orphans killed at the end were the test binary, two `nikaia` processes (one per test), three `cargo` processes, and one more `nikaia` under a `cargo`. That last one is the rustc wrapper, lowering a package.

The same job passed at 0.0.666 (run 685), and `bootstrap_compiler` passed this test in run 692 itself. So it is intermittent, not a broken test.

**Why it matters beyond CI:** a hung job holds every later run on `develop` in the queue, because pushes do not cancel a run in progress. Nothing could reach `main` for 90 minutes.

#### Suspected cause (not confirmed)

Nested cargo builds that take locks in different orders:
- An outer `cargo` builds a test's project. Its wrapper lowers the package, and a grammar action or `comptime` there starts an inner `cargo` on a **target directory shared by the whole machine**: `grammar_run::shared_target()`, and the build-time bundle under the user cache since 0.0.645.
- `comptime_run::bundle` relies on cargo's lock on that directory so that two builds wait for each other instead of colliding.
- With two tests doing this at once, each outer build can hold one lock (its project target, or the package cache) while its inner build waits for the other's. That is a cycle.

#### To build

1. Reproduce: run `build_time_dependencies`'s two grammar-action tests concurrently in a loop, release mode (`cargo test --release -p nikaia --test build_time_dependencies a_grammar_action`). Capture `ps` and the `Blocking waiting for file lock` lines when it stalls.
2. Fix the lock order rather than serialising the tests. Options to check: give the inner build its own `CARGO_HOME` lock scope, or take a lock of nikaia's own around the shared target that is never held while waiting on an outer cargo.
3. A test: two builds of two projects that each run a grammar at build time, started at once, both finish.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-08](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/550#issuecomment-6062538083):

A first reproduction attempt, debug build on the stable toolchain, the two tests together five times:

- **Cold** (shared parser target and bundle not yet built): 71 s. It prints the same two *"has been running for over 60 seconds"* lines CI showed, and then passes.
- **Warm:** about 4 s each, four times.

So those two lines alone are the cold build of the shared targets, not a hang. What CI hit was still running 80 minutes later, in release on the floor toolchain with a cold cache. That makes step 1 a cold, release, floor-toolchain run: `rustup toolchain install 1.88`, a fresh `NIKAIA_CACHE_DIR`, and the two tests together in a loop.

---
_Generated by [Claude Code](https://claude.ai/code)_

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-08](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/550#issuecomment-6062630470):

The floor reproduction did not reproduce it: Rust 1.88, `--release`, a fresh `NIKAIA_CACHE_DIR`/`XDG_CACHE_HOME`, the whole `build_time_dependencies` file. All six tests passed in 82 s; four of them ran past 60 s on the cold builds and then finished. The same CI job did not hang in run 706 either (0.0.689).

So it is rare. Neither a cold cache nor the floor toolchain is enough on its own. What remains specific to run 692 is a CI runner, which has fewer cores than this machine, with every other test file of the suite running before it in the same `cargo test` process tree. Reproducing it needs a loop in that environment, or a reading of the lock order (step 2) without a reproduction.

**`rust_floor` is switched off** (`1132cae`, owner's decision): until Nikaia is stable the suite runs on the current Rust only, and the oldest supported Rust becomes a goal again later. The job stays in `.github/workflows/nikaia.yml` behind `if: false`, and `promote` no longer waits for it.

This hang no longer holds `develop` up. The suspected cause, nested cargo builds on a target directory the whole machine shares, can still meet a user who builds two projects at once, so the issue stays open for that.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-08](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/550#issuecomment-6063842373):

It hung again in run 707 (0.0.690, job 113375175899). The last lines before the cancel were:

```
Running tests/language.rs (target/floor/release/deps/language-…)
running 2 tests
test the_build_time_package_passes_its_own_tests has been running for over 60 seconds
test the_language_package_passes_its_own_tests has been running for over 60 seconds
```

So both hang in `tests/language.rs`. Two `nikaia` child processes and nested `cargo` processes were still alive at cleanup. That suggests the two package test runs block each other, for example on a shared cargo target or build-directory lock, when they run in parallel on the floor toolchain.

On the owner's decision, `rust_floor` is switched off for now (commit 1132cae, `if: false`), and `promote` no longer waits on it. This issue stays open for when the floor job comes back.

### <a id="issue-541"></a>[#541](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/541) Installing before 0.1: `cargo install` from the repository, a missing sysroot said in Nikaia's words, the first `std` build announced (ADR-336 D1)

`tools`, `ready` · opened 2026-10-07 · updated 2026-10-08 · 1 comments

Records: Part III 13 (*a stable Rust toolchain and nothing else*), 13.2b (the sysroot, `NIKAIA_SYSROOT`, `std` lowered at release time), [ADR-002](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/main/docs/specification/adr/adr-002.md) D4.

**Where it stands.** The only way to get the compiler is `cargo build --release -p nikaia` in a clone, and then *"leave the checkout where it is"*, because the sysroot defaults to the source tree the binary was built from. There are no GitHub releases. A tester has to build a compiler before running a first program, and can't move or delete the checkout afterwards.

The design already allows something better: `std` *"ships as sources, with its Nikaia half already lowered"*, and building it needs only `rustc`. A release is that sysroot plus one binary.

**Proposal**

1. **A versioned archive per built target** (`x86_64-linux`, `aarch64-linux`): `bin/nikaia` and `lib/nikaia/` (the sysroot: `std`'s sources with the lowered `.rs`, `std.contracts` inside the binary as now). The binary finds the sysroot relative to itself (`../lib/nikaia`) before falling back to the build tree, so an unpacked archive works anywhere with no environment variable.
2. **Built by a workflow on a version tag**, with the archive's SHA-256 published beside it, and the build reproducible from the tag.
3. **One install line in *Getting started*** (download, unpack, add to `PATH`); building from source moves to the second section.
4. **Later, once the archive is settled**: `.deb` / `.rpm` and the VS Code extension under `editors/vscode` attached to the same release.

**Not changed.** A Rust toolchain stays a prerequisite: the archive ships the compiler, not `rustc`. The first build still compiles `std` once per machine into the user's cache (about 15 s). The guide should say so up front, or a first build that is silent for 15 s reads as a hang, so whether `nikaia` prints a one-line *compiling std, once per machine* notice is part of this.

**To decide.** How the binary finds its sysroot (relative path, then `NIKAIA_SYSROOT`, then the build tree, or `NIKAIA_SYSROOT` first), and whether the version in the archive name is the specification's (`0.0.644`) or a separate release number.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-08](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/541#issuecomment-6064011019):

#### Decided (owner, 2026-10-08)

There are no GitHub releases before 0.2, and no crates.io before 0.1. Until then the compiler is installed with `cargo install` from the repository. GitHub releases from 0.2 hold the optimised binary and also a container image, with `nikaia`, for a quick start. A `.deb` comes with 1.0.

* **Why:** [ADR-336](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-336.md) (D1-D5). This issue's second question is D5: a release carries the specification's version, with no second number.
* **The specification is unchanged.** Part III 13.2b's default, *the source tree the compiler was built from*, already covers D1, because Cargo keeps the checkout it built from.
* **D2 (crates.io) and D3 (GitHub releases and the container image)** move to #551, together with this issue's first question, how the binary finds its sysroot when it is not a checkout. D1 does not need that answer.

#### What was checked

* `cargo install --git https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia --branch develop --locked nikaia --root <dir>` builds in about 3 minutes. The binary has `~/.cargo/git/checkouts/nikaia-…/2e9904a/crates` compiled in as its sysroot. A hello world then runs from an unrelated directory; its first build compiles `std` (about 30 s on this machine).
* With the sysroot gone (`NIKAIA_SYSROOT=/nonexistent/crates`), `nikaia run` ends in Cargo's words: `failed to read /nonexistent/crates/nikaia-std/Cargo.toml`. A checkout that Cargo's cache cleaning removed would look the same.

#### To build

1. **`guide/getting-started.md`**: the first way to get the compiler is
   `cargo install --git https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia --locked nikaia`. The guide says that it builds the compiler (a few minutes) and that `--tag`/`--rev` pins a version. Building from a clone becomes the second section, and *leave the checkout where it is* goes with it. The guide also says up front that the first program compiles `std` once per machine.
2. **A missing sysroot is reported in Nikaia's words** (Part III C.1): the directory looked for, that it holds `std`, and how to get it back (run the `cargo install` line again, or set `NIKAIA_SYSROOT`). Test: `NIKAIA_SYSROOT` pointing at a missing directory gives that message and no Cargo error.
3. **The first build says why it is slow**: when the cache has no compiled `std` for this key, `nikaia` prints one line, *compiling std, once per machine*, before Cargo starts. Test: the line appears on a build into an empty `NIKAIA_CACHE_DIR`, and not on the second build.

Steps 2 and 3 change the compiler, so they raise the version. Step 1 does not.

### <a id="issue-539"></a>[#539](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/539) Formats and protocols are official packages (`json`, `toml`, `csv`, `html`, `http1`), not `std`; listed in `std` and taken in by their first `use` (ADR-338)

`libraries`, `grammar`, `ready` · opened 2026-10-07 · updated 2026-10-13 · 3 comments

Records: Part III's list of key modules (`std::json`: *serialization using compile-time code generation*), [ADR-331](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/main/docs/specification/adr/adr-331.md) (`@json::Name("createdAt")` is its first example), [ADR-014](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/main/docs/specification/adr/adr-014.md) (half of `std` is Nikaia).

**Where it stands.** One of the README's five promises is *formats are part of the language*. The examples prove it at program level: `examples/json` (a tree of unbounded depth) and `examples/config` (INI with its own whitespace) are grammars and test themselves. **`std` holds no format at all.** `std::json` is named in Part III and not built, and CSV and TOML aren't named anywhere. A program that reads a config file or a spreadsheet export starts by writing a grammar for a format everyone already agrees on.

**Proposal.** Each of these is a grammar in `std`'s Nikaia half, so that:

* it's the showcase: the reference grammar for the format is the one a user reads to learn grammars;
* it's dogfooding of the kind the self-hosting road does for the checker: every format moved into `std` is a test of the grammar protocol's corners (CSV's quoted fields and `""`, TOML's dotted keys and tables, JSON's escapes and zero-copy strings);
* the typed half reads `@json::Name` and its siblings through ADR-331, so a struct is read from JSON without a hand-written walk.

**Order and what it waits on**

1. `std::json`: already specified, `examples/json` as the starting point. Needs a grammar a package offers, #520 (ADR-296 D25), and how a `dsl` block names it, #485. #518 shows the lowering's side.
2. `std::csv`: the smallest; mostly a question of the row type, so it shares #477's matching rules (*a block's rows are a declared struct, matched by name*).
3. `std::toml`: `nikaia.toml` is read by the toolchain through the `toml` crate today (ADR-250's adapters), so this one also takes an adapter off the self-hosting road.

**To decide.** Whether CSV and TOML belong in `std` at all, or in packages beside it once there's a registry (#99). The argument for `std`: the toolchain needs TOML itself, and each one is a reference grammar. Also whether a format module is only the grammar plus the attribute-driven reader, or also a writer.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-09](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/539#issuecomment-6074089422):

#### Decided (owner, 2026-10-09): not in `std`. Official packages, taken in by their first `use`

**How it went.** The proposal put JSON, CSV and TOML in `std`. The owner asked first whether a large `std` makes every program larger, then whether it makes the modules inflexible, and pointed out that `std::html`, `std::http1` and `std::json` had entered `std` without anyone deciding it.

**Measured (release build, 2026-10-09).** A program carries only what it calls:

* a hello world has 519 674 bytes of code and no symbol of `std::html` or `std::http1`;
* with `html::escape` it has one, and 2 244 bytes more.

Size is no argument either way. What decided it is that a module in `std` moves with the language's version, so its mistakes stay (Java's `Date`; Go's `encoding/json` until a second version). Deno (`@std/json`, separately versioned) and Kotlin (`kotlinx`) keep formats official but versioned on their own.

**Side finding.** The same hello world binary is **57 MB** on disk, of which about 0.5 MB is code. The rest is debug information, in a release build.

**Where it is written:** [ADR-338](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-338.md) and Part III 17, 17.3 (0.0.714).

* `std` is the core the language stands on.
* `json`, `toml`, `csv`, `html` and `http1` are **official packages**:
  * on crates.io as `nikaia_json` and so on (the prefix rule, ADR-336 D2);
  * shipped with the toolchain, so they work offline;
  * each versioned on its own.
* `std` carries the list of official packages with their versions. A `use json` that nothing else names takes it in at that version and records it in `nikaia.lock`.
* The toolchain's own Nikaia uses `toml` as any program would.

**Still open (ADR-338 §6):**

* whether each format package also writes. The recommendation is that JSON and CSV read and write, and TOML reads first, since writing with comments and order kept is its own design question;
* whether `std::db`'s protocol traits stay in `std`.

#### To build

1. **The list and the automatic `use`.**
   * `std` carries the list (name → shipped version).
   * The resolver takes in a listed package for a `use` that `[dependencies]` and the project's directories do not name.
   * The resolved version goes into `nikaia.lock`.

   Tests:
   * `use json` with no manifest entry builds offline;
   * a `[dependencies]` entry wins over the list;
   * an unlisted, undeclared name is still refused.
2. **`html` and `http1` out of `std`.** Move them into packages under the toolchain's shipped packages, and change the callers (`dsl html` lowering, `examples/http`, ADR-017's escape call). Outputs stay the same.
3. **`json`** as a grammar package, starting from `examples/json`. It waits on #520 and #485.
4. **`csv`**, sharing #477's row rules.
5. **`toml`** (reading), then the toolchain's `nikaia.toml` reader on it instead of the `toml` crate.

### <a id="issue-538"></a>[#538](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/538) A directory of a project is a package and a `mod` of one crate: `use a::b`, cycles allowed, `[directories]` (ADR-286 D37-D40)

`language`, `ready` · opened 2026-10-07 · updated 2026-10-09 · 1 comments

Records: Part I 9.1 (*a package is a directory*), 9.2 (visibility is per package), the roadmap's *walls you will hit*.

**Where it stands.** Modules are one level: a `.nika` file beside `main.nika`. `src/a/b.nika` is not found. The roadmap lists it as a wall, and nothing in the specification says what a subdirectory *is*, so this is a decision before it is work. Any project past a few files (and the toolchain's own Nikaia, which is one package today, [ADR-294](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/main/docs/specification/adr/adr-294.md) D18) runs into it.

**The two readings**

| | `src/a/` is a package of its own | `src/a/` is more files of the same package |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| follows from 9.1 | yes: *a package is a directory* | no: a package would be a tree |
| a name from it is written | with its prefix, `a::Row`, as every other package's | bare, as now |
| `pub` | is the boundary between `a` and the rest | changes nothing |
| what it buys | the structure is visible at every use; a directory is a unit of privacy | only file organisation |

The first reading looks like the one the rest of Chapter 9 already implies. It needs three answers:

* **How it is reached.** `use a`, with no `[dependencies]` entry because it is inside the project. And a nested one: `use a::b` would be the second `use` with a path in it beside `use std::fs`, or `use b` with the path given only by the directory.
* **The mapping is fixed.** The directory's path *is* the package's name, with nothing to declare and nothing to disagree with, so the tree on disk and the prefixes in the code can't drift apart.
* **Whether `src/a/` may reach its parent** or only its own subdirectories and other packages. A strict *down only* rule keeps the graph acyclic without a check.

Cost to weigh: today a package is one crate in the generated workspace (ADR-002, ADR-053). A package per directory multiplies crates and so `rustc` invocations, so measure a ten-directory project before deciding.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-09](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/538#issuecomment-6073753924):

#### Decided (owner, 2026-10-09)

**How it was reached.** The issue offered two readings: a subdirectory is a package of its own, or more files of the same package. The owner chose the first, lowered as `mod`. The trigger was a special treatment in the tests that this removes:

* `tests/language` is one package of 78 topic files sharing one namespace, so every helper carries its file's name by hand (`jumps_read_them`, `catch_handler_parsed`);
* the tests are split into `tests/language` and `tests/build-time` only because build-time code runs over a whole package (#468).

A `mod` per package is also how the build-time program already writes a dependency (`pub mod <package>`, ADR-321 D13, 0.0.590).

Refined in the same round:

* **The `mod` tree is the directory tree, declared automatically**, so no declaration cycle can exist.
* **Cyclic `use` is allowed** within a crate, as Rust allows it. The owner pointed out that the build-time program is already one unit, so a cycle of build-time values is not a question of packages. It is already refused today as `NK1168` (*`A` depends on itself*, checked with two `comptime`s in one file).
* **A directory is a Nikaia package by what it holds**, as ADR-286 D26 reads a crate. My first draft said a directory with a `Cargo.toml` is never a Nikaia package. The owner corrected that: a published Nikaia package has a `Cargo.toml` (D25), and so does `nikaia-std`.

**Checked (0.0.710):** `src/model/row.nika` with `use model` in `main.nika` is refused with *there's no dependency called `model`* and a suggested `[dependencies]` path entry.

**Where it is written:** [ADR-286 D37-D40](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-286.md) (D19 now covers dependencies only), Part I 9.1 and Part III 13.3, 0.0.711.

* **D37:** a directory under `src/` with `.nika` files is a package of the project, named by its path. It is reached with `use model::rows` and no `[dependencies]` entry; the prefix is the last segment.
* **D38:** the packages of a project are `mod`s of one crate, nested as on disk.
* **D39:** cycles of `use` are allowed, except through a package marked `crate`.
* **D40:** `[directories]` marks a directory `ignore`, `resources`, `tests` or `crate`. `crate` is also the way for a directory with Rust beside its Nikaia.

#### To build

1. **Discovery and naming.** Read every directory under `src/` that holds `.nika` (and that `[directories]` does not mark) as a package named by its path. `use a::b` resolves to it. A name that is both a dependency and a project directory is refused.
   Tests:
   * `src/model/row.nika` with `use model` and `model::Row` builds;
   * `src/model/rows/` is reached as `use model::rows` and `rows::Row`;
   * a project directory and a `[dependencies]` key with one name are refused.
2. **One crate.** Emit the project's packages as nested `mod`s of the project's crate, each against its own ledger entries, `pub` per D2. Tests:
   * two packages that both declare `Row` build;
   * a private item of `model` is `NK1110` from `http`;
   * the generated workspace has one member for the project.
3. **Cycles.**
   * Tests: `http` and `model` using each other build and run.
   * The ledger of packages in a cycle is inferred together.
   * A cycle through a `crate` directory is refused in Nikaia's words.
4. **`[directories]`.** Tests per kind:
   * `ignore` (a `.nika` there is not read);
   * `resources` (published, read by `asset`);
   * `tests` (built under `nikaia test` only);
   * `crate` (its own crate; one with Rust beside its Nikaia builds).
5. **`tests/language`.** One directory per topic, the file-name prefixes removed. Check whether `tests/build-time` can then join it (#468); if not, say why here.

### <a id="issue-537"></a>[#537](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/537) `nikaia new`, and a `#!` line so a `.nika` file runs as a script

`tools`, `ready` · opened 2026-10-07 · updated 2026-10-09 · 1 comments

Records: Part III 13.1 (`nikaia new my_project` generates the structure), 13.2 and [ADR-260](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/main/docs/specification/adr/adr-260.md) D4 (`nikaia run f.nika`, built in #132).

**Where it stands.** One file already runs on its own: `nikaia run f.nika` builds it as a project kept in the user's cache, keyed on its path. What is missing is the two ends around it:

1. **`nikaia new`** is specified and not built. `guide/getting-started.md` says so (*"There is no `nikaia new` yet, so create the two files by hand"*), so the first thing a tester does is type a `nikaia.toml` from a page.
2. **A file cannot be its own command.** Nothing in the parser treats a leading `#!` line, so a small tool can't be written as

   ```nika
   #!/usr/bin/env -S nikaia run
   fn main() {
       println("Hello, Nikaia!")
   }
   ```

   and started with `./hello.nika`. This does not touch the *no REPL* decision: a file is still a whole program, and the analysis still sees all of it.

**To decide**

* What `nikaia new` writes: the manifest with `user-parallelism` spelled out (as the guide does) or the defaults only; a `tests/` directory with one output test, since every example package has one; a `.gitignore` for `target/`; and whether it refuses a non-empty directory.
* The `#!` rule: only on line 1, only in a file that is an entry point, ignored as a whole line (its span still counted, so diagnostics keep their line numbers). Whether `nikaia run` accepts arguments after the file without `--` when started this way, since the kernel passes the script's arguments straight after the path.
* Whether the guide's *Getting started* then opens with `nikaia run hello.nika` and moves projects to the second step.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-09](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/537#issuecomment-6073792628):

#### Decided (owner, 2026-10-09)

**Checked (0.0.711):**

| Try | Result |
| :--- | :--- |
| `nikaia new x` | `unrecognized subcommand 'new'` |
| `#!…` on line 1 | `Didn't expect '#' here.` |
| `nikaia run s.nika a b` | `unexpected argument 'a'` (arguments only after `--`) |
| `nikaia run s.nika -- a b` | runs with `a b` |

The third row decided the second question. The kernel starts a script as `nikaia run ./s.nika a b`, without `--`, so a `#!` line alone would not be enough.

**Where it is written:** [ADR-260 D6-D8](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-260.md) and Part III 13.1-13.2 (0.0.712).

* **D6:** everything after the file is the program's, including `-x`, as `go run` does. A switch of `nikaia run` stands before the file, and `--` still works.
* **D7:** a `#!` in the first two bytes of the file that holds `main` skips that line, which still counts for line numbers. Elsewhere `#` stays an error.
* **D8:** `nikaia new name` writes, and refuses a directory that exists and is not empty:
  * `nikaia.toml` with `name` and `version` only;
  * `src/main.nika` printing `Hello, Nikaia!`;
  * `tests/main.stdout` with that line;
  * `.gitignore` with `target/`.
* **The guide** opens with a single file (`nikaia run hello.nika`); projects come second.

#### To build

1. **D6:** arguments after the file go to the program. Tests:
   * `nikaia run s.nika a -v b` hands the program `a -v b`;
   * `nikaia run --release s.nika x` still reads `--release` as a switch;
   * `nikaia run s.nika -- a` still works.
2. **D7:** the parser skips a first line starting `#!`. Tests:
   * the script above runs as `./s.nika a b` (execute bit, `#!/usr/bin/env -S nikaia run`);
   * an error on line 3 of a file with `#!` is reported on line 3;
   * `#!` in a file without `main`, or on line 2, is refused.
3. **D8:** `nikaia new`. Tests:
   * `nikaia new hello && cd hello && nikaia run` prints `Hello, Nikaia!`;
   * `nikaia test` passes in it;
   * `nikaia new` on a non-empty directory is refused and writes nothing.
4. **`guide/getting-started.md`:**
   * step 1 is a single file with `nikaia run hello.nika`;
   * step 2 is `nikaia new`, replacing *"There is no `nikaia new` yet"*;
   * a short section on `#!` scripts.

   No version change.

### <a id="issue-535"></a>[#535](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/535) A refusal is tested as `tests/NAME.nika` with the compiler's message in `NAME.stderr`, blessed like output tests (ADR-269 D22)

`tools`, `ready` · opened 2026-10-07 · updated 2026-10-10 · 1 comments

#### Where this comes from

0.0.650 and 0.0.651 moved 375 tests of what a program computes from Rust into `test` blocks (`tests/language`, `tests/build-time`). The owner's aim is to have as few Rust tests as possible. What stayed in Rust is mostly **refusals**: a program the compiler must reject, with a given code (`NK1132`, …) and a given help. In `jumps.rs` alone that is 11 of the 19 tests that remain. A `test` block cannot hold one: a refused program does not build, and in a package it would take every other test down with it.

#### What exists

* `tests/errors/` holds one broken `.nika` per case and `EXPECTED.txt` with what each produces (`errors.rs` compares). That is a corpus of messages, not of codes per rule.
* ADR-269 D13 gives `nikaia test` output tests (`tests/NAME.stdout`) but nothing for a program that must be refused.
* Other languages: rustc's UI tests (`.rs` + `.stderr`, `//~ ERROR` annotations), trybuild's compile-fail tests, Go's `// ERROR "regexp"` comments in test files.

#### The question

Whether `nikaia test` gets a refusal test, and in which form, for example:

* **A**: a package's `tests/NAME.nika` with `// refused: NK1132` lines (the code, optionally a fragment of the help) on the line that must carry the finding; `nikaia test` checks it and compares the findings. Each file is checked alone, nothing is compiled, so these are cheap.
* **B**: the same files, checked by a Rust runner in this repository only (no language feature).
* **C**: stay as Rust tests.

To be prepared before it is asked: how many of the remaining Rust tests are refusals of a single program, and how many also check the help's wording (which an annotation would have to carry).

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-09](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/535#issuecomment-6073905255):

#### Decided (owner, 2026-10-09): A, as a feature of `nikaia test` like `trybuild`, in the form of the output tests

**Counted first (heuristic over `crates/nikaia/tests`):**
* 1 998 Rust test functions in 202 files;
* 654 of them name an `NK` code, in 129 files, with 152 distinct codes;
* 373 of those 654 also check text (a help or message fragment).

So an expectation has to carry wording, not just a code.

**The owner's correction:** the expectation is not an annotation in the source. It is a file, as `NAME.stdout` already is, like rustc's UI tests (`.rs` + `.stderr`). The owner's condition was that tests still run together. They do: a refusal never reaches rustc, so it costs a parse and a check, and the programs that build are compiled together as packages of one crate (#538, #549).

**Where it is written:** [ADR-269 D22](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-269.md) and Part III 14.1 (0.0.713).

* `tests/NAME.nika` is a test program that may `use` the package.
* `NAME.stdout` and `NAME.stderr` say what it must produce. A refusal is the compiler's whole message in `NAME.stderr`, with the path written `tests/NAME.nika`.
* What happened is compared with what is written. A refusal with no `.stderr` holding it fails.
* `--bless` writes both files.
* The package's own output tests take a `.stderr` the same way.

#### To build

1. **Discovery and run.**
   * `nikaia test` finds `tests/*.nika` beside a package and checks each as a program that depends on the package.
   * A refused one is compared against `NAME.stderr`, with paths normalised.
   * The ones that build are compiled together (ADR-286 D38) and run as output tests (D13), with `NAME.stderr` compared where it exists.
   * A test program that the backend rejects is rebuilt alone, to name it.

   Tests:
   * a refused program with a matching `.stderr` passes;
   * the same program with one word changed in `.stderr` fails, with a line diff;
   * a refused program with no `.stderr` fails;
   * a program that builds while `.stderr` holds a refusal fails;
   * `--bless` writes the `.stderr`.
2. **`.stderr` for the package's own output tests.** Test: a program that writes to standard error, with a matching `.stderr`.
3. **`tests/errors/`** becomes a package of test programs, one `.stderr` each, replacing `EXPECTED.txt` and `errors.rs`.
4. **Migration, in steps:** the Rust tests that refuse one program (`jumps.rs`'s 11 first) move into `tests/*.nika` + `.stderr`. Each step removes the Rust test it replaces.

### <a id="issue-525"></a>[#525](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/525) Compiled build-time code: NK1244 for a run that stopped, NK1165 retired, NK1102 bounds, NK1127 without a workshop

`ready` · opened 2026-10-07 · updated 2026-10-09 · 1 comments

#### Where this comes from

#468 builds ADR-321: a `comptime`, or a computed default, is compiled and run instead of interpreted. While building stages 1–4, the building session asked four questions in #468's comments (2026-10-05). None is answered, and none had an issue or a label of its own. They are gathered here so #468 can stay `ready` for its build steps (stage 3, stage 5) while these wait for the owner.

**What happens today** for each case is a placeholder, chosen so that it is a one-line change.

#### 1. Which code reports a build-time run that stopped?

`comptime C = sq(100000)` with an `i32` `sq` runs and aborts on the overflow, as the program would. The build reports it as `NK1152`: *`C` stopped while the program was built: attempt to multiply with overflow.*, with the caret on the line.
- `NK1152` means *not allowed*. Its text now also carries the step budget and the call depth.
- `NK1127` means *cannot be computed*.
- Neither says *it ran and stopped*.

Options:
- keep `NK1152`;
- move it to `NK1127`;
- give it a new code of its own.

#### 2. `NK1165` (an index into an `Array` at build time)

Part III still describes `NK1165`. With compiled build-time code, `xs[7]` on an `Array[i64, 3]` is the program's own abort, with the runtime's message (*index out of bounds: the len is 3 but the index is 7*), reported under question 1's code.

Options:
- retire `NK1165`;
- have the run translate the abort back into `NK1165`'s sentence.

Today the build-time and run-time words agree, which is ADR-321 D1's point.

#### 3. A bound that is not a whole number the build knows

`comptime(steps: one())`, or a negative bound: neither ADR-321 D11 nor Part III says what this gets.

The build gives `NK1102` for now: *`steps` takes a whole number the build knows, and this is not one.* Nothing runs.

Options:
- keep `NK1102`;
- use `NK1109`;
- give it a code of its own.

#### 4. A check without a workshop

The library API (`check::check`) can be called without a place to compile in; every CLI build has one. Once the interpreter's call path is gone (#468 step 9), what does such a check answer for a `comptime` that calls something?
- **(a)** `NK1127`, with a note that this check has no place to compile build-time code. More honest toward a caller that wants an answer.
- **(b)** Nothing: the value stays open, as the ledger's pass has done since 0.0.528. Quieter, but a caller could take a program as checked although its `comptime` never ran.

**Until this is answered, the interpreter stays for exactly this case, and #468 step 9 waits.**

#### What needs the answers

- Question 4 blocks #468 step 9 (removing `build_time.rs`'s call evaluation).
- Questions 1–3 are messages and codes. Programs work today; only the code a refusal carries would change.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-09](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/525#issuecomment-6075170632):

#### Decided (0.0.720, `a1b1f3e`)

**What was checked first.** I ran each case on the current compiler:
- `comptime C = sq(100000)` with an `i32` `sq` gives `NK1152` *stopped while the program was built: attempt to multiply with overflow*.
- `comptime Y = at(7)`, which indexes a 3-element list inside a function, gives `NK1152` with the run time's words. `let y = xs[7]` at run time gives the same words.
- `comptime Y = XS[7]`, a literal folded by the checker, gives `NK1165` *This reads element 7, but there are only 3 elements.* So the same mistake had two codes and two sentences.
- `comptime(steps: one())` and `comptime(steps: -5)` both give `NK1102` *takes a whole number the build knows, and this is not one*. That is wrong for −5.
- `check::check` without a workshop is called by `bounds_report.rs` and 52 test files.

**Decision.** The owner accepted all four recommendations:
1. A build-time run that stopped gets a code of its own: **`NK1244`**, in the run time's words, at the line where it stopped ([ADR-321](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-321.md) D16). `NK1152` keeps the rule and the bounds: steps, memory and depth.
2. **`NK1165` is retired.** A folded index past the end stops with `NK1244` and the run's words (D17, ADR-311 D4 changed).
3. A `steps`/`ram` bound the build does not know, or one not above 0, stays **`NK1102`**, with one sentence for each case (D18).
4. **(a)** A check with no place to compile refuses with `NK1127` and a note, and never leaves the value open (D19). In addition: spike #556 asks whether the compiler can make a workshop for itself, in memory if need be.

Specification: Part II 10.2, Part III C.3.

#### To build

1. **`NK1244`.** Move the compiled run's abort from `NK1152` to `NK1244` and keep the message. Budget, memory and depth stay `NK1152`.
   - Tests: `sq(100000)` with `i32` gives `NK1244`. `fib(50)` gives `NK1152`.
2. **Retire `NK1165`.** The checker's fold of `XS[7]` reports `NK1244` with *index out of bounds: the len is 3 but the index is 7*. Remove `a_build_time_index_is_not_there`.
   - Test: the literal and the function path give the same code and the same words.
3. **`NK1102`, two sentences.** Not a known number: *`steps` takes a number the build knows*. At or below 0: *`steps` takes a number above 0*. The same for `ram`.
   - Tests: `steps: one()`, `steps: -5`, `ram: 0`.
4. **No workshop gives `NK1127`** with the note *this check has no place to compile build-time code in*. After that, #468 step 9 (removing the interpreter's call path) is unblocked. Tests that check a `comptime` with a call either get a workshop or expect this refusal.
   - Test: `check::check` without a workshop on `comptime C = f()`.

### <a id="issue-518"></a>[#518](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/518) A `dsl` block names its entry (`dsl Json::value { … }`) unless the grammar marks one `main entry rule` (ADR-299 D21)

`compiler`, `grammar`, `ready` · opened 2026-10-07 · updated 2026-10-09 · 3 comments

Found while working on #505 (develop, 0.0.616).

**What the specification says.** Part II says *a grammar the program declares is named bare: `dsl Json { … } eod`* (ADR-299 D20). Part II's block #2 shows the Json grammar run at build time on a `dsl` block's body.

**What the compiler does.** The checker accepts the block. The emitter refuses it:

```nika
grammar Num {
    entry rule n -> i64 = d:dec[i64](digit+) { d }
}

fn main() {
    let x = dsl Num { 42 } eod
    println(f"{x}")
}
```

```text
error: `dsl Num { … }` has no `:name` holes, and `Num` isn't a language the compiler knows.
Add `:name` holes for the parameters, or use `dsl html`.
```

`emit::template` knows two shapes: `dsl html`, and a deferred statement with `:name` holes. A block over the program's own grammar is neither.

**To build**
1. A `dsl G { … } eod` over a grammar `G` of this program runs `G`'s entry rule on the body while the program is built, as a `comptime` grammar call does (ADR-177, ADR-321). The value is built into the program, and its type is the entry rule's result. That is also #505's remaining step 1: the checker gives the block that type.
2. A grammar with several `entry` rules: which one a block uses has to be stated. The specification does not say yet. Ask before building, or refuse with a message that names the entries.
3. A body that the grammar refuses is a build error, in the grammar's words, at the block.

Tests: the program above prints `42`; `let b: bool = dsl Num { 42 } eod` is `NK1103` (*`i64`*); and `dsl Num { x } eod` is refused at the block while the program is built.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-07](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/518#issuecomment-6034916852):

**On step 2 (which entry a block uses):** ADR-296 D25 says *no rule is picked as the grammar's main one - such a pick chooses wrong silently*. So for a grammar with several entries, a `dsl G { … }` block has no entry to use by that rule. The two readings are:
- a block is allowed only over a grammar with exactly one `entry`, and a grammar with several is refused with their names;
- or a block names its entry (`dsl G::value { … }`), which D20's path syntax already parses.

That is the owner's call. Step 1 can be built for the one-entry case without it.

---
_Generated by [Claude Code](https://claude.ai/code)_

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-07](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/518#issuecomment-6035217996):

**Steps 1 and 3 built in 0.0.620 (a7b798f)**, for a grammar with one `entry`.
- **Checker:** a block over a grammar of the program is the `comptime` of `G::entry("<the block's text>")`. It goes through `comptime_binding`, so the evaluator, its bounds and its refusals are the same as for any `comptime`. The value is recorded under the statement, and the block's type is the entry's result.
- **Emitter:** writes `{ const __NIKAIA_DSL: i64 = 42; __NIKAIA_DSL }`.
- **Text the grammar refuses** is `NK1178` at the block, in the parser's words.
- **Tests:** `crates/nikaia/tests/dsl_grammar.rs`: `dsl Num { 42 } eod` + 1 prints `43`, `let b: bool = …` is `NK1103` (*`i64`*), and `dsl Num { x }` is `NK1178`.
- **Unchanged:** the specification baselines. Part II's `dsl Json` blocks are fragments around the grammar of another block.

**Open, and the owner's (now labelled `decision`). Step 2, a grammar with several entries.** Today such a block stays as it was: the lowering refuses it, and there is no silent pick, per ADR-296 D25. The options are in my comment above:
- (a) a block over a grammar with several entries is refused with their names (this needs a code);
- (b) a block names its entry, `dsl G::value { … }`, which the path syntax already parses. Today that path would read as package `G`, grammar `value` (D20), so this collides with D20.

**Also found:** one statement with two such blocks shares one recorded value, because the record is keyed by the statement, as a `comptime`'s is. That is rare, and each block's own key would fix it.
