**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-09](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/518#issuecomment-6074996537):

#### Decided (owner, 2026-10-09): B, plus a `main` entry

**The question.** A `dsl` block over a grammar with several `entry` rules. ADR-296 D25 forbids the compiler picking one, so the choice was between refusing such grammars (A) and naming the entry where needed (B).

**The decision.** B: a block names its entry, written as the call is (`Json::value(text)` → `dsl Json::value { … } eod`; from a package, `dsl json::Json::value { … } eod`). The owner added that a grammar with several entries may mark one `main entry rule`, and a bare `dsl Json { … } eod` runs it. This is the grammar author's decision, not a compiler pick, so D25's reason stands. D25 now carries a *Changed by* note.

**Where it is written:** [ADR-299 D21](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-299.md), Part II 10.2 and 10.5, and Part III C.3 (0.0.719).

* One entry, or one marked `main`: no name needed.
* Several entries and no `main`: `NK1242`. The help names the entries, `dsl G::entry { … }`, and `main entry rule`.
* Two `main` entries: `NK1243`.
* `A::B` is a rule where `A` is a grammar, and a grammar where `a` is a package. One name denotes one thing, so the path is never read both ways.

#### To build

1. **Parser:** `main entry rule`. Test: a grammar with two entries, one `main`, parses; two `main` entries are `NK1243`.
2. **Block resolution:**
   * `dsl G::r { … }` runs entry `r` of the program's grammar `G`;
   * `dsl pkg::G::r { … }` runs it from a package;
   * bare `dsl G { … }` runs the single entry or the `main` one;
   * several entries and no `main` is `NK1242`.

   Tests: one per case, and `dsl G::helper { … }` on a rule that is not an entry is refused with the existing message.
3. **The bug from the comment above:** two `dsl` blocks in one statement share one recorded value. Key each block's value by the block, not by the statement. Test: `let p = (dsl Num { 1 } eod, dsl Num { 2 } eod)` holds `1` and `2`.

### <a id="issue-490"></a>[#490](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/490) ADR-327 D4-D11: memory budgets per task and per shared value — measured, decided, to build

`performance`, `ready` · opened 2026-10-06 · updated 2026-10-12 · 4 comments

The direction is decided in [ADR-327](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-327.md) D4-D11 (history in #121 and in the comments below). The specification states it in:
- Part I 6.3 (poisoning),
- Part II 12 ("A task has a memory budget"),
- Part III 15.2 and Part III's code table (`NK2506`).

- **D4:** Nikaia allocates its own memory. `List`, `String` and `Map` stand on `allocator-api2`/`hashbrown`, and every allocation can fail.
- **D5:** every task, and every shared value, allocates from a block of its own, and the block is its budget.
- **D6:** a full block ends the task, or poisons the shared value.
- **D7:** an exchangeable strategy sizes the blocks. No number is configured; a package may offer a strategy with a fixed ceiling.
- **D8:** toward Rust, views cross free. A list or text handed to a Rust owner crosses **as it is** wherever the compiler follows it back to where it was built: through locals, results, parameters of functions whose every call it sees, and fields of values built in the package. The compiler builds it there outside the block, counted against the budget. A value taken from a channel or handed back by Rust crosses as it is too. Where the compiler cannot follow the value (a `pub` function's parameter, a value read out of a shared value), the hand-over is refused with **`NK2506`** and the program writes `.clone()`. A copy is written or refused, never inserted (ADR-282 D7). The better the following, the fewer `.clone()`s are asked for. A `Vec`/`String` that Rust hands back is taken over without a copy.
- **D9:** a value that can outlive its task is never in the task's block: sent on a channel, stored into a shared value outside its door, handed to Rust to own, or the task's result. It is built outside the block and counted while the task holds it, so the block is emptied **whole** when the task ends. From the hand-over the value counts against its **receiver**: the task that takes it from the channel or joins the task that produced it, or the shared value it is stored into. A receiver it does not fit is a full block (D6).
- **D10:** a budget counts what a task holds, not what it has allocated. Memory a task frees can be allocated again.
- **D11:** no needless poisoning.
  - An `update` whose value copies (a number, `bool`, `char`) works on a copy and puts it in place at its end, so a panic in it poisons nothing.
  - An `update_all` copies only where **both** values copy, and puts both back together while it still holds both locks. Where one does not copy, neither is copied and a panic poisons both, because values changed in one door must agree.
  - `access`/`access_all` cannot change a value, so a panic in them poisons nothing.
  - A change made in an earlier door is not undone by a later panic.

**What exists today:**
- ADR-327 D1-D3 (poisoning, `SharedMut::supervised`) and ADR-326 D4 (a panic ends only its task) are built.
- A word-sized value that crosses threads already updates on a copy with compare-and-swap, outside `update_all` (Part II 12.2). A panic there never poisons.
- Nothing of D4-D11 is built: the emitter writes Rust's `Vec`/`String`, there is no allocator of Nikaia's own, and every other door poisons on a panic, `access` included (`held` in `lock.rs` and `std`'s mutex).

#### Measured (comments below)

| | instructions | mispredicted branches |
|---|---:|---:|
| a 64 KiB bump block per task, against `malloc` (`benches/taskblock`, 0.0.602) | −41% | about −50% |
| fallible collections on `allocator-api2`/`hashbrown`, against `std` (`benches/fallible`, 0.0.603) | +0.2% | +5% (1BRC), +7% (tasks) |

The owner approved the direction on these numbers (step 3, done).

#### To build

Measured first only where a step can be slower than what it replaces. A step that can only be faster is built without a measurement.

1. **Measure D10.** The bump block measured above never reuses freed memory, so it cannot serve a long-running task (a connection loop, `main`). Measure a block that gives freed memory back to its task (size classes with free lists inside the block, or a chain of blocks) against the bump block, on `benches/taskblock`. Add a second workload: one long task that frees and allocates in a loop. If reuse makes short tasks slower, a task may start as a bump block and switch to reuse at its first full block. The measurement decides; the owner is asked only if neither form keeps the 41%.
2. **D11, independent of the rest, can go first** (`crates/nikaia-std/src/lock.rs`, and the emitter's lowering of the doors):
   - an `update` of a value that copies runs on a local copy, written back at the block's end, with no poison mark while it runs;
   - an `update_all` where both values copy does the same with both, written back together before either lock is released; otherwise it stays as today;
   - `access`/`access_all` set no poison mark. On `Crossing`, take the guard so that a panic in a reading block does not poison `std`'s mutex.
   - **Tests:**
     - `kasse.update fn(mut v) { v += i64::MAX }` in a task panics; a second task reads the old value through `get`, without a panic;
     - an `update` of a `SharedMut[Vec[i64]]` that panics after a `push` still poisons;
     - `update_all(a, b)` over two `i64`s whose block panics after changing the first leaves both old values readable;
     - `update_all(a, b)` over an `i64` and a `Vec[i64]` that panics poisons both;
     - an `access` whose block panics leaves the value readable.
3. **The allocator** (D4, D5, D10):
   - a task-local allocator in `nikaia-std`;
   - `List`/`String`/`Map` on `allocator-api2`/`hashbrown`, with that allocator as a handle that is either *the task's block* or *outside* (D8, D9);
   - growth through `try_reserve`. A full block panics in the task, outside the allocator (D6).
   - Copy byte runs with one `copy_nonoverlapping`. `allocator-api2`'s `extend_from_slice` copies one element at a time, which doubled the tasks' count in step 1.
   - Text that is already valid UTF-8 is not checked again.
   - The panic path is a `#[cold]` function. Rust has no `#[unlikely]` attribute.
4. **The shared value's block** (D5, D6): inside a `SharedMut` door and in `Shared`'s construction, allocations go to the value's block; a full one poisons the value.
5. **Where a value lives** (D8, D9): the emitter decides per allocation site whether the value stays in the block. It is built outside when the compiler sees it can outlive the task (`send`, a store into a shared value outside its door, a move into a Rust owner, the task's result), and also when it cannot tell. Then the block is given back whole when the task ends.
6. **Receiver budgets** (D9): at `recv`, at a join, and at a store into a shared value, the value's bytes move from the sender's count to the receiver's. A value of one allocation (a list of numbers, a text) is one step; a nested value is a walk over its allocations. A receiver it does not fit panics there, or a shared value is poisoned.
7. **The Rust boundary** (D8):
   - the walk that follows a value back to where it was built, so that it is built outside the block;
   - such a value crosses as a `std::vec::Vec`/`String` without a copy (`into_raw_parts` → `from_raw_parts`; `allocator-api2`'s `Global` is `std`'s global allocator);
   - `NK2506` where the walk cannot follow the value, with the help naming the `.clone()`;
   - a `Vec`/`String` handed back is wrapped as it is and counted;
   - **the goal is few `NK2506`.** Each case the walk does not yet follow is a gap to close, not a reason to copy.
8. **The strategy** (D7): `std`'s default derives block sizes from the memory there is and the tasks running, with the same exchange point as admission (ADR-326 D2).
9. **Tests:**
   - a task that reads more than its budget into a `String` panics, and another task runs on;
   - a `supervised` cache that outgrows its block is rebuilt;
   - a `&[T]` passed to a Rust function is not copied (pointer equality, in a test crate);
   - a list built in the package and handed to a Rust function that owns its `Vec` arrives at the address it was built at;
   - a `pub` function that hands its parameter to such a Rust function is `NK2506`, and compiles with `.clone()`;
   - a long task that allocates and frees a megabyte a thousand times under a 64 KiB budget does not panic (D10);
   - a value sent from a task that has ended is still read correctly by its receiver, and counts against the receiver (D9);
   - a receiver whose budget a received value does not fit panics at `recv`, and the sender runs on.

Nothing in ADR-327 is left open except D10's measurement (step 1).

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-06](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/490#issuecomment-6026918814):

#### Step 2 measured: a block per task (0.0.602, `7fb1c22`)

`benches/taskblock/` runs one workload: 100 000 short tasks, each reading a request and writing a response, with 22 allocations and 7 reallocations per task. It is built as three binaries that differ only in their `#[global_allocator]`. All three print the same bytes. Counts are from callgrind with `--branch-sim=yes`, release build (`opt-level = 3`, no LTO), minus a run of no task.

| allocator | instructions / task | mispredicted / task |
|---|---:|---:|
| `global`: the system's `malloc` | 17410 | 180.3 |
| `budget`: `malloc`, each task's bytes counted against 64 KiB (D5's check without a block) | 17810 | 197.5 |
| `block`: a 64 KiB block per task, a bump pointer, emptied whole when the task ends | 10188 | 94.8 |

**What the numbers say:**
- A block per task is **cheaper** than `malloc`: 41% fewer instructions and half the mispredicted branches. The saving is `malloc`'s and `free`'s own work, about 7 200 instructions per task.
- Counting a budget on top of `malloc` costs 2.3%.

**Checked before quoting:**
- `global` declares `System` as its global allocator. Without a declaration, `std`'s default shims (`__rdl_*`) are not inlined and add about 420 instructions per task. That is a difference of the declaration, not of the strategy.
- `malloc`'s count depends on where the heap starts. The same binary called by another path moves `_int_malloc`/`_int_free` by about 177 instructions per task. The script always calls the binaries the same way.

**Not measured here:**
- handing a block back to the system when a task ends;
- a task that needs more than one block;
- values that move from a task into a shared value.

**Step 1** (fallible collections on `allocator_api2`/`hashbrown`, counted on `benches/brc`) is still owed. The owner's decision (step 3) waits for both tables.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-06](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/490#issuecomment-6027753452):

#### Step 1 measured: fallible collections (0.0.603, `d3a6eac`)

`benches/fallible/` writes two workloads twice:
- on today's `Vec`, `String` and `std`'s `HashMap`;
- on `allocator-api2`'s `Vec<T, A>` and `hashbrown`'s map, with an allocator that counts every byte against a budget and refuses past it. Growth goes through `try_reserve`, and a refusal ends the task.

The budget is set so that nothing is refused, so what is counted is the check and the collections, not a refusal. Both sides of each workload print the same bytes. Counts are from callgrind with `--branch-sim=yes`, release build (`opt-level = 3`, no LTO), minus a run over nothing.

| program | instructions | mispredicted |
|---|---:|---:|
| 1BRC's aggregation, `std` (per row, 200 000 rows) | 393.8 | 3.51 |
| 1BRC's aggregation, fallible | 394.4 | 3.67 |
| taskblock's tasks, `std` (per task) | 17872.4 | 181.12 |
| taskblock's tasks, fallible | 17882.3 | 193.21 |

**What the numbers say:** instructions are the same within 0.2%. Mispredicted branches rise by 5% (1BRC) and 7% (tasks).

**Checked before quoting:**
- `allocator-api2`'s `extend_from_slice` is `extend(iter().cloned())` on stable Rust, a loop of one byte at a time. Used as it is, it **doubled** the tasks' count (35 136 per task). The bench copies with one `copy_nonoverlapping`, as `std`'s `Vec<u8>` does. A `std` built on the crate would have to do the same.
- The fallible text is a `List<u8>`, handed out as a `str` without checking its UTF-8 again, as `String` does. Checking it cost about 230 instructions per task more.

**Not measured:**
- the collections behind a `nikaia-std` feature, with an emitted program against them. The emitter writes `Vec`/`String` today, so that is a change to the emitter, not to a bench;
- a refusal and the task it ends.

**Both tables are now here** (step 2 in the comment above: a block per task, −41% against `malloc`). Step 3 is the owner's: does the direction stand as measured?

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-09](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/490#issuecomment-6086150874):

##### Step 3 Owner Decision: APPROVED (Proceed to Step 4)

Based on the measurement results from **Step 1** (`0.0.603`) and **Step 2** (`0.0.602`), the direction set in [ADR-327](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-327.md) is fully confirmed:

* **Step 2 (Task-Block Arena):** **−41% instructions** and ~50% fewer mispredicted branches compared to system `malloc` (saving ~7,200 instructions per task).
* **Step 1 (Fallible Collections):** Negligible instruction overhead (**~0.2%**) when enforcing fallible allocation checks.

The measured gains far outweigh the checks. We are moving forward to **Step 4: Implementation**.

---

##### Consolidated Scope for Step 4 (To Build)

###### 1. Allocation Engine
* **Task-Local Allocator:** User-space chained slabs. Block expansion via embedded `next` headers. $O(1)$ bulk free when a task ends.
* **Shared-Value Allocator:** Switch allocator context to the shared value's block upon entering a `SharedMut` door.
* **Fallible Growth:** Growth implemented via `try_reserve`-style semantics.

###### 2. Policy & Configuration
* **Budget Exhaustion & Isolation:**
  * Task block full / budget exceeded $\rightarrow$ Isolated task `panic` (fault isolation, other tasks run on).
  * Shared value block full $\rightarrow$ Mark shared value as **poisoned**.
* **Pluggable `TaskStrategy`:** API for defining initial block sizes, growth factors, and hard budget ceilings.
* **Branch Optimization:** Budget checks and error paths marked with `#[unlikely]` for cold-path separation (BTB protection).

###### 3. Bulk Memory & Rust FFI Boundary (Zero-Copy First)
* **Borrowing (`&[T]`, `&mut [T]`):** 100% Zero-Copy. Rust borrows directly from the task slab.
* **Ownership Transfer (`Vec` / `String`):**
  * **Dual-Path Allocation:** Values destined for Rust ownership are allocated directly via system heap wrappers (`try_realloc`), strictly accounted against the task's budget, to avoid arena corruption while enabling zero-copy moves.
  * **Explicit Copy:** Fallback when an arena-born value must yield ownership to a Rust API.
* **Slice Operations:** Enforce `copy_nonoverlapping` for memory primitives and bypass UTF-8 checks (`from_utf8_unchecked`) on previously validated slices.

###### 4. Integration Tests
* **Task OOM:** A task exceeding its budget panics without disturbing concurrent tasks.
* **Supervised Cache:** A cache outgrowing its block is cleanly rebuilt.
* **FFI Pointer Equality:** Assert zero-copy semantics for `&[T]` across the C/Rust boundary via pointer comparison tests.
* 
​-- Generated via Gemini 3.7-flash-preview-0926 (Google Trusted Tester Program / Internal Tier)

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-12](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/490#issuecomment-6128817816):

#### Decided with the owner: D8 rewritten, D9 and D10 added; the body above is the plan now (0.0.856 `52e702c`, 0.0.857 `6807871`)

**How it came up.** An outside proposal on "pipeline fusion" assumed this arena was already built (it is not). Checking it against this issue showed that the step-3 comment above, written with Gemini, went beyond ADR-327 or against it in five places. The body now says what holds:

1. **"O(1) bulk free when a task ends"** needs every value to have left the block. ADR-327 §6 had left that open. It is now **D9**: a value that can outlive its task is never in the task's block (sent, stored into a shared value outside its door, handed to Rust to own, the task's result). So the block can be given back whole. From the hand-over such a value counts against its **receiver**: the task that takes it or joins its producer, or the shared value it is stored into. That was the owner's answer to the old §6 question of whose budget it is.
2. **"Dual-path allocation" for values that go to Rust** became **D8**. The compiler follows a value handed to a Rust owner back to where it was built, builds it outside the block, and hands it over as a `std` `Vec`/`String` without a copy. Where it cannot follow the value, the hand-over is **`NK2506`** and the program writes `.clone()`. A first version (0.0.856) let the compiler copy unseen there. The owner rejected that the same day: it breaks ADR-282 D7 (a copy is written or refused, never inserted), which is also a promise the language makes to its users. So the work goes into following values with few gaps, not into hidden copies. A `Vec` that Rust hands back is taken over without a copy.
3. **"Pluggable `TaskStrategy` with hard budget ceilings"**: D7 says no number is configured. A strategy with a fixed ceiling is one a package may offer, not the default.
4. **`#[unlikely]`** is not a Rust attribute. The panic path is a `#[cold]` function.
5. **"Chained slabs"** and the 41% are two different allocators. The bump block that was measured never reuses freed memory, so a long-running task would be ended for memory it no longer holds. That is now **D10** (a budget counts what is held), and it is the one step measured before building, because reuse can cost some of the 41%.

**Rejected on the way** (ADR-327 §3):
- Nikaia's blocks as Rust's `#[global_allocator]`. A crate that keeps a `Vec` past its task would point into an emptied block, and a refused allocation ends the process.
- Copies the compiler inserts at the Rust boundary.

**Measuring:** the owner's rule is to measure where a step can be slower than what it replaces, not to weigh a gain that can only be positive. Only D10's allocator is measured first (To build, step 1).

**Specification:** Part II 12 ("A task has a memory budget"), Part III 15.2 and `NK2506` in Part III's code table say it; ADR-327 says why. The pipeline side of the same proposal is #595 (ADR-342).

### <a id="issue-470"></a>[#470](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/470) A written `usize`, `i16`, `u128` or `str` is `NK1135` outside `extern "C"` (ADR-286 D36); `f32` joins the language in #552

`language`, `compiler`, `ready` · opened 2026-10-05 · updated 2026-10-08 · 1 comments

#### How it came up

While checking #467's remaining limit (*a use that asks for another width — `i16`, `usize` — still reads the `const`*), the following program reached `rustc`:

```nika
fn small() -> i64 {
    return 7
}

fn take_usize(x: usize) -> usize {
    return x
}

fn take_i16(x: i16) -> i16 {
    return x
}

comptime S = small()

fn main() {
    println(f"{take_usize(S)} {take_i16(S)}")
}
```

`nikaia run` reports `rustc`'s *Mismatched types … You can convert an `i64` to an `i16`*. The checker said nothing. The same happens without any `comptime`: `fn take(x: i16)` with `let y: i16 = 3` passes the checker for `usize`, `i16`, `i8` and `f32`. A made-up name (`Foo`) gets `NK1135`.

#### What is decided today

- **Part I 2.2:** *"These five are the integer types a program writes: `i64`, `i32`, `u8`, `u64` and `u32`. The specification does not offer others (`usize`, `i8`)."*
- **Part I 2.2:** `n as u128` and `n as usize` are refused with `NK1122`. Its note says *"The number types are `i32`, `i64`, `u8`, `u32`, `u64` and `f64`."*
- **Part III 15.1:** inside an `extern "C"` declaration, `usize` is C's `size_t` and marks a length beside a view (`NK1159`). That use is specified and tested (`tests/foreign_pointers.rs`).
- **Code:** `types.nika`'s `BUILT_IN` lists every Rust primitive (`i8` … `u128`, `isize`, `usize`, `f32`), so `NK1135` never fires for them. `tests/undeclared_type.rs::every_kind_of_declared_name_is_left_alone` asserts on purpose that `fn f(a: u8, b: usize, c: i128)` is accepted. Its comment calls this *"Part I 2.2's own set"*, which the text of 2.2 no longer matches.

So the specification does not offer these types but does not say that writing one is refused. Only `as` is refused, and the test expects the opposite. Today the program goes on to `rustc` and fails there on a file nobody wrote.

#### Question for the deciding session

Outside an `extern "C"` declaration, is a written `usize`, `isize`, `i8`, `i16`, `u16`, `i128`, `u128` or `f32` refused by the checker?

- **(a) Refused.** This is the same shape as `ref mut`, which is refused outside `extern "C"` with `NK1158`. Which code?
  - `NK1135`: *There's no type called `usize`.*, with a note naming the five integer types and `f64`, and help: *Use `i64`: lengths and indexes are `i64`, and the compiler converts them for the machine* (`f64` for `f32`).
  - Or `NK1122`'s sentence, extended from `as` to any written type.
- **(b) Offered.** Then #467's per-use spelling and the fits check need these widths (`integer_named` knows only the five).

(a) matches what 2.2 says now. Under (a), `undeclared_type.rs`'s case flips, and the `extern` tests stay as they are.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-08](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/470#issuecomment-6065531746):

#### Decided (owner, 2026-10-08): (a) refused with `NK1135`, except `f32`, which joins the language

**How it was answered.** #509 was decided first, as [ADR-286 D36](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-286.md): *what is written down is what can be called*, and a name Rust has and Nikaia does not is refused. D36 speaks of members (methods, functions, fields), so it did not cover a written type's name. The owner confirmed that the same principle holds there. `f32` is the exception: in the owner's words it is "clearly an issue", a type the language should have. It is not a Rust-only name to refuse.

**Checked (develop, 0.0.700).** `fn take(x: T) -> T` passes the checker for `usize`, `i16`, `i8`, `f32` and `u128`, while `Foo` is `NK1135`. The cause is `types.nika`'s `BUILT_IN`, which lists every Rust primitive and also `str`.

**Where it is written (0.0.701)**

* [ADR-286 D36](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-286.md), second paragraph: a written `usize`, `isize`, `i8`, `i16`, `u16`, `i128`, `u128` or `str` is `NK1135`. The exception is an `extern "C"` declaration, where `usize` is C's `size_t` (Part III 15.1, `NK1159`).
* Part I 2.2 says so, and C.3's `NK1135` names the case. The help is `i64` for an integer, `String` for text.
* `f32`: [ADR-285 D33](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-285.md) and Part I 2.2. It is built in #552.

#### To build

1. **`BUILT_IN`** in `crates/nikaia-std/src/tools/types.nika` keeps only the language's types. `usize`, `isize`, `i8`, `i16`, `u16`, `i128`, `u128` and `str` leave it. `f32` stays, for #552.
2. **`NK1135` for those names**, with the help `i64` (integers) or `String` (`str`), and a note naming the language's number types. Inside an `extern "C"` declaration nothing changes.
   * Tests: `fn take(x: usize)`, `let y: i16 = 3` and `fn f(s: str)` are each `NK1135` with the help.
   * `tests/foreign_pointers.rs` stays green.
   * `tests/undeclared_type.rs::every_kind_of_declared_name_is_left_alone` flips for `usize` and `i128`; `u8` stays accepted.
3. **#467's remaining limit** (*a use that asks for another width*) disappears with step 2, since no such width can be written. Note this on #467.

### <a id="issue-468"></a>[#468](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/468) Build ADR-321: compile and run build-time code against the dependencies, with a step budget

`language`, `compiler`, `ready` · opened 2026-10-05 · updated 2026-10-08 · 13 comments

#### How it came up

While deciding #115 and #467, three things were found in `build_time.rs`, the interpreter that runs a `comptime`'s calls:

- **It ignores the program's integer types.** With `fn sq(x: i32) -> i32 { return x * x }`, `comptime C = sq(100000)` lowered to `const C: i64 = 10000000000`, while `let n = sq(100000)` stops the program (*attempt to multiply with overflow*).
- **It cannot run a `std` function written in Rust.** This is why ADR-318 D6 had to move `seconds()` into `std`'s Nikaia half, which `NK1209` (no `impl i64`) blocks (#374).
- **It is slow and unbounded.** In the debug build, `fib(25)` took about 7 s and `fib(50)` hung the build without a word (ADR-287 D16: no step budget).

ADR-177 had already refused an interpreter for grammars, because an interpreter is a second implementation of the language. The same argument applies to ordinary code.

#### The decision round, briefly

1. **Interpreted or compiled?** The owner asked what to expect: compiling should have a fixed cost and win on large tasks. The levers: `opt-level=0`, `rustc` directly, one crate per package, a result cache, running beside the rest of the build, and folding only literal expressions.
2. **Measured, compiled path only** (callgrind, instructions; the owner asked not to measure the interpreter):
   - `fib(10)`, static: 133 M (rustc 44 M, lld 86 M). With `bfd` instead of lld: 436 M. `opt-level=1`: 148 M.
   - Cargo alone: 206 M.
   - Linking `nikaia-std` statically, with the generated project's own profile (found with `cargo build -v`; the first attempt used a mismatched rlib): 1 052 M, of which the linker is 948 M. With `-C strip=debuginfo`: 338 M.
3. **The owner asked why linking is so expensive.** Static linking merges the dependencies and their debug information into every binary. The owner proposed linking into the compiler's own process. That was rejected: the compiler holds only the part of `std` it calls, its symbols are not exported, it is built with its own profile, and a `std` shipped as a dylib would be fragile across platforms and options.
4. **Dynamic linking, measured:** 132 M to build (rustc 83 M, lld 47 M) and 36 M to start and run (the loader binding a 29 MB library).
5. **The owner asked about project dependencies and remembered that Cargo rebuilds everything for a dylib.** Checked: that is true when the difference goes through `RUSTFLAGS` or a profile. It is not true when only a generated bundle crate gets `--crate-type dylib` and `-- -C prefer-dynamic`. In that case `cargo rustc -p bundle …` reported **46 × Fresh** and compiled only the bundle. A `comptime` program reached `nikaia_std` through `bundle::nikaia_std` and gave the right result, at the same cost (132 M + 36 M).

#### The decision in short

[ADR-321](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-321.md) (0.0.513, `1bc83e5`). Changed with it: ADR-287 D16 and D18, ADR-318 D6. Specification: Part II 10.2, Part III 13.2b, `NK1127`, `NK1152`.

- **D1:** a `comptime` that calls a function is compiled and run, never interpreted. Literal-only expressions stay folded by the checker.
- **D2:** what may run is still the ledger rule (`sync`, touches ⊆ build parameters), now including `std`'s Rust half and the dependencies.
- **D3:** Cargo builds the dependencies, and a generated bundle crate is built as a dylib from the same rlibs. When cross-compiling, there is also a host build.
- **D4:** one crate per package, `rustc` direct, results cached by code, toolchain and parameters.
- **D5:** the run goes beside the rest of the build.
- **D6:** results come back as data and cross per ADR-311.
- **D7:** a step budget counted in loop turns and calls, refused with `NK1152`.

**Not decided (ADR-321 §6):** the budget's default and how a program raises it; memory; Windows; whether a grammar run joins the same crate.

#### To build

1. **The bundle crate.** Generate `<package>-build` beside the package in the generated workspace, depending on every dependency a `comptime` may reach (`nikaia-std` always). Build it with `cargo rustc -p <bundle> --lib --crate-type dylib -- -C prefer-dynamic`, in the same target directory and profile as the program.
   - Test: after `cargo build` of the program, the bundle build reports every dependency `Fresh`.
2. **The comptime crate.** Lower every `comptime` of the package whose initialiser calls a function into one crate: the initialisers, everything they call, and a `main` that writes each value in ADR-177 D4's encoding. Compile it with `rustc` directly: `--edition=2024 -C opt-level=0 -C overflow-checks=on -C debug-assertions=off -C prefer-dynamic --extern bundle=… -L dependency=…`, with paths through `bundle::`. Run it with the bundle's and the toolchain's library directories on the loader path.
   - Tests: `comptime FIB_10: i64 = fib(10)` gives 55; `comptime D = 5.seconds()` runs from `std`'s Rust half.
3. **Same semantics.** `comptime C = sq(100000)` with an `i32` `sq` stops the build at the multiplication, with the program's message mapped to the `.nika` line (ADR-300). Test.
4. **Ledger rule, unchanged.** A callee that is not `sync`, or that touches anything but the build's parameters, or that no ledger describes, is `NK1152` before anything is compiled.
   - Tests: `println` in a callee; an undescribed Rust dependency function.
5. **Step budget.** The lowering counts each loop turn and each call in the comptime crate; past the budget the run stops and the build gives `NK1152`, naming the `comptime` and the call path. The call depth stays bounded.
   - Test: `fib(50)` is refused rather than hanging; `fib(20)` passes.
   - The default number and how a program raises it are open (ADR-321 §6): use a provisional constant, mark it in code, and prepare the question for the owner.
6. **Result cache.** Key: each initialiser and its transitive callees' text, the toolchain, and the build parameters (ADR-287 D15).
   - Test: a second build with no change neither compiles nor runs the comptime crate. Changing a callee re-runs it.
7. **Parallelism.** Start the comptime build as soon as the ledger has decided what may run. Only `NK1116`'s fit check (ADR-287 D21, #467) and the writing of the values wait for it.
8. **Cross-compiling.** When `--target` is not the host, build the dependencies the bundle reaches for the host as well.
   - Test: `--target wasm32-unknown` with a `comptime` that calls `std`.
9. **Remove** the call-evaluating part of `build_time.rs` and its tests. Keep the checker's fold for literal expressions (ADR-285 D22).
10. **Follow-ups:**
    - #374's steps 5–8 no longer need `seconds()` rewritten in Nikaia (ADR-318 D6 changed).
    - #467's step 5 (typed arithmetic at build time) is this issue's step 3.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-05](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/468#issuecomment-5999706602):

#### Added: ADR-321 D8 and D9 — which dependency code may run at build time (0.0.515, `5c616a8`)

##### How it came up

After ADR-321 the owner asked whether foreign code could slip harmful code through its ledger. Checked:

- **Nikaia dependencies.** A consumer believes a dependency's shipped `nikaia.contracts` while its sources hash as recorded (ADR-100 D1, D3). That protects against a *stale* ledger, not a *false* one: a package can ship honest hashes next to `touches = []` on a function that writes files. A published package without sources is believed outright. `--locked` re-derives only path dependencies.
- **Before ADR-321 this did not matter at build time**, because the interpreter could not run dependency functions at all. With native build-time code it does.
- **Rust crates.** The description is the consumer's own and is reviewed (ADR-290 D5, `NK2505` on moved sources). But `touches` cannot be derived from Rust: it is a person's judgement.
- **Cargo already runs `build.rs` and proc-macros of Rust dependencies natively**, regardless of anything Nikaia does.

##### Decision

- **D8.** Before a Nikaia dependency's function runs at build time, this build derives that package's ledger from its sources. The derivation is keyed by the source hashes and done once per version, and D2's rule reads the derived entries. If they disagree with the shipped ledger, the build is refused with `NK1152`, naming the package and both answers. A package without sources runs nothing at build time.
- **D9.** A Rust crate's function runs at build time only where its description says `build_time = true`. The column is hand-written, absent means no, and `nikaia describe` never writes it. `std`'s Rust half is exempt.
- **Not decided (§6):** Rust dependencies' `build.rs` and proc-macros.

Specification: Part II 10.2, Part III 13.5 (new `build_time` column), `NK1152`.

##### To build (additional steps, after step 4)

4a. **Derived ledger for build-time calls.** For every Nikaia dependency whose functions the comptime crate reaches, derive its ledger from its sources (the same inference as `--locked`), cached by the hashes in its `nikaia.derived`. Check D2's rule against the derived entries.
   - Test: a path dependency whose committed ledger says `touches = []` for a function that calls `println` is refused with `NK1152`, naming the package, the function and both entries.
   - Test: an honest dependency passes, and its derivation is not repeated on the next build.

4b. **No sources, no build-time run.** Test: a dependency with a ledger and no sources, called from a `comptime`, gives `NK1152`.

4c. **`build_time` column.** Read it from `contracts/<crate>.contracts`. The describer never writes it; check `nikaia describe` output in a test.
   - Test: a described Rust function with `sync = true`, `touches = []` and no `build_time`, called from a `comptime`, is refused, and the help names `build_time = true`. With the column it runs.
   - Test: `std`'s Rust half (`5.seconds()`) runs without it.

#### Correction: ADR-321 D9 — no Rust code but `std`'s runs at build time (`3a23bb2`)

The `build_time = true` column from the previous comment was the session's own choice and was not discussed with the owner. The owner rejected the principle behind it: the compiler can vouch only for Nikaia code, whose `sync`/`touches` it derives. A Rust crate's description is a judgement, and a second claim beside it is the same judgement written again. **The column is gone.**

**Now (D9):** a Rust crate's function never runs at build time, described or not. Neither does a Nikaia function whose calls reach one. Both are refused with `NK1152`, naming the Rust function and the path to it. `std`'s Rust half is the exception: it is this project's own code. D2 and D3 are narrowed to match: the bundle names `std` and the Nikaia dependencies.

- A `comptime` calling a Nikaia function that calls it is refused too, naming the path.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-05](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/468#issuecomment-5999902404):

#### Build plan (the building session)

##### Checked before starting

- **Where the run fits in the pipeline.** `nikaia build` lowers every member in the driver, before `cargo build`. The `rustc` wrapper lowers each member a second time inside Cargo, and that second lowering hits the build cache. So:
  - the comptime run belongs in the driver's lowering;
  - the result cache (step 6) is needed from the start, so that a wrapper relowering inside Cargo never starts a nested Cargo, which would wait on the target-dir lock.
- **Where to build.** Every lowering already has a workshop (`Reads::building_in`, beside the build cache, with its own target directory), which the grammar runs use. The bundle and the comptime crates go there. That is a separate target directory from the program's, so the second lowering inside Cargo can never collide with Cargo's lock. The cost is that `std` is built once more per machine for build time, not once per program.
- **The mechanism, tried by hand.**
  - `cargo rustc --lib --crate-type dylib -- -C prefer-dynamic` on a bundle `pub extern crate nikaia_std;` builds `libnikaia_bundle.so` (34 s cold, debug).
  - A main compiled with `rustc --edition=2024 -C prefer-dynamic --extern nikaia_bundle=….so --extern nikaia_std=<the rlib in deps> -L dependency=…` is 17 KB and links `nikaia_std` from the dylib. The second `--extern` is what lets `nikaia_std::…` paths resolve in submodules; a `use bundle::nikaia_std` reaches only the crate root.
  - It runs with the bundle's directory and `rustc --print target-libdir` on `LD_LIBRARY_PATH`.
  - `5.seconds()` gives 5. An `i32` `x * x` with `x = 100000` panics with *attempt to multiply with overflow*.

##### Stages, one package each

1. **Compiled path, std only.**
   - The bundle (`nikaia-std` only).
   - One comptime crate per `comptime` for now: the program lowered without `main`, plus `fn __nikaia_comptime_value() -> T { return <initialiser> }`, plus a `main` that dumps it with `grammar_run`'s dumper.
   - Compiled with `rustc` directly.
   - The result cache keyed by the crate's text, `rustc -vV` and the bundle.
   - Earlier `comptime`s are cache hits when the sub-program is checked.
   - Used where the build has a workshop and the initialiser calls something.
   - The interpreter stays, until stage 4, for a bare `check` with no workshop and for initialisers that read an `asset` or run a grammar.
   - Tests: `fib(10)`, `sq(100000)` stops the build, `5.seconds()` inside a callee.
2. **Step budget and call depth** (D7, step 5) in the lowering of the comptime crate; `fib(50)` is refused.
3. **One crate per package** (D4), parallel to the rest of the lowering (D5, step 7).
4. **The interpreter's call path goes** (step 9). The tests move to a workshop.
5. **Nikaia dependencies in the bundle** with derived ledgers (D8, 4a/4b); **no foreign Rust** (D9, 4c); **cross-compiling** (step 8).

##### Question for the deciding session

**Which code reports a build-time run that stops as the program would**, for example on an overflow (step 3)?

- `NK1152` is *not allowed*, and its text now also carries the budget and the depth.
- `NK1127` is *cannot be computed*.
- Neither says *it ran and stopped*: `comptime C = sq(100000)` has run and aborted at the multiplication.

Until this is answered, stage 1 reports it as `NK1152`. The message names the `comptime`, the runtime's message (*attempt to multiply with overflow*) and the `.nika` line, where the runtime reports one. The code stays easy to change.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-05](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/468#issuecomment-6000012068):

#### Added: bounds for a build-time run (ADR-321 D7, D10–D12; `f88f917`, `72582eb`)

##### How it came up

The owner wanted a way to say how long build-time code may run. The point is not protection against malicious code but protecting the programmer from themselves: one wrong line and the machine hangs. The discussion found two ways a machine hangs. An endless loop is one, and the step budget covers it. Memory filling up and the system swapping is the other, and a step budget stops it too late. The owner then asked how to measure both:

- **Steps:** a counter in the generated code, decremented at every loop back-edge and every function entry, plus a stack of function numbers for the message. A call into `std`'s Rust half counts as one step.
- **Memory:** the generated crate gets its own `#[global_allocator]` that counts live bytes. It is deterministic per architecture, works on every platform, and covers `std` too. OS limits (`setrlimit`) were rejected because macOS does not enforce them and Windows has no equivalent in that form.

##### Owner's answers

1. A `comptime` raises a bound **at its own declaration**, not in the manifest and not on the command line.
2. No measurement first. Generous defaults: **4 GiB**, and a step budget sized for **about 20 s**. Taking 10⁸–10⁹ counted steps a second, that is **10 000 000 000 steps**. This is an estimate.
3. A memory bound: yes.
4. A progress message for a long run: yes.

**Not decided:** the spelling a `comptime` uses to raise a bound (ADR-321 §6). It is being asked now.

##### To build: step 5 replaced, 5a–5c added

5. **Step budget.** Lower a counter into the comptime crate: decrement at each loop back-edge and function entry, and abort past the budget, which is 10¹⁰ by default. Keep a stack of function numbers for the message. `NK1152` names the `comptime`, the bound and the call path.
   - Test: `fib(60)` is refused with the path `fib → fib → …`; `fib(25)` passes.
   - Measure the counter's overhead with callgrind on a tight loop, with and without the counter (instructions), and record it in the issue.
5a. **Memory bound.** A counting `#[global_allocator]` in the comptime crate: live bytes, abort past 4 GiB by default with `NK1152`.
   - Test: `let mut xs = []; while true { xs.push(1) }` is refused by the memory bound, not the step budget.
   - Test: `"x".repeat(5_000_000_000)` from `std` is refused.
5b. **Raising a bound at the declaration.** Wait for the spelling decision. Until then the defaults are fixed.
5c. **Progress message.** When a run passes a few seconds, the compiler prints `` `TABLE` in config.nika is running (5 s) ``, at most every few seconds. Test with a fake slow run: the message appears and the build output is unchanged.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-05](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/468#issuecomment-6000571063):

#### Stages 1 and 2 are built

##### 0.0.518 (4334347), stage 1: a `comptime` that calls a function is compiled and run

How it works:
- `comptime_run.rs` builds a sub-program: the file without its `main`, the earlier `comptime`s as the literals they came to, and `fn __nikaia_comptime_value() -> T { return <initialiser> }`. Its `main` writes the value in the grammar run's encoding.
- It is linked against a dylib bundle of `std`, built once in the workshop. The bundle is built with `cargo rustc --crate-type dylib -- -C prefer-dynamic`, and the program is compiled with `rustc` directly.
- The answer is cached by the program text, `rustc -vV` and the bundle (D4). A stop is cached too.
- The ledger rule (D2) is asked for each callee before anything is compiled.
- The stop is reported with its own site table: generated line → byte of the span. The checker turns the byte back into a place in the checked file.

What it does now:
- `comptime C = sq(100000)` with an `i32` `sq` is `NK1152`, *`C` stopped while the program was built: attempt to multiply with overflow.*, with the caret on `return x * x` (step 3).
- `text::digit_value('7')` inside a callee gives 7. The interpreter refused it with `NK1127`.

Limits of this stage, where the interpreter still answers:
- a program of more than one file, or one with a grammar;
- an initialiser with a method call or an `asset`;
- a bare check with no workshop.

##### 0.0.519 (3651a17), stage 2: the bounds

- **D7, steps.** `Build::counts_steps` makes the emitter write `nikaia_std::build_time::enter("f")` into every function's opening brace and `step()` into every loop body.
  - Past the budget: `NK1152`, *`F` took more than … steps*, with the path (*It was in `SPUN` > `spin`.*).
  - Past 10 000 calls deep: *went more than 10000 calls deep*.
  - A loop that never ends and `fib(50)` are refused; `fib(20)` passes.
- **D10, memory.** A `#[global_allocator]` in the run's own program is **not used** when `std` is linked dynamically: a counter in it stayed at 0. So the counting allocator lives in the generated bundle, and the run's `main` sets the bound. Past 4 GiB: *held more than … bytes at once*.
- **D12, progress.** After 5 s, and then every 10 s, the build prints *note: `X` is still being computed while the program is built (N s)*.
- **A run that dies without a word** (killed for its memory) now says how it ended, instead of an empty message.
- **D11, raising a bound.** The spelling is still open. The bounds travel with the workshop (`Reads::bounded`), so the tests ask for smaller ones.

Tests: `tests/comptime_compiled.rs` (9). The bench and example lowerings are unchanged.

##### Measured for the deciding session (D7's default)

Callgrind, instructions, on a loop that only counts:

| opt-level | instructions per counted step |
| :--- | ---: |
| 0 (step 2's setting) | 37 |
| 1 | 13 |

The slope was taken over 10 million steps, with `step()` as one relaxed `fetch_sub` on a static. That puts the default of 10¹⁰ steps at about 3.7·10¹¹ instructions at `opt-level=0`. That is minutes, not D7's estimate of about twenty seconds. `opt-level=1` would be about 1.3·10¹¹. The ADR measured `opt-level=1` at 148 M instructions to compile, against 133 M.

**Question:** is the default meant to be reached in about twenty seconds? If so:
- the budget would be 10⁹ at `opt-level=0`, or
- the comptime crate would be compiled at `opt-level=1`.

##### Next

Stage 3: one crate per package (D4), beside the rest of the lowering (D5).

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-05](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/468#issuecomment-6000976910):

#### The compiled path covers more (0.0.520–0.0.524)

Each package passes the build_time, comptime, grammar, project and examples tests, the bench and example lowerings are byte-identical, and clippy is clean.

- **0.0.520 (c63200a): any initialiser with a call is compiled.** This now includes method calls, from `std` or from the program: `"abc".to_uppercase()` gives `"ABC"`, and the interpreter had refused it. D2 is now read off the ledger entry derived for the sub-program's value function. That covers every call it reaches, methods included.
- **0.0.521 (4eb9c8b): programs of several files.**
  - Every file of the package goes into the sub-program, lowered the way `modules` lowers a package.
  - The site table now records the file as well as the byte.
  - A stop in another file is named by the function it happened in: *It stopped in `sq`, in another file of the program.*
- **0.0.522 (09fbb09): `asset("…")` together with a call** (`twice(asset("data.txt"))`).
  - The build reads the file under ADR-310's rules, and the read text replaces the `asset` before lowering.
  - `NK1175` and `NK1176` are reported before anything is compiled.
- **0.0.523 (ce50fb7): earlier `comptime`s of any shape are carried in.** Lists, tuples, structs and variants are written as literals (`ORIGIN.scaled(10).sum()`).
- **0.0.524 (8ab948f): programs with a `grammar`.**
  - The bundle now also names `winnow` and `winnow_grammar`, so such a program compiles its ordinary `comptime`s.
  - An initialiser that runs a grammar entry (`Cfg::file(…)`) still goes through the grammar run (ADR-177 / §6).

##### Where the interpreter still answers (stage 4 is to remove it)

1. **A check with no workshop.** This is the library API the unit tests use. Every CLI build has a workshop. The plan: move the tests (`build_time.rs` and the others) onto a shared workshop, then report `NK1127` where there is no workshop.
2. **Option defaults (ADR-318).** `contracts::evaluate_defaults` evaluates them during ledger inference, with `Reads::none()`. Compiling them means handing the workshop down to the inference, a pipeline change of its own.
3. **Grammar entries in an initialiser.** These go through the grammar run, which compiles rather than interprets. Its dispatch lives in `build_time.rs` and stays there.
4. **An initialiser whose type has no written form here** (`Ty::Unknown`).

Then stage 3 (one crate per package, run beside the lowering) and stage 5 (D8/D9 dependencies, cross-compiling).

#### 0.0.525 (4df38b9): build_time's tests run on the compiled path

The 47 tests in `tests/build_time.rs` now run through a workshop, as a build does, so they exercise the compiled path. 43 passed unchanged.

The D2 refusal now names the callee as the call resolves: `io::read_to_string` by its path, `Reader::read` by its key.

Three tests now expect ADR-321's answers instead of the interpreter's:

| Case | Before (interpreter) | Now (compiled) |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| `forever(n + 1)` | *too deeply* (depth 128) | *went more than 10000 calls deep* |
| `comptime N = io::read_to_string()` | `NK1127` | `NK1152`, *You can't call `io::read_to_string`…* (std's Rust half is under D2 now) |
| `xs[7]` on an `Array[i64, 3]` in a callee | `NK1165`, help *The indexes go from 0 to 2.* | `NK1152`, *`BAD` stopped while the program was built: index out of bounds: the len is 3 but the index is 7.*, with the caret on `return xs[7]` |

**One more question for the deciding session, beside the stop code asked above.** Part III's table still describes `NK1165` as an index into an `Array` at build time.

- With compiled build-time code, such an index is the program's own abort, with Rust's runtime message.
- Should `NK1165` be retired, along with whatever code a stopped run gets?
- Or should the compiled run translate the abort back into `NK1165`'s sentence?

Today the program's runtime message is the same one the program gives when it runs. So the build-time and run-time words now agree, which is D1's point.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-05](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/468#issuecomment-6002326554):

#### Step 5b decided: the spelling (ADR-321 D11, `a6525af`)

```nika
comptime(steps: 50G, ram: 16Gi) TABLE = build_table(asset("data.csv"))
```

##### How it was decided

The decision round weighed these forms:
- (a) options in parentheses after the word, as `dsl sqlite(schema: APP)` does (ADR-299 D3);
- (b) options after `;` at the end of the line;
- an `@comptime(…)` annotation. Rejected: Nikaia has no annotation system outside grammars, and building one for this case is too much;
- `comptime(expr; …)` as an expression. Rejected: all 60 real `comptime`s in the repository are package-level, and that would need a top-level `let`, or package constants turned into functions, which loses ADR-287 D21's open number;
- the owner's `comptime(step=1G, ram=4G) NAME =`. Adopted, with `:` instead of `=` (named arguments everywhere are `name: value`) and with scale literals from ADR-322 (#469's build), including `Gi` for binary.

##### To build

5b. **Bounds at the declaration.**
- Parse `comptime(steps: <int>, ram: <int>) NAME = …` at item level and in a body. Either name may be omitted, and `comptime NAME = …` keeps the defaults (`10G` steps, `4Gi`). Any other name gives `NK1109`.
- The values are build-time integers; with #469 they can be written `50G` / `16Gi`.
- Test: `comptime(steps: 100) X = fib(20)` is refused by the step budget, while `comptime X = fib(20)` passes.
- Test: `comptime(steps: 60G) X = fib(45)` passes where the default would refuse.

#### Stage 4: the tests run through a workshop (0.0.532 `e3d4d1d`, 0.0.533 `3e46d96`)

**What changed:**
- `tests/common` now has `workshop()`, `reads()`, `infer()` and `checked()`. These test files use them: `comptime.rs`, `comptime_item.rs`, `option_defaults.rs`, `modules.rs` and `specification.rs`.
- `specbook::verdicts` and `report` take a workshop. The example uses the user's `build-time` workshop, as a build of a loose file does. `EXPECTED.txt` is unchanged.
- `emit_program_reading` now also compiles defaults in its workshop. The workshop for defaults is kept per thread, so tests running side by side no longer share it.
- A program of several files computed every `comptime` a second time through the interpreter: `Needs::of` asked each file what it uses with a full check. `Build::leaves_values` now skips the values there.
- Two tests expected the interpreter's refusal of `"hallo".to_uppercase()`. That is now computed, as `"HALLO"`.

**What still reaches the interpreter.** Checked by making that path report itself and running every build-time test file:
- Outside a run: nothing.
- Inside a run's sub-program: computed defaults, which its own check and ledger evaluate a second time with no workshop. A compiled run would recurse into itself, so the workshop is cleared there.

**Next:**
1. Hand the outer ledger's computed defaults to the sub-program, so the run has nothing to compute again.
2. Then remove the call-evaluating part of `build_time.rs` (step 9).

**Question for the deciding session.** Once the interpreter is gone, what does a check with no workshop answer for a `comptime` that calls something? This is the library API, e.g. `check::check`. A CLI build always has a workshop.
- (a) `NK1127`, *can't be computed while the program is built*, with a note that this check has no place to compile build-time code.
- (b) Nothing: the value is left open, as the ledger's pass does since 0.0.528, and a later check with a workshop answers it.

(a) is more honest toward a caller that wants an answer. (b) is quieter, but a caller could then take a program as checked although its `comptime` was never run. Until this is answered, the interpreter stays for exactly this case.

The note on an undeclared callee is also stale and goes with the interpreter. It still reads *"it comes from `std` or a package, which is compiled Rust … the compiler can only run code written in your program"*, which ADR-321 made untrue.

### <a id="issue-453"></a>[#453](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/453) What a program needs to tune a text loop by hand: a byte search from a position, and reading text a word at a time (decision basis owed)

`language`, `libraries`, `performance`, `ready` · opened 2026-10-05 · updated 2026-10-08 · 5 comments

#### The question

Can a Nikaia program make a hot loop over text as fast as tuned Rust, when the author is willing to write more? Today the answer is no. The fastest Nikaia 1BRC is the one that writes the least: the grammar in `examples/1brc.nika`. Every hand-tuned variant was worse, because two building blocks are missing.

#### What was measured

Callgrind, 1 M rows, per row, the same output as `naive` in every case. The method is `benches/brc/README.md`'s. The compiler is 0.0.478.

| variant | instructions | mispredictions |
|---|---:|---:|
| `examples/1brc.nika`: grammar and `HashMap` | **300.1** | 1.58 |
| grammar, own open-addressing table, FNV-1a over every byte of the name | 330.5 | 2.66 |
| grammar, own table, hash of the length and the first 8 bytes at most | 309.3 | **1.49** |
| one hand-written loop over `text.bytes()`, hash computed while reading the name, own table | 428.0 | 4.39 |
| *Rust, `tuned`* | *272.2* | *3.70* |

Where it goes:

- **A hash of one's own costs more than the built-in one.** Text can only be read with `bytes()`, one byte per turn; `nikaia-std`'s hash and `tuned`'s read eight at a time. Every byte of the name was 30 instructions a row and a misprediction at the loop's varying end.
- **A parser of one's own is a state machine per byte.** Without a search for the next `;` or `\n` from a position, the loop above spends 275 instructions a row in itself. The grammar searches with `memchr` inside its runtime, which is why it beats hand work.
- The own table's index checks stay, because the prover does not know a field's length (#384).

#### What is missing

1. **A search for a byte from a position:** something like `text.find(b';', from)` → `i64?`, or an iterator of positions, backed by `memchr`.
2. **Reading text a word at a time,** or a hash of a text slice the program can call: `hash::of(name)` with `std`'s byte hash, so a table of one's own does not pay a byte loop.
3. Possibly **byte access by position** (`text.byte_at(i)`), which a tuned parser walks back with.

#### What a decision has to say

- Which of the three the language offers, under which names, and whether as `std` functions or as grammar features. A grammar already has the search; is it enough to say "write a grammar"?
- How each keeps Part I's promises: no `unsafe`, UTF-8 boundaries respected when a slice is cut, bounds checked or proved.
- How other languages hand this to programs: Rust's `memchr`/`as_bytes`, Go's `bytes.IndexByte`, Zig's `std.mem.indexOfScalar`, Java's `String.indexOf(int, from)`.

The decision basis (introduction, today's state, other languages, options, a recommendation) is owed before this is asked.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-05](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/453#issuecomment-5994140940):

#### Decision (owner, 2026-10-05)

Written in 9d7ddc11 (0.0.496):

- [ADR-320](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-320.md) says why;
- Part I 2.2 and the new **2.6**, Part II's grammar table and Part III's code table (`NK1212`-`NK1214`, `char` under `NK1135`) say what.

##### How it came to this

The question started as *what does a hand-tuned text loop need*, measured above. In the round with the owner it became what one character is in the language as a whole:

1. A search from a position, and a `Bytes` that can be read, were agreed first.
2. The owner rejected *it waits for a program that needs it* as a reason to leave a capability out. ADR-288 D11 was clarified in 0.0.485.
3. Positions were checked on today's compiler: `s.len()` counts bytes, `s[a..<b]` cuts at bytes and stops in Rust's words inside a scalar, and the specification said none of it.
4. Swift's model was weighed: views, `String.Index`, graphemes checked in the compiler and segmented again in the runtime. The owner chose views that name their unit, with graphemes kept out of the compiler (Rust's and Go's split).
5. In the owner's review the following were sharpened:
   - surrogates excluded, so the type is `scalar`;
   - brackets only where they cost nothing, so `s.scalars` and `s.graphemes` are sequences;
   - a decomposed `'é'` is normalised with a warning rather than refused;
   - `==` and a hash compare bytes, and normalising is written with its form.

##### In short

- Text is UTF-8, and a position in it is a byte position.
- **There is no `char`.** The types are `scalar` and `u8`.
- Text is read through views: `s.bytes` indexes and cuts; `s.scalars` and `s.graphemes` are sequences. There is no `s.len()` and no `s[i]`; `s.is_empty()` stays.
- A position the text hands out cuts it without a view: `s.find(needle; from:) -> i64?`, a grammar's match, either moved by the byte length of a literal. A made-up number needs a view (`NK1214`).
- A `'…'` is a `u8` or a `scalar` by its use (`NK1212`). A decomposed one that normalises to one scalar is taken, with `NK1213`.
- A `Bytes` is read as a run: `b[i]`, `b[a..<b]`, `len`, `find`, `text()`.
- Graphemes are `std`'s, by UAX #29 in `std`'s Unicode version.
- `==` and a hash compare bytes; `s.normalized(; form: nfc|nfd|nfkc|nfkd)` is written.

**Still open:** a hash a program can call.

##### To build

The order matters: each step keeps the tree building.

1. **`scalar` as a type beside `char`.** Make `char` an alias the checker accepts for now, then move `std.contracts`, `text::digit_value`, the grammar builtins (`any`, `digit`, `hex_digit`, `char`) and the self-hosted tools to `scalar`. After that, `char` is `NK1135` with the help naming `scalar` and `u8`.
2. **Literals:** a `'…'` typed by its use as `u8` or `scalar`, as a number is (Part I 2.4), defaulting to `scalar`; `'\xNN'` above `\x7F` only as a `u8`; surrogates refused; `NK1212`. `NK1213` normalises through `std`'s NFC.
3. **Views:** `s.bytes` (index, cut, `len`, `find`, `for`), `s.scalars` and `s.graphemes` (`take`, `skip`, `count`, `for`). Graphemes go through `std`, e.g. `unicode-segmentation` behind `nikaia-std`, with its Unicode version stated.
4. **`find`:** `s.find(needle; from: i64 = 0) -> i64?` on text, with a one-byte needle through `memchr`; `b.find(byte; from:)` on `Bytes` and `s.bytes`. This replaces the fall-through to Rust's `str::find` (#452).
5. **`Bytes`:** `b[i] -> u8`, `b[a..<b]`, `b.find`, `b.text()` (#452's `raw[0]`).
6. **Positions:** the checker keeps where a position came from (`find`, a grammar match, plus a literal's byte length) and lets it cut that text. A made-up number or another text's position is `NK1214`, with the view in the help. A cut inside a scalar stops the program with the language's own message, not Rust's *not a char boundary*.
7. **Remove `s.len()` and `s[n]` on text.** Move the self-hosted tools off `chars().collect()` + index, onto views or `find`.
8. **`s.normalized(; form:)`** in `std`.
9. **Tests:**
   - each row of Part I 2.6's literal rules;
   - `'\u{D800}'` refused;
   - a decomposed `'é'` taken with `NK1213`;
   - `line[..<sep]` after `find` compiles, and `s[0..<10]` is `NK1214`;
   - a cut inside `ä` stops with the language's message;
   - `"ä" == "a\u{308}"` false, and true after `normalized(; form: nfc)`;
   - `s.graphemes.count()` of a family emoji is 1;
   - `find` from inside a scalar finds the next match;
   - 1BRC by hand with `find` measured again against the table above.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-07](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/453#issuecomment-6039111840):

#### Step 4 built for text (329fd8e, 0.0.635)

`s.find(needle; from: i64 = 0) -> i64?` gives the byte position of the first match at or after `from`, or `null`.

**How it is built**
- `std.contracts` has a `str::find` entry, so `from:` is checked as any option is.
- The checker records each `find` on text, and the emitter writes it as `nikaia_std::search::find(&s, &needle, from)`. Rust's inherent `str::find` (one argument, a `usize`) would win over anything a library adds. This ends #452's fall-through to `str::find`.

**What it does**
- A search begun inside a scalar finds the next match. It moves to the next boundary; UTF-8 is self-synchronising.
- A `from` before the start searches from the start. One past the end finds nothing.
- A one-byte needle uses the byte search the language below's `str::find` already runs for it (`memchr`-style).

**Tests**
- `tests/text_find.rs`: from the start, from a position, nothing after the last match, inside `ä`, and a cut at the position (`line[0..<sep]`).
- Unit tests in `nikaia-std/src/search.rs`.

**Not covered by this step**
- `b.find(byte; from:)` on `Bytes` and on `s.bytes` belongs to step 5, with the `Bytes` reads.
- **Finding #527:** Part I 2.6's own example right after `find`, `line[..<sep]` / `line[sep + 1..]` (a cut with an open end), does not parse.

**Step 1: there is no `char`.**

- One ASCII character is either. Where its use wants a `u8` (an annotation, an argument, a comparison with a byte), it is written `b'a'` below. Everywhere else it is a `scalar`.
- `'é'` where a `u8` is wanted is `NK1103`.
- `b[i]` on a `Bytes` is now a `u8` in the checker too, and `b[a..<b]` a `Bytes`. Before, `bytes[0] == 'a'` reached `rustc` as a mismatch.

- A literal of several scalars: `NK1212`, and `NK1213` with NFC. This needs `std`'s normalisation, so it lands with step 8.
- `'\xNN'` above `\x7F`, which today's lexer does not read.
- A `let c = 'a'` whose *later* use is a byte. Today it is a `scalar`, because only a use at the literal itself decides. Numbers have open bindings (ADR-285 D23) and literals do not yet.

**Two things the records do not say, before steps 3 and 8 can be built:**
1. **What type `s.bytes` has.** It indexes, cuts, has `len`/`find`, is walked by `for`, and `s.bytes[0..<10].text()` makes text again. A copy into a `Bytes` would allocate on every use, which defeats #453's own measurement. So it needs a view type of its own, a borrowed run of the text's bytes. Its name decides what a program writes in an annotation (`let b: ? = s.bytes`) and in a parameter.
2. **What `nfc` is in `s.normalized(; form: nfc)`.** Every other variant in the specification is written in full (`supervisor::Strategy::OneForOne`, `fs::Root::Anywhere`). A bare `nfc` needs either a rule that a variant may be written bare where its type is known, or the form written as `text::Form::Nfc`.

### <a id="issue-390"></a>[#390](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/390) The default of `--optimization`, and whether its proofs belong in `nikaia.proofs`

`compiler`, `ready` · opened 2026-10-03 · updated 2026-10-07 · 7 comments

Two questions [ADR-271](../blob/main/docs/specification/adr/adr-271.md) §6 leaves open:

#### 1. The default
- **Today:** `remove-bounds-checks` and `remove-overflow-checks` are `off` unless a build asks.
- **Measured so far:** `aggressive` costs about +3 % over the 46 standalone files and +16 ms on `solver-kernels.nika` (`solver-workload.md` §8.3).
- **To decide:** whether the default becomes `basic`, or `aggressive`, once the compile time is measured over the whole corpus, `std` included.

#### 2. The proof file
- **Today:** the walk re-proves on every build, deterministically.
- **The other path:** [ADR-270](../blob/main/docs/specification/adr/adr-270.md) D19–D23 put every solver answer into the committed `nikaia.proofs`.
- **To decide:** whether the walk's queries go there too once the search may be nondeterministic, and what that means for the cache key (ADR-271 D1).

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-04](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/390#issuecomment-5983915542):

#### State after the owner's round (2026-10-04)

**1. `nikaia.proofs` - decided:** the walk's proofs go into it. This does not wait for the solver's search to become free. ADR-306 §6's *"when the solver's search is free"* is superseded.

**2. The default - not decidable yet.** The owner's view: `aggressive` is dangerous today. A proved index is emitted through `proven-index`'s `get_unchecked`, so a wrong proof means undefined behaviour, where a wrong overflow proof only means a wrong value. The certificate checker (ADR-270 D5) covers the solver's arithmetic, not whether the query the walk built says what the program does. The questions are now:

- Can the design let a build **buy safety or performance with the prover's depth or time**, and how?
- **Opt-in or opt-out?**

A decision basis is being prepared first:

- what each kind of wrong proof costs, and which part of the chain is checked by what;
- how SPARK, Dafny, LLVM/HotSpot and Wuffs handle budgets and defaults;
- the design options for a depth/time setting, including what a time budget means for reproducibility now that the answers are recorded;
- opt-in against opt-out;
- the measurements still owed: compile time over the whole corpus with `std`, and sites proved per budget.

---
_Generated by [Claude Code](https://claude.ai/code)_

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-04](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/390#issuecomment-5983947069):

**Budget, as the owner decided in another session:** the prover's effort is a **step budget, never time**. Only the **expensive steps are counted**. That makes counting nearly free, and in any case cheaper than reading the clock. `nikaia-logic`'s `Budget` already has this shape: `cases`, where each case is one elimination, and `bounds`, the bounds one elimination may hold. It is not counted per operation.

What remains open for #390:

- how a build sets this budget, its depth;
- whether the setting can be given separately for index checks and overflow checks;
- opt-in or opt-out.

_Generated by [Claude Code](https://claude.ai/code)_

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-04](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/390#issuecomment-5983996077):

**What makes `aggressive` dangerous (owner, 2026-10-04).** A wrong proof that **removes** a run-time check turns a defect into a bomb. Where the program would have stopped cleanly, it reads out of bounds through `get_unchecked` (undefined behaviour) or computes a silently wrong value through `wrapping_*`. A proof used to **find** errors is not dangerous: a wrong refutation is a visible build error, and `--refuted-claims warn` bypasses it.

So the decision basis treats the prover's two uses separately:

1. **Finding errors** (refutation, ADR-269 D8): harmless if wrong, and it gains from more budget. It is a candidate for on by default.
2. **Removing checks** (ADR-306): needs its own decision, and ways to defuse the bomb:
   - a test build that keeps every proved check as an assertion reporting *the compiler's proof was wrong*, so wrong proofs surface in CI;
   - index and overflow set separately (undefined behaviour against a wrong value);
   - checking the translation from program to query (translation validation, or differential runs at both levels).
3. **Budget:** counted in expensive steps only (decided). Open is how a build sets it.
4. **Opt-in or opt-out** for each use.
5. **Measurements owed:** compile time, and the sites proved and refuted per budget.

_Generated by [Claude Code](https://claude.ai/code)_

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-04](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/390#issuecomment-5984142828):

#### Decision (owner, 2026-10-04)

**Today's problem:** `aggressive` is one switch for two things. It means *search harder* and *remove what was proved* at once, and nothing lets a build set the budget. Checked in the code:

- `prove.rs` (written claims: `requires`, `ensures`, `assert`) always runs, and a refuted claim is an error (ADR-269 D8).
- `bounds.rs` (the checks nobody writes: index and overflow) runs only at `aggressive`. Its only effect is to remove checks: a check it could show *can* fail is not reported.
- Both use `Budget::default()` (1024 cases, 4096 bounds), and no switch reaches it.

##### Two switches for two things

**1. How hard the compiler proves**, e.g. `--proving=LEVEL` / `[build] proving`.
- The levels map to step budgets, counting expensive steps only (decided earlier).
- It governs both paths: written claims, and the checks nobody writes. `bounds.rs` therefore runs whatever the optimization.
- The answers go into `nikaia.proofs` (decided).
- More budget means more defects found at build time.

**2. What happens to a proved check:** `--optimization=remove-bounds-checks` and `remove-overflow-checks`, now `on`/`off`.
- It changes only what is emitted, never what is proved.
- Default `off`: a wrong proof that removes a check turns a defect into a bomb (`get_unchecked`, `wrapping_*`, and `debug-assertions = false` in the generated profile).
- Switching it re-proves nothing, so it causes no extra proving round and no second set of proofs.

##### A check nobody writes that is shown to fail is an **error**

Example: an index shown outside the list for a value the program reaches. The run-time check would only stop the program; it would not rescue it. So this is reported as a refused claim is, an error, with `--refuted-claims warn` as the bypass (ADR-269 D8).

- **Only on a refutation, never on *unknown*.** As `prove.rs::refutes` already demands, the model has to be checked, and the values have to be ones the program reaches; the facts are true but not all that is true. A refusal on a guess would refuse a correct program (Part III C.4).

##### To build

1. **The `proving` option:** command line and `[build]`, levels mapped to a `Budget`. Pass it to `prove.rs` and to the walk. Add it to the cache key and to the `nikaia.proofs` key.
2. **Run `bounds.rs` at every build**, independent of `--optimization`, and record its answers in `nikaia.proofs`.
3. **Reduce `remove-bounds-checks` / `remove-overflow-checks` to `on`/`off`.** `basic` either becomes a proving level or goes; decide when building, and say which. Removal reads the recorded proofs.
4. **A refuted implicit check is an error:** a new code (or `NK1202`'s family), naming the value that fails, and honouring `--refuted-claims warn`.
5. **Update ADR-306 and ADR-269 D8**, and Part III 13.3's `[build]` table.
6. **Tests:**
   - a provable index out of bounds is refused, and builds with `warn`;
   - an *unknown* index builds silently;
   - a higher `proving` level proves a site the default does not;
   - toggling removal re-proves nothing (no new `nikaia.proofs` entries);
   - removal `off` emits every check even where it is proved.
7. **Measure:** compile time over the whole corpus with `std` per proving level, and the sites proved and refuted per level. These numbers pick the default proving level.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-07](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/390#issuecomment-6038985203):

#### Step 7: sites proved and refuted per budget (measured on 0.0.634)

##### Method
- **Corpus:** every tracked `.nika` file (`nikaia lower --no-cache`), plus `std`'s self-hosted tools (`nikaia lower-std`).
- **Patch, not committed:**
  - `proofs::search` takes its `Budget` from an environment variable, with `cases = N` and `bounds = 4·N` (the default's ratio, 1024/4096);
  - it logs every solver answer;
  - recorded answers in `nikaia.proofs` are ignored, so that every query is searched.
- **Counted, not timed:** each row counts solver answers, so it is exact and machine-independent.

##### Results

| `cases` (bounds = 4×) | queries | proved | refuted | unknown: budget (cases / bounds) | unknown: no contradiction | unknown: overflow |
| ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: |
| 1 | 984 | 52 | 18 | 484 / 419 | 2 | 9 |
| 4 | 989 | 489 | 287 | 63 / 122 | 19 | 9 |
| 16 | 995 | 664 | 303 | 0 / 0 | 19 | 9 |
| 64 | 995 | 664 | 303 | 0 / 0 | 19 | 9 |
| 256 | 995 | 664 | 303 | 0 / 0 | 19 | 9 |
| **1024 (today's default)** | 995 | 664 | 303 | 0 / 0 | 19 | 9 |
| 4096 | 995 | 664 | 303 | 0 / 0 | 19 | 9 |
| 16384 | 995 | 664 | 303 | 0 / 0 | 19 | 9 |

There are 472 distinct queries; the same query is asked from several places. At budgets 1 and 4 the walk asks fewer follow-up queries, because earlier ones end unknown.

##### What it says
- **From 16 cases up, the budget changes nothing on today's corpus.** No query reaches the limit. The 28 unknowns that remain are outside what more search can answer: no contradiction exists in the facts given (19), or a coefficient overflowed (9).
- **Compile cost per level.** Above 16 the search is deterministic and never stopped by the budget, so the work done is identical at every setting. A callgrind count per level would show the same solver instructions, so it was not taken. The budget is a cap, not an effort setting, as long as no query reaches it.
- **Raising the budget therefore buys nothing today.** It would only pay where the walk asks harder queries: more facts per function, nested loops, or quantities the prover reads today but does not combine.
- **Lowering it is the only lever with an effect,** and only below 16, where proofs are lost (664 → 489 at 4).

##### For the decision
The measurements do not separate any levels above 16, so they cannot choose a default among them. There are two honest readings:
1. **No user-facing levels for now:** keep `Budget::default()` and let `nikaia.proofs` carry the answers. A `proving` option then only matters once a program exists that reaches the limit.
2. **Levels as caps for pathological programs:** a low and a high cap. The default stays far above 16, because there is no measured cost to it.

Not measured: queries from programs that do not exist yet. The corpus has 995 queries; its hardest is answered within 16 cases.

**The 303 refuted queries** are what step 4 (*a refuted implicit check is an error*) would read. Most are the walk asking whether a check is needed in a case it then rules out another way. They are not 303 defects. Step 4 still has to tell a refutation the program reaches from one under incomplete facts, as the decision says.

The status stays `ready`: steps 1 and 4 remain to build. A level design waits for a program that reaches the budget, or for the owner to choose reading 1 or 2.
