**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-10](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/88#issuecomment-6092811229):

#### Decision 3 of 3: codes for `std`'s errors (0.0.724, `c12e561`)

**Question:** which status an entry point returns for an error it throws from `std`. Today such a function is not exported (`not_yet` in `cexport.rs`). That covers every library that reads a file, since 38 ledger entries throw `io::IoError`.

**Options weighed:**
- **Numbering `std`'s variants after the library's own** (the building session's recommendation). Rejected: every `std` code moves when the library adds a variant, and every library code moves when `std` adds one. `NotFound` would also have a different number in each library.
- **One fixed number for all targets.** Rejected: `EILSEQ` is 84 on Linux, 92 on macOS and 42 under Windows' C runtime. A caller comparing a status with its own `EILSEQ` would misread it.
- **Refusing `std` errors at the boundary.** Rejected: every library that reads a file would have to translate its errors before it could be exported.
- **The owner's direction: POSIX numbers, and per target where they differ.** This is how libuv, Node, Go and Python do it: the platform's number, with a portable name beside it.

**Decided:** [ADR-284](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-284.md) D33, with D7 changed. Part III 15.1 holds the table.
- **`1` to `99 999` are `std`'s.**
  - A `std` error that is an `errno` has the number of the target the library is built for: `NotFound` is `ENOENT`, `PermissionDenied` is `EACCES`, `NotText` is `EILSEQ`. WASI's numbers apply under WASI.
  - `std`'s other errors have fixed numbers from `1000`, a block of a hundred per type: `Outside` 1000, `Other` 1001, `task::Crashed` 1100, `supervisor::Escalated` 1200, `cleanup::Failure` 1300, `Overtaken` 1400. A new variant is appended to its block, and a number is never moved.
- **The library's own variants start at `100 000`**, no longer at `1`.
- A function that throws several types has the codes of each.

##### To build
1. **Renumber the library's own variants from `100 000`.** This changes `examples/c-library`, its `use.c`, the Python and Node bindings, and `tests/c_library.rs`.
2. **Export `std`'s errors.**
   - An entry point that throws `io::IoError`, or several types, is exported. The header writes `<PACKAGE>_E_IO_NOT_FOUND` and so on, with the target's `errno` value (from `libc` at build time) or the fixed number.
   - Test: an entry point that reads a missing file returns `ENOENT`, and the C test compares the status with `<errno.h>`'s `ENOENT`.
3. **`last_error`** carries the message of `Other(String)`.
4. **Bindings.**
   - Python: `ENOENT` raises `FileNotFoundError` and `EACCES` raises `PermissionError`. Both are subclasses of the package's `Error` as well, if that can be done cleanly; otherwise the package's `Error`, with `errno` set.
   - Node: `code` is `'ENOENT'`, and `errno` is the number.
5. **WASI target:** the header uses WASI's `errno` values.

All three decisions on this issue are now taken.

### <a id="issue-605"></a>[#605](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/605) Unjoined tasks: what happens to an error nobody waits for (fatal ones included)

`blocked` · opened 2026-10-13 · updated 2026-10-14 · 9 comments

#### What this is about, from zero

`spawn fn { ... }` starts a task and hands back a handle; `h.join()` waits for it and gets back what the task produced, or its error. Most of the time someone waits. Sometimes nobody does:

```nika
use std::process

fn main() throws {
    spawn fn { process::exit(2) }   // handle dropped: nobody joins
    println("main goes on")
}
```

Since ADR-345 (#606), `process::exit` throws the **fatal** error `process::Exit`, and in a task it ends the task and goes to whoever joins it (D5). Here nobody joins. The same goes for a supervisor's child (the supervisor reads the failure, not a person) and an `overlap` branch that fails after a sibling already failed.

**Where this comes from:** the owner's decision of 2026-10-13 asked to check this before building, and to put it to the owner if a fatal error could vanish. Nothing in the repository needs the answer today (no program spawns an `exit`), but a fatal error that vanishes silently is the kind of failure ADR-345 exists to prevent.

#### What is true today (checked on develop, 0.0.883)

- **Spec:** Part I 8.2 and Part III A.2 say nothing about the error of an unjoined task. `TaskHandle` (crates/nikaia-std/src/task.rs): "a task nobody joins still runs"; its result stays in the slot and is dropped with it.
- **Checker:** a failing call in `spawn fn { ... }` is counted against the *enclosing* function (`NK2605` asks `main` for `throws`), as if the failure went to the spawner.
- **Lowering (defect):** with `main() throws`, a detached `spawn` whose body fails emits invalid Rust (`Result<i64, Thrown<E>>` used as the value); `h.join() catch { match error {...} }` over a failing task does not compile either (the join's `Result` is not flattened). So a task's failure does not reach anyone at all yet.
- **Supervisor children:** a failure goes to `Restart::decide`; a fatal one would be restarted like any crash.
- **`overlap`:** failing siblings join the first error as secondary errors (7.1), a diagnostic only.

So: a fatal error in an unjoined task vanishes; in a supervisor child it is restarted.

#### Options

| | Rule | Consequence |
| :-- | :-- | :-- |
| A | Lost, like any unjoined error | simplest; an `exit` in a fire-and-forget task silently does nothing |
| B | Every unjoined task error is printed (long form) on stderr when the handle is dropped or at program end; exit status unchanged | visible, no change of control flow |
| C | A **fatal** error of an unjoined task ends the program after the drain (`Exit` with its code) | `exit` from anywhere ends the program in order; drops ADR-343's "only `main` ends the program", which ADR-345 D5 kept |
| D | Supervisors never restart a fatal error (the supervisor fails with it, escalates); `overlap` hands on a fatal loser instead of the first error | consistent with "a catch takes a fatal error only by name" |
| E | Structured: every task belongs to a scope whose end waits for it (see below) | "nobody waits" no longer exists |

#### How other languages do it

Every row is **from memory** (marked (m)): no web access was used in this round. The well-known core (Go's crash, Python's GC message, C#'s event, Java's `Future`) is stable across versions; details of logging and defaults are where memory may be wrong, and should be checked before an ADR quotes them.

| Language / API | Error or panic of a task nobody waits for | Child under a supervisor / scope | "End the program" from inside the task |
| :-- | :-- | :-- | :-- |
| Rust `std::thread::spawn` (m) | the panic hook prints the panic on stderr when it happens; dropping the `JoinHandle` detaches, the `Err` is lost; a returned `Result` is lost silently | `std::thread::scope` joins every thread at its end and re-panics if one panicked | `std::process::exit` ends the process at once from any thread, no destructors |
| Rust `tokio::spawn` (m) | panic printed by the panic hook; the `JoinError` sits in the dropped handle and is lost; an `Err` return is lost silently | `JoinSet`: results collected with `join_next`; dropping the set aborts its tasks; no restart | `process::exit` ends at once; the runtime is not shut down |
| Go goroutine (m) | an unrecovered panic in **any** goroutine ends the whole program (exit 2, stack trace); an `error` return has nowhere to go and is lost | no supervisor; `errgroup` returns the first error to `Wait` and cancels the context | `os.Exit` ends at once from any goroutine; deferred calls do not run |
| Erlang/OTP (m) | an unlinked process that crashes is logged (crash report), nothing else happens | links propagate abnormal exits, monitors deliver `'DOWN'`; a supervisor restarts by strategy (`one_for_one`, `one_for_all`, `rest_for_one`) up to an intensity, then dies and escalates; reason `normal` does not kill linked processes, an abnormal one does | `exit(Reason)` ends only that process and signals its links; `init:stop()`/`halt()` end the node |
| Java threads (m) | `Thread.UncaughtExceptionHandler` (default: stack trace on stderr), the thread dies, the program goes on | `ExecutorService.submit` keeps the exception in the `Future` until `get()`: swallowed if nobody calls it (`execute` goes to the uncaught handler); `StructuredTaskScope` (preview) joins and can shut down on failure | `System.exit` from any thread ends the JVM (shutdown hooks run) |
| Kotlin coroutines (m) | structured: a failing `launch` fails its parent, which cancels the siblings; `async` keeps it until `await` but also fails the parent; in `GlobalScope` it goes to the `CoroutineExceptionHandler` / thread's uncaught handler | `SupervisorJob` / `supervisorScope`: a child's failure cancels neither siblings nor parent, the handler sees it; no restart built in | `exitProcess` = `System.exit`, from anywhere |
| Swift structured concurrency (m) | `async let` and task groups: the scope waits, the error comes out at `await`; an unstructured `Task { }` whose value nobody reads loses its error silently | a throwing task group cancels the rest when the body rethrows | `exit()` ends the process at once |
| Python asyncio (m) | the exception stays in the `Task`; when it is garbage-collected unread, "Task exception was never retrieved" is logged on stderr; `SystemExit`/`KeyboardInterrupt` in a task are re-raised out of the loop and end `asyncio.run` | `TaskGroup` (3.11): the first failure cancels the others, all failures raised as an `ExceptionGroup` | `sys.exit` = `SystemExit`, see left; `os._exit` ends at once |
| Python Trio (m) | no unowned tasks: every task starts in a nursery | the nursery's block waits for all children; a failure cancels the siblings and is raised as an exception group | `SystemExit` travels like any exception, through the nursery |
| C# / .NET `Task` (m) | kept in the `Task`; if never observed, `TaskScheduler.UnobservedTaskException` fires when the task is finalized; since .NET 4.5 the process goes on (4.0 ended it) | `Task.WhenAll` gathers an `AggregateException`; no supervisor | `Environment.Exit` from any thread |

**What the table shows.** Three families:
1. **Loss with a late trace** (Rust, Java `Future`, asyncio, C#, Swift `Task {}`): the failure is kept in the handle and lost or logged when it dies. Each of them later added a structured form (`thread::scope`/`JoinSet`, `StructuredTaskScope`, `TaskGroup`, task groups) because the unowned form loses failures.
2. **Crash** (Go for panics, Erlang for linked processes): an unhandled failure ends the program or the linked group. Visible, but one task takes everything down.
3. **Structured** (Kotlin, Trio, Swift groups, `TaskGroup`): a task always has an owner that waits, so "nobody waits" does not arise.

For "end the program": everyone except Erlang lets any thread end the process, at once and mostly without cleanup. Erlang's `exit` ends only the process, which is the choice ADR-345 D5 made.

#### The options in detail

**A — Lost.** *Pro:* simplest; only the lowering fix to build; no output nobody asked for. *Con:* `exit` in a fire-and-forget task does nothing and nobody notices; contradicts what ADR-345 exists for. *Who:* Rust (`Result` return), Swift `Task {}`, Go for an `error` value. *Worst case:* **silent loss** of a fatal error: status 0 from a program that meant to exit 1.

**B — Printed on stderr when the handle is dropped or at program end, status unchanged.** *Pro:* visible, no control flow changes; the long-standing practice of asyncio, C#, Java's default handler, Erlang's crash report. *Con:* the moment of the message depends on when the handle dies (asyncio's GC message is known to come late or never); a program that knowingly ignores failures gets noise and needs a way to say so (`detach`, or `catch` in the task); the status still says success. *Worst case:* **noise on stderr**, and a fatal `exit` that is shown but not obeyed: status 0 after a printed `Exit(1)`.

**C — A fatal error of an unjoined task ends the program after the drain.** *Pro:* `exit` means exit everywhere, as in Go, Rust, Java, C#; the status is right. *Con:* any task, a library's included, can end the program, which ADR-343 and ADR-345 D5 refused on purpose; the end comes at a moment `main` did not choose. *Who:* Go/Java/Rust/C# (immediately, no drain), asyncio (`SystemExit` out of the loop). *Worst case:* **unexpected program end** in the middle of `main`'s work.

**D — Supervisors never restart a fatal error; `overlap` hands on a fatal loser.** *Pro:* consistent with ADR-345 D2 (a fatal error is taken only by name); Erlang's supervisor likewise does not restart forever (intensity). *Con:* a supervisor tree whose child calls `exit` ends; whoever wants a restart catches `Exit` by name in the child. *Worst case:* a supervised service ends because one child exited, which is what that child asked for. Independent of A, B, C and E; covers the owned cases only.

**E — Structured: every task belongs to a scope, and the scope's end waits for it.** A bare `spawn` goes into an enclosing scope (Kotlin's `coroutineScope`, Trio's nursery, Swift's task group); `main` is a scope; a task's failure goes to the scope, which cancels the siblings and throws at its end, fatal included. *Pro:* no lost error, no stray program end, no noise; the error arrives at a place written in the source; where Rust, Java, Python and Swift all moved later. *Con:* a language change: fire-and-forget (a background logger, a per-connection task) needs an explicit long-lived scope passed down, as Trio's `nursery` parameter; `spawn`'s move and crossing rules (8.3, Part II 11.2) must be re-read; existing `spawn` programs change meaning. *Worst case:* **a program that waits** at a scope end for a task that never finishes: a hang, visible, not a wrong value.

**How close Nikaia is to E today (Part I 8, Part II 12.7).** Closer than it looks, not there:
- `overlap { … }` (8.1.2) is already structured: it waits for all branches, the first failure in written order wins, the others go along as `secondary`. Only the fatal loser (D) is open.
- `task::scope fn(s) { s.spawn ... }` (Part II 12.7) is a nursery: it waits for every task, a panic cancels the others and the scope throws `task::Crashed`. It does not say what happens to a task's **error** (as against a panic); it should hand it on as `overlap` does.
- `spawn` (8.2) is unstructured: "a background task may keep running after the function that started it has finished" (8.3) is exactly the sentence E removes. The checker already acts as if the spawner owned the failure (`NK2605` counts it against the enclosing function): that is E's rule, not today's specification.

So E means making the `task::scope` rule the only one and giving `main` an implicit scope that a bare `spawn` joins. That changes 8.2/8.3 and needs its own decision; it needs no new machinery.

#### Recommendation (revised after the comparison)

**E as the direction, D now, and for a bare `spawn` a drain at `main`'s end.**
1. **Now:** D, and `task::scope` hands on a task's error as `overlap` does (first in order wins, the rest as `secondary`).
2. **Now, for a bare `spawn`:** at `main`'s end the drain joins every unjoined task. The first error (fatal ones first) is printed in long form; if it is fatal, `main` ends with its code; a non-fatal one is printed and the status stays. This is C limited to the moment `main` already ends: `main` keeps the *when*, so ADR-345 D5's reason holds where it matters, nothing ends `main` in the middle. A dropped handle no longer loses anything.
3. **Next, as its own decision:** whether a bare `spawn` becomes a `spawn` into `main`'s scope (full E), which turns step 2 into the ordinary scope rule.

Why not B+D as before: B leaves the fatal case wrong (the program prints `Exit(1)` and returns 0), which in the table only asyncio's GC message tolerates, and Python replaced it with `TaskGroup`. Why not C: everyone who has it (Go, Java, Rust) ends the program at an arbitrary point, which ADR-343 refused. **Risk of the recommendation:** step 2 makes `main`'s end wait for every still-running unjoined task (a hang where today the program ends and kills them); a long-running background task then needs an explicit cancel or `detach`. A visible cost, the one Trio and Kotlin accepted. Against it: a server whose `main` never returns gets nothing from step 2; for it only E (or B's print at drop) helps.

Whatever is decided, the lowering defect above has to be fixed; it blocks the run-time test "exit in spawn, the joiner gets `Exit`" of #606 (only the checker half is tested there).

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-13](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/605#issuecomment-6157448644):

#### Decision (owner, 2026-10-13)

**Structured concurrency as the direction: every task error has a receiver.** Recorded in [ADR-346](../blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-346.md) (D1-D7); the rule is in Part I 8.2, Part II 12.7 (scope, supervisor), Part III A.2 ("How the program ends"), 17.1 (`std::task`), the C ABI code table (`task::TimedOut` = `1101`) and C.3 (`NK2109`). Version 0.0.889 (commit 782d043).

In short:
1. A `spawn` whose result is not bound is detached (D2).
2. A bound handle leaving its scope on some path without `join()`, `join(within: d)`, `detach()` or `cancel()`, and not handed on (returned, stored, passed), is `NK2109`; the message names `join()` and `detach()` (D3). Taken in one branch only = not taken.
3. `h.join(within: 5.seconds())` (an option without a positional zone, Part I 6): on time-out the task is cancelled like `cancel()` and `join` throws `task::TimedOut` (D4).
4. `join` throws what the body throws, plus `task::Crashed` (D5); a fatal one reaches `main` and sets the status.
5. Detached: the error is printed in long form on stderr; a fatal one also replaces the exit status at `main`'s end (first one wins); drain/`cleanup-deadline` unchanged (D6).
6. Supervisors do not restart a fatal error; `task::scope` hands errors on like `overlap` (D7).

Rejected: A, B, C, forbidding a bare `spawn`, exiting at once from a detached task (ADR-346 §3).

#### To build
1. **(a) Body errors to `join`.** The ledger carries the body's error set on `TaskHandle[T]`; `join`'s throws = body's + `task::Crashed`; the body lowers with `?` into a `Result` the slot holds; `join` flattens it. Tests: `h.join() catch { match error { E::X => … } }` over a failing body runs; `NK2605` asks the *joiner* (not the spawner) for `throws`; turn on "exit in spawn, the joiner gets Exit" in `crates/nikaia/tests/process_exit.rs`.
2. **(b) `join(within:)`** with `task::TimedOut` (contract, std type, runtime timer + cancel). Tests: a task sleeping longer than `within` → `TimedOut` and its cleanup runs; a fast task → its value.
3. **(c) `detach()`** (contract + `TaskHandle::detach`). Test: compiles and runs, task completes during drain.
4. **(d) `NK2109`**: path-sensitive check; tests for: never used, used in one branch only, `return h`, stored in a struct/list, passed to a function, `cancel()`. Adapt existing programs/tests that bind an unjoined handle (grep `let .* = spawn` in `crates/nikaia/tests`, `examples/`, `crates/nikaia-std/src/tools/*.nika`, `crates/rs2nika`).
5. **(e) Detached errors**: runtime records a detached task's error, prints it long form; a fatal one's code replaces the status after `main`, before the drain; first one wins. Tests: detached `exit(3)` → status 3 and main ran to its end; detached non-fatal error → stderr line, status 0.
6. **(f) Supervisor/fatal and `task::scope`**: a fatal child error escalates without asking the policy; `task::scope` throws the first task error in start order with the rest as secondary. Tests for both.

The last commit of this list says `Fixes #605`.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-14](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/605#issuecomment-6160199859):

Built in 32d4c23 (0.0.906): every `impl Fatal for T` type now counts as fatal in tasks, not just `Exit`. A detached task's fatal error sets status 1 at `main`'s end, a box learns a type is fatal through a registry `main` fills, and a supervisor does not restart a fatal child. In that case `supervisor::run` throws the new fatal `supervisor::Handed`, which a plain catch does not take.

Still missing:
1. `task::scope` hands body errors on (ADR-346 D7). `Scope::spawn` takes `fn()`, so a lambda that can fail is refused with NK2606. To build it: change the contract to `fn() throws`, and give the four runtime scopes (`Scope`, `PoolScope`, `PausedScope`, `PausedPoolScope`) an error list where the first error in start order wins and the others become secondaries. Then decide which channel `scope` returns in: a `fn() throws` lambda fails in the box, and `?` from the box into a typed sum does not compile. Tests: a typed catch over `task::scope` at both parallelism settings.
2. (g) On the pool, `BoxedTask`/`Carried` keep only the message, the Exit code and the fatal code, so a typed catch at `join` still sees only `Exit`. To fix it, require `Send` of the body's errors and carry them as `Box<dyn Error + Send>`.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-14](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/605#issuecomment-6160871798):

Status after 0.0.909 (7dbc7af), steps A and B done:

* **A**: a task on the pool now hands its body's error to the joiner as the error itself, so a typed `catch` arm at `join` takes it. `main` registers every one of the program's own error types through `task::Cross`, an autoref probe that registers only `Send` types. `Carried` keeps the error as `Box<dyn Error + Send>`, and `holds`/`opened` find it. A type that cannot cross threads still crosses as its message, status and fatality. This adds no new visible rule and needs no NK code. Test: `process_exit::a_typed_error_crosses_the_pool`.
* **B**: `supervisor::Handed` stays, because `run`'s channel is typed, but it now carries the child's fatal error. A catch arm that names the child's own fatal type takes it (`error::handed`), and the hand-over passes on only when no such arm matches. ADR-346 D7 and Part II 12.8 are updated. One limit: this works where `run`'s errors arrive in a function's channel. A `catch` written directly on `supervisor::run(..)` still hands only `Escalated` to the handler.

**C is still open** (`task::scope` handing body errors on). What I tried: changing the contracts to `body: fn() throws` (Scope::spawn) and `fn(ref task::Scope) throws -> T` (task::scope).
1. The checker then refuses `task::scope fn(s) {..}` with NK1245, "can't tell what type `task::scope(…)` is". Lambda type inference does not handle `throws -> T`.
2. The emitter does not write a lambda passed to a `throws` parameter in the caller's channel. For example, `each(fn { work(7) })` lowers to `async || { work(7) }`, which returns `Thrown<Bad>` while the parameter expects `Box<dyn Error>`.

To build:
1. Fix (1) in check.
2. Write the spawned lambda as `|| -> Result<(), CHANNEL> { ..; Ok(()) }` in the scope function's channel.
3. Make the four scope runtimes generic over E, keeping `(start index, E)` in each scope. The first error in start order is thrown, and the rest join it as secondary errors (`error::Joined`). On the pool with the box channel, errors cross as `Carried` (A).
4. Add a test: a scope with three tasks, two of which fail. A typed arm takes the first started, at both `user_parallelism` settings.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-14](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/605#issuecomment-6161941568):

Step C landed in c463fed (0.0.915). `task::scope` now passes its tasks' errors on at both settings and in all four runtimes: the first error in start order is thrown and the later ones are joined to it. A `catch` on the scope opens the box by type, and a fatal error passes a plain `catch`.

Still missing before this can close:
1. **E0373.** When a `catch` sits directly on `task::scope(..)`, the scope's channel is `Box<dyn Error>`. In that case a failing task that borrows a local of the function is refused by rustc: the borrow must be `'static`. The cause has not been found yet. Without a catch on the scope, the typed channel borrows fine. To reproduce: `let data = [1, 7]` + `task::scope fn(s) { s.spawn fn { work(data[1]) } 5 } catch {..}`.
2. **Sums drop later errors.** For a sum channel, `Joined` keeps only the first error. The later errors are dropped, not joined.
3. **Site lost.** A fatal error that passes a scope `catch` into a typed channel loses its raise site.
4. **The scope function's own body cannot throw yet.** Its contract has no `throws`; that is NK1245, still open.
5. **Supervisor catch.** A `catch` written directly on `supervisor::run` still hands only `Escalated` to its handler. Not looked at in this round.

7400ac5 (0.0.917) did items 1-3 of the open list:
1. A `catch` placed directly on `task::scope` now lets a task borrow a local. The scope's channel is now `task::Boxed`, a newtype with no lifetime of its own. With `Box<dyn Error>`, rustc inferred the object lifetime inside the function, so the `for<'scope>` closure required every borrow to be `'static` (E0373).
2. A sum channel now joins a later failure of the **same** member.
3. A fatal error that passes a scope catch into a typed channel keeps the site it was raised at (`error::reopened`). On the pool, `Carried` now carries the site across.

- (4) A scope body that throws itself is refused with NK2606 / NK1245. The contract needs `fn(ref task::Scope) throws -> T`, the checker has to infer `T`, and the emitter has to flatten `Result<Result<T,E>,E>`.

Opening the box by type works, but the `else => throw error` and fallback arms then need the child's type in the function's channel. That breaks when the channel is the `{Escalated, Handed}` sum. **Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-14](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/605#issuecomment-6162982007):

#### Decision basis: a `catch` written directly on `supervisor::run(..)` (item 5)

**What it is.** `supervisor::run([...])` runs children until it gives up. A child that ends with a *fatal* error is not restarted: `run` throws `supervisor::Handed`, which carries the child's own error (ADR-346 D7, Part II 12.8). The spec says: *"a `catch` arm that names the child's own error type takes it there"*.

```nika
enum Doom { Over(i64) }
impl Fatal for Doom {}
impl Error for Doom { fn message(ref self) -> String { return "doom" } }

fn main() throws {
    supervisor::run([supervisor::child(fn { work() })]) catch {
        match error {
            Doom::Over(n) => println(f"over {n}"),   // (a) an arm naming the child's type
            supervisor::Escalated => …,
            else => throw error,                     // (b) a fallback that passes it on
        }
    }
}
```

**Where it comes from.** #605's open list (item 5), found while building D7 (7400ac5: "tried, reverted"). Nothing in the repository needs it today: no program, test or example writes a catch directly on `run`; it is the spec's promise.

**What is settled, what is not.** D7 and 12.8 settle (a): an arm naming the child's type takes it. They do **not** say what `error` *is* in such a catch, and so not what (b) `throw error`, an `else` arm, or `{error}` mean. Today `error` is typed as `run`'s channel, `Escalated | Handed`; an arm `Doom::Over(n)` compiles, but making the generated code open the box means `throw error` / fallback arms would put a `Doom` back into a channel that only allows `Escalated` and `Handed` - where the previous attempt broke (no `Doom` member in the function's channel).

**Options.**
1. **`error` widens to `Escalated | Handed | <every fatal type the children declare>`.** The checker reads the children's lambdas' error sets, keeps the fatal ones, and the catch's sum includes them; `throw error` of a child's type needs the enclosing function's channel to take that type. + (a) and (b) mean what they read as; exhaustiveness is honest. − The children's types leak into the caller's channel; new checker inference across `supervisor::child` lambdas; a child list built elsewhere (`Vec[Child]` passed in) has no visible types, so it works only where the list is literal.
2. **`error` stays `Escalated | Handed`; a child-type arm is `Handed` plus opening it.** `throw error` in any arm rethrows the `Handed`. + No channel widening; works for lists built elsewhere. − `Doom::Over(n) => throw error` throws a `Handed`, not a `Doom`: the arm's pattern type and `error`'s type differ.
3. **Refuse a child-type arm directly on `run`** (new code); the program matches `Handed` and opens it explicitly. + Simplest. − Contradicts 12.8's sentence; the spec changes.

**Risk if wrong.** Option 2 wrong: a rethrown fatal error arrives upstream as `Handed`; the exit status is the same (Handed ends the program as the child's error would), so the harm is a different type at a further `catch`, not a wrong process outcome. Option 1 wrong: a caller's channel gains types it did not expect (a refusal, not a wrong value).

**Recommendation:** Option 2 (keeps 12.8 true, no checker inference, exit behaviour already right), with one added sentence in 12.8: *in a catch on `run`, an arm naming a child's type binds that error; `error` and `throw error` stay `run`'s own (`Handed`)*. Option 1 only if a program needs to rethrow the child's type itself.

Status: this item needs the owner (label `decision`); items 2b and 4 are built.

_Generated by [Claude Code](https://claude.ai/code)_

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-14](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/605#issuecomment-6163821385):

#### Item 5: how others do it (addendum to the decision basis above)

What does the code around a supervisor see when a child fails in a way the supervisor will not handle?

| System | What the caller receives | The child's own error |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| Erlang/OTP supervisor | the supervisor exits with reason `shutdown` once its restart limit is reached | logged by the supervisor, not handed to the caller |
| Akka (classic) `Escalate` | the parent's strategy receives the child's exception itself | yes, the original exception |
| Kotlin `supervisorScope` | nothing: a child's failure does not cancel the scope; it goes to a `CoroutineExceptionHandler` | only through the handler |
| Swift `withThrowingTaskGroup` | the group rethrows the first child error itself | yes, the original error |
| Java `StructuredTaskScope.ShutdownOnFailure` | `throwIfFailed()` throws an `ExecutionException` wrapping the child's exception | wrapped; `getCause()` opens it |

So two families: **hand on the original** (Akka, Swift: option 1's reading of `error`) and **wrap it** (Java's `ExecutionException`, which is exactly option 2: the arm can open the wrapper, but rethrowing rethrows the wrapper). Erlang and Kotlin hand nothing on, which ADR-346 already rules out (every task error has a receiver).

Option 2 is Java's model; option 1 is Swift's, which works there because Swift's `throws` is untyped, so widening a channel costs nothing. Nikaia's channels are typed, which is the cost option 1 names.

### <a id="issue-520"></a>[#520](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/520) Build ADR-296 D25: `pub grammar` - a package offers its grammar's entries

`blocked` · opened 2026-10-07 · updated 2026-10-07 · 1 comments

Split out of #485, which waits on it. There was no issue for it.

**What is decided (ADR-296 D25).** `pub` belongs to the grammar. `pub grammar G` offers every `entry rule` of `G` to other packages, the way a `pub enum` offers its variants. A rule is never `pub` on its own: `pub rule` is refused, and the message names both words. No entry is distinguished.

**Today.** The parser refuses `pub grammar` with *decided and not built* (`parser/mod.rs`, around line 2398). So no package can offer a grammar, and `dsl pkg::Sql { … } eod` (ADR-299 D20, #485) has nothing to resolve to.

**To build**
1. **Parser:** accept `pub grammar`, and refuse `pub rule` with both words named (if that is not done already).
2. **Ledger:** a `pub grammar`'s entries are recorded as the package's entries (`G::rule`, as a grammar call is keyed, ADR-296 D24), with their signatures. So a consumer resolves `pkg::G::rule(…)`, and a `dsl pkg::G { … }`.
3. **Lowering:** the package's crate exports the grammar's entry functions, and a consumer's call reaches them.
4. **#485's remaining steps:** `dsl pkg::G` resolves to the `pub grammar`. `NK1228`'s help lists the package's `pub grammar`s. A package with two grammars has each reachable by its own path.

Tests: a path package declares `pub grammar Num { entry rule n -> i64 = … }`, and a consumer calls `pkg::Num::n("42")` and prints `42`. A non-`pub` grammar is `NK1110` (private). `pub rule` is refused.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-07](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/520#issuecomment-6035079677):

**Steps 1-3 built in 0.0.618 (67a67a4).**
- **Parser:** `pub grammar` parses. `GrammarDef` carries `public` (`ast.nika`). `pub rule` stays refused, with both words named, as before.
- **Ledger:** each entry's `public` is the grammar's. Before, it was always `true`.
- **Lowering:** a consumer's `lib::Nums::number(input)` becomes `nikaia_std::grammar::parse(&*input, lib::Nums::parse_number())?`, as an entry of its own file's grammar is. The emitter recognises the entry by what the package's ledger records for every `entry rule`: one input, `input`, and `ParseError` as its one failure.
- **Test:** `grammar_lowering.rs`, `a_pub_grammar_is_entered_from_another_package`. Two packages: the consumer prints `42`, and calling the package's non-`pub` grammar is `NK1110`. The test that expected the *not built* refusal is replaced by it.

**Left:**
1. A **`par_fold` entry** across packages, which needs `parse_…_pieces` and the parallelism. The ledger does not say an entry is a fold, so such a call would be lowered as a sequential parse and refused by rustc. It needs a column, or the dependency's units.
2. **Step 4**, `dsl lib::G { … }`: a `dsl` block over a grammar is not lowered at all yet, its own or a package's. That is #518, so it waits there.

### <a id="issue-505"></a>[#505](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/505) The checker types a `dsl` block's result

`compiler`, `blocked` · opened 2026-10-07 · updated 2026-10-07 · 2 comments

Part of #497 (failing closed is the goal; the gaps are closed first).

**The gap.** The value of a `dsl … { … } eod` block has no type in the checker, and neither have the fields read from it. About 9 root causes were measured in #497:
- `dsl html` in `escaping`, `inventory/page`, `benches/page`, `benches/template`;
- `dsl mysql` in the `sql_statement` fixture, with `bound.id` and `bound.active`.

**What the type is.** The grammar's entry rule declares it (`entry rule … -> T`). For a database block it is the row type the grammar declares (ADR-299 D2; #114 and #477 build the row).

**To build**
1. A `dsl` block's type is its grammar's entry rule result type.
2. Where the grammar declares columns, it is the derived row type, which joins #114.

Tests:
- `return dsl html { … } eod` from a `-> String` function checks;
- the `sql_statement` fixture's `bound.id` is typed;
- re-run #497's measurement.

**Step 1 for `dsl html` built in 0.0.611 (d85751e).** A `dsl html { … } eod` block without `:name` holes is a `String` to the checker (`templates.rs`, `an_html_block_is_text_to_the_checker`). The re-measurement (#497) has no `dsl html` root cause left.

**Still missing:**
1. A `dsl` block for a **grammar** (the program's own, or a package's): its type is the grammar's entry rule result. Not built. Which entry a block uses when a grammar has several is what `dsl.rs` decides, and the checker has to ask the same.
2. The row of a database block (`sql_statement` fixture: `bound.id`, `bound.active`), which joins #114.

### <a id="issue-485"></a>[#485](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/485) Decide: how a `dsl` block names a grammar that comes from a package

`language`, `blocked` · opened 2026-10-06 · updated 2026-10-07 · 5 comments

Moved from `docs/open-decisions.md`, which is being retired: open questions for the owner are now issues labelled `decision`.

**What the feature is.** A `dsl NAME { … } eod` block hands its text to a grammar, which parses it while the program is built. A grammar can come from a package: a database driver brings the SQL grammar ([ADR-299](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-299.md) D1, Part II 10.5).

**Today.** ADR-299 D1 says *`dsl sqlite { … } eod` names the grammar of the `sqlite` package*. That works there because the package and the grammar have the same name. Nothing says what a program writes when they differ, for example a package `nikaia_sql` with a grammar `mysql`, or a package with two grammars. Part I 9.2 says a name from a package is reached through the package's name, but the qualified spelling does not parse (probed on 0.0.541):

```
error: Expected `from` or `{` here, but found `:`.
 4 |     let q = dsl nikaia_sql::mysql {
```

(An earlier version of Part II 10.5 wrote `use nikaia_sql` and then `dsl mysql`; the page now uses `sqlite`, so the example no longer shows the problem, but the rule is still missing.)

**Why it is the owner's.** It is the spelling every program with a DSL from a package writes.

**Options**
1. `dsl package::grammar`, as every other name from a package is written.
2. A package's grammar is named bare, and the package's name is its grammar's name where it has one.
3. `use` brings the grammar's name in, the one exception to 9.2.

**Recommendation: 1.** It is 9.2's rule without an exception, and `dsl sqlite` can remain the short form when package and grammar share a name. Costs if wrong: option 1 wrong means a longer line; option 3 wrong means the exception 9.2 was written to avoid.

**To build after the decision:** the parser accepts the chosen spelling; ADR-299 D1 and Part II 10.5 state it; a test with a package whose grammar has a different name.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-06](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/485#issuecomment-6018423033):

#### Decided (owner): option 1, and no short form

**What was checked first:** the qualified spelling still does not parse today (`error: Expected \`from\` or \`{\` here, but found \`:\`` at `dsl nikaia_sql::mysql {`).

**Options presented:**
- Option 1, with a sub-question on when the short form `dsl sqlite` would still hold: (a) when the package has one grammar, or (b) only when the grammar is named like the package. The recommendation was (b), since (a) breaks a program the day a package adds a second grammar.
- The owner chose option 1 and **no short form at all**.

**Rule:** [ADR-299 D20](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-299.md), Part II 10.5, `NK1228`.
- A grammar from a package is always `package::Grammar`: `dsl sqlite::Sql(schema: APP) { … } eod`.
- A grammar the program declares stays bare: `dsl Json`.
- `dsl sqlite { … }` is `NK1228`, and the help names the package's `pub` grammars.
- The specification's and ADR-299's examples now write `dsl sqlite::Sql`. `Sql` is the example driver's grammar name; a driver chooses its own.

#### To build

1. **Parser:**
   - `dsl` takes a path, `NAME (:: NAME)*`, before the optional build-time arguments, `-> T` and `{`.
   - Remove the `from`-or-`{` expectation's wording for `::`.
2. **Resolution:**
   - A single name resolves to a grammar the program declares.
   - `package::Grammar` resolves to a `pub grammar` of a `use`d package.
   - A single name that is a package is `NK1228`, with a help listing that package's `pub grammar`s (`dsl sqlite::Sql`).
3. **Tests:**
   - A local `grammar Json` used as `dsl Json { … } eod` still works.
   - A path package with `pub grammar Sql` used as `dsl pkg::Sql { … } eod` works.
   - `dsl pkg { … } eod` is `NK1228`, naming `pkg::Sql`.
   - A package with two grammars: each reachable by its own path.
4. **Corpus:**
   - `examples/fortunes.nika`'s `dsl postgres` becomes `dsl postgres::Sql`.
   - Update the sources in `crates/nikaia/tests/refusals.rs` and `stored_views.rs` that write `dsl postgres`.

#### Built as far as today's compiler goes: 0.0.582 (ff43698)

- **Parser:** `dsl` takes `NAME` or `NAME::NAME` before `{`. The AST's `Dsl` now carries `package` (`ast.nika`), and the emitter names the block by its whole path. There are no build-time arguments or `-> T` on a `dsl` block yet; those belong to #115 and #114.
- **`NK1228`:** a bare name that a `use` line brought in, standing where a grammar stands. The message is *`http` is a package, and a `dsl` block names a grammar.*, with help *Write `dsl http::Grammar`, naming the grammar `http` offers.*

### <a id="issue-478"></a>[#478](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/478) ADR-270 D24-D26: the logic layer as a package any package may ask, also at build time

`blocked` · opened 2026-10-06 · updated 2026-10-06 · 1 comments

#### How it came up

While deciding the NULL rule for `dsl … -> T` (#477, ADR-299 D18), the question was how a really good driver finds out that a column cannot be `NULL`. For example:

```sql
WHERE (email IS NOT NULL OR age > 30) AND age <= 30
```

Here `email` is never `NULL`, but only via `age > 30 ∧ age ≤ 30 ⇒ false`. That is linear integer arithmetic with `∧ ∨ ¬`, which `nikaia-logic` answers.

The encoding:
- one `0/1` variable `n_c` per nullable column;
- W(p) for "p is true" and F(p) for "p is false", following SQL's three-valued logic;
- the facts are `W(where)`, and the goal is `n_c = 0`.

**What was checked:**
- ADR-270 D1 already decided that the logic layer and solvers are *a Nikaia package that depends on nothing but `std`*, so that *another language or tool* can feed it.
- Today it is the Rust crate `crates/nikaia-logic`, used only by `prove.rs`. A driver cannot reach it, and as a Rust crate it may not run at build time (ADR-321 D9).

#### Decision (owner)

The three points, in ADR-270 and Part III 14.2:
- **D24** — The logic layer is a package any package may depend on, also while it is built (ADR-321 D2, D8, no exception). Public: terms, queries, answers, `verify`. Not public: solvers, representation, strategy.
- **D25** — A build-time query is not an entry of `nikaia.proofs`. It gets the reference solver's answer under a budget counted in work, the same on every machine. `unknown` proves nothing; in a driver, the column stays nullable.
- **D26** — A package with its own version, not `std::logic`.

**Still open:**
- whether a grammar may call its package's functions and Nikaia dependencies while it runs (ADR-321 §6), which a driver needs first;
- the package's name in `use`.

#### To build

Prerequisite: ADR-270 D8 step 2, the reference solver in Nikaia.

1. **Package boundary:**
   - Mark the public surface: term building (`Arena`, `Term`, `TermId`), `Query`, the answer, `verify`.
   - Keep the solvers (`FourierMotzkin`, the later CDCL(T) core) private to the package.
2. **Budget:**
   - The reference solver takes a budget in units of work and returns `unknown` when it is spent.
   - The answer depends only on the query and the budget. Test: the same query gives byte-identical answers at 1 and N threads.
3. **Build time:**
   - A package that depends on it calls it from a `comptime`.
   - Test: a `comptime` that proves `x > 30 ∧ x ≤ 30 ⇒ false` builds and records nothing in `nikaia.proofs`.
   - Test: the ledger of the logic package derives `sync` and `touches = []` (ADR-321 D8), so `NK1152` does not fire.
4. **Determinism:**
   - Part III 13.5's guarantee holds with the package's version in `nikaia.lock`.
   - Test: `--locked` passes with such a `comptime` and an unchanged lock.
5. **Later, with ADR-321 §6 decided:** the `sqlite` driver (#117) uses it for `WHERE` nullability:
   - W/F encoding as above;
   - `unknown` means nullable;
   - tests for the `OR`/`AND` example and for a `LEFT JOIN`.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-06](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/478#issuecomment-6014184550):

The prerequisite is decided: ADR-321 D13 lets a grammar call its own package's functions and its Nikaia dependencies, so a driver can ask the logic package. Build: #479.

### <a id="issue-477"></a>[#477](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/477) `dsl … -> T { … } eod`: a block's rows are a declared struct, matched by name, strictly, `NULL` only into `T?` (ADR-299 D15-D19)

`blocked` · opened 2026-10-06 · updated 2026-10-06 · 0 comments

#### How it came up

[#114](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/issues/114) asked how a `dsl` block's row type is made. ADR-323 answered how a grammar describes a type, and ADR-299 D12-D14 answered how the compiler-written row's fields are named.

The owner then asked why a program cannot simply say which of its own structs a row is. The compiler-written row is a type of its own, and Nikaia's structs are nominal (tried: a `Person` where a `Contact` is expected is `NK1102`).

#### Decisions (owner, one at a time)

- **Syntax:** `dsl sqlite(schema: APP) -> Contact { … } eod`, the arrow as in `fn`. Each row is a `Contact`. The grammar is not told about `T`.
- **By name only:**
  - Column order is not read.
  - A field of a scalar type matches the flat column of its name.
  - A field of a struct type, named like an alias or a table, matches that table's columns, recursively.
  - A flat field whose name two tables share is refused, with a hint to `AS`, a group field, or the block without `-> T`.
- **Strict by default:**
  - A column without a field and a field without a column are both refused.
  - `extra_columns: true` leaves extra columns unread.
  - `missing_fields: true` makes a missing `T?` field `null`.
  - Both flags belong to the compiler and are never handed to the grammar; a grammar may not declare them.
- **NULL:**
  - A `Maybe(T)` column goes only into a `T?` field.
  - A column that cannot be NULL may go into a `T?` field.
  - A NULL that reaches a field that is not `T?` at run time makes `execute` fail with an error naming the field, the column and its table. A driver's mistake is a named error, never a broken promise.
- **Fields:** a scalar, its `T?`, or a struct for a group.
  - Anything else is refused, `Shared[T]` included. Sharing is [#475](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/issues/475), after the MVP; the refusal can later become a permission.
- **Not decided:**
  - a column of one number type into a field of another (until then: the column's own type);
  - the name of the run-time error's type (`std::db`'s).

Records:
- [ADR-299](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-299.md) D15-D19;
- Part II 10.5;
- Part III codes `NK1218`-`NK1223`.

#### To build

Prerequisite: `meta::column` and the compiler-written row (ADR-299 D2, ADR-323), which are not built yet.

1. **Parser:** `-> Type` after a `dsl` block's build-time arguments and before `{`. Without arguments: `dsl sqlite -> T { … } eod`.
2. **Checker, the target:**
   - `T` must be a struct the program declares; else `NK1218`.
   - Each field's type must be a scalar of ADR-323 D1, its `T?`, or a struct (a group); else `NK1218`, also for `Shared[T]`.
3. **Checker, matching** (from the grammar's declared columns, which carry their table or alias):
   - A scalar field matches the flat column of its name. A name two tables' columns share is `NK1222`, with help: `AS`, a group field, no `-> T`.
   - A struct field named like an alias or table matches that table's columns, recursively, with the same rules.
   - A column with no field is `NK1219`, unless `extra_columns: true`.
   - A field with no column is `NK1220`, unless `missing_fields: true` *and* the field is `T?`.
   - A `Maybe(T)` column into a non-`?` field, or a type that is not the field's, is `NK1221`, with help: `T?`, `COALESCE(…)`, `WHERE … IS NOT NULL`.
4. **Build-time arguments:**
   - `extra_columns` and `missing_fields` (both `bool`, default `false`) are taken by the compiler from a block with `-> T` and not passed to the grammar.
   - A grammar that declares either is `NK1223`.
5. **Conversion (D8):**
   - Writes the `T` directly.
   - A `NULL` for a non-`?` field fails `execute` with an error naming the field, the column and the table.
   - A missing `T?` field under `missing_fields` is `null`.
6. **Tests** (with a stub grammar that declares fixed columns, so no SQL is needed):
   - `-> Contact` with `email, name` in reverse order builds, and the rows are `Contact` (pass one to `fn send(c: Contact)`).
   - Each of `NK1218`, `NK1219`, `NK1220`, `NK1221`, `NK1222`, `NK1223` once, with its help.
   - `extra_columns: true` builds past an extra column.
   - `missing_fields: true` with a `T?` field gives `null`; with a non-`?` field it is still `NK1220`.
   - A group field `a: Account` beside `b: Bank` with `a.id` and `b.id` builds without `AS`.
   - At run time, a stub driver returning `NULL` for a `NOT NULL`-declared column: `execute` fails with the named error and does not crash.
   - Without `-> T`, `extra_columns: true` is refused by the grammar as an unknown argument (ADR-299 D3).

### <a id="issue-452"></a>[#452](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/452) A mapped file cannot be used as text: three spellings pass the checker and fail in rustc

`libraries`, `compiler`, `blocked` · opened 2026-10-05 · updated 2026-10-06 · 2 comments

#### What happens

`fs::map` returns an `fs::Mapped`. A grammar entry takes one (`Measurements::file(data)` in `examples/1brc.nika`), but code that wants the text itself has no spelling that compiles. Each of these three passes the checker, and the build then fails in `rustc`, about generated code (Part III C.1):

```nika
use std::fs

fn main() throws {
    let data = fs::map("m.txt", fs::Root::Anywhere) catch { return }
    let a = data[0..<7]              // rustc: the trait bound `Mapped: Get<_>` is not satisfied
    let b = data.deref()             // rustc: no method named `deref` found for struct `Mapped`
    let c: ref String = data         // rustc: mismatched types
}
```

The ledger has `fs::Mapped::deref`. The emitted call needs `std::ops::Deref` in scope, or the method written as `&*data`.

#### Found while

Hand-tuning `examples/1brc.nika`: a byte-level parser over the mapping had to fall back to `fs::read_to_string`, which copies the file.

#### To build

1. Make a mapped file usable as a `ref String` wherever a view of text is wanted. Either write the deref, or refuse in the language's words with a way out. Today it is neither.
2. Make `data[a..<b]` on a `Mapped` a slice of its text, as it is on a `String`.
3. Add tests: each of the three lines above compiles and reads the mapping, with the same output as `fs::read_to_string`.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-05](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/452#issuecomment-5991999871):

Two more of the same kind, found while preparing #453. Both pass the checker and fail in `rustc`:

```nika
let raw = fs::read("m.txt", fs::Root::Anywhere)
let first = raw[0]                 // rustc: the trait bound `Bytes: Get<{integer}>` is not satisfied

let s = "Hamburg;12.3"
let at = s.find(";") ?? 0          // reaches Rust's `str::find`, which no ledger describes
let n: i64 = at                    // rustc: mismatched types (`usize`, not `i64`, ADR-285 D1)
```

`str::find` is not in `std.contracts`, so the call fails open into Rust's method with Rust's types. What `find` and `Bytes`' content access should be is #453's decision. Until that decision, both lines should be refused in the language's words: `NK1210`'s rule for a method of a number `std` does not describe, applied to text and `Bytes` too.

---
_Generated by [Claude Code](https://claude.ai/code)_

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-05](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/452#issuecomment-5999161156):

**The three items of "To build" are built** in 0.0.512 (c8ff4a7). Each of the three lines now compiles and reads the mapping, with the same output as `fs::read_to_string`.

- **`data[a..<b]`**: `index::Get` for `fs::Mapped` slices its text, by the same rule as a `String`'s.
- **`data.deref()`**: `Mapped` now has `deref` as a method of the type itself. The lowered call no longer needs `std::ops::Deref` in scope.
- **`let c: ref String = data`**: a `let` that declares a view a container derefs to now lends the container (`lent_lets`, written `&data`). Before, it was handed over, so `data` was gone after the line (`NK2105`) and `rustc` refused the line. Handing `data` to a `ref String` parameter already worked.

**Test:** `defects_found_running::a_mapped_file_is_read_as_text`. Every bench and example lowers byte for byte as before.

**Still open, from the comment above:** `raw[0]` on `Bytes`, and text's `find`, which no ledger describes.

The comment proposes refusing both with `NK1210`'s rule, extended from numbers to text and `Bytes`, until #453 decides. That would refuse every text or `Bytes` method `std` does not describe. Programs that use one today and reach `rustc` would become refusals in the checker.

**Question for the deciding session:** should `NK1210` be extended this way now, or should this wait for #453's decision on `find` and `Bytes`' content? Until it is answered, I'm leaving this issue open and building nothing further here.

### <a id="issue-276"></a>[#276](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/276) WebAssembly: where a parser's hash keys come from (`getrandom` on wasm32)

`targets`, `blocked` · opened 2026-10-01 · updated 2026-10-08 · 2 comments

#### What

Since #275, every grammar's interner hashes with `ahash`, keyed at runtime through `getrandom` 0.3. On `wasm32-unknown-unknown`, `getrandom` 0.3 does not compile until the final application picks a backend:

```
error: The wasm32-unknown-unknown targets are not supported by default; you may need to enable the "wasm_js" configuration flag.
```

This is a **second** blocker, not the first one. `nikaia-std` does not build for that target today with or without `ahash`, because `polling` refuses it (`polling does not support this target OS`). Reproduction:

```
rustup target add wasm32-unknown-unknown
cargo check -p nikaia-std --release --target wasm32-unknown-unknown
```

#### Why the choice matters

A parser built with Nikaia interns names from its input. If the hash keys are known in advance, an input can be built in which the names collide. Each lookup then probes linearly, and the parse becomes quadratic in the number of names (HashDoS).

| Seed source | Keys | Fit |
|---|---|---|
| `getrandom` with the `wasm_js` backend (`crypto.getRandomValues`) | random per instance | **yes**, wherever there is a JS host |
| `getrandom` on WASI (`wasm32-wasip*`) | random per process | yes |
| `ahash-compile-time-rng` | baked into the `.wasm`, readable by anyone who has it | only where there is no entropy at all, and said so |
| std's `RandomState` on `wasm32-unknown-unknown` | the addresses of a stack and a heap slot (`sys/random/unsupported.rs`: *"isn't particularly secure"*), close to fixed in a wasm address space | no |

#### Work

When #111 adds the target:
- the build selects `getrandom`'s backend for wasm (`wasm_js`, or the WASI one), so keys stay runtime-random;
- `ahash-compile-time-rng` is used only for a target with no entropy source, and that build says so where the user sees it.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-08](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/276#issuecomment-6064239544):

I measured how far `nikaia-std` is from `wasm32-unknown-unknown`. Command: `cargo check -p nikaia-std --release --target wasm32-unknown-unknown`, at 0.0.698.

1. **`getrandom` 0.3** is the first stop, as this issue says. It is reached only through `winnow-grammar` → `ahash` (`cargo tree -i getrandom`). With `getrandom = { features = ["wasm_js"] }` as a wasm32 dependency and `--cfg getrandom_backend="wasm_js"`, it compiles.
   - But the `wasm_js` backend goes through `wasm-bindgen`. A plain `.js` loader (D22, no wasm-bindgen glue) could not instantiate that module.
   - The option that fits D22: `--cfg getrandom_backend="custom"`. `std` defines `__getrandom_v03_custom` over one import, for example `env.nikaia_random(ptr, len)`. The generated `.js` provides that import with `crypto.getRandomValues` on the module's memory. The keys stay random per instance, and no wasm-bindgen is needed.
2. **`polling`** is next: "polling does not support this target OS". It comes in only through `crates/unsafe/polled`.
3. With `polled` made a non-wasm dependency, **five errors** are left, all at name resolution:
   - `rt/readiness.rs`: `std::os::fd` and `polled`;
   - `rt/mod.rs:1143` and `:1174`: `io::wait` and `io::waiting` take `AsFd`;
   - `net.rs:269`.

   Type checking does not run past those, so there may be more behind them.

**What is behind the compile errors** is the actual work of #111. `rt::start` spawns the I/O worker and the poller thread, and `wasm32-unknown-unknown` has no threads, so a spawn traps. D24 and D25 say the executor is driven from the host's event loop, "through the mechanism the target already uses for its I/O worker". That mechanism does not exist yet. It needs a decision basis before it is built:
- an exported `<prefix>_poll` that the `.js` calls from the event loop, or an import the module calls to ask for a wake-up;
- how `time::sleep` reaches `setTimeout`;
- what the OPFS-backed `fs` of Part III 17.1 does without a worker thread.

A sync-only first step is possible without it: `.wasm`, `.js` and `.d.ts` for entry points that do not pause, with a pausing entry point refused on this target until the bridge exists. That still needs `rt::start` to start no threads on wasm32.

---
_Generated by [Claude Code](https://claude.ai/code)_

### <a id="issue-112"></a>[#112](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/112) A binding is a generated file over the C library (`nikaia bind python` / `js`)

`targets`, `blocked` · opened 2026-09-30 · updated 2026-10-08 · 1 comments

Record: [ADR-131](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/main/docs/specification/adr/adr-131.md), all of it. `nikaia bind python` writes a `ctypes` binding from the ledger (exceptions per variant, `str` and `bytes` for buffers, classes with `close()` for handles, `IntEnum`, `None`, generators for streams, an awaitable for `_async`); `nikaia bind js` is the WebAssembly build's `.js`. No second artifact, no native Node add-on: a Nikaia library does not ship twice.

**Order (§5):** the Python generator over the example library; the streamed and async forms; the `js` name; a test that imports the binding.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-08](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/112#issuecomment-6061634227):

**`nikaia bind python` is built** (0.0.684, `2a22b7c`), against ADR-284 D26, D27 and D29.

`nikaia bind python` builds the library and writes `target/nikaia/c-library/<package>/__init__.py`, a `ctypes` module over `lib<package>.so` beside it. It is generated from the same reading of the package as the header, and carries the same ledger hash.
- **Statuses** become exceptions: `Error` with a subclass per thrown variant, and `BoundaryError` subclasses for the seven negative codes (`ReentrantError`, `PanickedError`, …). Each carries `code` and what `last_error` said.
- **Text and bytes** go in as `str` and `bytes`. Results come back with the size query done by the binding.
- **A handle** is a class: the `extern` constructor is `__init__`, plus its methods, a property per `pub` field, `close()`, a `with` block and `__del__`. A closed handle raises `ArgumentError`.
- **An `extern` struct** is a `ctypes.Structure` (nested ones in order), an **`enum`** an `IntEnum`, and **`null`** is `None`.
- **A callback** is any callable, kept alive for the call. What it raises is raised by the call once the call has returned, since `ctypes` cannot carry an exception through C.
- `init()` and `shutdown()`.
- Only the entry points (D29).

Test: `tests/c_library.rs: python_calls_the_library_through_its_binding` runs a Python script over every kind of entry point in the test library. That includes a callback calling back into a held handle, which raises `ReentrantError`.

**Still open:**
- The `_async` form as an awaitable under a running loop, with `cancel()` as the ticket. Today a pausing function is called in its blocking form.
- A stream as a generator.
- `nikaia bind js`, which is the WebAssembly build's `.js` (D22, D26) and waits on #111. That is why this issue stays `blocked`.
- `nikaia bind node` (D28).

`targets`, `blocked` · opened 2026-09-30 · updated 2026-10-06 · 0 comments

Record: [ADR-129](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/main/docs/specification/adr/adr-129.md), all of it. The `_async` form ends with `<package>_op** op` (or `NULL`); `<package>_cancel` cancels the task at its next pause point with `cleanup` run; `done` is called exactly once, `E_CANCELLED` (`-7`) when the cancellation came first; `<package>_op_free` after `done`. A function producing many results takes `fn(item) -> bool sync`, whose `false` stops it, and the next item is produced only after the callback returned; a returned list of text or handles is refused naming that shape.

**Order (§5):** the ticket, `cancel`, `op_free` and the code; the `bool` callback row and the refusal message; a streamed file and a cancelled fetch in the C example.

### <a id="issue-108"></a>[#108](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/108) A struct crosses the C boundary by value (`pub extern "C" struct`)

`targets`, `blocked` · opened 2026-09-30 · updated 2026-10-06 · 0 comments

Record: [ADR-127](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/main/docs/specification/adr/adr-127.md), all of it. `pub extern "C" struct` has C's layout (declaration order, C padding, `#[repr(C)]` below) and crosses by value, in, out and as a field; a `Vec` of them is an array in and the caller's buffer out, counted in elements; `T?` of one is the `NONE` status. Fields are numbers, `bool`, `char`, payload-free enums and other such structs, every field `pub`; anything else is `NK1145`, naming the handle as the shape. No lock around its methods: it is the caller's memory. The layout is in the ledger, so `--locked` catches a change.

**Order (§5):** the parser; the field check and `NK1145`; the emitter's `repr(C)`, passing, array and buffer; the header's `typedef struct` and the ledger's field record; an example beside the library's. Plus §4's `[f64; 3]` field ([ADR-152](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/main/docs/specification/adr/adr-152.md) step 4): `Array[T, N]` is built, so what is left is a `repr(C)` struct to put one in and the test that it is laid out as C lays it out.

- **The `postgres` block**, a deferred-parameter DSL ([ADR-007](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/main/docs/specification/adr/adr-007.md) D4): the statement has to reach a driver intact. What is left is `examples/http/` declaring `route` (step 4 of [ADR-122](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/main/docs/specification/adr/adr-122.md)).
