### <a id="issue-644"></a>[#644](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/644) Low priority: warn on a list literal of one range, `[0..181]` (NK1281, ADR-362 D5)

`enhancement`, `note` · opened 2026-10-17 · updated 2026-10-17 · 0 comments

**Low priority — decided, not to be built now** (owner, 2026-10-17). Labelled `note` until the owner moves it to `ready`.

#### How it came up

The owner wrote `let safety_buffer = [0..181]` meaning the numbers 0 to 181. It is a list of one element, the range: printing an element is `NK1201` (#638), `safety_buffer[110]` aborts at run time. Rust has the same trap (`vec[0..n]`), and Clippy warns on it (`single_range_in_vec_init`).

#### Decision

A list literal whose **only** element is a range is the warning `NK1281`: *a list of one range*; the help writes `(0..181).collect()` for the numbers and `[0; 182]` for that many equal values (#643). A written type (`let r: Vec[Range[i64]] = [0..181]`) says the list is meant and silences it. Several ranges (`[0..5, 10..15]`) are not warned. Rejected: an error (a list of one range is rare, not wrong); nothing.

* Why: [ADR-362 D5](../blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-362.md). What: Part I 4.5 (lists), Part III's table (`NK1281`).

#### To build (when it is `ready`)

1. Checker: a list literal of exactly one element whose type is a range (`a..b`, `a..<b`, `a..`), with no type written for the binding, argument or field it goes to → `NK1281` warning at the literal, help as above (the `[0; n]` count is the range's length, inclusive or exclusive).
2. Tests: `[0..181]` warns with both helps; `[0..<181]` names `[0; 181]`; `let r: Vec[Range[i64]] = [0..181]` and `[0..5, 10..15]` do not warn; the program still builds.
3. Better after #643, so the `[v; n]` help names something that builds.

[Back to the list](#note)

### <a id="issue-639"></a>[#639](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/639) Reminder: where unrolling a loop for a proof could pay off, in special cases

`note` · opened 2026-10-17 · updated 2026-10-17 · 0 comments

#### Why this exists

The owner chose the direction for loops in the prover: the compiler finds invariants itself, from the program's flow and the relations between the variables, and as strongly as it can. An `assert` in a loop may become an invariant, but nobody has to write one. This is option C of the loop decision, which grew out of the owner's second example program, and it matches #599, point 4.

Unrolling a loop for a proof (option D) was not chosen as the general way:
- Its cost grows with the number of turns, not with what a claim needs.
- It stops at an arbitrary limit.

The owner asked for this reminder so it can be looked at again **for special cases**.

#### What to check later

- **Short loops with a known number of turns.** SPARK unrolls a `for` over a range of at most 20 turns that has no written invariant (ADR-314 §2). Find where invariant inference fails in such loops and unrolling would prove the claim.
- **Values that grow nonlinearly over a few turns.** For example `value = value * 10 + d` over at most two digits, ADR-314's case. Interval widening loses these, and only unrolling or a written invariant keeps them.
- **`T::fields` loops.** These are already unrolled once per type (ADR-304). Check whether a proof could read those unrolled bodies.
- **As a fallback.** Unroll only where inference answers *unknown* and the number of turns is bounded by a known small number. The cost must stay within ADR-258's rule that only the claim that needs it pays.

Nothing needs this yet. It is a note, not a decision.

[Back to the list](#note)

### <a id="issue-599"></a>[#599](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/599) Deliver a standalone, API-accessible general-purpose proof assistant

`note` · opened 2026-10-13 · updated 2026-10-17 · 0 comments

Deliver a standalone, API-accessible general-purpose proof assistant

Goal

Nikaia should provide a standalone, independently executable proof assistant that can be used by external tools and other programming languages through a stable public API.

«Nikaia provides a standalone, externally usable proof assistant that can systematically model and prove properties of data structures, functions, and loops, and independently check its proofs.»

This is a product-level goal, not a claim that all these capabilities are already implemented. The existing logic and solver architecture in "ADR-270" (https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/blob/main/docs/specification/adr/adr-270.md) is a starting point. The current limitations described in "ADR-269" (https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/blob/main/docs/specification/adr/adr-269.md) need to be addressed deliberately.

Subtasks

1. Standalone executable and stable public API

- [ ] Deliver a standalone executable that accepts proof problems without requiring the Nikaia compiler or a Nikaia project.
- [ ] Define and document a stable public API for submitting queries and retrieving results, proof certificates, and countermodels.
- [ ] Provide a documented integration path for external tools and other programming languages, through a library API and/or a language-neutral protocol.
- [ ] Specify supported input formats and compatibility/versioning guarantees for the API and proof formats.

2. General properties over data structures

- [ ] Extend the logic beyond arithmetic-focused theories to express properties of structured data, including records, fields, tuples, algebraic data types, constructors, and pattern matching.
- [ ] Define the semantics required for equality, aliasing, and mutable or shared data.
- [ ] Provide a way to express assumptions, preconditions, postconditions, invariants, and goals independently of Nikaia-specific syntax.
- [ ] Make supported theories explicit and extensible; report unsupported or undecidable cases as "unknown" rather than treating them as proved.

3. Function behaviour and effects

- [ ] Model function behaviour through contracts, including preconditions, postconditions, and frame/effect specifications.
- [ ] Support reasoning about function calls and composition of contracts, including propagation of relevant facts across call boundaries.
- [ ] Define how side effects, mutable state, and state changes are represented and reasoned about.
- [ ] Specify sound behaviour when a function implementation or contract is unavailable, incomplete, or cannot be proved.

4. General loop-invariant reasoning

- [ ] Provide a way to state or infer loop invariants and prove initialization, preservation, and the loop-exit consequence.
- [ ] Support invariants over structured data and program state, not only simple scalar arithmetic.
- [ ] Define the intended balance between user-supplied invariants, invariant inference, and automated proof search.
- [ ] Test loops involving nested control flow, mutation, and function calls.

5. Independently checkable proofs

- [ ] Preserve distinct outcomes: "proved", "refuted" (with a countermodel where supported), and "unknown" (with an explanation).
- [ ] Produce proof certificates that can be checked independently of the solver that generated them.
- [ ] Keep the trusted proof checker small and document the trusted computing base.
- [ ] Ensure compiler optimizations and removal of runtime checks rely only on independently checked proofs, never on unchecked solver answers.

6. Logic coverage and solver roadmap

- [ ] Define a roadmap for quantifiers, nonlinear arithmetic, and theories beyond the initial QF_LIA/QF_LRA focus.
- [ ] Specify how additional theories and data types will be supported, including dedicated decision procedures or solver backends where needed.
- [ ] Publish a capability matrix distinguishing implemented, planned, experimental, and unsupported features.
- [ ] Add conformance tests and benchmarks against standard problem formats and established solvers.
- [ ] Add soundness tests for certificates and regression tests for countermodels and "unknown" results.

Acceptance criteria

- The proof assistant can be executed as a standalone program without invoking the Nikaia compiler.
- An external program can submit a proof problem through a documented, versioned interface and consume a structured result.
- The system can express and prove representative properties involving structured data, function contracts and effects, and loop invariants.
- Unsupported cases are reported honestly rather than silently treated as proved.
- Successful proofs include certificates accepted by an independent checker; refuted claims include usable countermodels where supported.
- Documentation clearly describes the supported logic, limitations, soundness boundary, and roadmap for broader theories and automation.

Related design records

- "ADR-270 — Logic and solver architecture" (https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/blob/main/docs/specification/adr/adr-270.md)
- "ADR-269 — Current contract/proof limitations" (https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/blob/main/docs/specification/adr/adr-269.md)
- "ADR-306" (https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/blob/main/docs/specification/adr/adr-306.md)

Notes

“General-purpose” describes the intended direction, not a promise that every mathematical theory is decidable or every claim can be proved automatically. The system must remain sound, expose its limits, and allow users to supply specifications and invariants when automation is insufficient.

[Back to the list](#note)

### <a id="issue-577"></a>[#577](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/577) A tree rebuilt by value reuses the memory of the node it replaces (Lean 4 / Koka "functional but in place")

`compiler`, `note` · opened 2026-10-10 · updated 2026-10-10 · 0 comments

#### Where this comes from

From the decision on #576 / #558 (2026-10-10, [ADR-094](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-094.md) D7). Two ways change a tree in Nikaia:

* **In place**, through a `mut` parameter: take the parts in a `match` arm, change them, assign the whole again. ADR-094 D7 allows this, with two conditions: the parameter is whole on every way out, and no `throw` and no call that can fail stands between taking and giving back (the owner chose this strict form, "A1").
* **By value**: `fn wrap(e: E) -> E { return match e { E::Add(a, b) => E::Add(wrap(a), wrap(b)), … } }`. This runs today.

Below, a recursive enum's children are `Box<E>`. The by-value form frees each node's box and allocates a new one of the same size, once per node. Lean 4 and Koka write the same functional code. Where the old node is no longer referenced, their compilers hand its memory to the new node ("reuse analysis", Perceus / "functional but in place"), so the program reads functionally and runs in place.

#### Why it is low priority

* **D7 already gives the in-place path.** A rewrite that needs no failing call is written with a `mut` parameter and needs no new node at all. That path depends on how #576 lowers it: it should keep the boxes (`*e = E::Add(a, b)` with the same `Box`es) rather than unbox and rebox them. That belongs in #576, not here.
* **The allocator often does it by accident.** A `free` immediately followed by a `malloc` of the same size class usually returns the same block from the thread cache. What is left is the cost of the two calls, and that is not measured.
* **Nothing measured asks for it.** No benchmark or self-hosting module is known to be bound by this.

#### What value is left once D7 (A1) is built

1. **Rewrites that can fail.** A1 forbids a failing call while a part is out, so every tree rewrite that calls something `throws` must be written by value. This is the main remaining case. Checker and emitter walks that report errors may be among them once they are Nikaia (#558).
2. **Code that is by value by nature**: desugaring, constant folding (`fold.nika`), `text_tiers`-style rewrites that build a new tree from an old one, and code moved from Rust that was already written that way.
3. **Cheaper in Nikaia than in Lean/Koka.** Their reuse checks a reference count at run time. In Nikaia a value held by a `mut` or by-value parameter has one owner, which the compiler already knows (ADR-005, ADR-094). So the reuse can be decided statically, with no check left in the program.
4. **Not for `Vec`**: Rust's `into_iter().map(..).collect()` already reuses the buffer when the element layouts match. The gain is boxed enum nodes, and possibly `String`.

#### To find out first (the measurement is owed before anything is built)

1. A tree of 10^5-10^6 nodes rewritten once by a by-value function (`wrap` above) and once by the D7 in-place form, both through `nikaia run`. Count instructions and mispredicted branches with callgrind, against hand-written Rust that reuses the box (CLAUDE.md: count, never time). The same output first.
2. From `callgrind_annotate`: what share of the by-value version is `malloc`/`free`.
3. Only if that share matters: a design for the lowering (where a `match` arm takes a node by value and builds a node of the same variant size, write into the old box), and a record (ADR) for it.

Until 1 and 2 are measured this issue stays `note`.

[Back to the list](#note)

### <a id="issue-549"></a>[#549](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/549) Doc tests built once: each block a package of its own, all of them modules of one crate - measure first

`tools`, `note` · opened 2026-10-08 · updated 2026-10-08 · 0 comments

#### Where this comes from

Three places in this repository check Nikaia programs that live outside a package's own tests. Each one carries its own loop:

* `crates/nikaia/tests/examples.rs` runs `nikaia test` once per package in `examples/`.
* `crates/nikaia/tests/specification.rs`, through `specbook`, takes every `nika` block of Part I-III and lowers it.
* `tests/guide/` (a Nikaia package) builds every `nika` block of `guide/` as a project of its own and runs it with `nikaia run`.

`nikaia test` checks one package (Part III 13.2). It has no doc tests, and no way to take several packages in one call. Every caller therefore builds the loop itself, and every block or package is its own Cargo build. One package is one crate today (ADR-286 D19, `project.rs` `cargo_workspace`), so 14 guide blocks mean 14 rustc and link runs for 14 small programs.

The owner's view is that compiling each block alone is the wrong default, because it costs time for no gain. There should be a way to build them together with the same isolation, and a switch to build them one by one.

#### The idea

**The isolation is the language's own: a package is a directory and a namespace** (Part I 9.1). Two blocks that both declare `main` or `Item` cannot share a package. So each block becomes a package of its own: a directory, its own names, its own ledger. Blocks marked `// file: src/X.nika` join the package of the block that follows, as `guide/syntax.md`'s module example already writes.

**The Rust side keeps those packages apart without a crate each.** For a doc-test build, each package lowers to a `mod` of **one** crate instead of a crate of its own. Its `main` becomes an entry the test binary dispatches to. A Rust module is a namespace as separate as a crate for everything a package can declare, so the separation drawn by directories survives. What it saves is the per-crate rustc and link work.

**At run time each block is still alone**: one process and one empty directory per block, which is how `nikaia test` already runs `test` blocks and output tests (14.1).

Further rules:

* A block that a page marks as refused (`NK…`) is settled by the checker and never reaches rustc.
* Blocks whose build settings differ (`user_parallelism`, target, dependencies) form separate groups, one crate each.
* Anything the lowering makes crate-global (crate attributes, `extern` symbols such as `examples/c-library`'s, assets) moves the block to a build of its own, automatically.
* `--separately` builds every package as its own crate, as today. If the merged build fails in rustc, the blocks are rebuilt one by one to name the culprit; that failure is a C.1 defect in any case.

Prior art:

* Rust 2024 merges doctests into one crate, one module each, runs each in its own process, and keeps `standalone_crate` as the opt-out.
* Go compiles a package's `Example` functions into its one test binary.
* Python's `doctest` shares one namespace per docstring; that is rejected here as a default, because a block must stay copyable on its own.

#### To measure (before anything is built)

Count, never time (callgrind, `--branch-sim=yes`), release builds:

1. **Where one block's build goes**: the instructions in Nikaia's front end (parse, check, ledger, lowering) against Cargo, rustc and the link, for the 14 projects of `tests/guide/` run one by one as today.
2. **A merged build by hand**: the lowered Rust of the same 14 blocks, each wrapped in `mod block_N` with `main` renamed, plus a dispatching `main`, compiled as one crate with the same settings. The output of each block must match first, byte for byte.
3. The same comparison for `examples/`' packages, to see whether "several packages, one crate" is worth having beyond doc tests.

If (1) shows the front end dominates, or (2) saves little, this stops here with the numbers. Otherwise it becomes a `decision`: where doc tests are declared (the manifest naming Markdown files, or a `nikaia test` argument), the block markers, and whether `specbook` and `examples.rs` move onto it.

[Back to the list](#note)

### <a id="issue-514"></a>[#514](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/514) Use cases for `f[T]()`, a type named in brackets at a call (NK1233 asks for them)

`note` · opened 2026-10-07 · updated 2026-10-07 · 0 comments

#### What this issue is for

Nikaia allows a call to name its type arguments in brackets: `parse[Config](text)`. Every such call draws the warning `NK1233`, which links here.

**Please leave a comment** with:
- the call you wrote;
- why the type could not come from an argument (`f(x)`), or from the type passed where the parameter list holds one (`schema(User)`, ADR-330 D1);
- what you would have written instead, if anything.

#### Why it is allowed, with a warning

This came up in the decision round on describing a type without a value (#121, [ADR-330](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-330.md)).

- **Two forms were weighed.** `schema(User)` was chosen for describing a type: the type sits where the parameter list holds it. `f[T]()` had been rejected in ADR-323 §3 as *"a new form for the whole language, for one use"*.
- **Hints that it may be needed:**
  - a function whose result type is the only place `T` appears;
  - `meta::column`.
- **Why a warning:** the owner chose to allow it and collect the real use cases. `NK1233` exists for that purpose.

**Known costs:**
- `f[x]` reads like an index; only the declaration of `f` tells the two apart.
- There are two ways to name a type at a call.

#### What happens with the answers

- The use cases decide one of three outcomes:
  - the warning goes, and `f[T]()` stays;
  - `f[T]()` is removed in favour of `schema(T)`;
  - `f[T]()` is limited to the cases that need it.
- The status stays `note` until enough cases are in. The decision itself is an issue of its own, labelled `decision`.

[Back to the list](#note)

### <a id="issue-497"></a>[#497](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/497) Decide: the checker stays silent about a type it does not know (Part III C.4) - is that right? -> no

`compiler`, `note` · opened 2026-10-06 · updated 2026-10-10 · 7 comments

**What it is.** Part III C.4 says: *A message appears only where both sides are written down. Where a type is not known, such as a method on a receiver `std` has no signature for, or what a `?` unwraps, the compiler says nothing, which is not the same as approving. The checker never rejects a program that is correct.* ADR-286 D34 (*it fails open*) and ADR-010 D1's polarity are the same idea applied elsewhere.

**What a program sees today.** Where the checker says nothing, the program is lowered to Rust, and rustc's refusal reaches the user in rustc's words. ADR-005 D7 maps it to the `.nika` line, but it is still about a file nobody wrote (Part III C.1). This session alone found five such cases:
- a mixed integer comparison, `push` and a generic `T` (#482, now fixed);
- `Bytes::to_text` (#489);
- `return ref` (#442);
- `nowhere::wobble` (#488).

**Where the question comes from.** The owner, in the decision round on #488 (6 Oct): the silence for types the checker does not know is questionable and should be looked at closely. Nothing is blocked. But every silence is a place where a defect reaches the user as a backend message.

**A decision basis is owed before this is asked:**
1. **Inventory:** every place in `check/mod.rs` that returns *unknown* and stays silent, grouped by cause:
   - no ledger signature;
   - an unresolved method;
   - a `?`;
   - a generic;
   - a type the checker cannot work out;
   - others.
2. **Measurement:** of the programs in `tests/errors` and the examples, how many end in a rustc refusal, and from which group.
3. **How others do it:**
   - Rust and Swift: a name or type that cannot be resolved is always an error;
   - TypeScript `strict`: no implicit `any`;
   - mypy: `--strict` vs gradual typing.
4. **Options**, each with what goes wrong if it is wrong:
   - keep failing open;
   - fail closed (an unknown type is an error that names what is missing, e.g. a ledger entry);
   - fail closed per group, where the cause is a gap of this compiler rather than of the program;
   - a warning in between.
5. **Recommendation**, after the measurement.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-06](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/497#issuecomment-6026625145):

#### Measured: refuse every unknown type, and see what breaks (6 Oct)

The owner's direction: refuse, and look at what breaks. Hard-coded on a local branch and not pushed. The patch is kept by the deciding session.

**What was changed.** In `Checker::expr`, every expression whose type comes out `Ty::Unknown` became an error. Three things were left out:
- an integer or float literal, and an open number (ADR-285 D23), which are typed later by their uses;
- structural expressions (`+`, `if`, block, `match`, `return`, `throw`, `catch`, range, `break`, `continue`), which are only unknown because a part is;
- an expression with an unknown part that was already reported (a cascade).

So each count is a **root cause**. Only a top-level `Unknown` was counted; an unknown inside a type (`Vec[?]`) was not.

**Corpus:**
- every `.nika` under `examples`, `tests`, `benches` and the test fixtures: 62 files, 59 of them clean before;
- `std`'s Nikaia half (`nikaia lower-std`).

##### Result

| | Programs (62 files) | `std` tools (80 files) |
|---|---|---|
| Root causes | 139 distinct (187 with repeats) | 120 distinct |
| Programs clean before that now fail | **24 of 59** | lower-std would stop at the first |
| Real program errors found | **0** | **0** |

**By cause** (programs + std):

| Cause | Programs | std | Kind |
|---|---|---|---|
| A grammar's bindings (`s:until(…)`, `t:term`) and what is computed from them | ~42 | ~94 | checker limit: a grammar binding gets no type |
| `std` paths the ledger does not type: `fs::Root::Anywhere`, `http::Method::Post`, a constructor as a value (`Summary`) | 20 | 2 | ledger gap |
| The error bound in a `catch` (`f"{error}"`) | 20 | - | checker limit |
| A float `let` typed by its use (`let mut px = 0.0`), and indexes into a list of them | ~15 | - | checker limit: open floats are not tracked like open integers |
| `x.parse()` typed by the annotation (`let n: i32 = …`) | 7 | - | checker limit: result typed by use |
| A lambda's parameters (`fn(acc, m)`, `fn (row)`) | ~7 | - | checker limit |
| A `dsl` block's result and its fields | ~9 | - | checker limit |
| Function values passed as arguments (`holes_of`, `name_is`), `match` on such a call | - | ~14 | checker limit |
| Methods with no signature: `sort_by_key`, `as_str`, `is_some` | 5 | - | ledger gap |
| `html::escape`, `hyper_shim::serve_once` (foreign) | 5 | - | ledger gap / foreign |
| `panic(…)` | 2 | - | checker limit: no never type |
| loop counters, others | a few | ~10 | mixed |

##### What it says

- **Today, failing closed would break 24 of 59 working programs and most of `std`'s Nikaia half**, and it would find no program error.
- **Almost all of it is the checker's own blind spots**, not the programs':
  - grammar bindings (by far the largest group);
  - `catch` bindings;
  - lambda parameters;
  - open floats;
  - typing by use (`parse`);
  - `dsl` results;
  - function values;
  - the never type.

  Each is a gap that can be closed: the type is knowable in every one of these cases.
- **The ledger gaps are a smaller group** (about 30): `std` enum variants and constructors used as values, and a few methods.

So the useful order is to close the gaps first, then turn on failing closed. The five cases found today that rustc reported were all in places where the checker *did* know the types and simply did not ask (#482, #489, #442, #488). They are a different class from this one.

##### Possible next decisions (for the owner)

1. **Fail closed as the goal:** C.4 changes once the gaps are closed.
2. **Close the gaps in order of size:**
   - grammar bindings;
   - `catch`;
   - lambda parameters and function values;
   - open floats;
   - typing by use;
   - `dsl` results;
   - never type;
   - ledger entries.

   Each is a build issue.
3. **Measure again after each one** with the same patch; switch on failing closed when the count of programs that were clean before reaches 0.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-07](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/497#issuecomment-6030853101):

#### Decided (owner, 7 Oct): failing closed is the goal; the gaps are closed first, one issue per kind

- **Goal:** the checker refuses an expression whose type it does not know. C.4's *says nothing* is changed in the specification **when the switch is turned on**, not now.
- **Order:** one build issue per kind of gap, linked below. Re-measure after each with the patch below. Turn on failing closed when no program that was clean before breaks; that step gets its own decision round (ADR + C.4).
- This issue stays open as the tracker, labelled `note`.

##### The measuring patch (apply on a local branch; never push)

Measure: `nikaia lower` every `.nika` under `examples tests benches crates/nikaia/tests`, then `nikaia lower-std`, and count `NK9999` / `STRICTSTD` lines.

```diff
--- a/crates/nikaia/src/check/mod.rs  (in Checker::expr)
         self.reached = self.reaching.take();
+        let strict_before = self.checked.findings.iter().filter(|f| f.code == "NK9999").count();
         let ty = self.value_of(expr, span);
+        let strict_cascade =
+            self.checked.findings.iter().filter(|f| f.code == "NK9999").count() > strict_before;
 …
             self.a_value_with_a_cleanup_made(&ty);
         }
+        let open_literal = matches!(expr, Expr::LitInt { .. } | Expr::LitFloat(_))
+            || matches!(expr, Expr::MethodCall { receiver, .. } if matches!(**receiver, Expr::LitInt { .. }));
+        let open_number = match expr {
+            Expr::Variable(name) => self.binding(self.parsed.text(*name)).is_some_and(|l| l.open_number.is_some()),
+            _ => false,
+        };
+        let structural = matches!(expr, Expr::Binary { .. } | Expr::Unary { .. } | Expr::If { .. } | Expr::Block(_)
+            | Expr::Match { .. } | Expr::Return(_) | Expr::Throw(_) | Expr::TryCatch { .. } | Expr::Range { .. }
+            | Expr::Continue | Expr::Break);
+        if matches!(ty, Ty::Unknown) && !open_literal && !open_number && !strict_cascade && !structural {
+            let kind: String = format!("{expr:?}").chars().take_while(|c| c.is_alphanumeric()).collect();
+            self.checked.findings.push(Finding { severity: Severity::Error, span: *span, code: "NK9999",
+                message: format!("STRICT unknown type: {kind}"), notes: Vec::new(), help: None, labels: Vec::new() });
+        }
         ty
--- a/crates/nikaia/src/sysroot.rs  (in lower_tools_at, before `let refusals`)
+        let source_text = std::fs::read_to_string(&path).unwrap_or_default();
+        for f in checked.findings.iter().filter(|f| f.code == "NK9999") {
+            let line_no = source_text[..f.span.at().min(source_text.len())].matches('\n').count() + 1;
+            eprintln!("STRICTSTD\t{}\t{}\t{}", path.display(), f.message, line_no);
+        }
   … and `.filter(|f| f.code != "NK9999")` on `refusals`.
```

The full version adds the method, field or callee name to the message. It is kept in the deciding session's scratchpad, which is temporary.

##### Built in this round

- #504: `text::parse_<T>` (0.0.733).
- #509 and #507: `NK1171` for a member a known type does not have (0.0.734). The corpus run behind it found:

- 0.0.735-0.0.737: the kinds the measurement showed. Each is a type written down nearby:
  - an `if`/`match` of numbers is an open number (a branch that leaves is no value), and an `if` of a typed number and a literal is the typed one;
  - `for k in 0..<n` over literals, and `for x in` a literal list, bind the open number;
  - a bare pattern name binds the matched value, and a pattern inside a pattern binds from its part's type;
  - a lambda returned, put in a field or bound by an annotated `let` takes the declared `fn` type;
  - a field read through `Shared` is the held value's field;
  - a grammar binds `dec[f64](…)` as an `f64`, and a choice of rules that agree as their type.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-10](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/497#issuecomment-6097266830):

#### develop is green again under failing closed (0.0.739-0.0.741)

After c723cb8 (0.0.738, NK1245 switched on), `cargo test --workspace --release` had 72 failing tests. Three commits fixed them forward. CI run 765 on 52903c9 (0.0.741) passed.

| Commit | Failing tests (nikaia package) |
|---|---|
| c723cb8 0.0.738 | 72 |
| 28b96c0 0.0.739 | 52 |
| 8fecfa3 0.0.740 | 19 |
| 52903c9 0.0.741 | 0 (whole workspace) |

##### Now typed (the checker knows the type)

- A method of a generic type of the program's own binds `T` from the receiver: `h.seen()` on a `Holder[i64]`.
- `Vec()` whose first `push` is a number holds one open number (ADR-285 D34).
- `x ?? "none"` over a `T?` whose `T` nothing typed has the fallback's type.
- A lambda in a list of a declared `fn` type takes that type.
- A `dsl` statement with `:name` holes is its text.
- `full()` on a type with `impl Error`.
- `Seen[T]` answers `T`'s methods.
- A type seen through (`deref`: `fs::Mapped`, `Shared`) answers methods and indexes with what it holds.
- A package's enum case is a value of the enum (`http::Method::Post`).
- A dependency's files are checked against what that package itself reaches, under its own name for it (`c::Id` where this build calls the package `deep`).

##### Not refused where they stand (ADR-286 D41, Part III C.4)

The owner should look at these. Each is a place where NK1245 is not said, rather than a gap closed:

1. **NK1245 waits for the other refusals.** Where a file holds any other error, NK1245 is dropped. Usually its unknown type is that error's leftover.
2. **A name is asked where it is bound, not where it is read.** This covers a `let`, a loop, a lambda parameter and a spread, and also a member of a name bound without a type.
   - Risk: an untyped lambda parameter (`fn(x) { x + 1 }` in a place that declares no type) is no longer refused at `x`. Only the lambda value is refused, and only where it is not passed directly to a callee.
3. **What an open number passes through is open:**
   - `hand(7)`;
   - `Holder { value: 21 }.value`;
   - a task's `join()`;
   - an index or member of a value whose type argument is still `?`;
   - `xs ?? 0` and `xs ?? return` over a `T?` nothing typed.

   Risk: `rx.recv() ?? return` over a channel of a struct nothing typed is silent too. The channel's element type is not inferred from `send`.
4. **Left to a later refusal or another build:**
   - `asset("…")` takes its `comptime`'s type;
   - a DSL driver's `Self::dsl` is a type of each call site;
   - a call through a package whose ledger this build does not read is left to that package's own build;
   - `dsl markdown {…}` and a missing grammar entry are refused by the lowering, which names what is missing.

##### Tests and examples changed

- `typecheck::a_field_of_an_unknown_type_says_nothing` is now `…_is_refused`: it expects NK1245.
- The "fine" program in `lambdas.rs` read `.id` of an `i64`; it now reads it of a `Row`.
- `run_names_the_file` needed a program the checker passes and rustc refuses. It now uses `u8 << 9`.
- `tests/language/src/kept_functions.nika` used Rust's `Duration::from_millis`; it now uses `1.millis()`.
- Three specification blocks rustc refused are now NK1245: Part I 4.3, 4.7 and 9.1. They are fragments that use a `User` declared elsewhere, or an `http::read` the package does not have. `EXPECTED.txt`, `COMPILES.txt` and the floor (60 to 57) follow.
- Already red before NK1245, and fixed on the way: a clippy `question_mark` lint on `std::text` and a `clone_on_copy` lint in `check/mod.rs`.

##### Still open from this issue

- Channels: the element type should come from `send`, so `rx.recv()` gets a real type instead of item 3's exemption.
- Untyped lambda parameters (risk in item 2).
- D34 for a map's values.

### <a id="issue-475"></a>[#475](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/475) After the MVP: a query's rows without repeated values in memory (Shared fields, zero-copy rows, SQLite specifics)

`performance`, `note` · opened 2026-10-06 · updated 2026-10-06 · 1 comments

**After the MVP.** Nothing here is decided, and nothing here is built for the MVP. This issue keeps the ideas from the decision round on [#114](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/issues/114) / [ADR-299](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-299.md), so that the rules for rows and target structs, decided now, leave these optimisations open.

#### The problem

Take a chain of many-to-one links: `accounts.user → users.address → addresses.country → countries { id, name, code }`. A query asks for every account together with its country's name and code:

```nika
struct Land  { name: String, code: String }
struct Konto { id: i64, iban: String, c: Land }

let q = dsl sqlite(schema: APP) -> Konto {
    SELECT a.id, a.iban, c.name, c.code
    FROM accounts AS a
    JOIN users AS u ON a.user = u.id
    JOIN addresses AS d ON u.address = d.id
    JOIN countries AS c ON d.country = c.id
} eod
```

- The `JOIN` gives one row per account, so the number of rows does not multiply.
- The values repeat, though. Each row owns its strings, so 100 000 German accounts hold `"Deutschland"` 100 000 times once the program collects them.
- A `for` loop that drops each row holds only one row at a time.

ORMs solve this with an identity map. Nikaia has no ORM (ADR-299 D5), so the question is whether the language can still let a program avoid the repetition.

#### What works today, by hand

A program can run two queries and assemble the result itself with `Shared[T]` (Part I 6.2):

```nika
struct Land       { id: i64, name: String, code: String }
struct KontoZeile { id: i64, iban: String, land: i64 }
struct Konto      { id: i64, iban: String, land: Shared[Land] }

let nach_id = laender.execute(db).map(|l| (l.id, Shared(l))).collect()
let alle = konten.execute(db)
    .map(|z| Konto { id: z.id, iban: z.iban, land: nach_id[z.land] })
    .collect()
```

- The row struct is an intermediate. The program builds its own struct from it.
- No rule for "fields filled by hand" is needed, and none was made.
- `missing_fields` stays what it is: a missing `?` field becomes `null`.
- Not checked: the exact spelling of `Shared(l)`.

#### Ideas

##### 1. `Shared[T]` on a field or a group of a target struct

```nika
struct Konto {
    id:    i64,
    iban:  String,
    c:     Shared[Land],     // a group: equal countries once
    stadt: Shared[String],   // one column, no key: equal names once
}
```

The compiler-written conversion (ADR-299 D8) would merge equal values within one `execute`.

**Why value equality is enough, without a notion of record identity.** A `Shared[T]` cannot be changed, and as far as the specification goes a program cannot ask whether two handles point at the same memory (there is no `ptr_eq`). So merging is invisible: it saves memory and means nothing.

A primary or unique key from the schema would then only speed up the comparison (compare the id instead of every field), not define it. The DDL grammar reads `CREATE TABLE` and so sees `PRIMARY KEY` and `UNIQUE`, but `meta::column(name, type)` does not pass them on. A call such as `meta::key(table, columns)` would.

**Costs:**
- **Memory can grow instead of shrink.** The conversion keeps every distinct value until the `Seq` ends. A loop that drops rows would then hold every distinct value of a column, which is worse than without `Shared` for a column of mostly unique values.
  - Open: release a value early once no row holds it any more.
- **Hashing costs on every row**, also for values seen only once.
- **Merging only within one `execute`.** A program-long interner, like the grammars' `Symbol` table (Part II 10.6), never frees anything, which is wrong for rows from a database.

Today a group field of type `Shared[T]` is refused because it is not a struct. Turning that refusal into a permission later changes no existing program, so the target-struct rules do not block this.

##### 2. Rows as views into the driver's buffer

Where merging happens matters less than *when*:

- **Copy first, then compare:** 100 000 allocations, 99 800 of them freed again.
- **Compare first, then copy:** hash the bytes in place and allocate only for a new value, about 200 allocations.

The second needs the `Connection` trait to hand back a row as a view into the driver's buffer, valid until the next row. That is what SQLite (`sqlite3_column_text`, valid until the next `sqlite3_step`) and libpq hand back anyway. Without `Shared` it also saves one copy per cell: today it would be into a `db::Value` and then into the field.

Open:
- ADR-299 D8 only says *"hand back rows of `db::Value`"*. Whether a `db::Value` owns its memory is not defined, and `std::db` does not exist yet.
- Whether Part I 6.6's views can express a lifetime bound to "the next row" in a trait is not checked. Compare `rusqlite`'s `ValueRef<'stmt>`.
- Cost: a driver cannot hold a row past the next one, and a driver that fetches ahead must buffer.

##### 3. What the server delivers

| Database | How a row arrives | `"Deutschland"` for 100 000 accounts |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| SQLite (in process) | a pointer into SQLite's buffer, valid until the next row | rewritten 100 000 times into the same buffer |
| PostgreSQL | one `DataRow` message per row, each column as length and bytes; no dictionary in the standard protocol, as far as known | 100 000 times over the wire |
| MySQL | as PostgreSQL; the wire can be compressed (zlib/zstd) | fewer bytes on the wire, 100 000 times after unpacking |
| Columnar (Arrow Flight SQL, DuckDB, ClickHouse) | blocks per column, text often as a dictionary plus indices | once, plus 100 000 small numbers |

With row protocols a driver can find repeats only by hashing, as the conversion can. Only columnar protocols deliver the merge for free, and using that needs a trait that hands back blocks rather than rows. That is a separate question about the shape of `std::db`.

The normalisation lives only in the database file. The `JOIN` undoes it on the server.

##### 4. SQLite through `extern "C"` (ADR-302)

`examples/sqlite/main.nika` binds SQLite directly; `crates/nikaia/tests/foreign_pointers.rs` builds and runs it. What being in process offers:

- **Zero-copy reads** (`sqlite3_column_text` and `sqlite3_column_bytes`: pointer and length).
  - ADR-302 cannot express this today. A view lives for one call (D5), and text C hands back is a `foreign::CStr` that is copied (D8).
  - It would need a view owned by a C handle, valid until a named function is called on that handle.
  - Side finding: a `CStr` has no lifetime the language knows. One kept past the next `sqlite3_step` points at freed memory, and ADR-302 checks nothing there.
- **Prepare each statement once per connection** (`sqlite3_prepare_v2` on first `execute`, then `sqlite3_reset`). This is the driver's work and needs no decision.
- **Letting SQLite check the SQL at build time** (`sqlite3_prepare` against an empty database built from `schema.sql`, plus `sqlite3_column_table_name` / `sqlite3_column_origin_name`) would know exactly which table each column comes from.
  - It is ruled out by [ADR-321](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-321.md) D9: only Nikaia code and `std`'s Rust half run at build time. The driver's grammar parses the SQL itself.
  - This is a real loss in precision, accepted on purpose.
- **What SQLite does not give: reliable types.** A column declared `INTEGER` can hold text unless the table is `STRICT` (3.37+). The conversion has to check each value's type at run time (`sqlite3_column_type`), whatever the schema said at build time.

#### What has to stay open now

- A group or field of type `Shared[T]` in a target struct is refused today. That refusal may later become a permission.
- The shape of `db::Value` and of the `Connection` trait's rows (owned values or views) is not fixed by the MVP in a way that excludes views.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-06](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/475#issuecomment-6013404877):

**Why SQLite may not check a statement while the program is built** (asked by the owner).

The idea would be: the driver's grammar opens an in-memory database from `schema.sql`, calls `sqlite3_prepare_v2` on the statement, and reads `sqlite3_column_origin_name` / `_table_name` / `_decltype`. Four things stand in the way, in order:

1. **Build-time code may touch nothing but the build's parameters** (ADR-287 D14), and that is read from the ledger's `touches`.
2. **A C declaration touches everything** (ADR-302 D4, fail-closed), because a C signature says nothing about what the function does. So `sqlite3_prepare_v2` is refused at build time by D14 already (`NK1152`).
3. **A package may not claim otherwise** (ADR-321 D9). If a driver could write *touches nothing* over a C function, any package could write it over `system()` or `unlink()`. The compiler cannot tell `sqlite3_prepare_v2` from `system()`: both are names in an `extern "C"` block. That is Rust's `build.rs` / proc-macro hole, a supply-chain path onto the developer's machine and CI.
4. **Reproducibility.** `examples/sqlite` links the machine's `libsqlite3`, and versions accept different SQL (`RETURNING` since 3.35, `STRICT` since 3.37). One project would build on one machine and not on another, and the build cache key (ADR-321 D4) does not contain the system library.

**What it costs:** the grammar has to replicate SQLite's name and type resolution.
- Where it is stricter than SQLite, a program is refused and rewritten.
- Where it is looser, `sqlite3_prepare_v2` reports the error at the first `execute` at run time.

Neither gives a wrong value.

**The one way it could be allowed, not decided:** the compiler ships and vouches for one fixed SQLite, as it does for `std`'s Rust half (ADR-020 D5). The price:
- a database engine in the compiler, for one driver;
- PostgreSQL could never have the same, since it needs a server and so network access while building;
- the grammar route, by contrast, works the same for every database.

### <a id="issue-423"></a>[#423](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/423) Standard input as a full pipe pays the worker hand-off per 8 KiB chunk (ADR-263 §6: measure first)

`libraries`, `note` · opened 2026-10-04 · updated 2026-10-06 · 1 comments

#### What it is about

ADR-263 D1 (#412) reads a **regular file** on the calling thread when the runtime is idle. Standard input stays on an I/O worker (ADR-263 §6). That holds up for a terminal or a slow peer: the read waits far longer for the input than the hand-off costs.

It does not hold for **standard input as a pipe that is already full**:

```sh
cat measurements.txt | prog
```

Here the bytes are there at once, as a file's are. A program that walks the input a line at a time (`io::lines`, Part III 17.1) reads it in chunks through `rt::io::stdin_chunk`:

- `CHUNK` is **8 KiB** (`crates/nikaia-std/src/io.rs`), so 13 GB of input means about **1.6 million** hand-offs to a worker and back. Each one is a channel send, a worker wake-up, a reply and a park.
- Each chunk's buffer is **zeroed** before the read fills it (`vec[0_u8; want]` in `rt/worker.rs`, `Op::StdinChunk`). That is the same waste ADR-263 D3 removed from the file path.

`io::read_to_string` / `io::read` (all of stdin at once) make a single hand-off and are not affected.

#### Work

1. **Measure first.** Run a program that counts lines from a large piped stdin under callgrind, against the same program reading the same data from a file. Record the difference per chunk.
2. **Cheap parts before any decision:**
   - Offer the buffer as capacity instead of zeroing it (as D3 did for the ring).
   - Check whether a larger `CHUNK` (e.g. 64 KiB) changes the count. The single read per chunk stays, so a slow pipe still yields whatever is there.
3. **Only if it still measures:** a calling-thread path for stdin on an idle runtime, under ADR-263 D2's condition. It would need to:
   - read only when the runtime is idle (D2), so blocking on a silent pipe harms nobody, since nothing else is waiting;
   - leave a terminal on the worker, or show that it does not matter there.

   This would be an amendment to ADR-263 §6, not a new setting (ADR-303 D5).

#### Not in scope

Sockets. They are non-blocking and read on readiness, and the hand-off is noise beside network latency (ADR-263 §6).

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-04](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/423#issuecomment-5982462644):

Steps 1 and 2 are done in 0.0.442 (99f9961).

**The measurement.** I did not use callgrind here: under valgrind the threads run serialized, and every hand-off to the worker becomes a scheduler round, so the run did not finish in 20 minutes. Instead I took wall-clock times. A program that counts lines and their bytes through `io::lines()`, `opt-level = 3`, 4 M lines (60 MB, `handwritten gen`), best of 9, interleaved, 4 cores:

| | `cat data \| prog` | `prog < data` |
|---|---:|---:|
| 8 KiB a hop, buffer zeroed (before) | 791 ms | 671 ms |
| 8 KiB a hop, no zeroing (`take(want).read_to_end` into capacity, measurement only) | 799 ms | - |
| **64 KiB a hop**, zeroed (now) | **414 ms** | **389 ms** |

- **Zeroing the buffer costs nothing that shows**, so it stays. The variant without zeroing reads until `want` bytes have arrived and so breaks the one-read rule. It was only a measurement.
- **The chunk size was the cost.** `CHUNK` is now 64 KiB, a pipe's capacity on Linux. It is still one read a hop, so a slow pipe hands over whatever is there.

Step 3, a calling-thread path for a full pipe, is not built. It amends ADR-263 §6, and whether it still pays at 64 KiB has to be measured against a file read of the same lines first. §6 says that much now.

### <a id="issue-420"></a>[#420](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/420) 1BRC's stats update costs twice what tuned's does

`compiler`, `performance`, `note` · opened 2026-10-04 · updated 2026-10-06 · 1 comments

On `examples/1brc.nika` (0.0.424, `benches/brc/README.md`), recording a reading costs **23 instructions a row** against `tuned`'s 11, outside the hash table itself.

`Summary::record` writes `entry(m.name).and_modify fn (stats) { stats.add(m.temp) }.or_insert_with fn { Stats(m.temp) }`: two lambdas through the entry API, and `Stats::add` keeps the overflow checks of `sum += temp as i64` and `count += 1`, which are the language's (Part I; ADR-314 cannot prove them, a sum over a file is unbounded).

To find out first: how much of the 12 is the lowering of the `and_modify` / `or_insert_with` chain, and how much the checks. Only the first is this issue's; the example's spelling is not to get less elegant for it.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-04](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/420#issuecomment-5981850334):

**Measured, the question this issue asks first:** how much of the cost is the `and_modify` / `or_insert_with` chain and how much is the overflow checks. The generated Rust of `examples/1brc.nika` at 0.0.440 (`record` already `#[inline]`, #433) was built with the profile `nikaia build` writes: `opt-level = 3`, 256 codegen units, overflow checks in the program's crate only. Each variant changes one thing. Callgrind `--branch-sim=yes`, 1 M rows, every variant printing the same bytes.

| variant | instructions a row | mispredictions a row |
|---|---:|---:|
| as emitted | 300.2 | 1.57 |
| `Stats::add` without its two overflow checks (`wrapping_add`) | 298.2 | 1.57 |
| `record` as `match get_mut(k) { Some(s) => s.add(t), None => insert(k, Stats::new(t)) }` | **274.3** | **2.50** |
| `TrustedMap` on hashbrown directly, chain unchanged (#434) | 297.2 | 1.58 |

What this says:
- **The checks are 2 instructions a row.** They are always predicted, as #432 says, and they are not where the gap is.
- **The chain is most of what is left.** Without it the row costs 26 instructions less, now below `tuned`'s 313. But the variant pays 0.92 mispredictions a row more, which on the clock can eat what the instructions save. `get_mut`/`insert` is also not a general lowering: it probes twice for a new key (#434).
- **hashbrown directly** takes 3 of the 26 and is uniform: it also saves 3 % on an insert-heavy program (#434).

So the part that belongs to this issue is the lowering of the chain. A lowering that keeps one probe for a new key and does not move the cost into branches is still to be found. The clock has to judge the mispredictions, not callgrind alone.

### <a id="issue-373"></a>[#373](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/373) A `Shared` a call hands back stays atomic at `yes` even where it never crosses a thread

`note` · opened 2026-10-03 · updated 2026-10-06 · 0 comments

#### What

At `user_parallelism = yes`, `contracts::sharing` lowers a `Shared` to a plain count where it can prove the value never crosses a thread. ADR-037 D7 says the analysis may only take an atomic away, never add one.

A `Shared` that came out of a **call** is never given a plain count today. It is forced to the atomic floor through `Fallback::UnseenOrigin` (`origin_unseen` in `crates/nikaia/src/contracts/sharing.rs`), because the analysis did not see the allocation being made.

That seed was once needed. `task::both`, the pool the compiler used for automatic statement overlapping, could run a call on a thread of its own and hand its result back. That path is gone:
- the automatic grouping was withdrawn (ADR-292 D1);
- `overlap2`..`overlap8` poll every branch on the block's own thread (ADR-292 D4).

So the reason a returned `Shared` crosses has disappeared, while the seed stays. The result is correct (an atomic count is never unsafe, ADR-312 D11), but slower than it needs to be: about 9 ns per clone-and-drop pair (ADR-037 D6).

#### What it would take

- **Follow the allocation across the call:** the callee's ledger would have to say whether the `Shared` it returns was allocated there and never crossed. This is a new piece of the `sharing` contract (ADR-312 D11 keeps that contract at the floor, so the column's meaning would need a decision), or an intra-package analysis that reads the callee's body.
- **Keep the polarity:** a missed seed is unsoundness, not a missed optimisation (ADR-037 D7). Every crossing the call itself may perform must still force the floor.
- **Prove it pays first:** measure on a program that hands `Shared` values out of functions at `yes`, before building anything.

#### Not in scope

`user_parallelism = no` already makes every count plain (ADR-312 D10).

### <a id="issue-125"></a>[#125](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/125) Stage 1: `main`'s pipeline (read, check, lower) runs Nikaia end to end

`selfhosting`, `note` · opened 2026-09-30 · updated 2026-10-11 · 4 comments

[ADR-250](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/main/docs/specification/adr/adr-250.md) D5, road in [ADR-294](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/main/docs/specification/adr/adr-294.md). Reached when read, check and lower run Nikaia from end to end, with Rust left only as adapters to crates (`winnow`, `clap`, `toml`, the file system).

**Where it stands (0.0.426):** 29.1 % of the toolchain is Nikaia — 17 297 lines in 59 files under `crates/nikaia-std/src/tools/*.nika`, against 42 178 lines of Rust in `crates/nikaia{,-logic}/src` (`scripts/self_hosting.py`).

**Moved so far** (see `scripts/self_hosting.py` for the list, by version): the syntax tree, the constant fold, the checker's types and what it asks of them, the ledger (read, written, published), `describe`, the trait check, `throws`/`locks`/`touch` and their walks, views, text tiers, buffers (`contracts::keep`), the checker's words, type and call helpers, `NK1116`, written-back expressions, the build-time integer (`integers.nika`) and value (`build_values.nika`).

**Still Rust, largest first:**

| Module | Lines |
| :--- | ---: |
| `check` | 23 802 |
| `emit` | 14 597 |
| `contracts` | 4 926 |
| `parser` | 4 377 |
| `project.rs` | 3 519 |
| `bounds.rs` | 2 723 |
| `prove.rs` | 2 553 |
| `nikaia-logic` | 2 184 |
| `build_time.rs` (the evaluator's walk) | 1 318 |
| `modules.rs` | 1 105 |
| `diagnostics` | 871 |
| the rest (`main`, `sysroot`, `text_tiers`, `grammar_run`, `manifest`, `describe`, adapters) | ~4 000 |

**How a move goes:** one package per version, leaves first; a defect the move finds is fixed in the compiler in the same package (ADR-294 D3), never bent around in the `.nika`. Where the Rust reached for a wider number (`i128`) out of convenience, the move takes the better alternative instead (ADR-315's `checked_` names, `integers.nika`).

**Honest scale:** by the spike on #558 the walks and the emitter are about 77 % of `check` + `emit`; reaching 90 % of the toolchain needs all of the above, including `project.rs` and the grammar. Nothing in the language blocks it any more; what is left is volume, builds (10 to 30 minutes each on the shared machines) and the disk (it ran out three times).

**How to work** is in CLAUDE.md, section *Moving Rust into Nikaia*. The short form: one agent per large module, never two on one file, leaves first, clippy before every push, delete `target/` of a worktree you do not use.

### <a id="issue-100"></a>[#100](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/100) `nikaia describe` for a crate declared by version, and the rustdoc-JSON reader

`tools`, `note` · opened 2026-09-30 · updated 2026-10-06 · 0 comments

Records: [ADR-104](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/main/docs/specification/adr/adr-104.md) (steps 1-3 built, step 5 by hand), [ADR-195](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/main/docs/specification/adr/adr-195.md) D4, [ADR-196](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/main/docs/specification/adr/adr-196.md) D4. `nikaia describe <crate>` works today for a `path` dependency (a **signature scraper**, not a Rust parser).

**Open:**
- **The rustdoc-JSON reader** behind a toolchain check (it needs nightly; the same ADR-001 D1 wall as macro expansion).
- **A way to read a version dependency's sources**, which are in Cargo's registry cache and which ADR-002 D1 hands to Cargo. That is what would make the hash rule (`NK2505`) reach every crate rather than the ones with a `path`. It runs through `cargo metadata`, so it needs the **directory walk in `fs`** (the subprocess, `process::run`, is built: ADR-243).
- **A suspicion, not a defect:** a manifest declaring a crate whose word is one of `std`'s modules would have two answers for one name; `std` wins silently today. No manifest in the repository reaches it; the day one does it is a refusal to write.

**One rule to keep (ADR-193):** the describer **proposes and never claims**; it may propose `true` and must never propose `false` for `threads`/`crosses`.

The rewrite of the command itself in `.nika` is tracked under self-hosting.

`language`, `note` · opened 2026-09-30 · updated 2026-10-06 · 2 comments

Record: [ADR-246](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/main/docs/specification/adr/adr-246.md) D5. D1-D4 are built (`tests/recursive_types.rs`); three refinements are not, in this order:

### <a id="issue-91"></a>[#91](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/91) Self-hosting: Nikaia compiling Nikaia (Stage 1 and Stage 2)

`selfhosting`, `tools`, `note` · opened 2026-09-30 · updated 2026-10-06 · 0 comments

The owner's instruction is to take self-hosting **as far as it goes**. Records: [ADR-001](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/main/docs/specification/adr/adr-001.md) D4 (where the road ends), [ADR-250](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/main/docs/specification/adr/adr-250.md) (how it is walked), [ADR-252](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/main/docs/specification/adr/adr-252.md) (the tree), [ADR-257](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/main/docs/specification/adr/adr-257.md) (the checker's types).

**Where it stands** (`scripts/self_hosting.py`, also the README badge): **5.1 %** of the toolchain is Nikaia, about 2 500 of 48 500 lines.

**The road:** a module moves whole, behind its own interface, leaves first; the Rust module stays as the adapter (D1, D2). **Every move is a test of the compiler**, since each found defects the suite had not, and they are fixed in the compiler in the same package, never bent around in the `.nika` (D3). The share is measured and published (D4).

**Moved so far:** *did you mean*, a `dsl` body's parameters, `describe`'s reader, `Fixed`'s hash, the HTML template scan, the ledger's line reader, what `nikaia.toml` may say, **the whole syntax tree** (a `Span` is two `u32`, `Spanned[T]` is generic), the constant fold, `--trust`'s text, and `Ty`/`Shape` plus the ledger's small records.

**Stage 1** is reached when `main`'s pipeline (read, check, lower) runs Nikaia end to end, with Rust left only as adapters to crates (`winnow`, `clap`, `toml`, the file system). **Stage 2** is reached when the lowered compiler lowers its own sources to byte-identical Rust and a test compares the two (D5).

**Remaining rings, in order:** the checker's types and algorithms; the modules that read only the tree and the types (`dsl`, `traits`, `types`, `contracts::*`, `views`, `diagnostics`); then the ones that read everything: `check` (21 k lines), `emit` (13 k), `parser` (3.9 k), `project` (3.2 k).

Sub-issues carry the next steps and the measured costs.

### <a id="issue-90"></a>[#90](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/90) The HTTP server, second half: TLS, HTTP/2, more bodies

`libraries`, `note` · opened 2026-09-30 · updated 2026-10-06 · 0 comments

Owner's order: **last** of the five, and its minimal form went first. Record: [ADR-038](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/main/docs/specification/adr/adr-038.md) §4.5.

**Built:** the runtime before `main`, files on `io_uring`, sockets signalling readiness (D3-D5); a socket in `std` (ADR-198, 0.0.164); the socket layer (ADR-199, 0.0.165); a minimal HTTP/1.1 server with `http::listen` and its caps (ADR-194 D5, 0.0.166; `examples/hello-http` is driven over a real socket by `tests/project.rs`); its parser written in Nikaia as a grammar (0.0.248, `nikaia-std/src/tools/http1.nika`). `nikaia serve` is **cut** ([ADR-200](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/main/docs/specification/adr/adr-200.md) D1).

**Open** (sub-issues): `rustls` (D2), HTTP/2, the bodies of ADR-058 and ADR-018 D2, and many connections at once (the server handles one at a time today). Also route hashing, which has no target until a server carries routes.

**One piece was answered without the server:** a name the request chose reaching the filesystem is [ADR-108](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/main/docs/specification/adr/adr-108.md): the root is an argument of the call (`http::File(path, root)`), built for `fs` at 0.0.178.

**Measured before it is built:** `benches/sendfile/` and `docs/history/zero-copy-send.md` price the five ways a file reaches a socket; `mmap` per request is 2.5x worse than plainly reading at 4 KiB, the trap `http::File` would otherwise walk into.

### <a id="issue-89"></a>[#89](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/89) A database is reachable: `std::db`, and a driver that checks the SQL while the program is built

`libraries`, `note` · opened 2026-09-30 · updated 2026-10-06 · 0 comments

Owner's order: **first** of the five big pieces, since every later demo stores something in it. Records: [ADR-143](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/main/docs/specification/adr/adr-143.md) (all of it) and [ADR-254](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/main/docs/specification/adr/adr-254.md) (where the connection goes). Nothing built.

The compiler knows **no SQL**: a dialect is a grammar in a driver package. A grammar declares a result column with `meta::column(name, type)`, the third and last intrinsic of the hybrid binding, and the compiler derives the statement's row type from the columns as it derives the parameter type from the holes. A `dsl` block takes build-time arguments, named, no `;` (`dsl sqlite(schema: app) { … } eod`), resolved from `comptime` values, and the driver's grammar reads the schema with its own DDL grammar and refuses a missing column **at the query**. `std::db` is the protocol only (traits, statement, row values); `sqlite` and the rest are packages. No expression capture, no ORM; `raw(text)` for dynamic SQL. The connection is the statement's subject and is typed by its schema (ADR-254): `by_age.execute(db; min_age: 18)`, and a connection of another schema is refused at the call.

**What was blocking is gone:** running a grammar while the program is built is built ([ADR-177](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/main/docs/specification/adr/adr-177.md)), and the shape this needs, a flat list of declared columns and parameters, is one of the five that cross. What is left is the driver's own work.

Order of work is in the sub-issues (ADR-143 §5). `examples/fortunes.nika` waits on this and on the HTTP handler work.

**Open question:** how a schema is bound while the program is built (`let app = comptime asset(…)` vs `comptime APP: db::Schema = asset(…)`) is on `docs/open-decisions.md`, recommendation (1), the item form.

### <a id="issue-483"></a>[#483](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/483) ADR-324: no build script; a package links a C library by name (pkg-config or -l), with listed linker flags

no labels · opened 2026-10-06 · updated 2026-10-09 · 5 comments

#### How it came up

The question started from Cargo's build-script problem and how Nikaia avoids it. While preparing it, Part III 13.4 turned out to describe `build.nika`: a program compiled and run before the build, with a `std::build` API (`build::cc`, `build::rustc_link_lib`). That is Cargo's `build.rs` again. It had no ADR and was not built, and it contradicted ADR-287 D14 and ADR-321 D15.

The need behind it is real. `examples/sqlite` says *"`nikaia` has no option for linking a C library, so `-l sqlite3` goes to `rustc` through `RUSTFLAGS`"*.

**What was checked:**
- Cargo, Zig (`build.zig` is as powerful as `build.rs`), Go (`#cgo` directives, plus a flag allow list since CVE-2018-6574) and SwiftPM.
- The usual case: a package offering Nikaia over a C library should just build for its user. Network databases (MySQL, PostgreSQL) need no C at all: Go's drivers and `sqlx` speak the protocol natively. C is needed for in-process libraries: SQLite, codecs, crypto, OS APIs.
- **Compiling C from a package's sources** runs nothing of the package if the build sets the flags. But it **reads**. Tried: `#include "/…/secret.txt"` printed the file's content in the compiler's error. C23 `#embed` puts it into the object (GCC 15, Clang 19; here GCC 13 and Clang 18 lack it). No GCC or Clang switch forbids absolute paths, so it gets past ADR-310.

#### Decision (owner)

[ADR-324](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-324.md), Part III 13.4, `NK1224`, `NK1225`:
- **D1** — No build script: no `build.nika`, no `std::build`.
- **D2** — A package names the system libraries its `extern "C"` blocks need. Every program that uses the package links them, and its author writes nothing.
- **D3** — Found through `pkg-config` where a `pkg-config` name is given, else `-l name`. The compiler forms the arguments. Not found: `NK1224`, naming the package, the library and the `pkg-config` name.
- **D4** — From `pkg-config --libs`, only `-l`, `-L`, `-pthread`, and on macOS `-framework` and `-F`. Any other flag: `NK1225`.
- **D5** — No C sources in a package for now, because a C compilation reads any file.

**Open:**
- **where the library's name is written** (beside the `extern "C"` block, in the manifest, or both), the next question;
- cross-compiling;
- Windows;
- C sources.

#### To build

Blocked on the spelling question (D2) for steps 1-2.

1. Read a package's library declarations, wherever D2's answer puts them, and carry them in the package's ledger, so a consuming program knows them without the package's sources.
2. **Linking:**
   - For each library: with a `pkg-config` name, run `pkg-config --libs <name>` and filter by D4, else use `-l<name>`.
   - Pass the result to `rustc` for the program, with no `RUSTFLAGS` needed.
   - Per ADR-321 D14, never pass it for the build-time library.
3. **`NK1224`** when `pkg-config` fails or the library is not found at link time. Test with a declared library `nikaia_test_missing`.
4. **`NK1225`.** Test with a `PKG_CONFIG_PATH` pointing to a test `.pc` whose `Libs:` contains `-Wl,-plugin,/x.so`. The build is refused and the flag is named.
5. **`examples/sqlite`:** declare `sqlite3`, drop the `RUSTFLAGS` note, and check that `crates/nikaia/tests/foreign_pointers.rs`'s end-to-end test runs without `-l sqlite3`.
6. **Test:** a program depending on a package that declares `sqlite3` builds without naming the library itself.

**Comment** by keywan-ghadami, [2026-10-06](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/483#issuecomment-6016459305):

#### Spelling decided (owner): ADR-324 D2, D3 and D6 revised

The `extern` block names its library, and the manifest says how the library is found. Both are required.

```nika
extern(library: "sqlite3") { … }
```

```toml
[library.sqlite3]
pkg-config = "sqlite3"
```

**How it came up:** the owner disliked `extern "C"`, and proposed the *subject ; options* form, `extern(extern.C; library: …)`.

**What was checked:**
- ADR-284 D4/D22: `"C"` is the only convention on every target.
- ADR-298 D1: there are no contextual keywords, so `extern "C" library "…"` would reserve `library`.
- `extern.C` is no path this language writes, and `foreign::Abi::C` is an enum with a single variant.

So the convention is left out entirely. A second one, if ever needed, would come in as a subject before a `;`.

The owner then rejected a `pkg-config`-with-`-l`-fallback default, because the name would appear several times and the default would be invisible. `pkg-config` is a required value, *"written so rarely that explicit is better to copy"*.

**Rules now:**
- **D2:**
  - Every `extern(library: "x")` needs a `[library.x]` entry, and `pkg-config` is required in it.
  - A missing entry, or an entry no block names, is `NK1226`.
  - A block without `library` links nothing of its own.
- **D3:** a library is found only through `pkg-config`. No `-l` fallback. If `pkg-config` is missing, or the library is not found, the build stops with `NK1224`.
- **D6:**
  - `extern { … }`, `extern(library: …) { … }`, `pub extern fn`, `pub extern struct`.
  - `extern "C"` is `NK1227`, with `extern` as the help.
  - This changes ADR-302 D1's and ADR-284 D4's spelling.
  - The specification's four `extern` blocks are fragments to today's parser; `EXPECTED.txt` and `COMPILES.txt` record that.

**Not decided:** a minimum version, cross-compiling, Windows (no `pkg-config` there).

#### To build, replacing steps 1-2 and 5 above

1. **Parser:**
   - `extern` with an optional `(library: "<name>")`, before a block.
   - `pub extern fn` and `pub extern struct`.
   - `extern "C"` gives `NK1227`, help `extern`.
   - Update `ast.nika`'s `abi` field, which is no longer written.
2. **Manifest:**
   - Read `[library.<name>]` with a required `pkg-config` string.
   - `NK1226` both ways: a block naming a library without an entry, and an entry no block names.
   - Carry the libraries in the package's ledger for consuming programs.
3. **Linking:**
   - For each named library, run `pkg-config --libs <pkg-config>`, filter it by D4, and pass the result to `rustc` for the program.
   - Never pass it for the build-time library (ADR-321 D14).
4. **Rewrite everything to `extern`:**
   - the 15 spellings in the specification (done in text), `examples/sqlite`, and `crates/nikaia-std/src/tools/*.nika`;
   - drop `examples/sqlite`'s `RUSTFLAGS` note, and add `[library.sqlite3] pkg-config = "sqlite3"` to its project;
   - the end-to-end test in `foreign_pointers.rs` runs without `-l sqlite3`.
5. **Regenerate** `tests/specification/EXPECTED.txt` and `COMPILES.txt`. The four `extern` blocks of Part III 15.1 compile again.

#### Step 1, and steps 4 and 5 in part: 0.0.563 (2cf4d8d)

- **Parser:**
  - `extern { … }` and `extern(library: "<name>") { … }` parse. `library` is a keyword only in that position, like `opaque`/`released`/`by`.
  - In `ast.nika`, `Item::Extern` now has `abi: String?` (set only where a convention was written) and `library: String?`.
- **`NK1227`:** `extern "C"`, or any other string, still parses. The checker then refuses it with *`extern` takes no convention*, help `extern { … }` / `extern(library: "name") { … }`. The emitter refuses the same thing as a backstop. The lowering is Rust's `unsafe extern "C" {` as before.
- **Rewritten:**
  - `examples/sqlite` now uses `extern(library: "sqlite3")`. Its `RUSTFLAGS` note stays until step 3.
  - `foreign_pointers.rs` and `foreign_declarations.rs` use `extern`.
  - `refusal_lines.rs` now expects NK1227's message.
  - The old "unknown ABI" test is now a NK1227 test for `"C"` and `"stdcall"`.
- **Baselines:** Part III 15.1's four blocks and 13.4's `extern(library: "sqlite3")` lower and compile again (`EXPECTED.txt`, `COMPILES.txt`).

**Not part of this:** `pub extern fn` (with a body, an entry point) and `pub extern struct`. They were not built under the old `extern "C"` spelling either: the parser has no rule for them. I left them out rather than parse something nothing lowers.

**Next:** step 2 (`[library.<name>]` with a required `pkg-config`, and `NK1226` both ways), then step 3 (`pkg-config --libs`, filtered by D4, handed to rustc for the program only; `NK1224`, `NK1225`). After that `examples/sqlite` becomes a project with `[library.sqlite3]`, and the `-l sqlite3` in `foreign_pointers.rs`'s end-to-end test goes.

#### Steps 2-6 built: 0.0.567 (c95a7c1)

- **Manifest:** `nikaia.toml` reads `[library.<name>]`, with `pkg-config` required. Any other key in the table is refused (`manifest.rs`).
- **`NK1226`, both ways, per package, before anything is linked:**
  - A block's `extern(library: "x")` with no entry. The help shows the table to add.
  - An entry no block names.
  - The names are read from the package's own `.nika` files, parsed even on a cache hit (`libraries::declared`).
- **`pkg-config --libs`** runs for each entry (`libraries::link_flags`):
  - `NK1224` where `pkg-config` is missing or does not find the name. The message names the package, the library and the name looked for.
  - D4's filter: `-l`, `-L`, `-pthread`, and on macOS `-framework`/`-F`. Anything else is `NK1225`, naming the flag.
- **Linking:**
  - The build collects the flags of every member, writes them into the switches file (so a change rebuilds), and hands them to the wrapper.
  - The wrapper appends them, as `rustc` arguments (`-l x`, `-L native=dir`), only to the `--crate-type bin` crate.
  - The build-time library is built elsewhere and never sees them (ADR-321 D14).
- **`examples/sqlite`** is a project with `[library.sqlite3]`. `foreign_pointers.rs`'s end-to-end test runs `nikaia run --project` on a copy and passes no `-l`. It skips where `pkg-config` does not know `sqlite3`.
- **Tests (`tests/system_libraries.rs`):**
  - `NK1226` both ways.
  - `NK1224` with `nikaia_test_missing`.
  - `NK1225` with a `.pc` file whose `Libs:` holds `-Wl,-plugin,/x.so`.
  - A named library linked without a flag.
  - A program that depends by path on a package naming a library gets that library's `-l` in its link. It is checked with a `.pc` naming an absent library, whose link fails by name.

**What is left:** step 2's *carry the libraries in the package's ledger*.
- Today every package is built from its sources, so the build reads each one's manifest directly.
- The ledger entry only matters for a package used without its sources, which nothing here can do yet (registry undecided).
- I'm leaving the issue open for that one point. If you'd rather track it with the registry work, this can close.

### <a id="issue-449"></a>[#449](https://github.com/Nikaia-Foundation/Nikaia/issues/449) Build ADR-319: a file name is `fs::Path`, the platform's own bytes

`language`, `libraries`, `compiler` · opened 2026-10-05 · updated 2026-10-09 · 9 comments

#### How it came up

`docs/open-decisions.md` had *What `Path` is*. Part I 7.1 (`enum ConfigError { NotFound(Path), … }`) and every signature of Part III 17.1's `std::fs` wrote `Path`, but nothing declared it. The Part I example is refused with `NK1135` (*There's no type called `Path`*). ADR-096 §4 had left the question to the owner.

#### What was checked

- **`std` already treats names as text.** The `fs` entries take `path: ?` (Rust `impl AsRef<Path>`), `fs::Root::Dir` holds a `String` (`fs.rs`: "text in this language is one type"), and `io::IoError`'s variants all hold a `String`.
- **A `String` is UTF-8 by construction**, because it lowers to Rust's `String`/`&str`. Every entry point refuses bytes that are not UTF-8 with `NotText`. Part I only said so indirectly.
- **So a file whose name is not UTF-8 cannot be reached.** `fs::walk` stops at the first such name (`fs.rs:537`).
- **Other languages** (ADR-319 §2): Rust `Path`/`OsStr`, camino, Haskell `OsPath`, Swift `FilePath`, Python surrogateescape, Go/Zig bytes, .NET/Java lossy.

#### The course of the decision round

1. The first recommendation was "a path is a `String`, a byte-capable type when someone needs it". The owner rejected it: Nikaia is meant to be universally usable, and UTF-8-only contradicts that.
2. The owner proposed bytes or a byte view, like Zig.
3. The requirement *all platforms* then ruled out a plain byte view, because on Windows (WTF-8) not every byte run is a valid name. That leads to a type of its own with the platform's bytes inside.
4. `Path → String` can fail, and the failure looked target-dependent. Part I 1.2 (one source, every target) rules out a target-dependent contract. Besides, a Mac can mount NFS or SMB volumes. The answer was to let the **source** of the value decide, not the target.
5. To protect against `display()` text flowing back into the file system, the round first considered taint tracking. The owner then proposed that `f"{p}"` give back a `Path`. Letting the hole's contents decide would break ADR-309 D2, so the variant chosen is that **the use** decides (as in ADR-152 D4). `display()` is accepted and not tracked.

#### The decision in short

[ADR-319](https://github.com/Nikaia-Language/Nikaia/blob/develop/docs/specification/adr/adr-319.md); specification: Part I 2.2 (a `String` is always valid UTF-8), Part I 2.5 (`f"…"` builds a `Path` where its use asks for one), Part III 17.1 (`fs::Path`, `walk -> Vec[Path]`). Version 0.0.474.

- D1: `fs::Path` holds the platform's bytes (Unix raw, Windows WTF-8, UTF-8 where names are Unicode). It is made only from text, by the OS, or by its own operations, so no call fails for its encoding.
- D2: text stands wherever a `Path` is asked, as a view without a copy.
- D3: `==` against a path or text, `starts_with`, `ends_with`, `file_name`, `extension`, `with_extension`, `parent`, `join`. Each returns a `Path`.
- D4: `f"…"` builds a `Path` where its use asks for one. `print`/`println` write its bytes as they are. A `Path` hole in a text `f"…"` is refused.
- D5: `to_text()` `throws NotText` only for a path whose source is the OS, on every target.
- D6: `display()` shows `�` and is not tracked.
- D7: `IoError`'s name variants and `Root::Dir` carry a `Path`, and `walk` no longer fails on names.
- D8: a `String` is always valid UTF-8.

#### To build

- **Where the compiler can follow the name back to text,** `p.to_text()` resolves to an entry that cannot fail (`fs::Path::text_of`, not `pub`), and the function needs no `throws`. Text-made means: a literal, a `String`/`str`, an `f"…"` whose name holes are text-made, or `join`/`with_extension` over such names.
- **Anything else throws `io::IoError` (`NotText`),** the same on every target, with `NK2605` if the function does not say `throws`. That covers a parameter, a field, and what `walk` listed.
- **How it is followed:** within one function, binding by binding, which is ADR-010 D1's fail-safe direction (a barrier widens). It is the same scope as #431's per-map trust: a parameter's source is not followed across calls.

**Also changed:**
- `tools/sources.nika` writes `{name.to_text()}` where a walked name becomes a `BTreeMap[String, …]` key; it is relowered.
